# core-design

## deduction and elimination
- [definition] A straw man is a suspect built to be knocked down, so that later progress looks like real advance.
- [definition] Backward (analytic) reasoning starts from a known result and reconstructs the events that produced it, while forward (synthetic) reasoning predicts results from known causes. When: Investigating a known outcome such as a body or scene. Why: Reversing the direction clarifies which causes fit the result.
- [definition] A departure is the starting point a solver needs before forming any hypothesis; without one, reasoning about an unexplained object cannot begin.
- [definition] A closed crime is committed within a known circle of people; an open crime could have been committed by almost anyone. When: Defining who could be the culprit in a mystery. Why: The size of the suspect pool changes how the case is reasoned.
- [mechanism] A sequence of confirmations, each narrowing the suspect pool, lets the audience follow the logic and makes the later reversal feel earned.
- [mechanism] Each reappearance of a cryptic phrase that adds a detail, such as a count or a time, narrows the phrase from vague to a specific place or moment. When: a riddle is spread across several documents. Why: incremental detail constrains the set of possible meanings.
- [mechanism] When a sealed-room claim is disproved, the suspect pool widens to everyone with access through any adjoining room.
- [mechanism] Each new name produced by an unverifiable witness widens the suspect group, spreading suspicion to people with no other link to the case.
- [mechanism] A suspect board can close options as contradicting evidence is found, leaving one suspect who is provably the only remaining option.
- [mechanism] A deduction board lets players assemble clues into a theory, and the system then reports which items remain unexplained.
- [mechanism] A search by description returns several candidates, and each stable trait found removes others, so the field narrows only as stable traits are discovered. When: Players search for a character from a partial description.
- [mechanism] The order in which evidence reaches an observer shapes their conclusions; someone who perceives the scene before learning identities and stories forms impressions from the senses alone. When: When evaluating how a character or player interprets an unfolding scene. Why: Early information frames later interpretation, so sequence controls which conclusions feel natural.
- [mechanism] Physical evidence exceeding a cover's stated purpose, such as machinery far too powerful for the claimed work, exposes the false cover to a careful observer.
- [mechanism] A single constraint may match dozens of entries in a large table; several constraints together are needed to narrow the candidates to a few. When: when searching a large reference table. Why: one shared property rarely identifies a unique entry.
- [behavior] A calculated, self-interested culprit may see the victim as a risk to remove rather than a person to hate, so the motive can be cool rather than emotional. When: when reading or writing a planned act.
- [behavior] Several independent matching clues that agree with one another raise confidence far more than each clue adds alone, while a common trait adds little. When: Identifying a person or object from multiple details. Why: Each further matching detail multiplies the support of the others rather than adding a small increment.
- [behavior] Players who doubt a deduction often claim there is no positive evidence; restating the basis precisely usually makes the supporting facts visible. When: A player rejects a conclusion as unsupported. Why: Evidence is easy to overlook until the reasoning's starting point is stated clearly.
- [behavior] An objection to a search can come from pride, insult or vanity rather than guilt, so the reason behind each objection matters. When: when someone resists an inspection or search. Why: people object for social reasons as well as to hide things.
- [behavior] Vague doubt that does not commit to a testable claim does not advance an investigation; ideas must be tested, not merely accepted or rejected. When: A player or character voices uncertainty about a theory. Why: Unfocused skepticism produces no new information.
- [behavior] Investigators tend to accept a substitution explanation over admitting an event is unexplained, so records shape their conclusions. When: An escape or event lacks a clear cause. Why: A neat explanation is preferred to uncertainty.
- [behavior] Small illogical details are easily dismissed when many other contradictions are present, so players often miss them. When: when a scene contains many conflicting clues. Why: Attention is spread across many contradictions, so a single odd detail goes unnoticed.
- [behavior] Experts can overlook an obvious principle because it seems too simple to apply to a specific case. When: when a case looks too complex for a basic explanation. Why: simplicity seems beneath the problem.
- [behavior] Players and characters judge a fact by what it can prove, not by how interesting it is; an unrelated line of questioning is usually set aside once its bearing on the central facts is questioned. When: when a line of inquiry drifts from the central facts. Why: relevance is judged by evidential bearing on the outcome.
- [behavior] Players and observers who learn a regular routine notice a person who breaks it, and the deviation becomes a salient clue. When: A place or NPC follows a routine that others can observe. Why: A known baseline makes departures from it stand out.
- [rule] Establish clear routines, such as cleaner schedules or guard rounds, and a standard procedure, so deviations are recognisable and players can rule out explanations logically. When: designing scenes where an alternative explanation is possible or a routine players can learn. Why: Known routines turn exclusion into a checkable deduction; deviations read as meaningful only against a known sequence. (x2)
- [rule] Confirm a claim, especially a name recalled from memory, against records or the person best placed to know it before acting on it. When: a witness names a suspect in a tense moment. Why: an unchecked name can be wrong and then drive the investigation astray.
- [rule] Give players a fixed set of candidate causes or culprits, let observable clues eliminate them, and name the single answer only after enough independent clues exclude the rest. When: when designing a deduction puzzle or game investigation. Why: Premature single answers reward guessing rather than reasoning.
- [rule] Give players candidates such as suspects, motives or locations, each paired with a checkable clue or stated constraint that rules it out, so elimination rests on evidence. When: Designing a deduction puzzle with several candidates. Why: Players can verify exclusions instead of guessing; elimination against constraints turns scattered clues into a solvable set.
- [rule] Provide a visible deduction board where players list the crime's requirements, check each suspect against them, and drop theories that fail a check. When: designing a deduction interface or elimination puzzle. Why: Visible constraints let players follow the logic and see why theories fail; listing requirements makes elimination playable.
- [rule] Keep a deduction journal of facts, open hypotheses and candidate sequences, marking which remain unverified so players can revisit them as new clues appear.
- [rule] Design puzzles where players eliminate options using facts already revealed, and where each found object removes suspects so several clues must combine. When: a puzzle asks which suspect or option is correct. Why: elimination on revealed facts is solvable by reasoning rather than guessing; combining clues avoids reliance on one.
- [rule] Track eliminated leads and mark when a line of inquiry is exhausted, so cleared suspects no longer block progress and players switch trails.
- [rule] Treat a guess about a person, a proposed access route, or an elimination conclusion as provisional until independent evidence or a verified candidate list confirms it. Why: a hunch can be wrong and acting on it early compounds the error.
- [rule] Confirm a reveal with an independent source, such as a prior photograph, so the identification does not rest on one observation.
- [rule] Drop a candidate when their traits, such as character or age, contradict what the hypothesis requires.
- [rule] In a deduction mechanic, present remaining suspects as a weighted list after ruling out causes, keeping the candidate set complete.
- [rule] Let a narrow but true answer give the player a clue that becomes meaningful when compared with another character's account. When: Designing dialogue that reveals information through comparison. Why: Clues that only pay off when cross-referenced reward attention and reasoning.
- [rule] Only treat a trace as evidence if it can be matched to a specific object, using identical material or a known position, not mere resemblance. Why: resemblance alone can point to many objects.
- [rule] Give each suspect a list of evidence and let players weigh which items actually support guilt, rather than treating every item as equally strong. When: several suspects carry circumstantial evidence. Why: weighing evidence is the core of the deduction.
- [rule] Use shape-based clues only when the normal form of the object is known, since a deviation cannot be recognised without a baseline. When: judging whether an object's shape is anomalous. Why: Without a known standard, a deviation cannot be identified.
- [rule] Let the game test a candidate explanation against facts the player has not yet seen, so the hypothesis can fail.
- [rule] Give players a list of who knew each plan so they can deduce a leak by elimination. When: designing mystery or stealth puzzles.
- [rule] Design each clue to rule out a different wrong answer, so only the correct answer survives every test; a game can check each condition the player proposes. When: when building a deduction puzzle. Why: Distinct eliminations make the correct answer the sole survivor.
- [rule] Require two independent partial matches before a suspect is confirmed, so that one weak clue cannot settle the case. When: designing suspect confirmation in a mystery. Why: Independent evidence reduces the chance that a single weak match misleads the player.
- [rule] Check that every accused NPC has at least one clue against them and one defence, so no suspect is unfairly or trivially guilty. When: when designing a set of suspects.
- [rule] Confirm that the receiving party's visitors are genuine and that they hold the right to the object before taking custody of it. When: at a receiving point such as a shop or depot. Why: a receiver who is not satisfied may refuse, and a wrong handover loses the object.
- [rule] Track which suspects have been cleared by confession, so the true culprit's lead remains available to reopen. When: a quest or case closes on a confession. Why: a closed lead can otherwise be lost for good.
- [rule] When a witness's claim does not match visible facts, leave the gap open as a question rather than confronting the witness at once. When: A discovered mismatch is not yet strong enough to act on.
- [rule] In a tribunal mechanic, give the player's exculpatory evidence a clear route to the judge, and when a rule blocks it, show the block so the player can see the rigging. When: Designing a trial or tribunal where the player presents a defence. Why: A visible block lets players detect rigging rather than assume the defence was weak.
- [rule] A decree that invalidates a class of clues can remove an investigation branch mid-game without deleting the items involved. When: designing a mid-game change to an investigation.
- [rule] Offer several candidate locations for one past event, with one explicitly ruled out by an NPC, so players learn that matches are only probable. When: Designing a mystery where players link events to places. Why: Ruling out a candidate teaches players to treat matches as tentative.
- [rule] Give a descriptive clue, such as height or build, that narrows the list of suspects the player may question.
- [rule] Allow suspects to be eliminated only through in-game evidence, such as a verified location or documented trait, so the roster shrinks through play. When: when designing suspect elimination.
- [rule] Keep the candidate set small and explicit so players can run the elimination, and introduce any hidden candidate before the final reveal. When: a puzzle asks players to find the one actor who could have acted.
- [rule] Give each suspect a checkable movement window so players can test whether each alibi overlaps the time of the crime. When: In investigation puzzles with several suspects. Why: Players can only accept or reject an alibi if its times can be compared with the event.
- [rule] After the player clears an NPC, recompute the suspect shortlist by filtering on who was in the room where the key item sat.
- [rule] Track who has access to each key object, such as a potion or key, and remove suspects through state flags until a short list remains.
- [rule] Give a second suspect equally convincing evidence so players must test which chain of evidence is complete.
- [rule] Let players match a found object to a suspect's known routine, and reward them for reconstructing the sequence of events from it. When: Building a deduction mode. Why: Linking objects to routines turns evidence into play.
- [rule] A search mechanic that empties a character's pockets can clear an innocent character while revealing the item the culprit wanted hidden. When: Designing a search or inventory check in a mystery.
- [rule] Separate fact-gathering tools such as tracks, residue or scans from inference, letting the game supply facts while the player reasons about intent. When: designing investigation mechanics. Why: keeping data and reasoning apart makes deduction the skill tested.
- [rule] Show each eliminated candidate with the test that discounted them, such as access, strength or missing link, so the player sees why. When: Candidate suspects are listed in a game.
- [rule] Rule out a candidate when one measured dimension clearly falls outside the range of the trace, such as overall length or width. When: comparing a partial trace to a known sample. Why: A clear mismatch is reliable even when a faint trace cannot prove identity.
- [rule] Do not eliminate a suspect on one test alone, because a candidate may pass one check and fail another.
- [rule] Let an absent weapon imply its wielder still carries it, so players deduce enemy loadouts from what is missing. When: designing enemies whose equipment is hidden. Why: Missing items give players readable information without a direct display.
- [rule] Reason backward from a fixed conclusion: if the room was left by one route, that route must have closed itself after use. When: the exit used is known but appears closed. Why: the known outcome constrains which mechanism could have sealed it.
- [rule] Do not narrow options in a sequence where the player needs a clear path to a goal. When: when the player must reach a specific destination.
- [rule] Plant one observable trace, such as a stain or worn step, and let the player derive each link from facts found elsewhere in the level.
- [rule] Reward players who ask what a concealment protects, rather than what it claims to hide.
- [rule] Reason backward from an object's placement to the motive that moved it, such as an object left at an awkward angle by someone rushing.
- [rule] Let the player propose an alternative time window and have the game check it against recorded events, so the hypothesis is confirmed or rejected.
- [heuristic] Not every noticed detail is a clue; judge it against the whole case before acting, and treat odd details as meaningful only when the theory gives reason to expect them. Why: Unexplained details can be noise rather than evidence.
- [heuristic] Do not treat the suspect list as closed; ask who knew where an object was kept, since the first answer often omits outsiders who knew the secret. When: several people might know a hiding place. Why: Knowledge can leak to people who were never considered, so an unlisted outsider can go unnoticed.
- [heuristic] Test the opposite explanation when one account fits neatly, since a fully closed chain of evidence can be staged to look probable. When: one explanation fits the evidence neatly and no alternative has been examined. Why: Staged accounts are designed to look probable, so plausibility alone rewards the deceiver; a perfectly closed set of alternatives may have been engineered.
- [heuristic] Backward reasoning suits a known result needing its cause, while forward reasoning is more useful in ordinary situations. When: Choosing a reasoning direction. Why: Each direction fits different problems.
- [heuristic] After repeated checks find nothing, clear that character and redirect suspicion to another actor. When: Repeated surprise checks produce no detectable wrongdoing. Why: Keeps the player's suspicion from locking onto an innocent party.
- [heuristic] A search result that clears the obvious suspect can move the player's focus to another location or person. When: Designing the progression of a mystery. Why: Clearing the expected answer forces the player to reconsider.
- [heuristic] Treat everyone who has access as a suspect, however remote or trusted, so that no one is excluded by affection or habit.
- [heuristic] Deduce an unknown fact from known facts step by step, as in solving for an unknown quantity. When: when an investigation has partial information. Why: Working from established facts keeps each conclusion checkable.
- [heuristic] Clearing one suspect does not close the wider mystery, since the true actor may still be unknown. When: a suspect is cleared but the puzzle continues. Why: clearance removes one person, not the question.
- [heuristic] A case built from many facts pointing one way can collapse when one fact the suspect could not have done points elsewhere; treat that single mismatch as a break in the chain. When: many facts lean one way and one fits only a different person. Why: a single impossible act can invalidate an otherwise matching chain of evidence.
- [heuristic] When deciding which of two people was in a place at a given time, small traces often separate them more decisively than large, obvious clues. When: Two possible people could have been in the same place. Why: Minor details tied to one person or place can break a tie that broader evidence cannot.
- [heuristic] When a detail confirms a suspicion, ask one more question before moving on to the next line of inquiry.
- [heuristic] Have a second party confirm a disclosed motive; once confirmed, the investigator can shift from pursuit to pardon in a single exchange. When: a motive is revealed by an interested party. Why: Independent confirmation makes the account trustworthy.
- [heuristic] The outcome depends on the sum of evidence and mitigating facts, not on a confession alone. When: resolving a case with several strands of proof. Why: confessions are weighed alongside other proof.
- [heuristic] In a small, closed community with few people present, the list of possible culprits is short, so narrow suspects by checking each person's access and timing. When: an accusation rests on a small group of possible people. Why: isolation limits who could have acted.
- [heuristic] A suspect can be cleared when direct evidence is absent and an independent witness account does not fit them.
- [heuristic] Do not dismiss a witness only because a detail seems odd; an unlikely detail is not automatically false and needs a physical test first. When: When a witness's claim seems unusual. Why: Strange-seeming facts can be true, so they require checking rather than rejection.
- [heuristic] Avoid too many suspects with equal weight, since an even spread dilutes the trail. When: planning a mystery's cast. Why: equal weighting makes it hard to follow any single lead.
- [heuristic] A clue's value depends on distinctiveness: a common item fits many owners, while a unique mark identifies one.
- [heuristic] Give each culprit a defined set of facts, such as door codes or guard schedules, so players narrow suspects by which facts each could know.
- [heuristic] Assume a careful suspect has rehearsed an alibi and test it against independent checks rather than accepting it.
- [heuristic] Use the victim's profession to rule out missing items that someone in that role would be unlikely to carry. When: Several items could have been taken. Why: A victim's routine determines what they would normally carry.
- [heuristic] Ask who in a household shares an activity, since a shared activity narrows the pool of suspects.
- [heuristic] Compare each small object against its expected context; an item that does not fit suggests a second object or a second person.
- [heuristic] Handle accused people with previously unblemished reputations with extra care, because a sudden accusation damages them most.
- [heuristic] Use elimination when the set of possible agents is small and known; when the set is large or unknown, exclusion proves little. When: Choosing whether elimination can settle a cause. Why: Eliminating from a large unknown set leaves too many possibilities to rule out.
- [heuristic] Let players filter a suspect list by scene-derived markers, and make profile items checkable through testimony so an accurate profile unlocks the correct suspect.
- [heuristic] Present several candidate explanations with their supporting clues so the player can judge which fits the full set of evidence. When: Designing a deduction or mystery interface. Why: Players reason better when they can compare candidates against the same evidence.
- [heuristic] If an act targets a suspicion held by only one person, the actor must know that suspicion exists.
- [heuristic] When no regular option fits a required time, the remaining option is likely a special one. When: when known schedules constrain the options. Why: regular options are ruled out by their timetables.
- [heuristic] Test whether a companion of the suspect could be the real actor, and require evidence before accepting that alternative. When: A suspect has associates who had access to the scene. Why: Accomplices or companions can be the true actors and are easy to overlook.
- [heuristic] A regularity can be recorded and used before its cause is understood, so players and designers can act on a pattern without knowing the mechanism behind it. Why: Noticing a pattern does not require explaining it.
- [heuristic] Make the player's first interpretation of a scene plausible, then correct it with a visible clue such as a changed view or shifted object.
- [heuristic] Before assuming a room is empty or private, check each person present, since an unnoticed occupant may have heard everything. When: a conversation takes place in a room.
- [heuristic] A quest can restrict the player's knowledge to what an NPC reveals, then use a debrief scene to explain the full plan after completion.
- [heuristic] When a barrier's resistance to forcing is offered as proof of strength, first consider whether its material is weak or cheap.
- [heuristic] When a sealed space seems to forbid entry or exit, question the premise that something is wholly inside or wholly outside.
- [heuristic] Let players gather terrain knowledge under safe conditions earlier, so they can recognise a decoy guide later. Why: earlier surveys give a baseline for spotting false signs.
- [heuristic] Compare a space with your memory of it and note anything out of its usual place or angle, since small displacements reveal recent activity. When: a room looks untouched but may have been searched.
- [heuristic] Do not assume a locked room is impossible to enter; first look for a shared key or an open door. When: when a locked space seems inaccessible. Why: Locks often share keys or have unsecured entries.
- [heuristic] Give a prominent clue at least one plausible rival explanation that the player can build from other clues. When: designing a crime or mystery scene.
- [heuristic] Physical arrangement, such as body position or the state of a latch, can rule out explanations that would leave different traces. When: Testing whether an explanation fits the scene. Why: Each possible cause produces a characteristic physical state that can be checked.
- [heuristic] Rule out ordinary explanations one at a time before accepting an unusual cause, using a specific test whose outcome is a clear yes or no. When: player must choose between a natural and an unusual cause.
- [heuristic] Do not infer intent from every irregular act, since some deviations from a routine are harmless. When: Interpreting an observed deviation from a routine. Why: Many breaks from routine have ordinary causes.
- [procedure] 1) Examine each object for features and traces: maker, wear, dust, stains, smells, residue, missing parts, fittings, bulges. 2) Check each fact is specific and countable. 3) State each inference and its basis, marking certainty. 4) Infer height from where debris landed, then combine into a profile of build, age and habits. When: the suspect is absent and objects or the scene are the only evidence about a person. Why: Stating each basis lets inferences be checked, since objects carry traces of their users; showing facts before conclusions makes inference checkable.
- [procedure] 1) List every possible explanation or candidate cause. 2) Remove each one the known facts or scene rule out. 3) Treat the remaining one as the answer and check it against the evidence. When: the possibilities can be listed and tested against known facts, such as when designing a puzzle with several plausible answers. Why: Elimination narrows many options to the one the facts allow; players solve by elimination rather than guessing.
- [procedure] 1) Define the finite candidate roster, such as a manifest or participant list. 2) Eliminate candidates who cannot match each known attribute, such as physical profile. 3) Continue until one candidate remains. 4) Write each step so others can check it. When: a finite roster of candidates is known and a suspect must be identified from a partial description or physical profile. Why: A physical profile removes candidates systematically, and written sequential filtering narrows the pool to a checkable few.
- [procedure] 1) Collect partial facts from separate sources. 2) Combine them into a total or pattern. 3) Compare the result against the claim it may contradict. When: Checking a partner's or suspect's claim about money, quantities or events. Why: A sum of independent figures can expose a lie that no single figure reveals.
- [procedure] 1) List the actions the crime required. 2) For each suspect, ask whether they could have done it without help. 3) Clear those who could not have acted alone and look for a third party who completes the set. When: Several suspects each have partial access to the means of a crime. Why: A suspect who did not need an accomplice is unlikely to have sought one, so the logic points toward who must have assisted.
- [procedure] 1) List plausible candidates the witness might fear. 2) Name each one and watch the reaction. 3) Treat visible relief as a sign that person is not the feared one. 4) Ask about related traits to narrow further. When: when a witness withholds a name but the questioner can list candidates. Why: Reactions to each name rule possibilities in or out without the witness naming anyone.
- [procedure] 1) List candidates. 2) Exclude each one that observed physical facts rule out. 3) Explain the remaining facts with motive. 4) Check that explanation against all earlier evidence. 5) Obtain a test that confirms it. When: a short list of candidates can each be checked against physical facts. Why: exclusion narrows the answer, and motive and tests confirm it.
- [procedure] 1) Collect every instance of the recurring token. 2) Match each token to physical objects tied to the incidents. 3) Link events only when the physical match holds, not memory alone. When: a recurring mark appears across incidents.
- [procedure] 1) Locate the traces using light that makes them visible. 2) Compare depth and direction of each set. 3) Combine with other facts such as load and timing. 4) State the conclusion with its supporting facts. When: A vehicle or group has left a scene and tracks are the main trace. Why: Combining independent facts supports a conclusion more reliably than track depth alone.
- [procedure] 1) List who knew of the task. 2) List who visited the location. 3) List who knew its layout. 4) Intersect the lists to narrow who could have acted. When: An action must be traced to one of several people. Why: Each condition removes suspects who could not have acted.
- [procedure] 1) Start from one physical trace. 2) Ask what routine would leave it. 3) Link that routine to a habit, then to a person. 4) Connect the person to a recent statement or fact to reach a conclusion. Why: Each link is simple but depends on the one before.
- [procedure] 1) Read a general trait from the trace. 2) Match the trait to a group, such as a trade. 3) Match the group to a known roster. 4) Find the one member who fits the remaining facts. When: A culprit must be found from traces alone. Why: Each step narrows the field until a single person remains.
- [procedure] 1) Name the object or mechanism a theory implicates. 2) Inspect that object directly. 3) Compare its condition with the trace the theory predicts. 4) Accept the theory only if they match. When: a theory names a specific object or mechanism. Why: a physical match tests a claim faster than argument.
- [procedure] 1) Establish what the person could physically reach 2) Check whether reaching the observed point would leave a mark 3) Eliminate the person if no mark or route exists. When: Clearing a suspect who might have acted. Why: Access and physical traces can rule out a person without a confession.
- [procedure] 1) Test each suspect's alibi against a timeline. 2) Check whether one person could have caused the harm alone. 3) Check whether the suspect could obtain the instrument used. When: filtering suspects in a mystery. Why: timing, physical capability and access each exclude people.
- [procedure] 1) State one undisputed fact narrowly. 2) List everyone who could have had access to the means that produced it. 3) Record each candidate's presence during the relevant window. 4) Mark each candidate against the facts. 5) Identify the candidate who had reason to be present but is unsuspected.
- [procedure] 1) Note which object a suspect consulted. 2) Ask what purpose the consultation serves. 3) Propose a specific plan. 4) Verify the plan against the fixed schedule. When: a suspect's attention reveals an intended movement on a fixed schedule. Why: Checking the prediction against known facts makes it trustworthy.
- [procedure] 1) Compare accounts of one person from observers who saw different settings, such as work and home. 2) Treat sudden mood shifts or neglected routines as clues and ask their cause. 3) Note unusual requests and ask who handled them and what was said. 4) Check whether an action was hidden from normal confidants, and ask why.
- [procedure] 1) Deduce from the facts that a clue must exist. 2) Predict where it would sit. 3) Direct a search there to confirm the deduction.
- [procedure] 1) List each suspect's facts that incriminate. 2) List facts that clear them. 3) Weigh each item. 4) Test each suspect's account against independent facts. When: when several suspects have mixed evidence. Why: first impressions can mislead.
- [procedure] 1) List everyone present when the crime was found. 2) Remove anyone who could not have acted unnoticed. 3) Remove anyone without knowledge of the key object. 4) Remove anyone lacking the needed skill. 5) Remove anyone who could not have reached the item in time. 6) Restate all conditions for the survivor. When: several suspects share similar motives and physical clues must decide the matter. Why: each condition removes candidates until a few remain.
- [procedure] Build motive by chaining discovered facts, such as a secret marriage, a same-day announcement, a stormy confrontation, and a changed will, checking each link. When: when assembling a case against a suspect. Why: A chain of connected facts is stronger than any single fact.
- [procedure] 1) Match each physical trace to an item belonging to the suspect. 2) Check each link for an alternative cause. 3) Accept the suspect only if every link holds. When: Physical traces in a confined space are compared with a suspect's belongings. Why: Each link can be explained by another cause, so every step needs testing.
- [procedure] 1) Ask one question. 2) Confirm and mark the answer. 3) Ask the next only after the previous link is confirmed. 4) Summarise confirmed links. Close every gap before concluding, even if checks seem odd to others. When: facts must be confirmed in order when building a multi-step explanation. Why: one missing link breaks the entire chain of reasoning.
- [procedure] 1) List the obvious alternative suspects or candidates. 2) Test each against access, character fit, physical capacity and any real link to the motive. 3) Discard each with a stated reason. 4) Confirm the last remaining candidate before accepting it. When: initial evidence leaves several candidate explanations or the suspect list is short. Why: Eliminating candidates on explicit grounds makes the final choice traceable; checking facts rather than instinct narrows the candidates.
- [procedure] 1) List every possible route or place the object could have taken or be. 2) Test each against established facts or evidence. 3) Reject those that conflict. 4) Treat the single survivor as the answer once the list is confirmed complete. When: a case seems unsolvable and the possibilities are finite and checkable. Why: Elimination only yields the answer if no candidate is missing; fact-based elimination narrows the solution.
- [procedure] 1) Write each assumption the current theory depends on, numbered. 2) Verify each with the simplest available source, including evidence never requested. 3) Drop or revise any failing premise and recheck every conclusion built on it. When: A theory resists clues, an investigation stalls on one suspect, or before accepting a motive, alibi or timeline. Why: Stalls and tidy arguments often rest on an untested premise, and one false premise invalidates the whole chain.
- [procedure] 1) Give each suspect one test at a time. 2) Clear or hold each name. 3) Repeat until only the guilty remain. Partial coverage is not enough; any incomplete check leaves a suspect unexcluded and can derail the case. When: A long crowd of suspects must be filtered using documentary and physical checks. Why: A long list is hard to clear, and a gap in the search leaves a suspect unexcluded.
- [procedure] 1) List every route into and out of the sealed location. 2) Give evidence ruling out each, checked against timing. 3) Leave at least one route standing, or admit an unknown method remains. Too many rejections frustrate players. When: designing an impossible-access mystery. Why: players test each candidate against evidence and accept the answer as fair only if every rejection holds.
- [procedure] 1) List every condition the solution must satisfy, including minor details. 2) Propose a candidate answer. 3) Test it against all conditions. 4) Discard it if any condition fails. When: when an official explanation leaves loose ends. Why: One unexplained contradiction is enough to reject an answer.
- [procedure] 1) List who could physically have been at the scene at the event time. 2) Remove those with a verified alibi. 3) Check motive for the remaining people. When: building a suspect list. Why: Opportunity narrows the field before motive is weighed.
- [procedure] 1) Pull a trade directory. 2) Filter it by trades that would plausibly use the vehicle or resource described. 3) Contact the candidates privately. When: a new candidate pool is needed. Why: filtering by plausible users keeps the pool manageable.
- [procedure] 1) List the facts a crime depended on: who was present, who was absent, where the victim was, and the room layout. 2) Mark which facts an outsider could not know without leaving a trace. 3) Keep only suspects who could have known those facts. When: when the crime's method shows precise local knowledge. Why: the people who could know such facts are usually far fewer than those who could physically be there.
- [procedure] 1) Check the collection for signs of use. 2) List its subjects, authors, and condition. 3) Compare the list with the owner's known interests. 4) Treat each unexplained gap as a question. 5) Record findings in writing, then ask the owner and others in the room. When: a room's contents seem to belong to someone other than its attributed owner. Why: A written comparison exposes gaps that a glance misses.
- [procedure] 1) List suspects who were dropped earlier. 2) List those never examined. 3) Test each against new facts. When: a suspect list has stalled and new information has arrived. Why: Earlier dismissals may no longer hold once new facts appear.
- [procedure] 1) Form a provisional inference from a sound or object. 2) Check it against the next piece of evidence. 3) Keep the conclusion provisional until a later detail confirms or contradicts it. When: A puzzle should reward reasoning from indirect evidence rather than from direct statements.
- [procedure] 1) List each object and mark in the scene. 2) For each, ask who used it and for how long. 3) Ask what the arrangement was meant to show.
- [procedure] 1) Examine construction: tool marks, timber density, ceiling height and the source of foundation stone. 2) Read the grounds for earthworks and old paths. 3) Compare with period surveys and plans. 4) Check deed and record wording for the named occupant.
- [procedure] 1) Establish the observed effect and a reliable baseline of the prior state. 2) List the agents present. 3) Exclude each agent the evidence rules out. 4) Accept the remaining cause, noting that the list may be incomplete. When: Tracing an effect with no visible cause and a short list of possible agents. Why: Elimination only works from an accurate baseline and a complete list of candidates.
- [procedure] 1) Pick a scene item. 2) Place matching items in a suspect's inventory. 3) Present each match in sequence, each plausible alone but damning together. 4) Add a common-item decoy. When: when designing a deduction puzzle. Why: Decoys force the player to judge distinctiveness.
- [procedure] 1) List the possible methods of the act. 2) Rule out each method that the traces contradict. 3) Treat the remaining options as the question still to answer. When: several methods could explain an act.
- [procedure] 1) Write the traits the culprit must have. 2) Apply each trait in order to the suspect list. 3) Keep only the suspects who satisfy every trait. When: Many suspects and a clear chain linking the crime to specific traits. Why: Each trait removes candidates, shrinking the list to survivors.
- [procedure] 1) For each clue, ask what it rules in or out. 2) Apply the constraints one at a time to the candidate list. 3) Check the final candidate against every original clue. When: when the search space is large and clues are cryptic. Why: each clue removes a class of candidates.
- [procedure] 1) Identify each person with motive, means and opportunity. 2) Check whether each person's conduct has another explanation. 3) Rank suspects by how many factors they meet and how unexplained their conduct is. When: Direct evidence is missing and suspects must be ranked. Why: Circumstantial evidence can rank suspects when direct proof is absent.
- [procedure] 1) List every possible suspect. 2) Sort each into columns for means, motive and opportunity. 3) Mark uncertain entries with a query instead of deleting them. 4) Rank suspects with all three flags first, two flags second. When: Many people had contact with a victim and the method limits who could act. Why: A structured grid exposes gaps and forces each suspect to justify their place.
- [procedure] 1) Name the ordinary principle that could cause the outcome. 2) Predict how quickly the effect follows and what visible signs it leaves. 3) Predict the traces that should remain. 4) Search for those traces. When: when an outcome seems natural but circumstances suggest a deliberate method. Why: a known principle can explain the case when experts overlook it.
- [procedure] 1) Map the space of possible routes or answers. 2) Partition it into regions. 3) Test each region against a physical or logical constraint. 4) Dismiss a region only with a stated reason. 5) Repeat until one candidate remains. When: The solution space is large and each region has a checkable constraint. Why: Dismissals resting on stated constraints keep the elimination verifiable rather than based on preference.
- [procedure] 1) Identify a measurable physical limit, such as height or reach, from the scene geometry. 2) Check which suspects can meet it. 3) Exclude those who cannot. 4) Inspect the remaining suspects' own objects to confirm.
- [procedure] 1) List everyone who could have been in the space and time, including the culprit. 2) Compare the list with people actually seen together. 3) Clear any candidate seen alongside the culprit at the same moment. 4) Treat the remaining candidate as a hypothesis until physical proof confirms it. When: The suspect set is small and the timeline is well recorded. Why: Sightings at the same moment can rule out people and expose the hidden one.
- [procedure] 1) Identify who had access to the target. 2) Check which of those suspects had the means to act. 3) Establish what evidence would settle the question, and weigh each suspect's initial likelihood before deciding. When: Several suspects share a motive or a clue links to more than one person. Why: Separating access from means and proof keeps the case from closing on a single clue.
- [procedure] 1) List candidate objects. 2) For each, ask what trace it would leave. 3) Eliminate candidates whose trace does not match. 4) Keep the candidate whose trace fits best. When: when several objects could have caused an injury. Why: reasoning from expected traces narrows the cause.
- [procedure] 1) Note details that contradict the stated purpose. 2) Build a timeline from physical limits. 3) Test the working theory against the clock. 4) Revise the theory as each new fact arrives. When: Inferring where a suspect is really going.
- [procedure] 1) List every access point to the restricted place or item. 2) For each, identify who held the means of access. 3) Check whether those holders kept that means on the relevant night. When: an unauthorized entry or removal must be explained. Why: Testing each access point rules people in or out by custody.
- [procedure] 1) List each victim's condition at the time. 2) Find the state all victims share. 3) Infer methods that would work only on someone in that state. 4) Eliminate methods whose expected traces are absent. When: when the method of harm is unknown but victims share a state. Why: the shared state narrows which methods are possible.
- [procedure] 1) Pick the least unlikely alternative from what is known about who is connected to the affair. 2) Give that person a motive that would explain the crime and justify framing the accused. 3) Build a step-by-step sequence of entry, means and planting of evidence that fits known facts. 4) List every condition the sequence requires, such as presence at places and dates. 5) Test the first condition, since the theory stands or falls on it. When: when the accused's innocence depends on showing someone else could have committed the crime. Why: A concrete set of checkable requirements turns vague suspicion into testable claims.
- [procedure] 1) Check each suspect's clothing against the outfit the item's carrier must have worn. 2) Test physical fit against the item's size. 3) Test access to plausible hiding places. 4) Search suspects' property for substitutes. When: A suspect list is long and each suspect can be tested against one physical or logistical condition. Why: Each test removes candidates that fail a condition.
- [procedure] 1) Establish whether the witness stayed in one room throughout. 2) Collect each household member's movements and cross-check them. 3) Compare every other room with its earlier state. 4) Confirm each unchanged room, so the change is narrowed to the rooms left. When: a set of connected rooms must be searched for who could have entered. Why: Eliminating verified rooms bounds the set of possible entry points.
- [procedure] 1) Forbid anyone to leave. 2) Check each person's presence. 3) Require a list of absent people with their reasons. Together these account for everyone on site. When: when a set of possible suspects must be accounted for.
- [procedure] 1) List every impossible-looking element in a level. 2) Confirm each has a fair answer placed somewhere in the level. 3) Check that the constraints allow the intended solving order. When: Designing a puzzle-driven level. Why: Unresolved impossibilities frustrate players, and ordering tests solvability.
- [procedure] 1) Identify who entered the space. 2) Identify who was sent out. 3) Identify what was interrupted. Use the results to build the sequence of events. When: reconstructing who touched what in a disturbed scene.
- [procedure] 1) Describe the victim's condition. 2) Identify what method would produce that condition. 3) Work backward to when and how that method had to be applied. 4) Test each step as a separate numbered claim. When: A crime's method seems to need an unlikely ability or circumstance. Why: The victim's state often reveals an earlier action.
- [procedure] 1) Fix a starting premise firmly. 2) Extend it one step at a time. 3) Test each new observation against the bounded set of what is already established before accepting it. When: Working out a solution from incomplete evidence. Why: Building from a firm premise keeps conclusions consistent and blocks shallow guesses.
- [procedure] 1) Fix known hard facts such as date and time window. 2) Apply a physical constraint such as tide height. 3) Eliminate candidates that fail distance, direction or timetable checks. 4) Keep the result provisional. When: when a time and place must be inferred from scattered clues. Why: combining hard constraints shrinks the candidates until few remain.
- [procedure] 1) Frame each possible explanation as a separate heading. 2) Record why it fails and why it might hold. 3) Use physical arrangement to rule options in or out. 4) Weigh the remaining options and state the result as provisional. When: Organizing scattered observations into candidate explanations. Why: Separating hypotheses and recording evidence for and against prevents a favoured theory from hiding contrary facts.
- [procedure] 1) Note an object's abnormal proportions relative to its normal wearer. 2) Ask what would make it look that way. 3) Infer the unseen person's trait, such as height. 4) Filter suspects by that trait. When: A suspect description is vague but an object or costume was seen clearly. Why: Object fit reveals hidden attributes that narrow the suspect pool.
- [procedure] 1) Make several items missing or altered in a way that shares one property. 2) Let the player list them. 3) Use the shared property to identify the thief. When: designing an inventory-based deduction puzzle. Why: A common property across missing items points the player toward a single cause.
- [tradeoff] Clearing a convenient suspect early removes the misdirection; keeping it to the end requires a later reversal to sustain interest.
- [tradeoff] Elimination by presence works well with a small suspect set and complete records, but patchy observation across many people produces many false candidates. When: Choosing whether to apply presence-based elimination. Why: Short unseen gaps multiply possible candidates when observation is sparse.
- [tradeoff] Deduction reasons from a fixed, supplied record with little fieldwork, but inherits every error in that record; induction gathers new evidence, costing more time but tolerating thin or incomplete records. When: Choosing an investigation approach. Why: Each route trades effort against reliance on the available facts.
- [pitfall] Narrowing candidates by assumed constraints or profile traits can exclude the true answer before evidence is checked; an all-or-nothing deduction is only as reliable as its profile. When: eliminating candidate explanations from constraints or building a culprit profile. Why: An incorrect assumption removes options that should have stayed open. (x2)
- [pitfall] Hindsight makes deductions look trivial: audiences are surprised before reasoning is shown, then find it obvious. Test each link; show the sign and its meaning so reasoning keeps its credit. When: a deduction is revealed to an audience or a solved explanation is reviewed. Why: Each step looks obvious only after it is stated, and knowing the outcome biases perception of the path.
- [pitfall] A clue that only the investigator understands can seem like a trick to the audience, so the reasoning behind it must be explained step by step. When: A deduction depends on a reference the audience does not yet know. Why: Unexplained reasoning reads as arbitrary, so the audience does not accept the conclusion.
- [pitfall] Layered deduction collapses if any figure is wrong or unsourced; it holds only when each number comes from an independent source the reader can check.
- [pitfall] Each route deduction rests on assumptions, such as no side openings and shortest-route travel; test them before trusting the conclusion.
- [pitfall] A list can make answers look settled when its candidate answers are only guesses.
- [pitfall] Explaining the reasoning only after the event works for a reveal but leaves the audience uncertain during the scene.
- [pitfall] A crowd tends to assume the strongest person is responsible when a crime seems to need extreme strength, and such extreme methods can mislead. When: a crime's method appears to require unusual physical power. Why: Assuming an extreme method points to an extreme person skips testing whether ordinary people could have done it.
- [pitfall] Reading behaviour from a trace requires knowing how the act is normally performed; without that baseline, a staged trace can mislead in either direction. When: the observer is unfamiliar with the activity.
- [pitfall] Clearing one suspect can open a second false lead, so the investigation may keep chasing decoys. When: When suspects are eliminated one at a time. Why: Each cleared lead can point toward another plausible but wrong suspect.
- [pitfall] Accepting an informant's theory because it fits the observed pattern makes the theory look more credible than its evidence supports. When: An informant's account matches a pattern. Why: Fit with the pattern is not independent evidence for the account.
- [pitfall] A questioner's inference can be accepted by a witness who would not have reached it alone, so recovered details may be suggestion rather than memory. When: When asking a witness to agree with a proposed inference. Why: Agreement is easier to give than independent recall.
- [pitfall] Clearing an alibi can remove a witness who might have been useful later, especially if the investigator promised to keep them out of the case. When: Clearing a witness's alibi. Why: A cleared person may still hold information of value.
- [pitfall] Judging a person by one shelf is an error, since gifts, shared possessions, or unusual collecting can explain a mismatch. When: a collection seems out of character. Why: Ordinary explanations for odd possessions are common.
- [pitfall] Starting from an object instead of asking why it was chosen can send an investigation the wrong way. When: Investigating a crime built around a chosen item. Why: The choice of object often reveals the culprit's intent more than the object does.
- [pitfall] Reading a new death through an old, similar one can push an investigation toward suspicion that the evidence does not support.
- [pitfall] Assuming an offender thinks like an expert, or that high rank shields him from suspicion, can lead investigators to misjudge the culprit. When: Building a profile of a skilled, careful criminal.
- [pitfall] A valid syllogism can still be worthless when its premises already contain the conclusion, since its first step assumes the very point it claims to prove. Why: Analysing the same assumption again creates no new evidence.
- [pitfall] The same calm can be read as a hardened criminal's habit or as concealed feeling, so one observation can support opposite conclusions.
- [pitfall] An investigator who traces every strange event to one hidden source will over-read coincidence; accepting only what plain facts support counters this.
- [pitfall] Stretching one innocent explanation to cover several incriminating items weakens the review, since each item needs its own support. When: Building counter-explanations for a multi-item evidence list. Why: A single explanation rarely fits unrelated facts.
- [pitfall] Using only physical evidence or only psychological inference undervalues one kind of reasoning; the two must be combined to reach reliable conclusions. When: An investigation relies heavily on one type of reasoning. Why: Each kind of reasoning covers gaps the other leaves.
- [pitfall] Assuming a room is the one expected from its position can send an investigator to the wrong place.
- [pitfall] A problem stated too broadly can hide which goal should be pursued first. When: Restating an investigation with several goals bundled together. Why: Bundled goals obscure priorities and which obstacle blocks progress.
- [pitfall] Precedent matching fails when the method is new and no reference case covers it, so the theory cannot be checked this way. When: when an investigation involves an unprecedented method. Why: No documented case exists to compare against.
- [pitfall] A person seen with others at one moment may still have had time to act alone, so an apparent alibi can be partial rather than complete. When: Evaluating an alibi that covers only part of the timeline.
- [pitfall] Missing parts block any conclusion that depends on them, such as cause of death, and an absent feature cannot be read as proof it never existed. When: when reasoning from incomplete remains. Why: the missing part may hold the decisive evidence.
- [fact] Deduction supports suspicion, but proof requires physical evidence that can be presented to an authority; reasoning alone can be dismantled by a challenge.
- [fact] A clue pointing to one person narrows the inquiry to that person and to others with access to the same source; it does not settle who is guilty.
- [fact] In a closed setting every entry and exit route can be listed, so questions about who could come or go become checkable.

## hypothesis and inference
- [model] A hypothesis tracker holds each explanation as an object the player can accept, test, or discard as facts arrive. When: designing deduction systems. Why: explicit hypotheses make revision a visible part of play.
- [model] Reasoners keep frequently used knowledge ready in memory and store rarer material in a reference to consult when a case needs it. When: a case needs knowledge the reasoner rarely uses. Why: ready knowledge speeds reasoning while rare material is looked up.
- [model] Analysis supplies the outline, proportion and relations between parts, and invention fills and colours that outline; analysis also means careful attention to small details as well as large ones. When: Structuring a mystery that must be exact but still unsettling. Why: A sound structure lets creative surface detail stand on firm relations.
- [model] A conclusion becomes reliable when several independent observations each confirm it point by point, and the chain is laid out in order. When: A conclusion rests on inference and must be defended to others. Why: Independent confirmations that could not all be coincidence make the inference hard to dismiss.
- [definition] A hunch is a strong feeling about who is guilty; it stays unproven, and may fail to convince others, until backed by evidence others can check. When: an intuitive judgment is held without evidence. Why: a private feeling cannot be shared as proof.
- [definition] A working hypothesis is a provisional explanation that joins known facts into one sequence of who was where, what was done, and what was hidden. When: When many facts must be arranged into one account. Why: A complete sequence shows where gaps remain.
- [definition] Analysis is the ability to take a situation apart and observe its parts, while ingenuity is the ability to construct or combine them; they are distinct skills.
- [definition] A falsifiable prediction states a precise time and place for an event, specific enough that its non-occurrence could prove the theory wrong. When: when checking a theory about someone's routine or a perceivable event.
- [mechanism] A player's hypothesis works as a prediction of what should or should not be observed, so recorded evidence can test it. When: when players hold theories about a mystery. Why: predictions make theories checkable.
- [mechanism] Attacking individual links and offering an alternative suspect whose evidence fits equally well creates reasonable doubt.
- [mechanism] Rewarding the player when a hypothesis matches later evidence, and using that match to open the next area, reinforces the investigation loop. When: Designing progression in a deduction game.
- [mechanism] A rule fixed in advance, that an unseen agent cannot wear an innocent person's shape, decides every later case once witnesses are believed.
- [mechanism] When any unusual misfortune or unconventional conduct is attributed to a hidden cause, new suspicions become easy to form. When: When a community has a widely accepted explanation for strange events. Why: A ready cause turns ordinary oddities into accusations.
- [mechanism] Changing a character's label or category changes how readers interpret that character, even when the underlying events stay the same. When: When presentation labels or names are applied to characters. Why: Labels set expectations about a character's identity and behavior.
- [behavior] Committing early to a theory makes an investigator read each new fact, even ambiguous ones, as confirmation, ask confirming questions and stop seeking other evidence, risking a wrong conclusion. When: a theory or suspect is chosen before the evidence is complete, such as after one partial clue or an early accusation. Why: Commitment makes new facts serve the existing theory; a held conviction shapes how later facts are interpreted.
- [behavior] A fixed working theory blinds an investigator to contradicting objects and evidence in plain view, which a rival with a different focus may notice, delaying the correct reading. When: examining a scene after forming a first explanation or committing to a single one. Why: Attention filters for evidence that fits the theory.
- [behavior] A fixed interpretive frame, such as a group's adopted theory, decides in advance what counts as evidence, so outside evidence goes unnoticed. When: a group or investigator has adopted an explanation for a situation. Why: the theory decides what counts as relevant.
- [behavior] Wanting a particular answer, such as quick closure, makes an investigator accept a matching claim without verifying it and read the same trace in its favoured way. When: a claim matches a strong hope or the investigator is eager for a conclusion. Why: desire substitutes for verification and biases interpretation toward the preferred reading.
- [behavior] People accept a theory because it seems natural, neat or reasonable, and stop checking once it fits; a neat theory that dismisses contrary evidence in one step feels convincing. When: a clue has a natural and an unlikely explanation, or a theory explains the case cleanly or fits visible evidence. Why: Natural, neat or reasonable readings feel like proof and need no further checking.
- [behavior] Familiar explanations, such as a well-known similar incident or a shared frame like illness, are accepted without proof and close the question because they seem to need no further questioning. When: a separate similar incident is well known, or someone's strange conduct is given a familiar explanation. Why: The familiar case makes the new one seem explained; familiar explanations seem to need no further questioning.
- [behavior] Close, fresh observation of a person's face, habits and conduct can overturn an earlier first impression of them. When: a character was judged quickly and new evidence arrives. Why: attention to detail reveals traits that were missed.
- [behavior] Authorities may keep a fixed premise even when others show it lacks a solid basis, so a sound reply can still lose.
- [behavior] Players who judge by manner are led astray: a courteous, calm, well-presented figure is trusted over a blunt skeptic. When: A helpful figure meets an honest but rude expert. Why: First impressions of charm override later evidence.
- [behavior] Listeners judge a speaker first by appearance and manner, and a sense of education makes them more willing to hear the speaker out. When: when a stranger begins to speak. Why: appearance and manner signal status and credibility.
- [behavior] Observers who notice one striking sign, such as asphyxia, often miss a subtler posture clue unless they check what each trace implies. When: examining a body or scene. Why: attention locks onto the most vivid sign.
- [behavior] Observers fixate on a vivid, suspicious detail and treat it as evidence of guilt, even when the supporting evidence behind it is weak. When: A single striking detail is the main basis for a suspicion. Why: Vivid details draw attention and feel significant before their context is checked.
- [behavior] Players first read a sudden change as catastrophe, such as a collapse or trap, and correct that reading only when visible cues contradict it. When: a scene changes unexpectedly.
- [behavior] Asking a witness to consider a hypothetical invites an estimate of probabilities and reveals their own view without committing them to facts. When: when probing a witness's beliefs. Why: hypotheticals lower the stakes of answering.
- [behavior] A frame set in advance holds only while the audience trusts the source that set it; once that trust drops, the frame loses its force. When: the audience is judging a claim through a prior warning. Why: the warning gets its authority from its source.
- [behavior] When a character refuses to share a secret, the listener infers that it is serious and involves a third person, which fuels curiosity. When: One character holds information another needs and refuses to explain. Why: A refusal signals high stakes and implicates someone else.
- [behavior] A confident false conclusion set up before a reveal makes the eventual truth sharper, because the audience sees the gap between belief and reality. When: Structuring a mystery where an authority holds a wrong view. Why: Contrast between certainty and evidence heightens the payoff.
- [behavior] Players who think without new material grow frustrated and read the pause as a problem rather than deliberate pacing. When: an investigation stalls with no fresh evidence. Why: Reasoning without new input feels like wasted motion, so tension drains.
- [behavior] Audiences dislike mysteries left without an answer, so unsolved cases are often set aside rather than presented as open. When: presenting a story or case. Why: Readers dislike mysteries without answers.
- [behavior] Stating specific conclusions early, each tied to a visible cue, makes the observer seem competent and makes the listener trust the reasoning. When: A character should seem competent or a listener must be impressed. Why: Specific, checkable-sounding claims read as evidence-based expertise.
- [behavior] A third party's endorsement of a cover name makes the false identity hard for others to challenge. When: a character vouches for an assumed name. Why: the endorsement lends credibility without anyone checking the claim.
- [behavior] A clue often looks weak or irrelevant until the context that explains it is known, so early dismissal is common. When: a clue is first encountered without context. Why: its meaning depends on facts not yet gathered.
- [behavior] Readers treat a stiff, formal register as a sign of seriousness and authority, though some may find the same wording comic or evasive. When: when a document uses heavy vocabulary and formal phrasing. Why: Readers judge weight from register, and their habits shape whether it reads as serious or absurd.
- [behavior] Audiences who guess the resolution early lose interest, so the clue structure must hold up against common expectations. When: A mystery follows familiar patterns. Why: Readers arrive with expectations about how stories resolve.
- [behavior] Players become frustrated when a puzzle's solution cannot be confirmed by evidence available in the game. When: the puzzle requires a confirmable answer. Why: an unprovable answer gives no way to verify progress.
- [behavior] How members vote on sharing facts can reveal who is ready to theorise, who is ready to gather evidence, and who already holds a theory. When: a group votes on pooling discoveries. Why: the vote reflects each member's current stance on the case.
- [behavior] Circling a topic with a hypothetical question before stating the fact keeps tension high and lets the listener approach the answer gradually. When: A speaker is about to disclose a sensitive fact. Why: An indirect question invites the listener to think toward the answer.
- [behavior] An existing reputation can make a new damaging rumour seem plausible even when it is untrue.
- [behavior] A player who has passed through a space assumes it is secure and tends not to re-check details such as an open door. When: A space the player already passed through. Why: Earlier passage creates a belief the player does not revisit.
- [behavior] A person holding an absolute belief reads each new event as confirming it, so every accusation seems to fit the belief. When: someone treats a central belief as the fact that explains their world. Why: The belief serves as the premise that interprets all evidence.
- [behavior] Investigators may have procedures and tools for gathering physical evidence but lack the insight to draw conclusions from it, so cases stall without a specialist. When: Evidence is collected but no one interprets it. Why: Collecting evidence and inferring from it are separate skills.
- [rule] Build two or three plausible readings, each supported by its own clues and differing testably, such as evidence of theft versus none, so players weigh rival explanations. When: Designing a mystery or scene with more than one explanation. Why: Players then have to weigh rival explanations rather than follow one path; a testable difference lets them choose between explanations through evidence. (x2)
- [rule] Give players a hypothesis to test via checkable consequences, such as travelling to a predicted waypoint or performing an action like reading an inscription to see if its effect ends. When: Designing a puzzle or investigation level. (x2)
- [rule] Treat explanations, reconstructions and clue rankings as unconfirmed working hypotheses, never final verdicts, revised as new facts arrive until a physical test, witness or other evidence confirms them. When: When a hypothesis is formed from partial evidence. Why: Untested hypotheses can mislead the whole chain; a plausible picture can form before any proof exists.
- [rule] Do not treat a coherent story as proven; each link in an inference chain needs its own support, since untested links let plausible chains drift from the evidence. When: When a reconstructed explanation fits the known facts. Why: A coherent account can rest on unverified assumptions.
- [rule] Predict expected physical traces before searching; a specific hypothesis focuses the search and guards against confirmation bias, while unfocused searching wastes time. When: Searching a scene or space for a hidden feature, or after a reconstruction. Why: A hypothesis tells the searcher where to look, and predicting first guards against confirmation bias.
- [rule] Give players a journal logging each theory with its reason, marked supported, contradicted or untested, struck out when evidence rules it out, and reveal eliminations before the final answer. When: When the player tracks several explanations or a deduction is presented. Why: Open versus closed status shows progress, and visible elimination lets the player follow the reasoning.
- [rule] Give the player a confidence scale, such as likely or confirmed, that distinguishes firm inferences from probable ones and changes only after a test. Why: Players see how far each conclusion has been checked.
- [rule] Mark reconstructed or guessed conclusions as inferences, not established facts, since reliability checks often rest on reconstruction; revisit guesses when new information arrives. When: When deduction mixes established facts with uncertain assumptions. Why: Keeping weak points visible prevents guesses from being treated as facts.
- [rule] Theories matching one's sympathies, dislikes or wishes can distort a reconstruction; label them as such and compare rival theories by motive and evidence, keeping them only until checked. When: When choosing between rival explanations, or a theory rests on personal feeling rather than evidence. Why: Personal sympathies bias which explanation seems credible; motive-driven theories gain support they have not earned.
- [rule] Ensure the level's evidence supports at least one reading besides the obvious culprit, so players are not led to a single answer. When: Building a mystery with suspects. Why: A single supported reading makes the obvious answer trivial.
- [rule] Close a hypothesis when a conflicting witness line is found, but keep the core fact open until another clue explains it. When: tracking a player's hypothesis list. Why: Closing a theory is not the same as solving the event.
- [rule] Proceed on a stated working presumption and work out its consequences, treating it as a basis for the next test rather than as proof. When: Evidence points strongly but not conclusively to one explanation. Why: A presumption lets investigation advance while remaining open to revision.
- [rule] Treat outside answers such as bank replies, telegrams and police reports as fixed points that the explanation must respect.
- [rule] Use a short, fixed checklist of questions for characters so players learn the pattern and can apply it to other characters. When: when designing a repeatable investigation interaction. Why: consistent structure lets players transfer the learned method.
- [rule] Keep an investigator's hypothesis private until it is firm, since premature disclosure can endanger the case or alert a suspect. When: a hypothesis is not yet proven. Why: an unproven claim spoken aloud can warn the target or mislead allies.
- [rule] Separate a workable explanation available early from the true solution revealed later, so the player can discover the difference.
- [rule] The prosecution need not prove every alternative route the poison could have taken; reasonable doubt about deliberate administration is enough to weaken the case. When: Evaluating a case where a poison's route is uncertain. Why: Ruling out every alternative is not required, only showing that deliberate administration is doubtful.
- [rule] Keep a working theory built on the most logical expert explanation, and mark it as unconfirmed until it is tested. Why: An untested theory can mislead the investigation.
- [rule] Set aside a small object for later examination rather than discarding it. When: When an item may matter later. Why: Items discarded early cannot be tested later.
- [rule] Weigh a witness's theory against the physical facts, since a witness's framing can lead investigators off course. When: when a witness proposes a story about the crime. Why: Personal framing can steer attention away from the evidence.
- [rule] Treat a reading of a phrase as a hypothesis and test it against what you already know of the people involved. When: a single document is the only source of a fact.
- [rule] Weigh each innocent explanation for a trace, such as lending the item, and set it aside with a stated reason rather than ignoring it. Why: An unexamined innocent explanation can hide the real cause of the trace.
- [rule] Let an apparent cause, such as a trap or a fall, be overturned by an examination check that reveals the true cause, such as a hidden projectile.
- [rule] In a game, let one support character run verification tasks for a player's hypotheses, and leave each hypothesis unconfirmed until its verification succeeds. When: designing collaborative investigation gameplay. Why: Keeps hypotheses meaningful without making them automatic facts.
- [rule] Let the game log the player's inference chain so the player can check their own assumptions.
- [rule] Check whether a conclusion depends on the unknown fact at all; if it holds either way, the open question does not need resolving. When: An unresolved fact sits inside a deduction. Why: Robust conclusions need no answer to the open question.
- [rule] When an assumed fact proves ambiguous, restart the reasoning from a fresh basis rather than forcing the old interpretation to fit. When: When a key fact admits more than one reading. Why: Forcing an ambiguous fact preserves a wrong assumption.
- [rule] When new facts fall outside a working theory, reconsider the theory; do not keep it by default. When: When a prediction fails or new facts contradict the working theory. Why: Holding a theory against contrary facts leads to acting on error.
- [rule] Keep independent player theories separate until a reveal, then compare where each one ended up. When: Running a multi-player or group investigation exercise. Why: Separation keeps each reasoning path unbiased by others.
- [rule] Give investigation interfaces a structured hypothesis view rather than unlimited inspection of a single object.
- [rule] Give in-world documents a visible confidence rating in the clue journal, so players can weigh conflicting notes before acting on them. When: designing a clue journal for conflicting sources. Why: visible ratings make trust a player decision.
- [rule] Track each theory as supported, contradicted or unresolved as clues arrive, so the player can see which hypotheses remain open. When: a game presents player hypotheses. Why: explicit status keeps reasoning visible and organized.
- [rule] Separate a visible effect from its mechanism, and test the mechanism before accepting the effect's apparent cause. When: a scene presents a set of coordinated effects.
- [heuristic] Give players a journal or case board tracking rival hypotheses with supporting and contradicting clues, so confirmed facts promote one explanation and broken ones are retired. When: players must track multiple clues and rival hypotheses across a long case. Why: Recording and revising theories supports deduction over a long investigation; visible rivals make reasoning legible and reward updating.
- [heuristic] Do not discard an unlikely or surprising hypothesis merely because it seems implausible; test it, and drop it only when an established fact rules it out. When: A hypothesis conflicts with expectations and has not been checked. Why: Dismissing it early can hide the true answer.
- [heuristic] Keep several readings or candidate locations open until a second clue selects one, since early commitment to one clue can hide the correct answer. When: a clue supports more than one plausible meaning or several locations remain plausible. Why: early commitment to one reading can hide the correct answer.
- [heuristic] Adopt a candidate explanation as a working hypothesis to see how many difficulties it removes, without treating it as proven.
- [heuristic] A hypothesis gains strength when it explains several otherwise unrelated residues at once.
- [heuristic] Set up a mimicry or shared trait early, so a later resemblance reads as already established rather than as a new coincidence.
- [heuristic] Voicing each deduction in order while working a case keeps the reasoning sequenced and easier to check.
- [heuristic] Make a misleading explanation reasonable rather than absurd, since misdirection works best when the wrong answer is plausible.
- [heuristic] When a credible witness conflicts with a strong theory, look for a hidden motive such as fear before discarding the witness; ask which assumption must change. When: the cause of the conflict is still unknown.
- [heuristic] Test facts under a clear working hypothesis that decides which to examine first, rather than checking every detail indefinitely. When: Once early examination has begun and the investigator must prioritize. Why: Unbounded checking wastes effort, while a hypothesis directs the tests.
- [heuristic] Let a suspect's first impression differ from the eventual truth, so the player learns to revise judgements.
- [heuristic] Reading a culprit's own words aloud to a group can persuade witnesses who had doubted a trusted person. When: after the reveal.
- [heuristic] An early, plausible theory may steer an investigation astray, so weigh each supporting item rather than accepting the theory whole. When: A motive looks obvious early in an investigation.
- [heuristic] A newly arrived figure's first impressions can shape later decisions, so record which people that figure met first. When: tracing decisions made by a new official.
- [heuristic] The depth and direction of a mark show how much force was used, which can rule out an accidental cause. When: judging how a mark was made.
- [heuristic] Determine what action caused a physical mark before deciding what it means, since the cause sets how the mark should be read. When: interpreting a mark within a timeline.
- [heuristic] Treat the language of a fragment as a hypothesis, since a reading may fail in one language and fit in another. When: Decoding a message whose language is uncertain. Why: The wrong language can make every candidate reading fail.
- [heuristic] A witness who confirms a suspect's routine or background does not establish innocence; testimony helps only when it covers the specific moment that matters. When: when deciding what a witness can prove. Why: General confirmation is consistent with guilt, and only the critical hour matters.
- [heuristic] After reaching a conclusion, ask what follows from it, such as what became of the carrier's own item, and use that as the next question. Why: Consequences expose new evidence to test.
- [heuristic] Design two candidate solutions: one fits all clues, and one fits only by ignoring a single detail, so the player must test which survives scrutiny. When: Building a deduction puzzle with one intended answer. Why: Players must weigh evidence rather than accept the first neat answer.
- [heuristic] If a walk returns to its starting wall, the space is one circuit; wrongly assuming a second pass doubles the estimate. When: a room is estimated by pacing.
