- [heuristic] An expected sound or event that is missing can serve as evidence about route and timing, but only if the listener could plausibly have heard it. When: When a conclusion depends on something not being heard or seen. Why: Silence counts as evidence only when detection was possible.
- [heuristic] Physical effort shown in a scene, such as the labour of digging, is evidence of the actor's physical capacity and intent, and may contradict a conclusion drawn from one feature. When: Judging who acted from the physical work done. Why: The work required reveals capability that a single wound or method may not.
- [heuristic] Judgments about a cleaner's skill or interruption drawn from the quality of a cleanup are impressions to verify, not proof. When: Concluding who tampered with a scene from the quality of their work. Why: Sloppy work can come from carelessness or interruption, not intent to hide.
- [heuristic] When hard evidence is thin, build an interrogation on a suspect's temperament and nerves rather than on a single fact. When: The suspect is nervous or impulsive and the evidence is circumstantial. Why: Reading character can apply pressure where proof is missing.
- [heuristic] Suspect what looks probable and begin by testing what appears incredible, since a long-shot lead can still yield information. When: When the most obvious lead is weak or the evidence is ambiguous. Why: Unlikely leads can produce side information that likely ones do not.
- [heuristic] A theory resting on a single suspect and a long chain of assumptions is fragile, because each unverified link can fail. When: when assessing how strong a conclusion is. Why: Confidence does not compensate for weak links.
- [heuristic] Consider harmless explanations for physical signs, and leave them open until evidence favours one over the others. When: A physical sign suggests a suspicious cause. Why: Ordinary causes often explain signs that seem significant.
- [heuristic] Offer players a visible list of ruled-out methods so the remaining choices are clearly narrowed. When: a mystery is solved by elimination.
- [heuristic] Record uncertain readings with explicit confidence markers, such as question marks, rather than presenting them as facts. When: when clues are partly unclear. Why: marked guesses can be revised when new evidence arrives.
- [heuristic] Show which evidence supports or contradicts each suspect in a theory-tracking view, so overcommitment to one suspect is visible.
- [heuristic] A reasonable-sounding answer that ends a discussion should be tested before it is accepted, since it may close inquiry rather than resolve it. When: someone explains away a complaint or anomaly with a cost or convenience argument. Why: plausible reasons can end scrutiny early.
- [heuristic] Do not take the first version of an event as final when rumour holds several explanations, each with some support.
- [heuristic] Accept an extreme or supernatural explanation only after ordinary human causes, such as sentiment, drafting error or incentive, are tested; dramatic readings stop inquiry early. When: when clues contradict, an unusual event or motive seems uncanny, or the investigator is stuck. Why: Ordinary causes usually account for strangeness, and extraordinary explanations used before ordinary ones are checked lead to unfounded conclusions.
- [heuristic] When several incidents seem unrelated, check whether they share a target object or goal, since a common target can reveal the real motive. When: When a string of harassments or attacks appears random. Why: Identical objectives behind scattered events point to one hidden purpose.
- [heuristic] Let an investigation system hold unrevealed hypotheses and act on them only once a confirming clue is found. When: Designing a deduction system for players. Why: Players can then test theories without exposing them early.
- [procedure] 1) Establish one confirmed, undisputed fact and set aside speculation not connected to it. 2) State a hypothesis plainly. 3) Ask what it would explain among earlier observations. 4) Test each implication against the evidence. 5) Keep the hypothesis provisional until tested. When: Many scattered observations or hypotheses seem unrelated, with only a few hard facts. Why: A single anchor gathers scattered observations under one explanation and keeps unsupported theories from crowding out existing evidence.
- [procedure] 1) Name the most obvious explanation for what is seen. 2) List what it requires in the setting, such as physical features. 3) Test each requirement against the map or direct observation. 4) Drop the explanation wherever a requirement is missing. When: When an attractive explanation is untested and the setting can be inspected. Why: A convincing general story can still fail on local facts.
- [procedure] 1) List each suspicious act. 2) For each hypothesis, ask why every act would happen under it. 3) Keep the hypothesis that explains all acts, including traces that seem to contradict it. 4) Do not treat it as proven until it accounts for every trace. When: Evidence points both ways. Why: A theory that fits only some acts is incomplete.
- [procedure] 1) State what the favoured theory predicts. 2) Run repeated checks against those predictions. 3) If results never fit, re-examine the theory and its target, since holding it despite empty results wastes effort on the wrong target. When: An investigator holds a favoured theory. Why: Predictions give results a way to contradict the theory.
- [procedure] 1) Build a plausible sequence of events that accounts for the physical evidence. 2) Treat it as a working hypothesis. 3) Replace it when a better explanation accounts for more evidence. When: Physical evidence needs organising. Why: A hypothesis organises evidence and generates questions to test.
- [procedure] 1) Build the theory as an ordered sequence of events. 2) For each event, state the trace it should leave. 3) Check for that trace; events without one are doubtful.
- [procedure] 1) List every explanation that fits the first facts. 2) Remove the impossible ones. 3) Test the remaining explanations against the evidence, one at a time. 4) Treat the survivor as a working hypothesis and check it against a later clue. When: The set of possible explanations is small.
- [procedure] 1) Assume the event occurred. 2) List every candidate explanation, including people who merely had access. 3) Test each against the facts. 4) Commit to a theory only after testing. When: Early in an investigation, while suspects are few and one may look obviously guilty. Why: Testing all candidates prevents an early favourite from closing the question.
- [procedure] 1) Form a sharper hypothesis that explains the odd details. 2) Find a trusted source with relevant knowledge. 3) Test the hypothesis against that source before relying on it. When: a few small clues fit a hidden role. Why: an untested idea can feel like established fact.
- [procedure] 1) Eliminate unlikely explanations. 2) State a working hypothesis. 3) Predict where evidence should appear. 4) Go and check that location. When: when the search area is large and no lead stands out.
- [procedure] 1) List every plausible explanation before testing any. 2) Test each against physical facts. 3) Test each against motive and state-of-mind facts. 4) Weigh both strands together. 5) If all fail, record the means as unknown. When: when only a few explanations seem plausible and the facts appear contradictory. Why: testing each alternative on separate evidence prevents an unchecked conclusion.
- [procedure] 1) State the obvious explanation. 2) Raise separate objections, each testing a different fact. 3) Use constraints such as a locked exit. 4) Test the theory with physical evidence that must match. 5) Ask whether the motive supports the story. When: an accepted theory has a visible flaw. Why: independent objections test each link in the chain.
- [procedure] 1) State each inference as an explicit guess. 2) Label each guess as uncertain. 3) Derive the next action from the chain. 4) Revise the chain if a test fails. When: Turning a strong but incomplete clue into a testable lead. Why: Marking uncertainty keeps the hypothesis open to testing and revision.
- [procedure] 1) Keep several candidate solutions consistent with the clues placed so far. 2) Hold back a clue that separates them. 3) Reveal it in a later room or conversation. When: Designing a multi-answer puzzle or mystery.
- [procedure] 1) Collect confirmed facts. 2) Mark each inference separately. 3) Assemble a theory from the facts. 4) List questions it cannot answer. 5) Derive predictions and test them against new evidence. When: investigator has several facts but no single explanation.
- [procedure] 1) Start from a known regular pattern. 2) Treat an observed condition as evidence of an unseen state. 3) State each inference explicitly, noting where it could fail. 4) Ask what the conclusion changes for action. When: when direct observation of the hidden state is limited.
- [procedure] 1) List every requirement the physical act demanded. 2) Match each requirement to a skill, access, build or location. 3) Set aside any candidate who fails one requirement. 4) Verify the assumptions behind each filter. When: a physical act has been done and its performer is unknown. Why: Working backward from the act narrows the field by requirement.
- [procedure] 1) Start from one odd detail. 2) Propose a simple innocent explanation and test whether it fits all facts. 3) Build a competing explanation that fits the anomaly better. 4) Check which explanation also accounts for the other facts. 5) Use the leading hypothesis to choose the next check. When: Several facts are known and an anomaly needs explaining. Why: Competing hypotheses prevent committing to the first plausible reason.
- [procedure] 1) List each explanation for the evidence. 2) Test each against what is known of the people involved. 3) Check for further evidence, such as a changed document, that may favour one. 4) Keep the explanations open until the key source is checked. When: Several explanations fit the same evidence. Why: Character knowledge and further documents separate explanations that the evidence alone cannot.
- [procedure] 1) List each hypothesis. 2) State what each must explain. 3) Check each against the evidence and keep only those that survive. When: A puzzle has several plausible explanations. Why: Plausibility alone does not confirm an explanation.
- [procedure] 1) List what each element of the observed scheme must accomplish for it to work. 2) Infer the signal that lets an unaware agent identify the target. 3) Test the reconstruction against the remaining evidence before accepting it. When: Repeated, regular traces suggest an unseen process. Why: A reconstruction that fits the constraints and survives testing is more reliable than a plausible story alone.
- [procedure] 1) Identify what the move delayed or prevented. 2) Infer the action that the delay enabled. 3) Use that action to narrow the possible routes or times of access. When: A body or object has been moved to a new position that seems purposeful. Why: The purpose reveals a required action and a timed window.
- [procedure] 1) Write the explanation as a sequence of steps. 2) Test each link against physical evidence. 3) When a link fails, revise the chain rather than defend it. When: Several explanations fit a scene. Why: A chain with a broken link does not hold, however coherent its story.
- [procedure] 1) List the observable signs. 2) Map each to a general class. 3) Intersect the classes. 4) Ask where that combination of hardship and injury could occur to reach a specific place. When: Designing or reading a deduction that moves from a broad category to a specific conclusion. Why: Successive intersection turns vague signs into a precise inference.
- [procedure] 1) List the evidence a rival theory would predict. 2) Search every place that evidence could be. 3) Count an empty search as support only if it covered those places. When: Reviewing a theory before accepting it. Why: Tests the theory against alternatives rather than only its own predictions.
- [procedure] 1) State a fact that must be true. 2) Ask what must have happened for it to be true. 3) Eliminate candidates who lacked the needed resources or character. 4) Link the remaining candidate to a physical object. 5) Check each link against evidence. When: Reasoning from a required condition to the person who could have supplied it. Why: Each link narrows the field and tests whether the chain holds.
- [procedure] 1) Park a tentative idea explicitly as a hypothesis to confirm or disprove later. 2) Do not act on it. 3) Label it when it is set aside or dropped. When: When an early idea appears before enough evidence exists. Why: An unlabelled provisional idea can quietly harden into belief.
- [procedure] 1) Identify the point where a neat first solution leaves loose ends. 2) Return to the first event in the chain. 3) Rerun the sequence of events against the existing evidence. When: A proposed solution leaves loose ends. Why: Errors often lie at the start of the chain, and earlier evidence is still valid.
- [procedure] 1) State a theory as a prediction of an observable event at a specific time and place. 2) Choose an observation that could prove it wrong. 3) Plan watch points and what to record based on the prediction. When: When a theory can be checked at a particular time and place. Why: A falsifiable prediction tells the investigator where to look and what would refute the theory.
- [procedure] 1) List broad possibilities. 2) Subdivide each into hypotheses about who acts, then alternatives. 3) For each branch, state the inquiry method that would test it. 4) Eliminate branches with decisive checks. 5) Ask which hypothesis explains all the evidence. When: Several explanations fit the first facts and the investigator must choose which to test first. Why: Structured branching keeps tests focused and exposes explanations that fail to account for all evidence.
- [procedure] 1) List each familiar explanation for an odd physical signature. 2) Test each against every detail of the signature. 3) Keep the one that fits all details and reject the rest. When: when several explanations fit a physical signature. Why: a tidy explanation can fail on a single anatomical detail.
- [tradeoff] Stating which parts of a solution rest on inference and which on proven fact separates reasoned theory from certainty, and keeps the solution credible. When: A final explanation depends partly on interpretation of evidence. Why: Acknowledging inferential gaps makes the remaining claims more trustworthy.
- [tradeoff] A fixed charge template gives a consistent, checkable crime definition, but it fails when the definition itself is disputed. When: Choosing how to define crimes in a game. Why: Consistency helps play until the definition is contested.
- [tradeoff] Keeping a working theory partly hidden can build suspense but makes collaborators trust an unexplained method, which can cause resentment and slow progress. When: An investigator leads a team through a case. Why: Unshared reasoning reduces the team's ability to check and contribute.
- [tradeoff] A broad, compelling theory is strong but hard to test, so it cannot by itself link a place to a crime.
- [pitfall] The first plausible or dramatic explanation, such as a medical, financial or signal-break reading, may be incomplete or wrong; test it against rivals, including the favoured one, before committing. Why: Early fit is not verified fit; a group that settles early on one reading can miss the real cause.
- [pitfall] Accepting the first tidy or sensible explanation, such as a medical or financial one, before testing it against physical evidence or exposing details locks in a false account. When: A plausible story fits the first clue found. Why: Early fit is not the same as verified fit.
- [pitfall] Record an untested route, such as theft from a hotel, as a hypothesis, since a plausible route is only a possibility until evidence confirms it. When: reasoning about how a suspect could have moved.
- [pitfall] Accepting the first tidy theory that closes every gap or explains only the central event leaves real or peripheral anomalies unexamined and can convict the wrong person.
- [pitfall] Accepting a clue's first reading, such as a deficit, because it fits the official theory, without checking records or the suspect's possessions, can convict the wrong person. When: A clue supports an existing accusation. Why: Confirmation of a theory replaces testing the clue.
- [pitfall] Fixating on one suspect, often from a strong first impression, stops an investigator retracing steps or considering others, so new evidence and the real culprit are missed. When: an investigator clings to one suspect despite new evidence. Why: a fixed theory filters out what does not fit.
- [pitfall] Broad knowledge still has gaps; a reasoner's method works only on material already gathered. Why: deduction cannot reach facts the reasoner lacks.
- [pitfall] Reasoning cut off from observation and experiment tends to rest on hypothesis, because imagination fills the gaps that evidence should have filled. Why: Without outside checks, conclusions are shaped by what the mind already expects.
- [pitfall] Trusting a sudden flash of insight without testing it can lead to a false conclusion, even though the first reaction may be disbelief that later proves warranted.
- [pitfall] Accepting a fortunate outcome as a sign rather than a test result skips the ordinary explanation of a coincidence.
- [pitfall] The most reasonable-sounding theory can be the one a deceiver wanted investigators to adopt.
- [pitfall] Accepting the first deduction drawn from a clue can lock in an error.
- [pitfall] Accepting the first plausible explanation of a clue can mislead, especially when an accomplice offers a ready theory that points at the accused.
- [pitfall] The first theory about a locked room is often wrong, and the scene itself can contradict it.
- [pitfall] Long reasoning passages slow pacing unless they are tied to a decision or a danger. When: Writing extended deduction in a story or game. Why: Readers lose momentum when reasoning has no stakes.
- [pitfall] Building an elaborate theory on an unverified premise or clue, without corroboration, produces circular and fragile reasoning, since the conclusion is assumed inside the premise. When: A case has an unexamined assumption shaping later steps, or a clue is treated as needing no corroboration. Why: Unverified premises make the whole chain fragile.
- [pitfall] A theory built to fit a wished-for outcome or held with felt certainty is hard to drop and can survive contrary evidence; treat such satisfaction as a warning, not proof. When: A suspect or observer invests in a favoured theory, or an investigator feels certain before testing. Why: The stake or certainty makes contrary facts easy to dismiss and stops the search for contrary evidence.
- [pitfall] A hypothesis that predicts nothing checkable, or explains every outcome, cannot be tested and carries no evidential weight; it needs a result that could contradict it. When: When evaluating whether a theory has been validated. Why: Without a result that could contradict it, a theory carries no evidential weight.
- [pitfall] Widely repeated claims or inferences spread as accepted theory before evidence confirms them, and repetition makes a conclusion feel proven even when its premise is faulty. When: When a conclusion rests on a widely repeated claim. Why: Repetition makes a claim feel proven without examination.
- [pitfall] State and test assumptions about a person's knowledge, memory or attention before building a reconstruction or plan on them; false assumptions can ruin it. When: Building an account of what a suspect did, or planning contact based on a guess about the target. Why: Hidden assumptions can make a reconstruction look more certain than the evidence allows; wrong assumptions can ruin a plan.
- [pitfall] Committing to a first impression or first plausible interpretation anchors the reading, so a later correction or small discrepancy can overturn the whole conclusion. When: Forming an early judgment from a witness account, or committing to a first plausible interpretation. Why: Early impressions anchor the interpretation, and a correction can invalidate the conclusions built on them.
- [pitfall] People treat a general impression of manner, such as a tired or clean-living look, as proof, though it fits several causes and should not settle suspicion alone. Why: Impressions feel conclusive to their holder, and one look is consistent with multiple causes.
- [pitfall] A conclusion whose supporting signs or reasoning steps were never shown feels like a trick, undermining trust; show the visible signs in advance so players can follow the chain. When: The reveal of a conclusion happens without its supporting signs shown in advance. Why: Players cannot follow or appreciate a chain whose links were never visible.
- [pitfall] Investigators can spend long stretches on dramatic, sinister theories and miss an ordinary habit that explains the anomaly. When: an inquiry stalls with competing explanations. Why: dramatic explanations feel more meaningful than mundane ones.
- [pitfall] Treating a routine as fixed is a mistake, since staff may deviate from it.
- [pitfall] A case built only on reasoning, without physical evidence, can be dismantled under challenge and fails to deliver a conviction.
- [pitfall] Assuming an office title always names the same person across a whole period is a mistake, because acting holders can change during it.
- [pitfall] Presenting analogy as certainty misleads the reader, especially when the inputs were measured only in conditions far from the case at hand. When: Reasoning from indirect or extrapolated data. Why: Extrapolated figures can fail at extremes where they were never tested.
- [pitfall] Adopting the opposing side's conclusion before the evidence is weighed leads to confusing an apparent case with proof.
- [pitfall] Accepting a single obvious explanation such as a hasty search for all disturbances leaves contradicting anomalies unexplained and hides the real sequence of events. When: evaluating a disturbed scene with many signs of disorder. Why: Anomalies ignored under a single story can reveal a second, concealed story.
- [pitfall] Fixed ideas and strong reactions to small gains or setbacks cause repeated reasoning failures. When: An investigator is building conclusions from early results. Why: Overreaction locks the reasoner into a belief before the evidence supports it.
- [pitfall] Each link in a chain of necessary conditions rests on an assumption, such as a judgment about character, so a plausible story must still be confirmed. When: Building a deduction from character judgments. Why: Character-based assumptions can feel like proof without being verified.
- [pitfall] Felt irregularities in darkness can be read as complex shape when they are a few shallow niches, producing a false picture of the space.
- [pitfall] Repeated failure can harden an interrogator's conviction until the case becomes a contest of personal honour rather than evidence.
- [pitfall] A single matching symbol can create false certainty. Inferences linking a clue to a person rest on a statement, not a confirmed identity, and should be treated as provisional. When: Solving a clue by matching one symbol or reference. Why: One match can seem conclusive while the underlying link is still unverified.
- [pitfall] An elaborate theory that explains nothing can hold attention as well as a side quarrel while advancing nothing.
- [pitfall] Players tend to read a vague answer as a clue in itself, which wastes attention and can mislead them. When: A witness gives an empty or evasive answer. Why: Ambiguity invites over-interpretation unless the answer is checked against facts.
- [pitfall] Whoever frames the questions decides what the answer can confirm. A check drafted by the claimant can look cooperative while limiting what is actually tested. When: The claimant offers to supply or draft the verification. Why: The framing controls the scope of confirmation.
- [pitfall] Fixation on one theory can draw attention away from a concrete clue the theory does not explain, or lead a real clue to be rejected.
- [pitfall] Building a general theory from a few sensational cases, or assuming every unexplained death is murder, overstates what detected cases show. When: Drawing conclusions from notable or unusual cases. Why: Famous cases are unrepresentative, so conclusions drawn from them are biased.
- [pitfall] Investigative analysis can drift into vague reasoning with no concrete test; a clue must be stated plainly to be acted on. Why: Unstated or vague clues cannot be checked or used.
- [pitfall] Choosing a suspect because a story pattern should end that way is confirmation bias presented as deduction; motive must be shown, not assumed from genre. When: when selecting a suspect from a story pattern. Why: Genre expectations substitute for evidence of motive.
- [pitfall] A neat theory held too firmly may get its general shape right while a specific detail, such as the particular occupant, is wrong. When: When a theory feels elegant and complete. Why: Confidence in the overall shape can hide errors in its specifics.
- [pitfall] A claim stays open until a test is actually carried out. When: Relying on an untested physical claim. Why: Without the test, the disputed fact is unresolved.
- [pitfall] A hypothesis tree can grow so large that the investigator loses track of the evidence that matters, and attachment to a favoured branch can shape which tests get done.
- [pitfall] Keeping a conclusion after the premise behind it has collapsed leads to wrong deductions. When: When new evidence contradicts an assumption a past rejection relied on. Why: The rejection depended on a premise that no longer holds.
- [pitfall] Dismissing a theory as too improbable without testing it against the physical facts can discard the true explanation. When: Ruling out candidate explanations. Why: Intuitive improbability is not evidence against a theory that the physical data could support.
- [pitfall] The observer's expectations shape how ambiguous marks are read, so a faint record can be read as a shape that is not really there. When: Interpreting ambiguous evidence after a suspicion has formed. Why: Prior expectation makes weak resemblances look like confirmation.
- [pitfall] Treating a plausible innocent explanation for a suspicious act as proof of innocence wrongly closes a lead; it should stay possible but not decisive. When: A suspicious act has several harmless explanations. Why: Multiple innocent reasons can all be true at once, so one plausible explanation does not settle the question.

## clue design
- [model] A scene can contain two crimes: a staged crime that is solved first and a hidden second crime that remains. A later killer may reuse the original planner's prepared devices. When: Solving a staged death reveals unresolved anomalies. Why: The planner's devices can become a trap for a later offender.
- [model] Unverifiable evidence has three uses: opening an inquiry, adding presumption alongside other facts, or serving as sufficient ground for judgment. It should never be sufficient ground for judgment alone. When: deciding what weight unverifiable testimony carries.
- [definition] Fair play gives the audience the same characters and information as the investigator, showing every clue, suspect and fact needed before the reveal, so the answer can be reasoned out. When: when designing a deduction-based reveal that must feel earned. Why: Readers can check the explanation against the facts they were given, so the solution feels earned.
- [definition] A fair-play clue sits in plain view from the start, presented as ordinary and unemphasised, so an attentive audience reconstructs its meaning only after the reveal. Why: Players can only reason from clues they actually saw.
- [mechanism] A confessed detail that can be checked elsewhere opens a new line of inquiry, so the player is rewarded for verifying it. When: designing an interrogation mechanic.
- [mechanism] A document with two layers of correction rewards careful inspection. A thorough reader who looks beneath the top change can recover the earlier version. When: Designing a clue that can be altered and then concealed. Why: Layers reward closer examination without making the clue unreadable.
- [mechanism] A glyph repeated at several trap or death sites lets players connect separate rooms to one hidden faction. When: players must discover a hidden group through scattered sites. Why: the repeated mark is the only visible thread linking the sites.
- [mechanism] Tie a physical clue's result to a specific examiner, so a player who requests a second opinion from a skilled NPC can change the outcome. When: when designing evidence checks. Why: different examiners can read the same sign differently.
- [mechanism] Once a material is identified, an ambiguous tactile cue can gain a new meaning, such as seams between plates, forcing players to revise their mental map.
- [mechanism] A puzzle state that cannot be reached points the player back to an earlier assumption, such as a door believed to be locked.
- [mechanism] A body dressed in a player character's clothes can carry one inconsistency, such as mismatched shoes or a hidden item, that a careful player can check. When: Designing a body or identity swap for a mystery. Why: A single planted inconsistency lets an attentive player expose the swap.
- [mechanism] A document found on a victim can supply a motive, so a physical trace carries meaning beyond its appearance. When: When a found document may explain why a person was targeted. Why: Contents of a found document can reveal a financial or personal reason for the crime.
- [rule] Build findable tells into staged or fabricated clues, such as a trimmed page, missing name or mismatched detail, and reward players who check them against NPC's known abilities. When: Designing a fabricated document or staged clue. Why: Tells let careful players detect the deception. (x2)
- [rule] Treat absences as clues: a key NPC removed from a planned event, or a missing key, should be noticed and pursued by attentive players. When: Designing an NPC-dependent event, or a missing key that could reveal an access route. Why: Unexpected absence rewards attentive players; dismissing it removes the trail. (x2)
- [rule] Every clue or fact a solution depends on must be shown before the reveal, in plain words without interpretation, so the solution is reachable; otherwise it reads as a cheat. When: when designing or writing a mystery reveal, or promising a solvable mystery. Why: Players accept only reveals they could have reasoned out from earlier evidence; holding back clues breaks the promise of a solvable mystery.
- [rule] Plant small, seemingly irrelevant details early, such as a note, odd object or repeated phrase, and trace each later payoff back to them so the reveal feels fair. Why: Players can only accept a payoff they could have anticipated; fair-play reveals reward attentive players.
- [rule] Use verifiable fixed anchors, such as timestamps, a bell or a door, and plant clock discrepancies the player can compare, so a reconstructed timeline is fair. When: when players must check the sequence of events or alibis. Why: Shared timestamps let players order events themselves; discrepancies give a fair route to the truth.
- [rule] Require players to find in-world records, such as a bank ledger, rent receipt, laundry ticket or insurer's file, to verify a claim rather than guess. When: when designing evidence the player must confirm. Why: Verifiable records turn suspicion into a checkable fact, and locating them makes confirmation an active step.
- [rule] Give a full, ordered record of what was discovered and when, with a recap showing each link, so the audience can check each step. When: when the solution depends on earlier discoveries the reader must rebuild. Why: Visible links let players confirm the solution follows from evidence.
- [rule] Leave a clue's meaning open until later evidence settles it; ambiguity may stay unresolved in the background, since a guess presented as fact misleads later reasoning. When: when a clue is meant to pay off later or its meaning is not yet supported. Why: Unresolved meaning keeps the mystery alive; premature answers can mislead later reasoning.
- [rule] Plant physical tells, such as a moved rug or fresh floor patch, so players can infer that a body was moved.
- [rule] A fact used to contradict a commitment must be verifiable, or the resulting shift in view proves nothing.
- [rule] Test equally placed suspects against the same motive question, so differences in the result come from their stakes rather than from how each was examined. When: two or more suspects look equally placed.
- [rule] Restate the governing constraint of a key rule in plain, non-technical terms before the audience needs it, so they hold the same facts as the investigator. When: A later deduction depends on a rule or limit the audience must understand. Why: Shared facts let the audience judge later conduct as evidence rather than surprise.
- [rule] In a fair-play mystery, plant small observable reactions early so the audience can reinterpret them when the hidden fact comes out.
- [rule] A mystery depends on keeping its answer hidden while still giving the audience fair access to the clues that solve it.
- [rule] Give each suspect a schedule that the player can verify through NPC witnesses, and let the alibi break when the player finds a gap in it. When: when designing a murder or theft puzzle.
- [rule] Confront a suspect with a verifiable document, such as a receipt found in a desk, rather than with an unsupported accusation. When: When a suspect denies involvement and a physical record exists. Why: A document that can be checked is harder to deny than a claim.
- [rule] Show or mention a concealed method earlier in the story; a method never shown will feel like a cheat to the audience.
- [rule] Plant clues before a late identity reveal; withholding information that was never planted feels like cheating, not a fair reversal.
- [rule] Plant a note with a tell, such as an unusual letter form or ink, tied to one NPC, and let players find it by comparing against a known sample. When: Designing a clue that points to a specific character. Why: A comparable sample keeps the solution fair and checkable.
- [rule] Plant a legend that players can verify through two independent sources, such as a shop record and a witness NPC.
- [rule] Give each planted document an answer that the player can reach using evidence available in the level. Why: Otherwise the document reads as arbitrary rather than a fair clue.
- [rule] Seed a chain of NPC deaths reported by different sources, and include one independently verifiable death so that players gain trust in the rest. When: Designing a series of reported events. Why: A verified event lets players judge which unverified reports are reliable.
- [rule] Give letters a hidden layer, polite text that the player decodes using what they know of the sender's motive. When: Writing a letter that carries a concealed meaning. Why: Players reward effort when surface wording must be read against known motives.
- [rule] Balance temperament-based elimination with evidence the player can verify, so that elimination feels fair. When: Designing suspect elimination in a game. Why: Unverifiable profile reasoning feels arbitrary to players.
- [rule] State every contradiction in the evidence plainly so the audience can test explanations against it.
- [rule] Plant the decisive clue early in the scene, visible but not emphasised, so a later reversal feels fair. When: When designing a late twist that overturns the leading theory. Why: A clue that stands out gets noticed and spoils the reversal; a buried one feels unfair.
- [rule] Write the true handler's habit into a readable detail the player can compare, so planted-item puzzles stay fair.
- [rule] Let the audience test each competing theory against the available evidence before it collapses, so a false lead is discredited by facts rather than by decree.
- [rule] Every clue needed for a final reveal or accusation must appear earlier, before it matters, so the player can rebuild the chain by their own reasoning. When: Designing a mystery, deduction puzzle or late reveal. Why: Fair play lets players reach the solution through their own reasoning.
- [rule] Plant every clue needed for a late reveal or open question earlier in the world, so the final explanation recalls known facts and the ending rewards collection over guesswork. When: Designing a late reveal, finale or open question. Why: Clues placed early let the reveal recall known facts rather than introduce surprises.
- [rule] Scatter distinct, linkable parts of an answer across sources such as a letter, record, portrait, memory aid, map fragment or worn mechanism, so only combining them reveals it. When: Designing a mystery answer, sealed vault or multi-source investigation. Why: Joining sources is the puzzle; no single source gives the answer.
- [rule] Place important clues in ordinary or fringe props, visible enough to notice but not labelled as clues, rewarding players who study the whole room. When: Placing ordinary objects that may matter later. Why: Subtle placement rewards observant players.
- [rule] Give hidden things a subtle visible cue, such as a loose bolt, open shutter or faint seam, early enough that observant players can notice it before the solution. When: Hiding a secret, panel or solution the player should find. Why: A cue lets observant players succeed; a solution they could not spot feels unfair.
- [rule] Make damaged, wrecked or concealed items, such as a torn note's surviving word, point toward a specific room or object to examine. When: Designing a damaged document or wreckage as a clue. Why: A surviving detail gives a concrete place to investigate.
- [rule] Place remains, ruins or cellar foundations that match or contradict witness descriptions and documents, so players confirm or refute a claim by finding them.
- [rule] Record who knew a suspect and for how long, so a player can test a claim against verifiable history.
- [rule] Give clues players can verify by inspection, and make sure a suspect's explanation can actually change the outcome. When: Designing an interrogation or deduction. Why: Testable clues and responsive explanations keep deduction fair.
- [rule] Before accepting confirming evidence for a theory, state which observation would count against it. Why: A conclusion chosen in advance makes every outcome look like confirmation.
- [rule] Design each rumour so a specific NPC can confirm or refute it, giving the player a clear route to verify it.
- [rule] Use an inconsistency in an NPC story as a clue that points the player to a second source for checking.
- [rule] Expose the reasoning path, from behaviour to pattern to motive, so players can test an inference rather than simply accept it.
- [rule] Let a puzzle test a guessed rule, such as an NPC reacting to an item in a room, so the player can recreate the setup and time the response. When: Designing a puzzle where the solution is a hypothesised rule. Why: Repeatable setup lets players confirm or refute their guess.
- [rule] Give partial-match feedback: an NPC that deviates slightly should yield an ambiguous result rather than a clean yes or no. When: A player's test of an NPC's reaction is close to but not exactly the trigger. Why: Ambiguous feedback keeps the deduction open and reflects real variation.
- [rule] Fix the reference's page layout so decoding is repeatable, and give a fail state for a wrong edition.
- [rule] Make environment features that enable climbing visible and inspectable, and signal an exceptional ability early, so an impossible-looking entry feels earned. When: Designing a locked room reachable by an ability-based route. Why: Visible features and earlier demonstrations make the solution fair.
- [rule] Set a reveal to the rules the player was given, so the solution reads as a clever use of allowed means. When: Designing a puzzle solution in a generated level.
- [rule] Build tracks puzzles so that two possible characters share a trace, forcing the player to find a second clue.
- [rule] In an escape puzzle, show all obvious exits as checkable and closed, and keep the real route off the initial list so it is discovered only after elimination. When: Designing a room or sequence where the player must find how an NPC or item escaped. Why: Players feel a satisfying reveal when the obvious options are exhausted first.
- [rule] Make a riddle depend on an item found elsewhere and reward the player when the key connects the two. When: Designing a cryptic clue. Why: Ties exploration to puzzle solving.
- [rule] Keep at least one essential clue findable even when decoys and lost evidence are present. When: generating or placing clues in a mystery level. Why: Otherwise the player cannot reach the solution.
- [rule] Ask a player who witnessed a destruction to vouch for it later, and let a puzzle hinge on whether they actually saw it happen.
- [rule] Place a side passage or service stair in the layout as the answer to an access puzzle, so the solution is fair and checkable. When: Designing a sealed-room puzzle. Why: A visible, checkable route lets players verify their deduction.
- [rule] Place a small unexplained anomaly, such as an unlocked door, and explain it later through an NPC encounter. When: designing a hidden arrangement the player will learn about. Why: the anomaly rewards attention and sets up the reveal.
- [rule] Make the matching step a physical action the player performs, such as placing an object on a mark, so the test feels earned. When: A clue must be verified by comparison. Why: Active testing makes the verification feel like the player's own discovery.
- [rule] Describe a hidden object's location relative to a fixed landmark and a count, such as steps from an opening, so players can find the exact spot. When: Placing a cache. Why: Landmark plus count gives a checkable position.
- [rule] Make a scripted encounter look like a fair fight, while leaving a procedural oddity, such as a mismatched detail, that exposes the setup to careful players. When: Designing a staged or deceptive combat encounter. Why: Players should be able to detect the trap through clues.
- [rule] Give an unexpected location a fair-play clue on its approach path, so the solution can be discovered in hindsight.
- [rule] Pair ambient foreboding with a concrete clue so the player knows what to watch for. When: a level builds dread through warnings or ambient signs. Why: Undirected dread gives the player nothing to act on.
- [rule] Design a puzzle sequence so the player can predict the next clue, and place the missing clue where the prediction points. When: Building a multi-clue puzzle. Why: Prediction rewards pattern recognition and confirms the solution path.
- [rule] Give a sealed room a stated boast, such as on a sign or in lore, and place the real weakness in an element the boast does not mention. Why: Players who look past the claim are rewarded.
- [rule] Use a fixed physical trace, such as a device defect, to link an anonymous object to one person, and make the player locate that object to make the link.
- [heuristic] Plant a deliberately unreliable clue, such as a dream-sourced or garbled retelling, that players can correct by checking it against other evidence. Why: Fair misdirection rewards verification. (x2)
- [heuristic] Show a character's routine or behaviour before its anomaly, so a later reversal or changed signal is recognised as fair. When: when a clue or twist depends on deviation from a routine or repeated action. Why: Players can detect a change only if they first saw the normal state.
- [heuristic] Deliver clues as offhand remarks, record them, and cross-check against objects and testimony, since a throwaway detail can become decisive. Why: A trivial-seeming detail can answer a question once a theory raises it.
- [heuristic] Design a single odd object or trace, such as a patched sign or relabelled crate, with both an innocent and a criminal explanation so players must test which fits all the evidence. When: when designing environmental clues or investigation puzzles.
- [heuristic] Named people, places and details from a delirious speaker are leads that can be checked, even when the speaker's mind is unreliable. When: a delirious witness names specifics. Why: concrete details can be verified independently.
- [heuristic] Holes in the ground combined with displaced material at height, such as loosened mortar on a wall top, can show where a ladder stood.
- [heuristic] Let an independent source confirm a deduced conclusion, so the inference is tested rather than accepted as a confident claim. When: A character or player reaches a conclusion from signs alone. Why: An outside confirmation separates a sound inference from an unchecked boast.
- [heuristic] Plant earlier fair clues, such as a mismatched detail in clothing, so that a later reveal feels earned rather than like a trick. Why: an unprepared reveal reads as a cheat.
- [heuristic] An object's wear, engravings and marks can be read in layers to infer occupation, habits and companions, and each inference should be tested. When: when reconstructing a person from a small set of possessions.
- [heuristic] Plant clues in offhand remarks, private fears or vague moods so the audience registers them long before their meaning is clear.
- [heuristic] In a fair-play puzzle, state a physical fact early that a later event must either contradict or explain. When: the solution depends on physical details the audience can check. Why: an early fact gives the audience a way to verify the later event.
- [heuristic] Plant a contradiction early in the story, such as a voice or accent detail, so a later reveal of identity reframes earlier scenes fairly. When: Writing a late reveal that recontextualises the story. Why: Clues seen before the reveal make the twist feel earned rather than arbitrary.
- [heuristic] An obscure case signals that the matter is not yet understood, so keep working rather than treating it as settled.
- [heuristic] Unfalsifiability matters most for claims with no independent measurable cause; claims that have one can be checked directly. When: Assessing a hidden-cause accusation.
- [heuristic] Separate evidence players can verify, such as a found object or second witness, from evidence only one NPC can see, and give the latter a visible cost to rely on. When: Designing witness or accusation systems. Why: Costly unverifiable claims discourage accusations based on them alone.
- [heuristic] Repeat a puzzle with one parameter shifted, such as a marker's position, to test whether the player's deduction depends on the intended variable. When: Designing or validating deduction puzzles in generated or authored levels. Why: A single-variable change shows which cue the solution actually relies on.
- [heuristic] Silence can be data: an NPC who does not react to a noise may have known its source, which the player can read as a clue. When: When designing NPC reactions to events in a level. Why: A non-reaction implies prior knowledge, which rewards attentive players.
- [heuristic] Use readable but cryptic marks, such as letters that can be read in reverse, as a puzzle that rewards a second look. When: Designing optional puzzles in generated environments. Why: Marks that resist a first reading reward closer attention.
- [heuristic] A refusing NPC can set a flag the player must interpret, with later events confirming whether silence meant protection or guilt. When: designing ambiguous NPC responses. Why: delayed confirmation rewards reasoning.
- [heuristic] Include predictive clues, such as lit windows that should be dark if a place is empty, so players can test their theories inside the game. When: When designing environmental clues for player deduction. Why: Testable predictions let players confirm or reject their reasoning.
- [heuristic] A puzzle can require the player to check one precise fact, such as an angle, object position or hour, to confirm or reject a theory. When: Designing a deduction puzzle. Why: A single precise check gives the player a clear, decisive test.
- [heuristic] A trap can be driven by a slow process such as burning fuel, closing a gate or dropping a bar after the player has passed.
- [heuristic] Give one object two meanings, a culprit's false clue and a victim's sign, so a player who reads it one way is led to the wrong suspect.
- [heuristic] Let a well-hidden opponent be exposed when an unrelated event forces a choice.
- [heuristic] Keeping word breaks makes frequency analysis easier, because one-letter words can validate a candidate solution.
- [heuristic] Place an unexpected physical trace, such as a fresh chip in an odd spot, as a clue that links to a later mechanism.
- [heuristic] Make paid or threatened informants give partial, cryptic clues that gain value only when cross-checked against other sources.
- [heuristic] A clue item can carry a material property that is checked in two stages, first visually, then by test.
- [heuristic] A coroner-style report can give a directional clue, such as a wound angle that places the shooter on a walkable staircase the player can reach.
- [heuristic] Design a weak clue so that it pays off only when the player also finds a second, stronger clue. When: Building a puzzle with clues of unequal strength. Why: Pairing clues rewards players who combine evidence.
- [heuristic] Give evidence that a supernatural explanation covers everything, then offer a test that breaks it, so the player can rule the explanation out fairly. When: A mystery is built on a supernatural or unfalsifiable cause. Why: A fair test lets the player reason to the answer rather than guess.
- [heuristic] Place small, mundane objects, such as a stopped clock, a single glass or a dropped key, that players can read to reconstruct what happened. When: designing a scene for investigation. Why: ordinary objects feel discoverable and grounded.
- [heuristic] Place a crumpled discarded note in a room so players who search the grate can recover the lure and reconstruct its timing. When: Designing a dungeon that rewards searching. Why: Players who search thoroughly can recover evidence of a trap.
- [heuristic] A notepad can hold a reversed impression that a tool reveals as a fragment pointing to a location, confirmed by a full note found elsewhere.
- [heuristic] Reason from the culprit's motives to find where a discarded object would physically end up, such as close to the scene and out of plain sight. When: When a scene has been searched and a clue is still missing. Why: A person hiding an object avoids carrying it away and avoids leaving it visible, so the likely location follows from that behaviour.
- [heuristic] Loose fibres, displaced material or an observer's nervous reaction can reveal that an item was concealed inside an ordinary container. When: Reconstructing where an object was hidden among everyday things. Why: Disturbances to ordinary objects leave traces that a careful searcher can notice.
- [heuristic] Design a clue's physical state, such as a crumpled note, so it supports more than one reading and can serve as a red herring. When: Designing evidence that should not point clearly to one answer. Why: A single physical state can be read as hurried concealment or as an innocent return.
- [heuristic] A puzzle can let the player plant a fake relic and observe which NPC reacts to it, giving a reliable tell. When: Designing identification puzzles in a game. Why: The NPC's reaction to a planted object reveals hidden knowledge.
- [heuristic] An item with no identifying marks still carries information, because the absence of marks can itself narrow or support a conclusion. When: When a clue object lacks obvious identifiers. Why: The lack of markings is a meaningful observation, not a null result.
- [heuristic] Design a series of traps or deaths that share one element, and let early ones look random, so the pattern rewards attention later. When: designing recurring hazards.
- [heuristic] Build an impossible scene first, with a single locked entrance, no hiding place and distracted witnesses. The solution must break one of these assumptions.
- [heuristic] Reward attention to small physical evidence, such as creases, reversed folds or wear, that proves a swap or tampering. When: when a puzzle's solution must be verifiable. Why: observable traces let players confirm a hypothesis.
- [heuristic] A decoy with mismatched details, such as seal, colour or address, can mislead players into believing the real target is a different item. When: when placing a false lead. Why: players read conflicting details as the true distinction.
- [heuristic] Open the mystery with a plausible natural explanation, then let one physical anomaly reopen the case at low cost to the player. Why: A calm first belief makes the later anomaly feel like a real discovery.
- [procedure] 1) List every fact the solution needs. 2) Confirm each appears before the reveal. 3) Explain the answer step by step, linking each step to a shown clue. 4) Move or add any missing clue onto a route the player must pass. 5) Let the reveal read as recognition of earlier evidence. When: when designing a mystery solution or reveal. Why: Players can check each step against clues they already saw.
- [procedure] 1) State the case against one suspect alone. 2) Add a second suspect or fact, tying each addition to a specific fact. 3) Present the combined case with each layer visible. When: evidence is circumstantial. Why: each layer can be judged on its own.
- [procedure] 1) Reread the whole work from start to finish. 2) Read it as a first-time audience would. 3) Note where the culprit becomes obvious. 4) Revise the structure from the start if needed. When: checking whether a mystery gives away its answer.
- [procedure] 1) Show the clue when it appears, with its sensory detail. 2) Withhold interpretation. 3) Supply context later that reveals its meaning. 4) Connect the clue to the answer in the final account. When: when a clue must be paid off later in the story. Why: the audience can catch up with the investigator.
- [procedure] 1) Show the key detail early as an ordinary habit or object. 2) Withhold its significance. 3) Later, trigger recall with an unrelated cue that connects the detail to the solution. When: Designing a mystery whose solution depends on a detail the audience has already seen. Why: A detail that seems ordinary when first shown can be recalled later as the answer, which makes the reveal fair.
- [procedure] 1) Give an inventory item wear, such as a chipped blade or worn sole, that implies a specific user. 2) Give one prop a condition that contradicts its expected owner. 3) Let the mismatch be the clue the player must notice. When: Designing clue-bearing props for investigation play. Why: Players infer ownership from physical traces, and a contradiction creates a discoverable deduction.
- [procedure] 1) Make the easy answer supported by visible evidence. 2) Leave one visible gap in that support. 3) Reward players who check the scene before accepting the group's view. When: When designing a mystery with a tempting wrong solution. Why: A gap in an apparently complete answer gives careful players a path to the truth without making the wrong answer unsupported.
- [procedure] 1) Check motive against financial or personal records. 2) Check means against a link to a workplace or training. 3) Check opportunity against a fixed schedule. Accept each layer only when records confirm it. When: when testing whether a solution accounts for all the timing and means.
- [procedure] 1) Define the trap as a chain of conditions, such as a note read, an item used and a timer. 2) Reveal the result to the player only through a later event. When: Designing delayed traps or consequences in a generated level. Why: A chain of conditions with delayed reveal sustains suspense and pacing.
- [procedure] 1) Enumerate every access path to the victim, such as food, drink, water and room items. 2) Let the player close paths one at a time through investigation. When: Generating a poisoning or sabotage puzzle. Why: Each closed path narrows the search and gives the player a sense of progress.
- [procedure] 1) Reconstruct the act that could have dropped the object. 2) Test whether the hiding place fits that act. 3) Accept the placement only if it is physically plausible. When: When judging how a concealed object came to be where it was found. Why: A hiding place that matches a plausible action gives a credible explanation of the clue.
- [tradeoff] Gate progress either behind one timed, exclusive decisive clue, or behind a threshold of weak cumulative clues that must agree; contributory clues alone may not establish a conclusion. When: Designing clue gates in a deduction mystery. Why: A single decisive clue gives a clean conclusion; a cumulative threshold makes players assemble the answer through reasoning. (x2)
- [tradeoff] Showing the audience every fact the investigator has, and the offender's actions, makes the challenge interpreting evidence rather than hunting for a hidden answer, but removes the whodunit surprise. When: Choosing between a hidden-culprit mystery and full disclosure. Why: Full disclosure shifts the challenge from identity to reasoning.
- [tradeoff] An identical object found in several places can link the people who carried it, but can also be a planted decoy pointing at the wrong person. When: A single object type could have many owners and the question is who carried it and when. Why: Matching items prove a connection only if the placement can be attributed; ownership alone does not establish guilt.
- [pitfall] A design that considers only the offender's route misses the investigator's route, so both routes must be written.
- [pitfall] A single odd detail is a lead, not a conclusion, since many anomalies have ordinary causes and an incomplete pattern can still point at the right suspect. When: when players build theories from one or two anomalies. Why: Coincidence produces many false anomalies.
- [pitfall] Treating an obvious trace as proof while overlooking a quieter one can send an investigation toward the wrong suspect. When: Several traces are present and one is conspicuous. Why: Attention drawn to the loud trace misses the subtle one.
- [pitfall] A signal too obvious to the characters invites the audience to ask why others miss it, which can break believability. When: A clue is visible to every character. Why: Characters who ignore an obvious signal look careless and undermine the mystery.
- [pitfall] Searching obvious hiding spots first can miss a concealment that the routine suggests.
- [pitfall] A single plan that shows only some sides of a property cannot fix its location with certainty.
- [pitfall] Assuming a search was exhaustive, or that a weapon must look like a known type, can hide the solution in plain view. When: when a method seems impossible given the items found.
- [pitfall] An imagined mechanism that outruns the evidence can waste effort, since a plausible-sounding idea can still be unsupported.
- [pitfall] Too many clues that do not link together leave players with no clear path through the case.
- [pitfall] Adding unneeded steps to a plan multiplies its points of failure, so extra complexity can make a mostly successful plan collapse. When: A plan is extended beyond what its goal requires. Why: Every added step is another dependency that can go wrong.
- [pitfall] A correct but limited clue can disappoint players if it leads nowhere, so even a narrow clue should point toward the next step. When: A clue confirms only a small fact. Why: Players expect clues to advance the case, and a dead end feels like wasted effort.

## puzzle design
- [definition] A mystery can be marvellous, meaning startling or supernatural, or complicated, meaning built from many interlocking parts; these two kinds must be kept separate. When: when deciding how a puzzle or case should be structured. Why: Mixing them makes a plain plan look like magic or a magic event look like a plot tangle.
- [definition] In a plain-view theft puzzle, the thief's intelligence rather than the object's hiding place is the puzzle, so the solver reasons about what the thief would choose.
- [mechanism] A puzzle that requires the player to test each conjecture against known facts before the next step unlocks makes reasoning, not guessing, the path to progress. When: Designing a puzzle that rewards deduction. Why: Gating progress on verified conclusions ties advancement to the player's reasoning rather than trial and error.
- [mechanism] Adjacent cells and brief co-location of prisoners can set up a witness-collusion puzzle for the player to solve. When: Designing prisoner placement in game levels. Why: Proximity creates opportunities for information exchange.
- [mechanism] A hidden identity behind a mask can carry an investigation, and the audience's expectations are reversed when the identity is revealed.
- [mechanism] A character can pass hidden knowledge forward to another with an instruction to recall it later, carrying the secret across time. When: A reveal must be delivered after a delay.
- [mechanism] A faction's official theory sets which leads are pursued and which are dropped as dead ends, so modelling it as data shapes the investigation.
- [mechanism] A hidden constraint on who may learn of the player's success restricts available actions and shapes the choices the player makes. When: when designing objectives that involve secrecy. Why: the constraint closes some options and pushes play toward others.
- [mechanism] When a clue contradicts a puzzle's starting assumption, players are forced to reopen the chain of reasoning and reconsider earlier conclusions. When: Designing a deduction puzzle with an explicit starting assumption. Why: Contradiction breaks a settled path and prompts re-evaluation.
- [mechanism] Removing nearby alternatives forces a target to search further, raising the chance they go to the spot where a substitute is kept. When: a target can satisfy a need from several places. Why: fewer local options shift their route.
- [mechanism] A key surrendered under pressure changes the NPC's relationship with the player, so it can work as a meaningful release mechanic.
- [behavior] A reply to a question that presumes an event reveals that the listener already knows of it, through the form of the answer. When: a question carries a hidden premise. Why: answers reveal knowledge the speaker did not intend to share.
- [rule] Design an ambiguous clue or action with two plausible readings, one false, and make the correct reading confirmable by a second checkable test. When: A clue should mislead briefly without being unfair; writing an action with ambiguous meaning. Why: A clue with only a false meaning cannot be solved fairly; single-cause motives leave players nothing to test.
- [rule] Tie each required condition, such as a debt, deposit or stolen item, to a playable trait or location, so players eliminate candidates who lacked it. When: Designing a deduction or suspect-elimination puzzle around a required resource. Why: Players can solve the puzzle by eliminating candidates who lacked the needed resource.
- [rule] Vary layout, method, reward and template details across generated or reused cases so earlier solutions don't carry over; repeat one signature deliberately only as a clue. When: When generating many encounters or reusing a case template. Why: Variation prevents easy memorisation; unchanged details let players carry over the earlier answer.
- [rule] Present a known result, such as a locked vault or dead guard, and let players reconstruct the event from body or object positions and missing items, not a cutscene. When: Designing a mystery puzzle. Why: Backward reasoning gives players a clear goal.
- [rule] A closing cutscene or journal entry can reconstruct the villain's plan in order, replaying events and linking each collected clue to its explanation so players can check them. When: when closing a mystery in a game.
- [rule] Hide a key to an alias or a puzzle solution in in-game text, such as a referenced play, library book or codex item description, that players must find and read closely. When: Designing puzzles around signatures, codes or items.
- [rule] When a puzzle seems impossible, check ordinary movement and routes before accepting magical or supernatural explanations. When: when designing or solving a seemingly impossible event. Why: Most apparent impossibilities resolve into plain routes once checked.
- [rule] Design evidence so it supports both innocent and guilty readings, and let the player decide whether the explanation fits. When: building mystery clues around a suspect's story.
- [rule] Do not rely on a staged threat against a target who can check the facts or call for help.
- [rule] Avoid staging a confrontation while the evidence is still conjecture, since a reconstruction built on guesswork can be challenged.
- [rule] Do not press a suspect toward a reversal of their account that the questioner cannot support with evidence.
- [rule] Tie a puzzle to a visible timer, such as a bell or a patrol route, so the player must act within a window.
- [rule] Hide the solution while showing NPCs' reasoning, so players can check deductions against the evidence themselves.
- [rule] Let the player see a hidden event, such as a cutscene or remembered vision, that in-world characters do not know, to create dramatic irony. When: Designing scenes where player knowledge should exceed character knowledge. Why: Player knowledge that characters lack generates suspense around their actions.
- [rule] Keep the resolution brief and confirm the outcome rather than revealing new information about the crime.
- [rule] Let a quiet guide NPC seem uninterested while quietly tracking the real lead, revealing this only at the reveal. When: designing a mystery. Why: a concealed lead rewards later recall.
- [rule] Offer character-based tells as an alternate route when a timeline check fails, but require the physical mechanism for final proof.
- [rule] Do not stage a false lead when it could harm an innocent person, or when the culprit is unlikely to react. When: planning a bluff or misdirection. Why: Misdirection costs more than it gains in those cases.
- [rule] Gate an NPC's cooperation behind a wait or a shift in tone, so a suspected player must spend time before the NPC talks. When: a puzzle NPC should pressure a suspected player.
- [rule] In a timed puzzle, use waiting periods, such as a transfer that completes after a set number of in-game days, so the player must infer its status.
- [rule] Let an NPC offer a theory, such as poisoning, that the player must check with a test before acting on it.
- [rule] Not all hidden or unsolved events in a game world should reach the player's investigation, so the visible cases should be presented as a sample. When: Designing a world with many events and few player-facing cases. Why: Players then understand that unsolved mysteries are not the full set of what happened.
- [rule] The designer should know the complete solution of a mystery from the start, even when the player-facing surface looks disordered. When: Designing a puzzle with an exact, checkable answer. Why: A fully known solution lets every clue be placed consistently.
- [rule] State the governing rule of a puzzle, such as what a lock accepts or what an item does, in plain text before the player faces the test. When: A puzzle's solution depends on a stated constraint. Why: A known constraint lets players reason to the answer rather than guess.
- [rule] Give puzzles a safe test, such as exposing a creature to a substance, so players can identify the dangerous item without losing the run. When: when designing identification puzzles. Why: A safe test lets players gather evidence through action.
- [rule] Make a motive discoverable through a recalculation puzzle that the player solves using figures given in the game. When: A financial motive is meant to be found by the player. Why: Solving from supplied numbers rewards careful reading.
- [rule] Put coded notices in an in-world newspaper or noticeboard, and let each message's content reveal its place in the sequence. When: Designing an ordering or decoding puzzle. Why: Players can rebuild the sequence from internal clues without dates.
- [rule] The true answer may be the least obvious one, provided it is the only explanation that fits all planted clues.
- [rule] In a rotating-suspect puzzle, give each NPC a clue and a motive that makes them plausible until the player clears them. When: designing a who-did-it puzzle with several NPCs. Why: each candidate must seem viable until evidence removes them.
- [rule] Keep a puzzle's intended solution path out of UI, tutorial text and NPC dialogue, so players cannot adapt to it in advance.
- [rule] State the intended sequence of events for a puzzle, then check that every clue supports it and that no clue points elsewhere too strongly. When: When designing a mystery or deduction puzzle. Why: Misleading clues break the intended solution.
- [rule] Use a family tree or inheritance record as a puzzle object, so players work out who gains before choosing a suspect. When: when designing motive-based puzzles.
- [rule] Build puzzles whose answer uses a general rule players already know, such as a mechanical or chemical principle, to reward lateral thinking. When: when designing deduction puzzles.
- [rule] Use objects with layered meaning, such as a sinister-looking item with a legitimate source, so players must check the source before drawing conclusions. When: Designing a clue object for an investigation puzzle.
- [rule] In a poisoning puzzle, make the food served to all guests safe, pushing the player toward the single item one NPC consumed. When: Designing a poisoning mystery. Why: Eliminating the shared source focuses the search.
- [rule] Make a puzzle's solution depend on information the player can reasonably gather, not on lucky timing, and include at least one fact that could contradict it. When: designing a deduction puzzle. Why: gatherable evidence keeps the answer fair and a falsifiable clue keeps it testable.
- [rule] Hold back one criterion until needed, so a final test can clear or condemn suspects late in the investigation. When: Designing a multi-stage deduction. Why: A withheld test keeps the final decision open.
- [rule] Let a dismissal or expulsion quest resolve through its procedural issue, while the real trigger surfaces through dialogue or documents. When: designing a quest driven by a hidden grievance.
- [rule] In measured-search puzzles, let a first-marker mistake grow along a line, rewarding careful measurement and landmark checks.
- [rule] Set a puzzle around a physical clock such as wet ink, drying blood or melting wax, so players can reconstruct when a door was opened or a trap set. When: designing timing-based puzzles.
- [rule] Encode a puzzle solution in fixed objects, such as a seated figure or a chair facing a doorway, so the player can infer where a threat must be.
- [rule] Make every item needed to unlock a puzzle visible before the unlocking step. When: When designing a puzzle that depends on found items.
- [rule] A room of scattered oddities can be a puzzle with several plausible readings, where only one covers every item and reveals a lore clue.
- [rule] Let players restart a mystery from its first event after a failed theory, keeping earlier clues in place so the rerun is informative. When: Designing a puzzle or investigation quest with several possible theories. Why: Retained clues let players rebuild the chain without losing progress.
- [rule] Let players re-enact a scene with NPCs in set positions to test whether sound or line of sight reaches a given spot. When: designing a physical reconstruction puzzle. Why: the test uses the game's own geometry and audio.
- [rule] Use object counts or shelf orderings as puzzle inputs that match a place name or code.
- [rule] Let players compare partial clues, such as a torn fragment or faint marking, and grade how strong each match is. When: designing a clue-matching puzzle. Why: Graded matches reward judgment about uncertainty.
- [rule] Switch the language of a puzzle so the player must reconsider earlier assumptions. When: Designing a multi-stage text puzzle. Why: A language change forces re-evaluation of prior readings.
- [rule] Make sure the true hiding location is among the candidates the player can check, or deduction by elimination collapses. When: Designing a deduction puzzle around hidden items. Why: Elimination can only reach the answer if it is among the candidates.
- [rule] Make paired puzzle objects differ in one visible detail, so the player notices it and infers that one was swapped or moved. When: designing matched puzzle props. Why: A single difference is discoverable without being obvious.
- [rule] Make a constraint physical and testable, such as a binding that can only be freed with a specific tool, so the player can reason about what is possible. When: Designing a restraint or obstacle with a clear limit. Why: A concrete constraint gives the player a definite rule to test against.
- [rule] Design scenes where body position or items on a table encode what happened, so players must notice what seems off. When: Authoring a crime scene that players inspect. Why: Visible posture and objects give players clues to reconstruct events.
- [rule] Make the decisive test a repeatable demonstration, so the player can watch the mechanism work before the final confrontation.
- [heuristic] Searching only for unusual objects misses the ordinary item that blends in; a stalled player may need a mundane solution in plain sight, like a common object used unexpectedly. When: when the player is stuck on an overcomplicated theory.
- [heuristic] Build puzzle rooms from readable traces, such as forced-lock scratches, an outside bar, mismatched cigar ends, ash, scuffs or footprints, each pointing to a specific actor.
- [heuristic] Hold back a puzzle answer behind a visible trigger or staged beat, releasing partial clues first; NPC hooks like 'wait and see' get resolved in a later scene. When: Designing a puzzle or final reveal that resolves in a later scene. Why: Visible triggers let players know the answer exists and when it comes; staged release keeps trust and engagement.
- [heuristic] Model a tampered or missing item as a chain of handoffs and open-access time window, then list everyone who could have acted within it. When: When building a poisoning or sabotage puzzle, or investigating a vanished clue. Why: Players reconstruct who could have acted and when; the window narrows the set of possible takers.
- [heuristic] Map a riddle's terms, such as stair counts, timed cycles, paces or landmarks, onto visible or journal-recorded level data so players filter candidate locations against each term. When: when designing a riddle that points to a location. Why: Each term can be tested against the level, and anchors must exist and be verifiable.
- [heuristic] Treat a crime arising from human acts as a problem with a solution, reasoned from knowledge of human motive and action, rather than as an unsolvable mystery. When: Framing a case that seems impenetrable. Why: Human actions follow motives that can be reasoned about and checked.
- [heuristic] Design an official storyline that explains most of the scene but leaves one object, such as a key in an unexpected place, that the attentive player can use to challenge it.
- [heuristic] A puzzle room can show a few items that jointly point to a guilty NPC, with one overlooked item that reverses the conclusion.
- [heuristic] Do not make every gathering a clue dump, since crowds mostly obscure evidence.
- [heuristic] Keep an investigation hidden, because an open appeal for information alerts suspects who may then flee. When: when a suspect could escape if warned. Why: publicity signals pursuit to the people being hunted.
- [heuristic] A mystery should be hard enough to resist guessing but never impossible, so players feel challenged yet not cheated at the reveal.
- [heuristic] Trace a witness's hidden knowledge to its source, such as a written scrap in a drawer, rather than relying on the spoken answer alone. When: when a witness seems to know more than they say.
- [heuristic] Hide a long setup in early scenes, such as a staged event or a gift, that looks random until the player links it to a later event. When: when designing a fair-play sequence of events. Why: the player can recall and connect setups once the payoff occurs.
- [heuristic] Prefer questions about hidden facts over visible ones, because a hidden fact is harder to obtain elsewhere and so tests the theory better. When: when choosing which question to ask.
- [heuristic] Be wary when a mystery's evidence is hidden or invisible and an authority accepts it without checks.
- [heuristic] Use transport schedules, walking times and opening hours as puzzle inputs, have each action consume game time, and hide the decisive act in the gaps between timed points. When: When designing a timeline-based mystery in a game with a clock. Why: Players must order timed events from documents, and gaps conceal the key act.
- [heuristic] Give a supply or key ingredient a plausible but thin in-world reason, and let a later location reveal its true use. When: designing a dungeon's supplies or key ingredients.
- [heuristic] Design a choice where both options look identical to the player, and let the outcome depend on a fact the player can learn later. When: Authoring a decision whose right answer should be discoverable. Why: Hidden stakes reward investigation without making the choice arbitrary.
- [heuristic] A document split into fragments across several rooms can make a puzzle that tests whether the player matches each fragment to the correct source.
- [heuristic] Introduce a puzzle object whose use is not yet explained, but make sure the facts needed to guess its purpose are already available to the player. Why: Lets the player earn the solution by reasoning.
- [heuristic] Make a puzzle's key a name the player has heard in dialogue, and let players find the cipher's structure by trial before the key is needed. When: when designing a cipher or code puzzle. Why: a key tied to story is recognisable, and trial builds understanding first.
- [heuristic] Reward a partial solution with a readable fragment, so the player gains confidence and motivation before the full solution. When: when a puzzle has several stages of solution. Why: early partial success encourages further effort.
- [heuristic] Build a provisional model of a hidden plan from scattered partial clues, and test it later rather than treating isolated fragments as firm conclusions. When: when only a few clues are clear and the meaning is unknown.
- [heuristic] Require exact event order only when a general window would not settle the outcome. When: Deciding how precise an investigation must be. Why: Unneeded precision wastes effort and invites contested testimony.
- [heuristic] Let a mundane system, such as a board game or a shop haggle, carry a state change that matters later.
