- [heuristic] Players enjoy solutions reached through logic more than solutions reached by instinct, so puzzles should reward reasoning from the presented facts. When: Designing a mystery reveal. Why: Reasoning from evidence gives more satisfying payoff than guessing.
- [heuristic] Set an explicit proof threshold in deduction puzzles, so a vague rumor plus any nearby object does not count as confirmation. When: Designing deduction puzzles. Why: Without a threshold, any object can be read as confirmation.
- [heuristic] A puzzle built on few simple rules and visible evidence tests analysis, while one with many interacting moves tests attention; choose the mix that matches the intended skill. When: designing puzzles.
- [heuristic] Give players a partial view, such as a window, grate or wall gap, rather than full access, so the puzzle is solved by observation. When: designing a locked-room puzzle.
- [heuristic] Plant origin mismatches on items as puzzle hooks, such as a label that contradicts where the item was made.
- [heuristic] Repeated sequences in clue fragments, such as a list read in order with a note after each item, can reveal the structure of a lock or code.
- [heuristic] An NPC who should have moved on but remains in place can serve as a clue, and a player who notices this has found the lead. When: an NPC's behaviour is inconsistent with its scripted timing. Why: A stalled NPC signals that something has interrupted the expected routine.
- [heuristic] A puzzle can require three linked checks answered in order, with each answer unlocking the next. When: Designing a puzzle built around a single clue. Why: Ordered unlocking keeps the player moving through the intended chain.
- [heuristic] Avoid plausible but weak deceptions when facing a sharp adversary, since a flimsy lie fails under close scrutiny.
- [heuristic] A timing puzzle can have the player decode times in a victim's notebook, with a fixed timer setting the window for the event. When: Designing a deduction puzzle around written times and a timed effect. Why: Fixed timers give players a bounded question to solve.
- [heuristic] An investigation puzzle can require the player to match three facts: who was present, who held the item, and who gained from its use. When: designing a deduction puzzle.
- [heuristic] A contamination puzzle can require the player to trace the victim's unique habit rather than the obvious source. When: designing a poisoning or exposure mystery.
- [heuristic] An impossible entry is fair when its mechanism is physical and can be inspected on the object itself.
- [procedure] 1) Run a continuous line through the hidden route. 2) Agree a tug pattern to confirm contact. 3) Agree a second pattern to signal that the exchange should continue. When: Two parties must communicate through a concealed passage. Why: A pre-agreed code avoids sound that could be overheard.
- [procedure] 1) Choose a target and list what their world exposes: associates, residence, routine, transport, servants. 2) Plan access, delivery and safety. 3) Write the full case. 4) Write how it would be detected from the investigator's side. When: building a puzzle or crime case. Why: Covers both the offender's route and the discovery route.
- [procedure] 1) Gather the day's personal notices from every relevant paper. 2) Read them in order for messages that fit the situation. 3) Check each candidate's details against known places and people. 4) Reply in a column the contact reads. When: A hidden suspect is known to read a public column. Why: A reply placed where the contact reads it keeps the channel under the investigator's control.
- [procedure] 1) Create a town where one respected figure's accusation spreads through letters and sermons. 2) Make doubters risk punishment. 3) Require the player to identify which sources are independent. When: Designing a community where belief spreads through authority. Why: The player must separate real corroboration from repeated authority.
- [procedure] 1) Present a will and a later deed that disagree. 2) Have the player check witnesses, dates and later transfers. 3) Let the player decide which document is valid. When: Designing a document-based puzzle. Why: Players must weigh formal and temporal evidence, not simply trust one paper.
- [procedure] 1) Split the account into separate claims. 2) Check each claim against independent sources. 3) Accept or reject each part on its own, not the account as a whole. When: when evaluating a multi-part statement. Why: Separate judgement prevents a confirmed part from carrying an unverified part.
- [procedure] Design an incident as a set of candidate explanations, each requiring specific evidence, so the player can mark each off as evidence is found. When: When designing a mystery or puzzle with several possible explanations. Why: Explicit candidates give the player a structured way to track progress.
- [procedure] 1) Locate residual marks such as impressions, dents, holes and dust outlines. 2) Infer each displaced object's original position from its marks. 3) Restore objects to those positions. 4) Trace lines of fire or movement through the restored layout. When: Reconstructing an arrangement that was disturbed. Why: Marks left behind are more reliable than the moved objects themselves.
- [metric] Rate each knowledge area on a six-level scale: nil, feeble, variable, practical, profound, immense. When: when building a character's skill or knowledge profile.
- [tradeoff] Hiding middle links of a deduction heightens the reveal but can look like magic; show the clue so the audience can verify reasoning. When: when a solution is revealed to the audience. Why: Unverifiable reasoning makes the solver seem to have guessed.
- [tradeoff] Ordeal results can look random if hidden variables drive them, but hidden or arbitrary outcomes feel unfair unless rules are visible and testable. When: designing trials, traps or tests of skill. Why: Hidden variables, such as a weighted object or a known tide, can stay fair; players can only learn and trust a challenge whose outcome follows from visible rules.
- [tradeoff] A marvellous effect should still have a simple, discoverable mechanism, while a complicated puzzle should be built from many traceable steps. When: when designing a puzzle with a striking surface effect. Why: Surface wonder pleases players, but discoverable method keeps it fair.
- [tradeoff] Puzzle-focused mysteries need full clue visibility, while mood- or character-focused stories can loosen fair-play rigour. When: choosing a story's primary aim.
- [tradeoff] Keeping a secret preserves leverage over the victim, while revealing or using it spends that leverage permanently. When: A quest item or secret can be held or exposed. Why: A secret's value depends on staying hidden.
- [tradeoff] A cipher that is too hard stalls players, while one that is too easy gives the solution away, so difficulty should sit between these extremes.
- [tradeoff] A decoy that looks like the prize must be explained afterwards, or players may feel cheated. When: A timed reveal delivers a substitute item. Why: Unexplained substitution breaks trust in the reveal.
- [pitfall] Plasters, bandages and jewellery can be changed or added; confirm a trace before relying on it rather than assuming it is genuine.
- [pitfall] Hiding a character's face or identity for too long makes the delay feel like a trick rather than fair withholding. When: Withholding a reveal to build suspense. Why: Excessive delay breaks trust and makes the withholding look arbitrary.
- [pitfall] Puzzle reasoning can rest on a hidden premise, such as the identity of a body, and the answers may finally overturn it. When: Solving a mystery where questions seem settled. Why: Unexamined assumptions can block the correct explanation.
- [pitfall] Some repeated actions are simply required procedure, so repetition alone does not prove hidden intent.
- [pitfall] Assuming a surviving fragment tells the whole story leads to wrong conclusions; combine it with other records before reconstructing its meaning.
- [pitfall] Correct guesses earn more praise than sound inferences with modest results, which encourages overconfidence.
- [pitfall] A fixed goal can hide assumptions, so check that the endpoint is actually reachable before building the plan on it. When: when an endpoint is set early. Why: an optimistic target may rest on unexamined premises.
- [pitfall] Overlooking how a weapon or tool works can rule out a suspect entirely, so the mechanism itself should be treated as evidence. When: Inferring the instrument behind a trace. Why: The mechanism constrains who could have used it and how.
- [pitfall] A lure fails if others share the code, because the target may recognise the message as a forgery.

## reveal pacing
- [mechanism] Withholding a fact, such as stopping mid-sentence, refusing a topic, or promising a reveal, builds suspense; the open question makes the answer feel important and holds attention until it arrives. When: A key reveal depends on information kept from the audience, the next fact will change the investigation, a long testimony requires a held question, or a character is near a key disclosure. Why: An open question creates a need to resolve it; the unanswered question keeps attention focused until the answer arrives.
- [mechanism] Delaying the explanation after a reveal, or of how a conclusion was reached, opens a gap that builds tension; the audience anticipates a close but unstated answer as clues accumulate. When: the audience already has most facts and only needs to see how they fit, or the clues are in place for the audience to check later. Why: The open question holds attention until resolved; tension rises when the audience senses a hidden answer close but not yet stated.
- [mechanism] A witness who refuses or evades a question, even under oath, reveals through avoidance; the gap may point to a protected third party, so ask what the account avoids. When: a witness declines, evades or refuses a line of questioning, including a complete-sounding story with one part refused. Why: Avoidance is a form of communication; concealment signals the hidden history bears on the case, and withholding is often protective.
- [mechanism] Refusing to explain a surprising situation at the moment it appears lets the audience feel the question before any answer, which builds tension.
- [mechanism] Withholding the end of a sentence creates suspense and prompts the listener to draw their own conclusion. When: when a character is about to disclose a sensitive point. Why: an unfinished statement invites the listener to complete it.
- [mechanism] Keeping key facts back from both reader and characters keeps their uncertainty shared, and the withholding itself signals danger. When: The story needs tension before the answer. Why: Shared uncertainty and implied scale raise suspicion without revealing the answer.
- [mechanism] If the player chooses the order of revealed evidence, each reveal can change the NPC's available responses and options.
- [mechanism] Withholding some names until early statements shape opinion lets the first accounts frame everything that follows. When: an investigation controls the release of names. Why: early accounts set the frame for later testimony.
- [mechanism] A bestiary or journal entry unlocked by matching a clue phrase can list symptoms as a checklist, letting players verify an identity before acting on it. When: Designing lore that supports identification of an unfamiliar threat. Why: A checklist of symptoms converts recognition into verifiable evidence.
- [mechanism] An outside interruption, such as an arrival or a warning of limited time, forces a decision and raises urgency at the moment of confrontation. When: when the player is about to confront a suspect or reach a key revelation. Why: A sudden external event compresses time and removes room for hesitation.
- [behavior] A sentence that breaks off signals that a fact is being kept back, not that nothing is there, and a listener absorbed in their own feelings often fails to pursue the gap. When: A character halts mid-sentence while speaking about something important. Why: Distraction lets the listener miss the omission while the reader notices it.
- [behavior] A character holding back knowledge often shows it through a shift in tone, a sudden hesitation, or a retort that sounds like a boast. When: A character knows more than they have said so far.
- [rule] Gate an NPC's disclosure or an item's hidden content behind a condition, such as finding a document or recognizing a claim, so players must obtain it first. When: designing items or NPCs that gate information behind progress. (x2)
- [rule] Give each withheld answer, such as a name, pending offer or key evidence, a firm point where it surfaces; overlong stalling frustrates and exhausts the audience and wears down trust. When: when extending suspense, or when a reveal is held back many steps without new information. Why: Players want resolution once they have enough clues and may reject vague build-up; long withholding without payoff makes readers lose patience, so balance the wait against the payoff. (x2)
- [rule] Every withheld fact or reveal must be paid off later and its clues placed in the record beforehand; overlong withholding without fair hints feels like cheating. When: when information is deliberately kept from the audience or delayed for suspense. Why: Unpaid reveals break trust; a reader can only judge a reveal fairly if the needed facts were available.
- [rule] Hold the answer back until the evidence is complete, so an accusation cannot be disputed and the player's own deduction is rewarded. When: timing a confrontation in a mystery. Why: Incomplete proof lets the accused dispute the claim; a reveal given early leaves the player nothing to deduce.
- [rule] Deliver the full motive as a retrospective account after the immediate question is answered, tying each revealed fact back to clues the player already holds. When: Closing a mystery whose motive depends on earlier backstory. Why: Re-reading earlier evidence rewards the player and avoids a stalled recap.
- [rule] Flag unusual details at intake, such as terms far beyond the normal rate, as signals that call for an explanation. Why: Anomalies often hide a strong underlying reason.
- [rule] Let an early character warn the player about a named faction leader, creating a pending objective the player can act on later.
- [rule] Place backstory items, such as a letter or a scandal record, early so they can later serve as motive or access clues. When: designing background material for a mystery. Why: items introduced early can be reused as clues without new exposition.
- [rule] Show a tool and its stated limits before the situation needs it, so its later use feels prepared rather than conveniently invented. When: when a later scene depends on the tool. Why: a stated limit makes later use believable and fair.
- [rule] Stage an unmasking before several witnesses so that its outcome can be verified. When: Designing a reveal scene.
- [rule] Use a withheld fact to build tension only when the listener has a reason to suspect it.
- [rule] Script a reveal in steps, breaking it at the most tense point and pausing briefly before the key item appears, so the audience wants the rest. Why: unresolved tension holds attention.
- [rule] Script some NPC reactions as instinctive and others as deliberate, so players can distinguish panic from premeditation.
- [rule] Withhold a final document or revelation until the player meets a condition, and do not let it resolve automatically. When: when designing a late reveal. Why: A withheld final point keeps the reveal incomplete until the condition is met.
- [rule] Keep the identity of an actor and the role of intermediaries unresolved until the player is ready for them, and mark each as an open question. When: Narrative threads share a middle figure whose role is not yet clear. Why: Revealing those answers early removes the investigation's remaining tension.
- [rule] Pace reveals so that a new development follows each moment the player gains sympathy for a suspect, keeping tension high.
- [rule] Close a mystery quickly with a confrontation where the culprit's admission ends the quest, instead of staging a trial. When: Designing the final act of a mystery quest. Why: An admission gives a fast close without extra scenes.
- [rule] Use lighting, seat placement and a late entrance in the accusation scene to stage the reveal of the culprit.
- [rule] Place a blocked route or a stranger needing help just before a key NPC, so the player anticipates the coming interaction. When: Pacing toward an important character.
- [rule] Leave one reading unresolved after the tests, so the question can carry into a later quest step.
- [rule] Gate cause-of-death or killer-identification steps so they unlock only after the victim is confirmed. When: Designing investigation case files in games. Why: Prevents players from reasoning about motive or method before a basic fact is anchored.
- [heuristic] Withhold the decisive fact or document until the scene where it matters, delivering it at peak tension, when the audience is most surprised and has context to feel its weight. When: staging a final revelation, or when a case or scene needs a turn. Why: Timing maximizes surprise and makes the answer memorable; delivery at peak tension increases impact. (x2)
- [heuristic] Plant a chain of small cues, such as hints in early dialogue, that pay off later and teach players to notice details that matter. When: designing a mystery, dialogue payoff, or reveal that will come later in the story. Why: A planted hint lets the player connect the reveal to earlier evidence; noticing trains attention for the reveal.
- [heuristic] Let each step of the solution arrive after a pause, tied to clues already in plain view, so the answer feels earned rather than imposed. When: a reveal must feel fair or reward attentive players, especially a multi-step solution. Why: Clues already seen let the audience match the answer themselves.
- [heuristic] Spread a reveal across several scenes or explanations; answering every oddity or question at once overwhelms the audience, blurs the logic and deflates suspense. When: when resolving a mystery or when a suspect's account explains an entire plot at the end. Why: Dense exposition overwhelms the audience and blurs the logic.
- [heuristic] Read responses before escalating; do not press every contradiction at once, moving on after a few, since escalation raises the witness's guard and may warn them. When: several contradictions have been found in one interview. Why: Escalating pressure on every point makes the witness more defensive and less revealing.
- [heuristic] Withhold full answers near a key revelation or when a character is about to disclose something important, so the audience waits with the protagonist while tension lingers and stakes grow. When: near a key answer, or a character is near a key disclosure. Why: Delay keeps the audience waiting and attentive.
- [heuristic] Show partial, precise details such as a partly seen hand or reflected light so the audience infers, then give a complete view as payoff when lighting aligns. When: building a tense scene. Why: Partial visuals direct attention and invite inference.
- [heuristic] Hold a fact back and release it later as a favour to create a natural pacing beat in an investigation quest.
- [heuristic] Introducing a figure through rumour or background before meeting them gives the later encounter weight and makes it feel earned.
- [heuristic] Reveal facts in the order they were experienced rather than by importance, so the audience must reconstruct their significance later. When: when the audience should reread for clues. Why: Delayed understanding rewards attention to earlier details.
- [heuristic] Uneven intensity within a mark, such as deeper marks at crossings, reveals the shape of the object that made it. Why: the object's structure is pressed into the mark.
- [heuristic] A secret message that is plainly simple, where a real one would be coded or technical, may be staged to be read at once. When: Evaluating a note or message meant to be secret.
- [heuristic] Avoid heavy withholding in short scenes, because a delay without a quick payoff becomes frustrating. When: short scenes. Why: little time remains for the payoff to justify the wait.
- [heuristic] Reveal a central fact only after minor witnesses have built suspicion, because revealing it early leaves the suspense unbuilt. When: the disclosure should change the audience's view of everyone present.
- [heuristic] End a section on a character's reaction to a new fact while withholding its content, shifting the question from what happened to what it means. Why: the reaction creates suspense before the detail is given.
- [heuristic] Sort known facts into groups that fit together and those that do not; a misfit element may be padding that hides the true purpose. When: Public activities and private interests seem inconsistent.
- [heuristic] Establish a suspect's debts, dependencies and history early, giving motive without yet pointing at a specific crime.
- [heuristic] Alternate heavy argument, sudden revelation and light relief to vary pacing, and place a jarring event where it undercuts the mood.
- [heuristic] Open a mystery with an early paradox and several plausible explanations, so players test theories rather than follow a single scripted path. When: designing the opening of an investigation. Why: competing explanations invite active reasoning.
- [heuristic] Pace a long explanation through questions from another character, since a plain list of clues feels like an information dump.
- [heuristic] Make a withheld entry the reward for a later investigation, revealing what the excerpted version hid. Why: Withheld content gives investigation a payoff.
- [heuristic] How quickly an institution is set up and starts operating is a detail worth weighing when judging whether its proceedings were fair. When: judging the fairness of a newly created body.
- [heuristic] Create quiet phases where players wait for a trigger, then cut to a scene showing the antagonist moving toward the player's goal. When: Designing pacing for a hunt or pursuit. Why: The cut shows the threat advancing while the player is still unaware of it.
- [heuristic] Let a discovery stay secret until the player chooses to reveal it, so the player decides when an NPC learns of it. When: Designing hidden-evidence or stakeout mechanics. Why: Player control over disclosure makes the timing of exposure a meaningful choice.
- [heuristic] Expect leaks and pre-label them as innocent defensive measures, so observers who learn of them interpret them wrongly. When: when a hidden activity is likely to be seen. Why: Observers accept an explanation given in advance more readily than one they discover later.
- [heuristic] Set a reveal window a few months after the apparent death, when the opponent has already committed to its plan. When: Planning when a survivor reappears. Why: Commitment makes the opponent's plan hard to change after the reveal.
- [heuristic] Hold a reveal with camera or cutscene pacing until the player has taken a step forward.
- [heuristic] Ending a level section at a door that opens onto an unknown area invites the player forward. When: closing a level section. Why: An unseen space raises a question the player wants answered.
- [heuristic] Withholding part of a character's reasoning while promising a later explanation keeps the audience uncertain about the next step. When: a mystery needs tension before the answer is revealed. Why: uncertainty about what comes next holds attention.
- [heuristic] Stage reveals as reproducible measurements or steps the player can follow and check, such as a measured line from a visible landmark, not a bare assertion.
- [heuristic] Answer the first riddle early to correct a false belief, and leave the second open, so the player keeps a live objective.
- [heuristic] Give an ambiguous event both a rational and a supernatural explanation, each backed by records or items the player can use to test it. When: when designing a mystery the player must judge. Why: The player can weigh evidence instead of accepting one authored answer.
- [heuristic] Place a trap or threat near the midpoint so the investigator's own safety raises the stakes before the answer is close. When: structuring mystery pacing.
- [procedure] 1) Deliver the full answer with method, motive and timeline. 2) Hold back one final piece of information. 3) Reveal it, forcing the audience to reinterpret earlier evidence.
- [procedure] 1) Introduce all characters present in the enclosed space before the incident. 2) Give a clear roster. 3) Let the player track who could have been where. When: Setting up a whodunit or locked-space mystery. Why: A fixed list of present characters lets the player reason about access and alibis.
- [procedure] 1) Show the marvellous surface first. 2) Withhold the plain method. 3) Reveal the mechanism behind the effect after players have explored its implications. When: when pacing a reveal in a mystery sequence. Why: Delayed explanation keeps the surface effect intriguing before it is explained.
- [procedure] 1) Establish the motive question. 2) Present the physical clues. 3) Build the proof chain. 4) Name the suspect last. When: Designing a finale where the audience must be able to follow the chain. Why: Building proof before the name makes the reveal the payoff rather than an unsupported claim.
- [procedure] 1) Offer the player a human or natural explanation first. 2) Let them test it against access and timing. 3) Reveal the supernatural cause only when the mundane options fail. When: Designing a mystery whose reveal is supernatural. Why: The reveal feels earned when the player can confirm that no mundane explanation remains.
- [tradeoff] Withholding a solution builds suspense until anticipation turns to boredom; long delays also lose the investigation's thread and let the culprit escape or destroy evidence. When: Deciding how long to hold back a solution or reveal. Why: Each extra beat of delay raises the cost of waiting and the risk of loss.
- [tradeoff] Revealing evidence early lets the opponent investigate, search for the item, and build a counter-account before testimony is tested; a late reveal catches them unprepared but can look like concealment. When: Deciding when to disclose decisive evidence or a witness account. Why: Timing trades the opponent's preparation time against the risk of seeming hidden; they can act while the account is still unmatched.
- [tradeoff] Giving an instruction before its reason builds suspense, but frustrates the recipient if the reasoning is never paid off. Why: The recipient must act before understanding.
- [tradeoff] Withholding the full explanation until the end preserves suspense, but makes the solution feel unfair if the clues were not presented as the reasoning develops. When: when planning how a mystery's solution is revealed. Why: Audiences need premises stated before they can follow the conclusion.
- [tradeoff] Withholding the suspect's name gives the reveal payoff but risks losing the audience if the delay runs past the point where they can still follow the argument. When: Choosing how long to hold back an identity in a mystery. Why: Suspense depends on the audience having enough clues to track the chain.
- [tradeoff] Offering choices at a reveal, such as accepting a bribe, pressing for confession or exposing the truth, branches the ending but must be recorded in world state. When: Designing a scripted confrontation with a trusted quest-giver. Why: Branching outcomes require the game to remember the player's decision.
- [tradeoff] Offering a choice between revealing a truth and preserving an official story gives players a moral decision with consequences for NPCs who hold the public narrative. When: Designing a moral decision about a public secret.
- [pitfall] Over-explaining after a reveal, through overt warnings, repeated phrasing or spelling out the answer, drains surprise and gives the trick away; explain only what the audience cannot infer.
- [pitfall] A reveal that lists too many details or narrates at length reads as a lecture and drains tension; keep each step short and tied to established clues. When: writing a final explanation of a complex case, or narrating a reveal. Why: Excess detail dulls the payoff and breaks the sense of a compact deduction.
- [pitfall] Revealing evidence or your own theory early warns the culprit or allies, letting them reshape their account or act first, removing the chance of a slip. When: the witness may pass information to the suspect. Why: Frank disclosure gives the other side time to respond; the culprit has no chance to slip.
- [pitfall] Stating a deduction or highly specific conditions in more exact detail than needed reveals its source and alerts a suspicious reader or suspect. When: presenting a prediction to others. Why: Precision beyond what is needed exposes the deduction's source.
- [pitfall] Revealing a schemer's plan, a puzzle's purpose or a hidden motive too early removes what the audience would discover or reinterpret; hold it until the context is available. When: staging a villain's preparations or a puzzle's purpose. Why: Full disclosure leaves nothing to uncover; a hidden motive held back lets players reinterpret earlier statements.
- [pitfall] A hint or hidden identity shown too plainly removes the surprise of the eventual reveal and can collapse its payoff. When: planting clues for a later reveal.
- [pitfall] Overusing interruptions, or repeating the same cut, wears out the revelation's effect, makes the plot feel contrived and erodes trust. When: when the same narrative cut is used across many scenes. Why: Repetition makes the device predictable.
- [pitfall] Overusing withholding makes a reveal feel like a cheat; a slow approach or long delay without new stakes or payoffs lets tension drop, so each stage must add something new.
- [pitfall] Revealing the full significance of evidence before the suspect commits to an account weakens the test of that account. When: during a witness interview.
- [pitfall] Too many officers approaching at once can provoke a suspect to fire first, which makes the sting dangerous. When: When closing in on an armed target. Why: A cornered suspect reacts defensively when surrounded by visible threats.
- [pitfall] Presenting a combined case as overwhelming can pressure the listener into agreement before they have checked each link. When: a case is presented to a decision maker. Why: the framing substitutes for independent checking.
- [pitfall] Entering too often can disturb a fragile person being cared for, so surveillance must be balanced against that person's welfare. When: The watched party is a patient or other vulnerable person.
- [pitfall] Setting description that runs too long delays the plot.
- [pitfall] A confession that tells everything removes suspense; withholding outcomes, such as a third party's fate, keeps tension alive until a later point. When: when a confession is delivered in the story. Why: Revealed answers leave nothing for the audience to anticipate.
- [pitfall] A code explained in full detail by the people who use it can be decoded by an investigator who finds that description.
- [pitfall] Treating weak leads as real clues to make an investigation look busy.
- [pitfall] Hiding a clue too well makes the reveal feel arbitrary, while revealing the answer too early removes suspense and the chance to reason it through. When: Pacing the disclosure of a mystery solution. Why: The reveal must be supported by earlier clues the audience could have assembled, yet withheld long enough to create suspense.
- [pitfall] Explaining a trick at length can make the writer look as if they are hiding something; keep the debunking in character and in proportion.
- [pitfall] Explaining every reinterpretation at once removes the audience's chance to deduce; hold it back until the earlier clue has been noticed. When: designing a reveal that reframes earlier clues. Why: deduction is what makes the reveal satisfying.
- [pitfall] Too much recap after resolution flattens the ending, so keep the summary short.
- [pitfall] A participant who names the culprit early breaks the planned structure, so the reveal should anticipate a player who guesses ahead. When: Designing a mystery reveal for players who may reach the answer early. Why: Early guesses short-circuit the sequence of clues the designer built to lead to the name.
- [pitfall] An overexposed aim can be read as clumsiness rather than deceit, so the audience may underestimate it.
- [pitfall] Too many promised reveals turn into a tease and frustrate the audience. Every promised payoff must arrive. When: Planting multiple open questions or deferred answers. Why: Unfulfilled promises erode trust in the narrative.
- [pitfall] Withholding a reveal too long lets the audience forget the earlier evidence needed to appreciate it. Why: Clues lose their force when distant from the reveal.
- [pitfall] Showing a clue before explaining its history keeps a mystery open, but if the explanation never arrives the reader cannot learn how the object was moved. When: When a clue is revealed early and its explanation is deferred. Why: Without a later explanation, the reader lacks the information needed to resolve the clue.
- [pitfall] Using interruptions too often turns them into a predictable device that stops creating suspense. When: when a scene repeatedly relies on an arrival or timer to force action. Why: Predictable interruptions lose their surprise and urgency.
- [fact] Layered safeguards can make a planner nearly impossible to convict, even when the planner's influence is clear.
- [fact] Institutional records, such as a patient or staff record, can confirm a person's presence at a place and time. When: when verifying a claimed presence. Why: Records made at the time are independent of the suspect.

## game clocks and timers
- [definition] A timed-release locked room delays a mechanical lock action with a slow physical process, so the room appears sealed by ordinary means after the actor has left.
- [mechanism] Objects changing at a known rate, such as bitten fruit browning, growing plants or weathering stains, encode elapsed time, letting players date an event by inspecting them. When: A scene contains organic or changing objects whose rate of change is known. Why: The current state of a decaying object encodes elapsed time. (x2)
- [mechanism] A character's deadline, such as a departure date, long wait or timed window before a forced action, raises pressure and shapes choices, since every hour feels scarce. Why: Every remaining hour is felt as scarce.
- [mechanism] A timer or nearby deadline makes waiting costlier, forcing a choice between gathering more evidence and acting first, since each step risks losing the outcome. When: A time-limited event is part of the scenario. Why: Once time is finite, each extra investigation step costs the opportunity to act before the event.
- [mechanism] Fixed-date money obligations combined with refused requests for help raise a character's pressure toward desperate acts. When: when a character faces a fixed deadline with no means to pay.
- [mechanism] When an opponent delays a deadline by holding the protagonist in place, the pressure moves to the next scheduled block of time rather than disappearing. When: An antagonist threatens to keep the protagonist from leaving.
- [mechanism] A hidden clock setting revealed by a specialist NPC can change which testimony is trustworthy and so reverse the player's reading of the case.
- [mechanism] A stated deadline with a named consequence pressures the recipient to decide and makes each lookup a choice. When: an NPC or player must act within a limit. Why: a set deadline with a stated outcome forces a decision.
- [mechanism] Fixed transport timetables limit escape options, since no departure before a set hour rules out that route and narrows the search area. When: Determining how a suspect could have left a location. Why: Schedules remove routes that were not running at the time.
- [mechanism] A deadline gains suspense through repeated clock checks and interrupted progress, with the outcome in doubt until the final moment. When: a fixed time limit applies.
- [mechanism] A fixed deadline, reinforced by shortening follow-up messages, raises pressure as the event approaches, narrowing the time the target has to act. When: building mounting tension toward a known event. Why: Each shortened timeline increases urgency and reduces the target's options.
- [mechanism] A legal rule change with a deadline can make an earlier death profitable for whoever gains under the old rule. When: when a death falls before a rule change takes effect. Why: The change would otherwise remove the gain.
- [mechanism] A rule with a deadline can change who benefits from an event, turning a sequence of events into a motive. When: when a rule sets a cut-off date. Why: the stake an event carries depends on the deadline.
- [mechanism] A deadline such as a wedding or a witness's departure can force an investigator to act before the evidence is complete. When: when a key witness's availability is time-limited. Why: The witness's availability sets the investigator's timetable.
- [mechanism] A device that fills or drains at a fixed rate measures elapsed time, so the amount moved shows how long it has run, without anyone watching. When: a character must act at intervals while unobserved. Why: constant flow converts volume into duration.
- [mechanism] A timer's overflow can be the trigger itself, such as spilling onto a sleeper to wake them, so one element both measures time and acts. Why: the overflow is the timing signal.
- [mechanism] Escalation can be built so that pushing an NPC too far triggers a harsher response.
- [mechanism] A fixed deadline sets the pace of a negotiation, and the waiting period before each reply builds suspense.
- [mechanism] A timed-event system can sum task and travel durations, compare them with logged timestamps, and flag inconsistencies that players can investigate.
- [mechanism] Timed events such as rain can serve as a clock that bounds when physical marks were made. When: Designing a world where marks must be dated. Why: Weather timing gives an in-world time bound.
- [mechanism] A sound that draws a target back to the room at the wrong moment breaks the timeline and forces a confrontation. When: a timed plan depends on a target staying away. Why: unplanned events can end a window early.
- [mechanism] When a key action is delayed, the trigger of a time-gated event shifts, forcing the player to re-plan. When: when events are gated by in-game time.
- [mechanism] An interruption such as an approaching sound sets a deadline without triggering failure, pressuring the player to finish searching. When: a search is underway. Why: a cue signals time running out while leaving the player's progress intact.
- [mechanism] Several unrelated events overlapping in time confuse the record, and an innocent event can look connected to a crime. When: Multiple events cluster at one time and the record is confusing. Why: Shared timing creates false links between independent sequences.
- [mechanism] Escalating interruptions, each resolved by a plausible excuse, build exposure tension while the search keeps resuming. When: a covert action is staged in a shared space. Why: repeated near-misses raise pressure without ending the action.
- [mechanism] A timeline puzzle can have the player combine a body-state estimate with a heard sound to fix the crime window. When: In investigation games with time-based deduction.
- [mechanism] A time-limited lease or deadline keeps a threat visible even when no active fight is taking place. Why: The clock sustains the threat between combat moments.
- [mechanism] Let one NPC change the world state without recording it, so an observant player who inspects the area discovers the change. When: Designing hidden changes that reward careful inspection.
- [mechanism] A countdown that marks itself in new locations each day can serve as a timer for a dungeon run, with each marking giving a clue about where the enemy has been. When: Designing a time-limited dungeon run. Why: Location-based markings link the timer to the enemy's movement.
- [mechanism] A device that acts silently without staff can carry an act across time and place, so its effect occurs while the actor is elsewhere. Why: The mechanism performs the act after the actor has left.
- [behavior] Under a deadline, suspects show urgency and reveal their intentions, because they must act before time runs out. When: a timed event such as a departure or clock strike pressures the scene. Why: time limits force decisions that reveal hidden motives.
- [behavior] A group whose standing rises during a crisis has a stake in keeping the crisis's belief alive. When: a crisis raises a group's authority. Why: Status gained from the belief links the group's influence to it.
- [behavior] A reply that uses the full allowed period can be read by an opposing party as evasion, even when it is procedurally valid. When: a summoned party answers near the end of an allowed window. Why: Observers judge the timing of a response, not only its legality.
- [rule] Let players check a character's stated time or whereabouts against anchors such as clocks, hourglasses, a lamp being lit or extinguished, bells or town notices, to confirm or contradict it. When: when a character gives a time claim that matters to the case, or when designing a timed-testimony puzzle. Why: Checking against a visible anchor lets the player judge the claim without relying on the character.
- [rule] Give in-world clocks a known drift or false reading, so players who notice and calibrate it against a second source or known event can rebuild the timeline. When: when designing timing puzzles. Why: Calibration rewards careful checking.
- [rule] Give players the known burn or change rate of consumable or slow-changing objects, so they can date past events and check their own timing conclusions fairly. When: designing time-based deductions from environmental evidence, or clues that rely on gradual change. Why: Players can only reason well from rules they have been shown; a stated rate lets players verify their own conclusions.
- [rule] Use burning, decaying or drying objects, such as a candle, fuse, drying puddle or dying fire, as hidden timers that date or bound an event within a short window. When: Designing a world clue that dates an event. Why: Visible decay gives the player an approximate time without a clock.
- [rule] Show a visible countdown or forced-action timer, such as a water level, so players can plan around it rather than only feel pressure. When: when a timed hazard is shown on screen. Why: Readable state lets players plan ahead.
- [rule] Tie a clue to a world schedule, such as a time of day, so the player can check the detail against something observable in the game world. When: designing a puzzle that depends on timing. Why: a checkable detail makes the clue verifiable in play.
- [rule] Model timed processes in the world, such as cooling lava, wet paint or fading scent, so players can infer when an event occurred. When: Designing environmental clues based on time. Why: Known process durations let players reason backward from the present state.
- [rule] Honour a stated deadline or explicitly move it; otherwise the audience stops trusting the threat. When: A deadline has been set in the story.
- [rule] Set a deadline against a thorough search to create tension between finishing the investigation and leaving the area.
- [rule] In game timers, separate the dose time, the effect time and the death time, so players must reason about each one.
- [rule] Pace reveals with a predictable in-game delivery such as mail or a courier at a fixed time, and delay arrival with a legal or logistic obstacle. When: Designing story reveals in a game.
- [rule] Use a sun or torch angle as a timed trigger, so the player computes a position from a known object height. When: when designing light-based location puzzles. Why: The light condition gives the puzzle a timing window and a measurable shadow.
- [rule] Keep one active clock at a time rather than running several competing deadlines together. When: designing a mission or story with time pressure. Why: A single clock keeps attention on one stake.
- [rule] Every timed action on the relevant night must be explained, because an unaccounted action is a potential gap in the case.
- [rule] Read the direction of a one-way trail against the timing of events, since a missing return trail may mean a person waited rather than fled. When: A trail runs in one direction with no return marks. Why: Missing return marks do not show the path taken or the reason for it.
- [rule] Encode each delayed effect's expected delay range, so timing mismatches can surface as clues.
- [rule] Present time-of-death style estimates as ranges, so players must weigh them against other evidence rather than read a single exact time. When: a game gives forensic or timing information. Why: ranges require reasoning and cannot be taken as exact.
- [rule] Record each brief access window in seconds and let players replay the sequence to test whether a character could have acted in time.
- [rule] Design a puzzle whose answer depends on timing, such as a clock reading that reveals who was missing from a game. When: Creating a deduction puzzle. Why: Timing data can hide the key fact in plain sight.
- [rule] Give game events timestamps and a confidence value, so players must assemble the timeline from partial sources. When: When designing investigation systems in a game. Why: Partial, uneven evidence makes the player weigh reliability instead of reading a complete log.
- [rule] Build a visible response clock so the player knows roughly how long before an NPC receives or replies to news. When: When designing timed quests or deadlines. Why: A known window lets the player plan action before the next event.
- [rule] Open a level with a visible sign of change, such as a messenger or an alarmed guard. Place the explanation behind a later door or NPC to keep pressure on the player. Why: Delay keeps tension high while the player infers meaning.
- [rule] Give the player a fixed deadline with a travel time they must plan around, and let a timing gap surface as a discoverable clue. When: Designing timed objectives or scheduled events. Why: Constraints that can be checked make planning meaningful and reward attention.
- [rule] Run a level on a visible clock with timed events and closing doors, so the player can reconstruct who could reach which room.
- [rule] Map travel times between zones so that an NPC's claimed location can be checked by the player. When: When a level has NPCs who claim to be in specific places. Why: Known travel times let the player test those claims.
- [rule] Use delays such as a locked door or a slow lift to stretch tension before a reveal. When: a reveal is near and the player should wait. Why: Waiting raises anticipation.
- [heuristic] Track events with timestamps even when the game world shows no clock, so the player can check the timeline against a known source.
- [heuristic] An automatic event log kept by the game can serve as ground truth against which player notes or NPC accounts are checked.
- [heuristic] Give the game a shared clock, such as a timer or call log, that fixes events, and let the player correct a wrong timeline by checking an anchor.
- [heuristic] Do not rely on a timer alone if the audience cannot track it. Repeat the sound so the passing time stays memorable. When: Using an in-world timer as a pacing device. Why: A timer the audience loses track of has little effect on tension.
- [heuristic] Timing a crisis to fall when a suspect is most exposed makes the timing itself a clue. When: placing events around a concealed motive. Why: coincidence of timing draws attention.
- [heuristic] Give a suspect a schedule or lease with a clear end date that the player can confirm from a third party, so it sets a deadline. When: Designing a time-limited element in a mystery. Why: A confirmed end date creates a fixed window.
- [heuristic] Objects left mid-use, such as a half-played game or a cooling brazier, show when an NPC last acted and give the player a timed clue. When: Designing scene clues for timing. Why: Unfinished activity records how recently someone was present.
- [heuristic] A deadline should not be the only source of tension; checks must also create uncertainty about what is true.
- [heuristic] A deadline that forces someone to leave by a set moment is evidence that they were following a plan. Why: a fixed departure time constrains when the person could have acted.
- [heuristic] A time limit forces a choice and gives the questioner leverage, especially when the other party gains from delay. When: when the other party benefits from waiting.
- [heuristic] A stated time window limits when an act could have occurred and gives the puzzle a fixed timer. When: when a puzzle depends on when an act happened.
- [heuristic] A file lodged with an NPC that releases automatically at a deadline unless the player returns creates tension and a risky timed return trip. When: designing an evidence or insurance system. Why: The threat of automatic release pulls players back in person.
- [heuristic] A timing mechanic can link player exertion to how fast a hidden effect acts, so player actions change how the effect unfolds.
- [heuristic] Use a slow-trigger lock only when the sealed state must form after the actor leaves and a rough time window is acceptable.
- [heuristic] Record timing results across repeated attempts so the player builds evidence over several tries. When: when a deduction requires several tests. Why: accumulated records make the player's conclusions traceable.
- [heuristic] Anchor a timed plan to several independent clocks that can be cross-checked, so the remaining margin is clear to the player. When: A timed escape must line up across sources. Why: Independent clocks confirm or contradict each other.
- [heuristic] A fixed deadline works poorly when the underlying cycle is uncertain, because the limit itself cannot be predicted. When: when a timed action depends on an unpredictable cycle. Why: a deadline is only useful if its timing can be known.
- [heuristic] Under a fixed deadline, count the days remaining and reassess which sub-goals still fit, then order tasks so the most time-critical ones come first. When: A hard date limits which goals can still be achieved. Why: Deadlines make some goals infeasible, so priority must follow what can still be completed.
- [heuristic] A legal or financial deadline can serve as a timer that builds pressure and orders the player's investigation.
- [heuristic] Use a timed event such as a scheduled patrol or arriving messenger to pull the player away from a conversation and create urgency, and leave a pending clue unresolved so the player returns to it. When: Creating urgency in a game scene or level. Why: Timed interruptions add pressure and reasons to revisit open threads.
- [heuristic] Tie a timed wait to a visible task with a risk, such as a boiling pot or approaching guard, because an empty wait loses attention. When: Designing a timed puzzle or delay. Why: Without a task or risk, the delay drags and players disengage.
- [heuristic] Let a stalling NPC take a timed task, giving the player a window to search the room while the NPC is occupied.
- [heuristic] Use fast-track gates with false urgency, such as claimed overcrowding, so players question whether the bypassed rule was needed. Why: Checking the stated reason can expose the gate's real purpose.
- [heuristic] Pair dread with ordinary, warm elements in the same space so unsettling moments stand out rather than becoming monotonous. When: pacing a sequence of tense spaces. Why: contrast makes dread noticeable.
- [heuristic] Let the world change during a timed event so the player sees that it changed while they were away, such as a new ladder appearing. When: When designing timed or off-screen events. Why: Visible changes show the player that time passed and the world responded.
- [heuristic] Hold back one key fact until the end of an area, then reveal it there, so the player's final understanding changes. When: when designing a late-game reveal. Why: a delayed fact reframes what the player already knows.
- [heuristic] A game's in-world clock can run differently from the player's sense of elapsed time, and a visible log or calendar lets players check the real timeline. When: when a night or event feels longer or shorter than the clock shows. Why: Players rely on perceived time unless a record shows the real time.
- [procedure] 1) Confirm the timer was running at the event time. 2) Check its setting against a reference, since visibility and motion do not prove accuracy. 3) Establish when it started running. 4) Corroborate with a routine or second witness. When: A timed mechanism is the only record of when an event happened, or a clock is used as evidence of timing. Why: A stopped or misset clock gives false timing; visibility and motion do not prove it is set correctly.
- [procedure] 1) Learn the route and its landmarks in advance. 2) Count the intervals between landmarks to predict slowdowns. 3) Prepare the exit point and any escape transport beforehand. 4) Act only during the predicted window of reduced attention. When: Planning an escape from a moving, guarded transit. Why: Planned timing replaces luck, and preparation ensures the exit is ready when the slowdown occurs.
- [procedure] 1) Record who held the keys at each point. 2) Record who patrolled the area and when. 3) Record when the container was last locked and last opened. These facts define who could have acted and when. When: A theft occurred in a locked space and the question is who could have opened it. Why: The time between last lock and first reopening bounds the window of opportunity.
- [procedure] 1) Calculate time remaining before the natural cycle closes access. 2) Work backwards to set a latest departure. 3) Set a meeting point and a contingency for lateness. When: when an action depends on a predictable cycle. Why: working backwards from the limit makes the deadline actionable.
- [procedure] 1) Identify a checkable world condition, such as weather. 2) Find when it changed. 3) Infer the earliest or latest time a suspect could have acted, bounding the window. When: A suspect's account includes a checkable detail. Why: Independent conditions narrow when an event was possible.
- [tradeoff] A short deadline stops a reluctant source from reflecting or verifying, but can produce a rushed confession that is hard to verify. When: Setting a time limit on a suspect's deal or answer; pressing a reluctant source for an answer. Why: Less time for reflection reduces the suspect's ability to resist, and also reduces the reliability of what is said. (x2)
- [tradeoff] Short windows suit pursuits, compressing choices and keeping pressure; generous margins suit timed challenges, since tight deadlines leave no room for hiding or waiting and turn small setbacks into failure. When: choosing time windows for pursuit or escape sequences versus timed challenges. Why: Brief windows maintain pressure, while unplanned delays are common and consume any slack.
- [tradeoff] A fixed-rate timer only approximates time, so use it where rough timing suffices and avoid it where exact timing matters. When: deciding whether a timer suits a design need. Why: flow-based timing is inexact.
- [pitfall] Repeated postponements, overused deadlines or waits running too long read as dead time and flatten tension; an unseen pursuer never resolved can feel like a cheat. When: when using forced waiting or deadlines as a pacing device. Why: Audiences stop believing in consequences when delays repeat without a payoff; pauses without a payoff stop feeling like pressure.
- [pitfall] Haste used as pressure seems contrived when the audience does not feel the deadline. When: Pushing a character to decide quickly. Why: A deadline needs visible stakes to feel real.
- [pitfall] Misreading a timetable can make an impossible journey look possible.
- [pitfall] A waiting deadline that passes with no action drains tension. The next step should be clear before the deadline arrives.
- [pitfall] If the only clock is hidden from the audience, a timeline becomes a claim to trust rather than a fact to check. When: When time evidence is withheld or unverifiable. Why: The audience cannot independently confirm the timing.
- [pitfall] Judging a seal unbreakable can end the search early and leave the real route unexamined. Why: once an investigation stops, untested exits remain unchecked.
- [pitfall] A single delay such as a traffic jam can consume most of a tight margin and decide whether a timed plan succeeds. When: Timed routes with little slack. Why: Small margins leave no buffer for unexpected time loss.
- [pitfall] Applying the decay rate of the wrong variety of an object gives a wrong time estimate. When: Reading decay of a perishable object. Why: Rates of change differ by variety, so the reference rate must match the object.
- [fact] Time-critical acts, such as moving a body before rigor mortis sets in, set a deadline for the actor and constrain the sequence of events. When: An action must finish before a physical change takes hold. Why: Physical processes impose hard limits on when actions can occur.

## forensic examination
- [mechanism] Let players collect reference samples from known suspects through ordinary interactions, such as a handed-over object, then compare them with marks on a vial or lock. When: Designing forensic evidence gathering in a game. Why: An ordinary action keeps the collection unobtrusive.
- [mechanism] Use a repeatable test, such as reproducing a noise or impact, as a puzzle mechanic that confirms or rejects a clue or hypothesis about what happened. Why: A repeatable result can be checked by anyone, so it confirms the clue convincingly.
- [mechanism] Heat-revealed inks appear when warmed, fade when cooled, and reappear on reheating, so the same hidden text can be inspected repeatedly.
- [mechanism] A gunpowder smell on an upper floor shows air was moving, so a door or window must have been briefly open during the shots.
- [mechanism] A lab result that narrows a source to a category rather than a specific place must be combined with other clues to locate it. When: Designing forensic results in a mystery. Why: Single category results rarely identify one source.
- [mechanism] A medical finding that rules out the obvious method, such as ingestion when no poison is in the stomach, shifts the explanation toward another route. When: A physical finding conflicts with the expected explanation of how a death occurred. Why: Each excluded method narrows the remaining possibilities until an alternative route becomes the most consistent explanation.
- [mechanism] A forensic mechanic can return a match with a confidence rating, and an NPC authority can treat a high-confidence match as enough to arrest, leaving the player to find the flaw. When: Designing forensic or evidence-based mechanics. Why: Confidence presented as certainty shifts the burden to the player to challenge it.
- [mechanism] A late examination mechanic can overturn a player's conclusion, and a hidden cause appears only when the player orders the correct analysis. When: Designing late-game investigation in a game. Why: A test chosen with the right hypothesis is what exposes the hidden cause.
- [mechanism] A test that reveals a suspicious item to be ordinary removes support from the theory it seemed to back, forcing the player to revise that theory. When: A promising item is analysed. Why: The result cuts the link between clue and theory.
- [mechanism] A scan or examine mode could reveal items buried in mud, with detection requiring close inspection.
- [mechanism] A handwriting or seal comparison against known samples works as a cheap, decisive verification mechanic that lets players test a document's authenticity. When: When a game offers a document-verification action. Why: A direct comparison resolves authorship quickly, so it rewards player checking without long investigation.
- [mechanism] A device left recording during an event can capture what the observer did not perceive, giving a delayed record of the scene. When: A witness cannot be trusted to report accurately, or events occur unnoticed. Why: Passive capture records state without relying on the observer's perception.
- [mechanism] A game can record which NPCs opened a container and when, so dust or disturbance states work as a readable access log.
- [mechanism] Tampering leaves small physical traces, such as a changed surface texture or a lingering scent, that reveal an object was altered before the player arrived. When: An object or container has been opened or changed. Why: Physical changes persist after the act and can be sensed.
- [mechanism] A physical clue that seems to prove identity can be exposed as a copy when a live demonstration reproduces it, shown to experts who judged it by appearance and repetition. When: A mystery where a key object is accepted as authentic at face value. Why: Experts trust appearance and pattern rather than method, so a reproduced test defeats them.
- [behavior] Fresh marks such as mud or a partial travel ticket reveal recent movement, while old marks do not, so freshness changes what can be inferred. When: a person arrives with small physical traces. Why: recent traces reflect current travel, old ones do not.
- [behavior] Expert estimates of time since death should be given as a bounded range rather than a single figure, and the point when stiffening began should be noted alongside it. When: When a cause or timing of death must be established from a body. Why: A range reflects the real uncertainty of physical signs and avoids overstating precision.
- [behavior] Partial remains support specific inferences: clean joints suggest practised handling, and a missing item with no earlier damage suggests removal after death. When: when a body or skeleton is partly recovered. Why: each trace reflects how the remains were handled or how the injury arose.
- [rule] Flag fragile evidence so careless handling, such as touching it wrongly or restoring it carelessly, degrades it or leaves a detectable flag, while careful handling succeeds. When: Designing evidence items or object interaction that players must collect or restore. Why: Handling choices create consequences, so outcomes that depend on care make the player's handling meaningful and teach careful collection.
- [rule] Move a suspected dangerous object with a tool rather than by hand and keep it with other evidence, since a careless touch can trigger harm, injure handlers and destroy the evidence. When: An object's mechanism is unknown or may be hazardous. Why: A careless touch can trigger the hidden harm and damage the evidence.
- [rule] Accept unusual bodily marks as evidence only when qualified physicians judge them, and never treat a reported bodily effect, such as harm from a look, as infallible proof of guilt.
- [rule] Give each material or residue from different sources a distinct visual signature, such as cell shape, visible only under magnifier inspection rather than from its icon. When: Designing identification mechanics or residues that players collect. Why: Distinct signatures reward careful inspection and make identification skill-based.
- [rule] Compare a trace with a reference using an enlarged photograph set against a numbered scale. Never mark the original exhibit. When: when the original must stay undamaged.
- [rule] Make body inspection a distinct action with a time cost, and penalize moving the body before it is examined.
- [rule] Make some puzzle objects change state when moved or inspected, and record the change, so players must handle them with care.
- [rule] Write precise lab instructions stating the order of tests, what to analyze and compare, and what to report and to whom.
- [rule] Give a forensic puzzle at least two possible outcomes that point to different suspects, so the answer is not fixed in advance. When: Designing a forensic or test-based puzzle. Why: Varied outcomes keep the investigation open.
- [rule] Let a player run a forensic check on a found object, such as a glove or hem, to rule out false causes before accepting a conclusion.
- [rule] Track who has handled each item in the game, since handling history changes the item's evidential value. When: items can be picked up, moved or used by characters. Why: handling history determines what an item proves.
- [rule] Have a second character correct a player's cipher decoding, so the decoding gains a verification step. When: A cipher puzzle is used. Why: Players can confirm their work instead of guessing whether it is right.
- [rule] A sample that must be tested before it is trusted can be made unrecoverable, so the player's testing choices carry lasting consequences.
- [rule] Offer a planted fake item that only a guilty NPC reacts to, so players can test suspects.
- [rule] Give a death-registration system a cause-of-death check with an autopsy step that can overturn a quick NPC verdict. When: designing death records.
- [rule] Measure a substance's potency with controlled dose tests on a test subject, observing the effect over time.
- [rule] Pair an intervention with a case where it does nothing, so the player can tell a real cause from coincidence.
- [rule] Keep the production method and the number of sources hidden from the examiner, so the test measures skill rather than knowledge of how samples were made. When: running a blind expert test. Why: Learning how samples were made invalidates the result.
- [rule] A look-closer mechanic can penalise over-inspection, nudging players to step back and survey the room. When: designing an inspection interaction. Why: penalising fixation encourages wider searching.
- [rule] Let players test a physical impression by wrapping it around a matching shape or holding it against the correct body part before it is accepted. When: designing a clue that must be verified physically. Why: a physical test makes the player earn the identification.
- [rule] Provide an in-game codex or lore entry that names a group once the player looks up its initials. When: a group appears in the game only as initials. Why: a lookup turns a cryptic clue into an identified faction.
- [rule] Give small physical marks on characters or props their own inspection text, so a careful player can examine them as clues. When: A prop or character carries a detail that may matter to the solution. Why: Small marks reward close inspection and make clues discoverable.
- [rule] Pair every symptom with a codex entry describing its known pattern, letting the player match what they observe against it.
- [rule] Provide an inspect mode that lets the player slow down and focus on one object, so details can be examined closely. When: Designing a scene where players must notice small clues. Why: Focused attention exposes details that quick viewing misses.
- [rule] Add a breathe-on-it interaction that reveals recent touches on a lever or relic, giving a hint without changing world state. When: designing hints for object handling. Why: A reveal that leaves state untouched keeps the world consistent.
- [rule] Let a torn note be completed by overlaying it on a matching copy, so players align pieces to recover a hidden clue. When: designing partial documents as puzzles. Why: Alignment turns a fragment into a readable clue through player action.
- [rule] Give NPCs skill tiers so that an inexpert method leaves a signature that a specialist NPC can identify. When: When designing crafting or forensic systems with varying expertise. Why: Low-skill actions leave traces that a higher-skill observer can read and interpret.
- [rule] Let projectiles carry traces of the cloth or cover they passed through, so a recovered item points the player to the garment used. When: Designing clues around ranged attacks.
- [heuristic] When a physical test contradicts a witness account or expectation, let the result arbitrate and override the theory, since measurement is independent of testimony. When: Two accounts conflict on a physical fact, or a lab result contradicts a witness's expectation. Why: Measurement is independent of testimony, and expectations can be wrong.
- [heuristic] Use magnification, including enlarged photographs, on faint or small marks to expose fine detail, separating real traces from background noise and making distinguishing features usable. When: A key feature is small or hard to see, or a scene holds faint or ambiguous marks. Why: Magnification makes fine detail observable and separates real traces from background noise.
- [heuristic] Bite marks or a bitten-through part suggest physical strength and a strong set of teeth in the owner. When: When an object shows teeth marks or bitten damage. Why: Biting through material requires force and dental strength.
- [heuristic] When examining a person, check the hands first, since they often record recent actions and contact.
- [heuristic] Examine apparently trivial objects, such as a blotter or waste paper, because they can record a writer's behaviour. When: A room contains mundane objects that may hold writing or traces. Why: Small everyday records can reveal what someone did.
- [heuristic] Orderly, recently altered space signals a present actor, while overgrown or neglected space signals a relic of the past.
- [heuristic] Do not apply premise revision when the observations themselves are uncertain. When: the observations are uncertain.
- [heuristic] An item that shows whether an action was completed acts as a state check the player can read.
- [heuristic] Build in a planned exception so the pattern feels real, then give a prediction the player can test against the next event.
- [heuristic] Tie an injury or missing item to one NPC while keeping the clue ambiguous, so it narrows the suspect list without settling the answer.
- [heuristic] Ask a distant specialist to examine a key object by telegraph, and get the result in writing, before travelling to the scene. When: when a specialist is far away and a physical finding must be verified. Why: a written report gives a checkable record.
- [heuristic] Repeat a timed reconstruction several times, including a long solo run, to check consistency before drawing a conclusion. When: when a timing claim rests on a reconstruction. Why: a single run may be an outlier, while repeated runs show consistent results.
- [heuristic] Use a confirmed fact in reverse to test an earlier assumption, which can expose a hidden substitution.
- [heuristic] Check whether a body's position fits how it arrived. Undisturbed cover beneath it suggests the body was placed there rather than dropped from above. When: A body is found in an odd or remote position. Why: A fall or drop through vegetation leaves breakage that placement does not.
- [heuristic] Show the player what a trial or ordeal actually measures, so a rigged rule that rewards a preferred posture can be spotted. When: designing trial or ordeal mechanics. Why: Transparent criteria let players detect manipulation.
- [heuristic] Use a glinting object as a visual cue that marks where an important action is taking place. When: When directing player attention to an action. Why: A reflection draws the eye to the spot.
- [procedure] 1) Identify a surface only specific people should have touched. 2) Dust it with fine powder to reveal latent prints. 3) Photograph the prints promptly with flash lighting and make a negative so the record survives handling. When: An object was handled and its prints are not visible, or a surface only specific people should have touched. Why: Powder reveals latent prints, and photography preserves them.
- [procedure] 1) List every item carried on the body, searching clothing pockets rather than checking cursorily. 2) Note any name, ticket, letter, coin, timepiece, key or note. 3) Record what each reveals about identity, travel or leads. When: Searching a body or victim's belongings, or examining a victim or servant. Why: Carried items reveal identity and recent movements, and small items such as keys or notes can link a person to a location or another lead.
- [procedure] 1) Survey the body and its surroundings first: limb posture, covers, signs of struggle or fear, and the face. 2) Inspect each object in the room in turn. 3) Check floors and furniture edges. 4) Record destroyed items and absences before anything is moved. When: examining a fresh death before the scene is disturbed. Why: A careful first record may be all that survives for later investigators.
- [procedure] 1) Note posture and limb position for signs of struggle. 2) Note facial expression. 3) Match each wound to a plausible weapon. 4) Check small marks such as neck spots. 5) Estimate firing distance. 6) Search pockets. 7) Estimate timing from witnesses. When: The cause, sequence or distance of a death is disputed.
- [procedure] 1) Inspect the mark under magnification. 2) Check for a human hand-print. 3) Check for drag from a loose fabric edge over a wet surface. 4) Rule out animal causes before accepting a cause.
- [procedure] 1) Locate where a visible trace originates. 2) Examine the source closely. 3) Only then name what caused the trace.
- [procedure] 1) Run the apparatus with a clean blank. 2) Test the paper or packaging alone. 3) Test the sample. 4) Confirm any positive with a second, selective reagent. When: a test result will be decisive. Why: blanks and materials can create false positives, and a second reagent separates substances with similar results.
- [procedure] 1) Split a compound item into its separate parts. 2) Test each part on its own with a safe method. 3) Identify which part produces the contradictory result. When: when a compound item gives a confusing result. Why: Separate testing isolates the cause of the result.
- [procedure] 1) Weigh the recovered bullet and compare it with the known weights of candidate pistol loads. 2) Use the bullet and expended shell to estimate muzzle velocity. 3) Use that velocity and energy to estimate penetration in a reference material at a stated distance. When: A bullet and shell are recovered and the weapon type or event must be inferred. Why: Weight identifies the load type, and velocity and energy then constrain the shot.
- [procedure] 1) Pair related items. 2) Compare their torn edges, marks or construction. 3) Mismatches indicate different origins or times, which must be dated before they support a conclusion. When: Two objects might come from one source or event. Why: Mismatched traces suggest separate origins, but the collection times must be known to interpret them.
- [procedure] 1) Have the witness describe personal marks before the body is shown. 2) Check each mark in turn, building up matches. 3) Ask a final yes-or-no question and record the answer. 4) Limit the witness's view of the face when not needed. When: A body is damaged, disguised or unsafe to view and a relative must confirm identity. Why: Pre-viewing description avoids shaping the witness's account, and sequential checks build a cumulative match.
- [procedure] 1) Examine the document as a physical object: paper stock, envelope, watermark. 2) Check how it was delivered, whether posted or left by hand. 3) Use delivery to infer where the writer must have been. 4) Compare its claims against the writer's character, resources and the scene's physical facts. When: A written note or letter arrives unasked and may be incomplete or self-serving. Why: Physical circumstances of delivery can place the writer at a location and test their claims.
- [procedure] 1) Estimate how long the damage would take with ordinary tools. 2) Examine the residue at the edges of the damage. 3) Apply a test that reacts to the residue. 4) Infer the mechanism and the skill it requires. When: Damage or method appears beyond the expected tools. Why: A residue test can reveal a mechanism that makes an apparently impossible method feasible.
- [metric] Age and build can be estimated from bone size and wear; height can be estimated from arm span width, with thigh bone length giving an independent second estimate. When: when estimating a body's physical profile from skeletal remains. Why: skeletal proportions correlate with overall stature.
- [pitfall] A forensic test that finds nothing does not prove innocence, and a positive may only confirm what is already known, so keep pursuing other lines rather than waiting for it.
- [pitfall] Mass-produced or common-material matches are weak evidence that narrows the source very little, so record them but test further. When: A trace matches a common item or a widely used substance. Why: Many people own the same item, and ubiquitous materials match many sources.
- [pitfall] Methods that depend on freshness can fail silently on older evidence, so players may trust a negative result that means nothing.
- [pitfall] Investigators pick up traces at the scene on their own footwear, so a trace that matches the environment may have come from them.
- [pitfall] Matching a print to a person by size alone, or assuming all tracks belong to the suspect without checking other traffic, leads to false attributions. When: Assigning footprints to a character. Why: Many people and objects can leave similar marks, so walking pattern and traffic must be considered.
- [pitfall] A specialist's conclusion depends on correctly identifying the artifact; a misidentified bullet type corrupts every inference built on it. When: Drawing conclusions from a recovered physical object. Why: Downstream calculations inherit any error in the initial identification.
- [pitfall] Physical clues only work if lighting or inspection tools make them readable to the player.
- [pitfall] Handwriting or notes in a record may be ambiguous or uninterpreted, so a record can prove less than it appears to. Why: unclear evidence should not be treated as settled.
- [fact] Documents and hidden objects serve as evidence, and what people write down or conceal shapes what an investigation can prove.
- [fact] A permanent mark on the body, such as a brand, can prove lifelong membership in a group, and clothing can hide it. When: when identity depends on a physical feature.
- [fact] A slightly sour smell at the lips can indicate a forced poison, though it is suggestive rather than conclusive. When: Examining a body with suspected poisoning. Why: Certain poisons leave a characteristic odour, but other causes can produce similar signs.
- [fact] An engraved mark such as initials on an object can identify its owner, linking a thief to that person when read. When: An object with a readable mark is taken. Why: The mark stays with the object and names its owner.
- [fact] Latent prints on an object can be raised quickly with dusting powder and an apparatus, and they survive best when the object is left undisturbed.
- [fact] A body becomes evidence only when it can be linked to a person. A corpse with no traceable link to the killer cannot convict them. When: When assessing whether a discovered body supports a case against a suspect. Why: Proof depends on connecting the physical remains to a specific individual.
