- [fact] Field handling of trace material does not replace the lab's own confirmation of results.
- [fact] A cast can be taken by pressing a finger into soft material, which suits a dead, sleeping, or unconscious person.
- [fact] Sampling tools are tweezers, a probe, litmus paper and small test tubes for collecting and testing samples in the field.
- [fact] An object's condition, such as mud staining, trampling or partial washing, reveals how it was handled and where it lay, and that handling can also erase or confuse the evidence. When: When an object found at a scene is used as evidence of location or movement. Why: Ground contact and cleaning leave traces, and later handling by others can disturb them.

## investigation planning and workflow
- [model] A small investigation network uses one headquarters that receives updates at set check-in times, while a messenger carries notes between team members. When: investigators are spread across a wide area. Why: timed check-ins keep reports current without constant contact.
- [mechanism] A location can carry a mood or hostility state that builds up over time and changes how its NPCs respond, instead of holding one fixed clue. When: when designing a location that the player visits repeatedly. Why: a changing state makes the same location respond differently over time.
- [mechanism] A constraint can be avoided: a route blocked for one vehicle or method may still serve through another path that permits it. When: A region is excluded by a capability the player might lack or bypass. Why: Each constraint applies to a specific mode of travel, so alternatives remain open.
- [mechanism] A tailing mechanic can let the player use browsing or idle animations as cover while a target moves through town. When: A game features stealth tailing through populated areas. Why: Idle actions that look plausible let the player wait unnoticed.
- [behavior] Investigations often feel exciting at the start and then stall, as leads run out and time is spent on inconclusive threads. When: When planning or evaluating the pacing of a mystery over a long case. Why: Leads fade naturally, so the middle of an inquiry typically has fewer active threads.
- [behavior] Investigators can spend months gathering proof against a decoy, and each confirming fact reinforces the trail, so the real culprit is overlooked. When: a plausible suspect has real incriminating features. Why: each confirming fact strengthens the lead.
- [behavior] Several small unexplained details, each harmless alone, build a sense of mystery even though no single detail proves anything. When: unexplained details pile up without a clear answer. Why: no single fact fixes the explanation.
- [behavior] Witnesses recall a memory tied to a concrete action more dependably than a general impression of events. When: when assessing witness testimony. Why: a concrete action anchors the memory.
- [behavior] Under time pressure, a searcher heads straight for the likeliest containers, which can leave the rest unchecked. When: when the clock is running out. Why: limited time forces prioritising the most probable hiding places.
- [rule] Post watchers when a suspect's contacts, movements or intentions are unknown, and expect their value to fall once they are discovered.
- [rule] Admit that a reproduction result may be wrong, and consider alternative causes before treating a successful test as proof. When: A reconstruction test appears to confirm a theory. Why: One matching result does not exclude other explanations that produce the same trace.
- [rule] Let collected traces accumulate on an inventory item so the final location is deduced from several weak matches rather than one clue.
- [rule] Impose a deadline on an investigation so the plan has a clock, and make grounds for delay explicit. When: designing time-pressured investigation.
- [rule] Do not chase a line of inquiry that offers no concrete next step. When: A lead produces only speculation or atmosphere. Why: Without a next step the lead cannot be tested or moved forward.
- [rule] Do not split one investigation into unrelated mission units when its clues belong to a single timeline; keep the clues in one connected sequence. When: An investigation's clues are linked in time. Why: Splitting breaks links between clues.
- [rule] Use only investigative tests whose legitimacy observers are unlikely to doubt, and follow established authorities on procedure.
- [rule] Establish whether the client seeks acquittal or a lighter outcome before starting work, since each goal requires different evidence and different effort. When: at the start of a case.
- [rule] Gate each investigation step on its own evidence threshold: a rumor opens an inquiry, a physical clue allows detention, and two independent sources allow a verdict.
- [rule] Choose a preservation method that can be carried out with materials actually at hand, not one that needs unavailable supplies. When: Time is short and the preferred materials are missing. Why: A method that cannot be performed on site protects nothing.
- [rule] Before acting on an unconfirmed theory, send word first, arrive with witnesses, and avoid forcing entry without cause. When: When a theory suggests action but predictions are not yet checked. Why: Acting on an unchecked theory risks error and harm.
- [rule] Carry the equipment needed to examine a scene on the spot rather than deferring analysis. When: Planning an investigation. Why: On-site examination captures traces that would be lost in transport or delay.
- [rule] Hold back an arrest while gathering one more item of evidence, and keep the suspect under watch during the delay. When: When the suspect may flee or destroy evidence, and watching will not tip them off.
- [rule] Relay observations to the decision maker promptly, because a late report loses most of its value. When: when a partner is reporting on a watched target.
- [heuristic] Avoid long stalls without new developments; long idle stretches with no new information read as padding, bore players and drain attention. When: an investigation or puzzle pauses for extended time.
- [heuristic] Reconstruct a custody chain mainly when it has gaps or unwitnessed transfers, since a complete, witnessed chain leaves little to reason about. When: deciding whether an object's history needs reconstruction. Why: reasoning is needed only where the record is missing.
- [heuristic] Act quickly after a crime, since each day of delay makes the culprit harder to find and guilt harder to prove. Why: Delay lets evidence disappear and weakens the case.
- [heuristic] The order of investigative questions can change as new evidence arrives, so a later question may be answered before an earlier one. When: New evidence makes a later question easier to answer than an earlier one.
- [heuristic] Fill waiting intervals with concrete small actions, such as lighting a pipe or listening at a door, so tension stays grounded in the scene. When: A scene must hold tension while nothing decisive happens. Why: Concrete actions give the audience something to track and keep the wait from feeling empty.
- [heuristic] Ask a witness for the concrete basis of an impression; this turns a vague claim into a stated, testable basis. When: witnesses give impressions rather than observations. Why: a stated basis can be checked.
- [heuristic] Formal descriptions reveal less than a companion's unguarded anecdotes; ask for concrete episodes rather than opinions to learn habits, temper and relationships. When: Building a profile of a person quickly through talk.
- [heuristic] Rely on records and informers when a crime looks like the work of a known group. When: a crime's method matches a known criminal network. Why: Known groups leave identifiable background.
- [heuristic] Use a systematic room sweep when the question is whether someone has been in a room or handled an object; skip it when evidence is documentary or testimonial. When: Choosing between physical search and other methods.
- [heuristic] A single primary document read directly needs no source-independence check; the check matters when several secondary accounts seem to agree.
- [heuristic] When an error would be costly, seek certainty rather than the most probable explanation before acting on a suspicion. When: when the cost of a wrong accusation is high. Why: Probable answers can still be wrong, and a wrong accusation is hard to undo.
- [heuristic] Investigation methods that take far longer than needed feel like failures to players. Keep the time cost of identification low.
- [heuristic] A contingency rule, such as waiting out a hazard until nightfall, gives players a fallback that changes tension without ending the run. When: when a timed action can fail. Why: a fallback lets failure become a delay rather than a loss.
- [heuristic] Use concealed observation when the subject's natural behaviour is the evidence and an observer's presence would alter that behaviour.
- [heuristic] When people cannot be reassembled, restoring the physical setting is often the more important condition to match. When: a reproduction cannot recreate every participant. Why: the physical setting shapes the outcome more than the people do.
- [heuristic] When the expected event timing is loose, design the watch so it tolerates long waits. When: the event is expected in a broad time window. Why: observers may wait a long time before anything happens.
- [heuristic] After a theory fails, the facts it cannot explain define the next lines of inquiry. Why: unexplained facts point to what to check next.
- [heuristic] Treat watchers' conclusions as interpretations; before escalating, remind the team what the current surveillance already covers. When: When a team member raises an alarm based on observed behavior.
- [heuristic] State your precautions against being followed and check them explicitly, since a missed tail can undermine an otherwise sound account. When: Relying on a route or meeting to stay unobserved. Why: Unexamined precautions can hide surveillance.
- [heuristic] Give the player a visible, persistent mark on a location so they can confirm it later, even when conditions make direct recognition hard. When: Players must return to or verify a specific place under poor visibility or after time has passed. Why: A persistent identifier lets the player confirm a location without relying on memory alone.
- [procedure] 1) List candidate checks. 2) Rank them by how decisively each tests the central question. 3) Work the top-ranked checks first. 4) Assign each line of inquiry a clear owner. When: Many leads exist and each costs time, so the order of checks matters. Why: Decisive checks first avoid wasted effort; routine tasks otherwise yield long lead lists with little progress.
- [procedure] 1) Find the independent dated record first. 2) Question the witness about details the record does not show. 3) Compare overlapping claims with other accounts.
- [procedure] 1) List each incident separately with its time and the people involved. 2) Ask what first event could have set them all in motion. 3) Fix that starting point, then order the other facts around it. When: Incidents seem unrelated but share a person, object, or place. Why: A single origin can explain separate incidents that look unconnected when viewed one at a time.
- [procedure] Before settling on any theory, work through four questions: What happened, Where it happened, How it was done, and Why it was done. When: a case has several possible explanations.
- [procedure] 1) Identify what should be present or should have changed by now. 2) Ask what it means that it is not. 3) Form a specific, testable claim about the cause. 4) Check the claim immediately against the evidence. When: a scene feels normal but something is out of place in timing or presence. Why: Noticing absences and testing a concrete claim turns a vague unease into a verifiable conclusion.
- [procedure] 1) Survey the scene before drawing conclusions. 2) Gain access through people who already have it. 3) Ask the informant directly what they may be withholding. When: Starting an investigation with a guarded informant. Why: Early conclusions and unplanned access give an incomplete picture.
- [procedure] 1) Ask whether the act was physically possible at all. 2) Seek evidence that confirms or rules it out.
- [procedure] 1) Record the time of every investigative step. 2) Order the steps into one timeline. 3) Check each stated time against the others. 4) Log failed contacts as timed events. When: when the order of events matters to the solution. Why: Timings that are never cross-checked can hide inconsistent alibis.
- [procedure] 1) Track means, opportunity and motive for each suspect. 2) Mark each element as supported, missing or contested. 3) Review which suspect has the fewest gaps. When: Building an investigation board or case tracker. Why: A structured board makes weak elements visible instead of hidden in notes.
- [procedure] 1) Revisit the scene and have each witness mark where each incident happened. 2) Interview each witness alone. 3) Compare paperwork with what was physically done. 4) Test each claim with a specialist. 5) Circulate a description and map sightings to infer direction of travel. When: When a case involves several people, a sequence of events and a set of records.
- [procedure] 1) List every fixed deadline and travel time. 2) Check each constraint against the timeline. 3) Mark any gap or exposure risk as a point of suspicion. When: A plan's success depends on timing, travel, or being unseen. Why: Clever plans can hide timing gaps that collapse under scrutiny.
- [procedure] 1) Search the desk drawers first. 2) Check the cheque book next. 3) Search the bedroom bureau last. 4) Note other fittings, such as the telephone and door latch, in passing. When: searching a private space for evidence. Why: a fixed order keeps the search complete and repeatable.
- [procedure] 1) Name the one outcome that must be reached. 2) Work backward from it, checking each earlier step against that endpoint. 3) Derive limits from the goal. 4) Compare methods by how far each moves toward the endpoint, then commit to one. When: when several objectives compete. Why: a single end point can resolve conflicting aims.
- [procedure] 1) Record each planted open question in a quest log or dialogue system. 2) Mark it open until resolved. 3) Review unresolved questions before release. When: Managing deferred answers across a game's narrative content. Why: Tracking open threads prevents forgotten questions reaching release.
- [procedure] 1) State the case as one question. 2) List what is known. 3) List what must be shown. 4) List the obstacles in the way. When: An investigation has partial leads and competing views at a turning point. Why: A single restated problem makes the next step clearer than scattered facts.
- [procedure] 1) Estimate the number of work sessions the operation requires. 2) Count those sessions in weeks or days. 3) Use a closing signal, such as a shut-down or removed notice, to show when the work is complete. When: Designing a multi-stage operation or long-running activity. Why: A visible end state tells observers the work is finished and the window has closed.
- [procedure] 1) Place observers at a known spot before a repeated event. 2) Agree rules of engagement in advance. 3) Have each observer record a sighting independently. 4) Compare records against physical evidence. 5) Mark claims that leave no trace as unverified. When: A repeated event has a known location and timing. Why: Independent, pre-agreed records can be compared and checked, and untestable claims are kept from passing as facts.
- [procedure] 1) Give the field agent specific questions with checkable answers. 2) Include the subject's condition and any alibi details to confirm. 3) Set a return deadline. 4) Mark items as urgent or later. 5) Tell the agent which leads to drop. When: A central investigator directs a field agent who is away from the central location. Why: A prioritized, checkable list keeps the field agent's results usable and timely.
- [procedure] 1) State the exact question to be answered. 2) Separate the possible answers into distinct cases. 3) Collect only evidence that discriminates between those cases. When: Starting an investigation or puzzle. Why: A precise question prevents irrelevant evidence from gaining weight.
- [procedure] 1) Check existing records or directories first. 2) Visit the witness only if records fail to answer. 3) Fix a time for the next report. When: Assigning follow-up tasks to field agents.
- [procedure] 1) Anchor the search to a key date. 2) Filter public records such as notices and clippings by that date. 3) Check each candidate that fits the subject's needs. 4) Track calendar constraints that delay the next step. When: a subject leaves a trace in public records while trying to start a new life or find an offer. Why: dated records narrow the candidate set and reveal timing constraints.
- [procedure] 1) Identify the object's marks, sizing or notes. 2) Match them to a seller's order book, cash record or delivery log. 3) Compare the object's condition with its condition at sale to date any damage. 4) Follow each linked record to the next person to question. When: a found object must be connected to a seller or buyer and records exist. Why: each record link leads to a new witness and a narrower chain of custody.
- [tradeoff] Acting on partial evidence saves time and prevents harm, but risks error and alerting targets; waiting for confirmation is safer but may let harm recur or leads fade. When: Choosing between acting on partial evidence and holding an investigation open for full confirmation or a focusing clue. Why: Premature action risks alerting targets and error; delay risks recurring harm and lost leads.
- [tradeoff] A digression can waste time and let the witness steer the conversation, and allies kept unaware of the plan may lose faith in the investigator. When: Using feigned indifference during an investigation. Why: Concealment that protects the suspect also costs time and team trust.
- [tradeoff] Following a departing target across open ground risks being seen, so spending the absence on searching the premises is often safer. When: the suspect leaves on foot across exposed ground. Why: a shadow on open ground is easily noticed.
- [tradeoff] Deferring conclusions keeps the investigation open to new evidence, but it works only if the investigator can tolerate an unresolved state. When: When facts are still incomplete and the investigator must choose whether to commit. Why: Premature commitment closes off alternatives; indefinite deferral stalls progress.
- [tradeoff] Taking a small risk for speed, such as a brief light flash, can expose the offender even when the rest of the cover-up succeeds. When: An offender must act quickly during a cover-up. Why: Speed reduces care and can leave a fresh trace.
- [pitfall] An investigation stalls when no fresh line of inquiry opens after a setback, or when repeated reviews re-examine known facts without new input, causing fatigue. When: an investigation has stopped producing new information. Why: the next step is lost, and re-examining known facts yields no new leads.
- [pitfall] Public outrage or threats to everyone can destroy the chance of catching a culprit.
- [pitfall] A secret can leak from the investigators' own side, so the internal chain of custody for information must be checked too. When: when a private fact appears in a suspect's knowledge.
- [pitfall] Casual repeated visits can look aimless and yield little visible progress, even when they gather useful impressions. When: when an investigation relies on social contact rather than direct evidence. Why: useful impressions accumulate slowly and are hard to see early.
- [pitfall] A tangled ruleset can look profound while mainly testing attention, so complexity should not be taken as a sign of depth.
- [pitfall] A search can be interrupted mid-way by a visitor, so a search plan must account for discovery at any point. When: A player or NPC searches a location where others may arrive. Why: Unplanned interruption can expose the search or force a hasty exit.
- [pitfall] Eliminating the obvious routes can leave a hidden one that still needs explaining, so the remaining route must be checked for consistency. When: Most openings have been ruled out. Why: A last route that seems plausible may still conflict with other evidence.
- [pitfall] Confidence earned on verified core facts can wrongly carry over to unverified points that were never checked.
- [pitfall] A trap plan that is too elaborate for the people who must carry it out is likely to fail in execution. Why: complex coordination is hard to keep under pressure.
- [pitfall] A deadline can push a team to act before the evidence supports a conclusion.
- [pitfall] A fallback that is too broad, such as checking everyone present, can grow into a large workload; keep fallbacks to short named lists.
- [pitfall] A red herring can consume time that should go to checking alibis and access.
- [pitfall] An unanswered petition shows what the petitioner claimed, not what happened as a result. When: When using a petition as evidence of an outcome. Why: A petition records a request, so it cannot confirm that the request was granted or resolved.
- [pitfall] Records show an application rather than a finished meeting, so they cannot settle a matter alone; waiting for a convenient hour can lose the trail, and sharing a lead early can let the subject or a rival move first. Why: records are partial evidence and time and information both affect pursuit.
- [fact] Writers of the time accepted only a confession or two credible witnesses to an act done by the devil's help as valid proof, rejecting spectral evidence, afterthoughts, quarrels and water trials. When: when reviewing whether a witchcraft verdict rested on sufficient proof. Why: these were the recognized standards of proof at the time.
- [fact] Convicting proofs can include a physical mark, the suspect's own words, observed deeds, corroborated testimony of co-accused, and a confession. When: when deciding which evidence can support a verdict.

## case notebook and fact tracking
- [model] Organize each suspect's case under the same fixed headings (hypothesis, place, opportunity, time, means, motive, conduct, physical height), so filled and unfilled parts show and accusations can be checked. When: When several suspects have overlapping evidence. Why: Fixed headings show which parts are filled and which are only apparent, and a consistent structure makes accusations checkable.
- [model] A case can be assembled from three strands: motive, opportunity (who knew of and could reach the key item), and conduct before and after the crime. Why: separating the strands shows which parts of the case are still unsupported.
- [model] An accusation can be tested against seven elements: admitted threat, motive, time, place, opportunity, conduct, and the criminal agent (instrument). When: Before deciding whether an accusation is ready. Why: A structured list shows which parts of the case are complete.
- [model] Case levels are enquiry (a clue justifies a question), presumption (evidence justifies suspicion), and conviction (proof justifies a verdict); many clues stop short of conviction.
- [mechanism] An NPC may withhold a detail or be unable to recall it, recoverable only by a specific follow-up question or earned trust, so the player must work to reach it. When: Designing NPC dialogue that holds clues. Why: Clues stay hidden until the correct question is asked.
- [mechanism] Small mismatches or separate transactions reveal a pattern, such as common beneficiaries, only when grouped on one board or list, letting a cluster trigger a lead no single entry shows. When: A case rests on many small transactions. Why: Comparing records exposes common beneficiaries that individual entries hide.
- [mechanism] Tagging dialogue lines by source, such as seen, heard, guessed, or loyal, lets players learn to weigh each line differently. When: designing NPC dialogue that conveys testimony. Why: visible source tags teach the player how reliable each statement is.
- [mechanism] Physical clues such as a note outlast witness memory and can later be traced to the person who wrote or caused them.
- [mechanism] An interrogation dialogue can check player answers against world state such as logs and sightings; consistent answers are accepted, while contradictions raise NPC suspicion. When: in a game with an interrogation scene.
- [mechanism] Building transit delays into letters makes a letter's send date differ from its arrival, which adds a timing clue for players to reason about.
- [mechanism] A clue pointing at one suspect can be checked against recorded locations, so players see which other suspects it rules out.
- [mechanism] Lore facts collected over time can later unlock a door or reveal a hidden route, rewarding players who kept earlier information.
- [rule] In a case file, mark each timeline entry or link as seen, heard, inferred or proven, so players weigh unverified oral claims against sworn records and see where knowledge stops. When: When designing an in-game investigation with uncertain reports. Why: Labeling evidence type lets the player judge reliability and see which parts are proven versus left to inference. (x2)
- [rule] Store original clue text and exact NPC and companion statements, not just summaries, so players can verify links, re-read them after a reveal, and expose contradictions. When: statements or clues will later be verified or challenged. Why: Without originals, a digest cannot be tested for shaping or editing, and later contradictions cannot be pointed out. (x2)
- [rule] Store unexplained items or coincidences as open leads for later review, not as proofs or forced explanations. When: the game tracks clues or suspicions, or an object fits no current explanation. Why: This separates tentative links from established facts, and the item may gain meaning once further facts arrive. (x2)
- [rule] Give each case-file report a provenance field recording its source and distance from the event, such as sworn deposition, later hearsay, local tradition or editor's note. When: Designing a case file the player investigates. Why: Provenance shows which source is weaker and which clues are firsthand versus rumour.
- [rule] Have shops and merchants log purchases and sales, so the game can check whether a purchase fits a character's plan and players can trace where items went. When: when designing shopping or crafting systems that support investigation. Why: Without records the item trail dies and forces players onto another route.
- [rule] Give players a notebook or case codex logging every NPC and object met, so later prompts recall them and the suspect list can be rebuilt and checked. When: game with many NPCs. Why: A logged record keeps the solution reachable by reasoning.
- [rule] Provide a cast list at the start naming each character and role (victim, clerk, doctor, publicist, lawyer), so the audience can track presence and match people to functions. When: Writing mysteries with several or many incidental characters. Why: Clear presence tracking lets the audience test alibis and access, and prevents complaints of unfairness when minor characters matter late.
- [rule] Keep a clue ledger marking each lead open, cleared or lost, recording negative results so players see progress and do not repeat exhausted checks. When: a case has many simultaneous leads. Why: A written record keeps cleared theories from retaining their place and lets players tell progress from confusion.
- [rule] Keep a clue journal logging all observations, and every culprit-identifying clue before any accusation, so the final explanation or elimination can be traced back. When: when designing a deduction game. Why: A record of observations makes the solution and the elimination checkable.
- [rule] Keep minor observations, such as a missing item or disturbed room, and flag mismatches in a visible list the player can review, so the pattern across them is readable. When: a puzzle relies on many small clues. Why: Flagging rather than filtering keeps mismatches available, and a reviewable collection lets the player compare items and see what they share.
- [rule] Track whether each clue was noticed and whether it was acted on, so ignored clues can return later as legitimate leads. When: Clues can be dismissed or acted on.
- [rule] Make omitted lines or qualifying clauses in in-game logs, letters or journals discoverable, so attentive players can recover what a partial record left out and reach its real meaning.
- [rule] Log a minor NPC the first time they appear so a later link to the crime is still fair.
- [rule] Build case files from several document types with dates and locations, such as receipts, logs and letters, so players can assemble a timeline. When: designing an in-game investigation.
- [rule] Give a case file a dated event log so players can order events to test an alibi, and treat mismatched times as clues. When: Designing investigation content.
- [rule] When an NPC is removed, the case should persist in the world's records and pass to another character.
- [rule] Give each suspect a discoverable stake, such as debts or an inheritance, so players can test motive for themselves. When: designing suspects for a mystery.
- [rule] Record small sensory details such as a scent, sound or odd choice as they arrive, without interpreting them yet. When: when a clue first appears. Why: the meaning becomes clear only once later evidence supplies context.
- [rule] Keep a case journal of known facts and open questions, so players see gaps as hooks to pursue.
- [rule] Collect all facts about a case impartially, without forcing them into an existing theory, and study each one however trivial it seems. Why: A theory chosen early decides which facts count as relevant and can hide the real evidence.
- [rule] Track which facts the player has shared with each NPC, so a slip is recognised only when it matches a hidden fact the player has revealed. When: Implementing reactive dialogue in a mystery game. Why: Matching slips to shared facts keeps reactions consistent.
- [rule] Design a dialogue query such as asking whether a witness saw someone so that its correct answers depend on facts the player has learned earlier. When: When designing witness dialogue trees.
- [rule] Maintain a case file the player can search for matching methods, enabling links across encounters. When: designing an investigation journal. Why: searchable records make pattern matching possible.
- [rule] Offer a case-file mode where players solve a mystery from documents and interview logs without walking the whole map. When: designing investigation gameplay.
- [rule] Make room objects collectible evidence that players can compare against testimony from characters.
- [rule] Make facts that refute a hypothesis discoverable, so players learn to revise rather than cling to a first answer. When: designing deduction puzzles. Why: discoverable contradictions train revision.
- [rule] Make a preparation constraint, such as a task that cannot be done from bed, discoverable through an NPC's statement so it can narrow the suspect list. When: when a constraint is needed to eliminate suspects. Why: A constraint the player hears from a character can be used as a deduction tool.
- [rule] Once several copies of an access item are found, collect them all so no other person can open the locked place. When: A locked place can be opened by duplicate keys. Why: Leaving any copy available keeps someone's access open.
- [rule] Store each character's roles, such as witness, accused or confessor, on a case board and update them as events unfold.
- [rule] Include property records and lot boundaries as discoverable documents that let players confirm where a building or route actually was.
- [rule] When an NPC reveals an apparently impossible secret, give the world an ordinary source, such as a relative, rumor, or old record, that the player can find. Why: Letting players confirm the channel makes the secret feel earned rather than arbitrary.
- [rule] Give an accuser a private grievance the player can uncover through dialogue or a letter, so the motive can be deduced. When: Writing an accuser NPC. Why: A discoverable motive rewards careful investigation and supports a fair reveal.
- [rule] Give an NPC's anonymous note a phrase or piece of knowledge only that NPC has, so players can attribute the note to them. When: Designing anonymous messages in games.
- [rule] Notice boards and handbills can serve as a reward system, but an NPC who cannot read will not see them.
- [rule] Give players a clue log that records which NPCs could have overheard or learned a secret, so they can narrow the list of possible knowers.
- [rule] Give each NPC suspect status and motive data, so players can build their own roster and mark who needs watching. When: when designing a mystery with many characters.
- [rule] Record each evidence item with its origin, such as where it was found and who checked it. Why: Origin records let others verify how an item was obtained.
- [rule] Track which facts the player has confirmed, and reveal the answer only after every rejected route has been ruled out. When: designing a deduction puzzle. Why: Gated reveals keep the deduction satisfying.
- [rule] Store a player's collected knowledge in a container that survives character death, so progress is not lost. When: When knowledge is central to progression.
- [rule] Check the institution's own records first; if nothing is missing, drop the explanation that depends on it. When: a suspicion rests on an item having been taken. Why: A complete record can close a line of inquiry quickly.
- [rule] Mark key assumptions explicitly in the case file so that a later clue can invalidate them.
- [rule] Open several parallel objectives and attach a fallback path to each, so a failed line gives the player a named set of next steps. When: when designing a quest system.
- [rule] Track NPC inventories so that when an item disappears or an extra copy appears, the mismatch can serve as a clue. When: When designing systems where NPCs carry items. Why: Visible and consistent inventories let players deduce that something was swapped or removed.
- [rule] Provide a journal section where players record details that do not yet fit, and let them revisit it as the case develops. When: when players collect clues that resist current theories. Why: A dedicated log keeps unresolved facts available for later comparison.
- [heuristic] A journal or clue log that keeps the player's notes of key lines lets the clue survive after the physical object is burned, lost, or the world evidence vanishes. When: world evidence can disappear during play.
- [heuristic] A journal or notebook can record small separate observations or partial clues that the player must link into a chain or combine over time to narrow suspects or locations. When: designing deduction systems with fragmentary evidence.
- [heuristic] Let players pursue several parallel leads on a case board, tracking failed ones so remaining options stay visible and the lead that confirms a fact is revealed. When: An investigation has many possible leads running in parallel. Why: Most leads fail, and only one may reach the truth; listing failures narrows the open options.
- [heuristic] Crimes share recurring patterns, so matching a new scene against past cases can suggest which clues to search for. When: A scene resembles a known method and the investigator is expected to know history. Why: Shared patterns make past cases informative about new ones.
- [heuristic] Record whether each fact was volunteered or asked for, since the difference can itself be a clue about a character's motives. When: When tracking NPC dialogue in a game or case file. Why: Unprompted information can reveal what a character wants the player to believe.
- [heuristic] A deduction board where players rearrange clue cards can trigger a solved state when the right combination appears, giving a sudden realisation. When: Designing deduction interfaces for mystery content. Why: Rearrangeable clues let players discover patterns by manipulation rather than only by reading.
- [heuristic] Two quest threads can merge when the player identifies a common NPC with the required access to both, shown on a corkboard linking each NPC to open cases. When: Designing multi-case investigations. Why: A visible shared link rewards the player for connecting threads.
- [heuristic] Running two deadlines at once, such as a return before an absence is noticed, compounds pressure and forces trade-offs in planning. When: when a player faces more than one timer. Why: each timer competes for the same time.
- [heuristic] Let NPC doctors keep ledgers players can audit, so a missing or inconsistent entry becomes a clue. When: Designing medical or supply records in a game world. Why: Inconsistency in records gives players a checkable lead.
- [heuristic] A game opening can make a minor delay, such as a missed meeting or late call, look consequential to hook the player into the case.
- [procedure] 1) Have the witness restate the whole account from the start, even if part was already heard, including seemingly irrelevant details. 2) Let them pace it and include background. 3) Hold off on theories until all facts are given. When: When a witness account is the only starting point. Why: Complete detail is needed before patterns can be judged, and trimming can lose a small detail's link to a later clue.
- [procedure] 1) State what is established as fact. 2) Assess motive. 3) Link each physical trace to a possible route or person. 4) Test feasibility. 5) Weigh persons, labelling inferred points. When: no witness saw the act and the case rests on traces. Why: separating proven facts from inference prevents overclaiming.
- [procedure] 1) Ask each witness to recount the same event. 2) Compare accounts for gaps and contradictions. 3) Check the accounts against facts that surfaced later. When: several people shared one event. Why: Independent accounts can fill each other's gaps and expose errors a single source would hide.
- [procedure] 1) Gather the available facts. 2) Keep the few judged essential and set aside the rest. 3) Arrange the essential facts in order to rebuild the sequence of events. When: Witness statements, records and traces accumulate faster than they can be checked. Why: Sorting and ordering turn a pile of facts into a chain that explains what happened.
- [procedure] 1) Begin with the culprit's background and the hidden identity, confirmed by outside records. 2) Lay out motive and planning in order. 3) Explain each concealment as part of one design. 4) Explain the roles of others, including unwitting tools. 5) Admit unresolved gaps. 6) Return to ordinary matters to close. When: when explaining a solved case to someone who missed the key events.
- [procedure] 1) List each fact that implicates the suspect. 2) Ask what role the suspect could have played as a victim or intervener. 3) Reorder the timeline with that role reversed. 4) Check the physical record and planted items against the new sequence. When: A suspect's apparent guilt rests on placed or circumstantial evidence. Why: Reversing roles tests whether the same facts fit an innocent sequence.
- [procedure] 1) Write each open question. 2) Attach the best candidate answer. 3) Mark any candidate with no supporting evidence as having no answer. 4) Review the list for gaps and new leads.
- [procedure] 1) List known facts. 2) List open questions. 3) State each assumption. 4) Test each assumption against timing. 5) Ask which important questions have never been asked.
- [procedure] 1) Shake the blotter or pad to see whether anything else falls out. 2) Hold the sheet only by its corners. 3) Seal it in an envelope. 4) Keep it in your own custody, not left in the room. When: A loose sheet found among ordinary objects may be evidence. Why: Checking for other traces and limiting handling preserves the evidence.
- [procedure] 1) Examine a found object minutely, including linings, bands and hidden surfaces. 2) Look for makers' marks, initials or wear. 3) Check its fit against the suspect. 4) Compare it with a known original. When: when a found object may link a person to a crime. Why: Glancing at an object misses marks that tie it to an owner or source.
- [procedure] 1) List each candidate clue. 2) Ask whether it can be proven in court. 3) Record why each fails, such as being worthless, untraceable, missing or only showing motive. When: when assessing which clues can support a case.
- [metric] Track the ratio of verifiable facts an NPC gives to trust requests it makes; a low fact ratio flags emotionally scripted dialogue. When: when reviewing NPC dialogue. Why: Heavy trust requests with few checkable facts signal evasion or scripting.
- [pitfall] Collecting apparent slips and near-misses as evidence can inflate suspicion, since each one may have an innocent explanation. Why: Ambiguous behaviour is easy to over-read once a suspect is in view.
- [pitfall] A clue shown without any marking of its relevance is more likely to be ignored than read as a clue. Why: Players do not recognize unmarked objects as significant.

## concealment
- [mechanism] A hidden item placed inside a common, routinely handled object in a familiar spot passes inspection, since searchers focus on pockets, containers and surfaces and overlook everyday fittings. When: a hidden item must survive routine checks, or is swapped for an identical-looking object timed to a search. Why: Ordinary appearance and handling draw no suspicion, and a substitution timed to a search goes unnoticed. (x2)
- [mechanism] Destroying or hiding evidence changes what others can know, and the gap can be noticed, becoming a source of suspense and later clues, even when it protects a private matter.
- [mechanism] Each cleanup action, such as moving objects, wearing gloves or discarding items, leaves its own physical or contextual trace that an investigator can check. When: a culprit tries to remove or disorder evidence, or a staged or cleaned scene is designed or analysed. Why: Cleanup is itself a physical act; using an incriminating item to wipe creates another clue, and a cloth used to wipe prints carries smudges of its own.
- [mechanism] Burning or keeping a written note removes the paper trail, so the party holding it can deny or prove a claim depending on what survives. When: a meeting turns on a written record. Why: the surviving document decides who can prove what.
- [mechanism] Burning a message before it reaches its target stops it from arriving, so the recipient never learns its content and the sender's secret is kept.
- [mechanism] An NPC dialogue can run in stages: scripted identity lines, a free-form statement, then pressure lines unlocked by stored contradictions the player has found. When: Designing interrogation-style dialogue for a game NPC. Why: Staging controls information flow and rewards players who gather contradictions.
- [mechanism] Fragile seals on doors, windows and objects show whether a space was entered unobserved; an intact seal indicates no entry, a broken one indicates entry. When: A room is left unwatched and the player must learn whether anything passed through. Why: Each marker changes state only when disturbed, so its condition is a record of entry.
- [behavior] Layered concealments such as masks, hats and cloaks can make one person look like several people, and an alibi breaks once the disguise is identified. When: when one character changes outward signs between scenes, or a disguise mechanic is designed. Why: Observers track the visible figures rather than the single person behind them. (x2)
- [behavior] A person who finds incriminating writing may destroy it at once, before anyone else has time to consider it. When: a written record could expose them. Why: Destruction removes the record faster than analysis can.
- [behavior] A deliberately clumsy act makes noisy movement read as accident, so a noisy trip is less likely to draw suspicion. When: An investigator must move in a way that would otherwise seem suspicious. Why: Observers attribute clumsiness to carelessness rather than intent.
- [behavior] People often keep a secret from the person most affected, judging that knowing would only burden them.
- [behavior] A witness who loves a suspect may flatly deny a sighting or statement, so a denial alone does not establish guilt or innocence. When: A family member or close ally denies a claim in front of an investigator. Why: Loyalty and sympathy push people to shield those they care about.
- [behavior] A person may stay silent before authority when confessing a minor secret would cost them their livelihood, even if they are innocent of the main charge. When: An innocent suspect hides a lesser wrongdoing under questioning. Why: Fear of losing their position outweighs the cost of concealment.
- [behavior] An NPC who is hiding something may lie further or fall silent when pressed directly. When: when a concealing character is confronted. Why: pressure threatens what they are protecting.
- [behavior] Naming a witness's motive for silence, such as fear of a strict authority, can help break that silence. When: witness refuses to speak. Why: Acknowledging the fear removes part of its hold.
- [behavior] A personal object with sentimental value can lead its owner to act against their own interest, such as taking a risk to keep it. Why: Attachment can outweigh practical cost.
- [behavior] When confessing offers survival and refusing means execution, most people confess for self-preservation, so the confession says little about whether the charge is true. When: A confession is the price of escaping death or severe punishment. Why: The benefit of confessing outweighs any commitment to the truth of the statement.
- [behavior] Framing a forced choice as the other side's responsibility makes them feel in control of the outcome. When: An accuser presents an ultimatum to a family.
- [behavior] After a violent act, an offender tries to remove blood and clothing, then acts composed in public and says nothing about what happened. When: when a character must appear normal soon after committing violence. Why: to avoid suspicion before traces are found.
- [behavior] A guilty party removes items linked to a visitor from the scene and disposes of them at sea. When: when an offender wants to erase a visitor's connection to a place. Why: to remove evidence that someone else was there.
- [rule] In poisoning or hidden-ingredient puzzles, let a strong ingredient or drink mask the substance, so players must match taste or residue to the right ingredient. When: when designing food, drink or potion poisoning puzzles. Why: A taste mismatch or residue gives players a checkable, concrete deduction.
- [rule] Protect a witness with layered defences: an outside watch, a fireproof building, a watched and lit hall, a stout gate, and messengers who can summon help. When: a witness must be guarded against attack. Why: each layer covers a failure of another.
- [rule] Design the steps of a cleanup so that each one reveals something about the actor's knowledge. When: Designing concealment in a scene.
- [rule] Make a note or item whose destruction changes what other NPCs learn, so that the player's handling of evidence becomes a real choice. When: Designing evidence items that can be altered or destroyed. Why: Consequential choices give players meaningful control over information flow.
- [rule] Let rumours spread between locations in stages, adding detail at each retelling, so players can trace how a rumour grew. When: when designing rumour propagation.
- [rule] Let a convincing trace point toward the wrong suspect, and provide a later check, such as drying time, that lets players correct the reading. When: designing clue-based mysteries.
- [rule] Write hidden-item puzzles as explicit constraints, such as who was present, what could be moved and what went unobserved, so the player can derive the location. When: Designing a puzzle where the player must find a hidden object. Why: Stated constraints make the answer derivable through reasoning rather than guessing.
- [rule] Make control impressions from a known source using the same technique, so the method's own effects can be separated from the trace. When: when preparing blood or print samples.
- [rule] Give an NPC a reason to conceal a fact that protects a third character, and make that reason discoverable through another clue. When: Designing a suspect or witness who appears guilty. Why: Players must be able to find the hidden motive to resolve the apparent guilt.
- [rule] Offer a consistency check, such as a careful NPC's usual habits, so attentive players can detect staged evidence. When: designing planted clues. Why: A checkable standard lets players spot fakes.
- [rule] Use a gift or item as a misleading lead so the most dramatic thread is not the one that solves the case. When: designing false leads in a mystery. Why: Dramatic threads otherwise tend to reveal the solution too easily.
- [rule] Give players a way to explain or hide sudden wealth, such as a stated source or a hidden stash, so spending becomes a puzzle rather than a sure tell. When: designing a wealth-based detection system. Why: a counter-option keeps the check from being automatic.
- [heuristic] A missing or torn item within an otherwise complete or ordered set, such as an emptied frame, archive gap or torn leaf, suggests deliberate removal rather than loss. When: a gap or tear breaks an otherwise complete setting, archive or document. Why: A gap breaking a full pattern is unlikely to be chance, neighboring records survive in order, and binding or wear can reveal where a leaf was taken out. (x2)
- [heuristic] A scene that is too tidy, too complete or too flawless for its event is likely staged, since genuine events show uneven, hasty disorder shaped by chance and error. When: judging whether a disturbed or complete scene was staged or authentic. Why: Deliberate staging follows a plan that real stress does not, and a perfect match to a template suggests arrangement.
- [heuristic] Destroyed or broken objects leave readable fragments that reveal the original's purpose; when a clue is destroyed before examination, reason from surviving traces and log the loss. When: evidence has been burned or broken, or a clue can no longer be examined. Why: Destruction rarely removes every trace, and a fragment can still support or limit a conclusion.
- [heuristic] Inspect a decorated or molded container's outer form and weight for anomalies; decoration hides contents, but molded layers still reflect the interior's outline. When: an observer suspects a hidden item inside a sealed or decorated object. Why: Decoration is designed to mislead but seldom erases the underlying form or weight.
- [heuristic] Use plausible, mundane reasons for each removal, and do not rely on one removal alone; the effect comes from several removals in sequence. When: Staging the isolation of a witness. Why: Individually reasonable reasons avoid showing the device, while a sequence of removals builds suspicion.
- [heuristic] A pile of ash concentrated in one place may indicate a deliberate attempt to remove a document, so identify which remnants were destroyed and why. When: Ash or burned material is found in an unusual concentration. Why: Intentional destruction leaves a pattern that accidental burning does not.
- [heuristic] If a person who rarely leaves a place is removed from it, the removal was probably planned, and the plan points to what the removal enables.
- [heuristic] Show partial words with blanks as a staged reveal, so each solved word unlocks the next clue.
- [heuristic] A delayed reveal can withhold a theory until a final clue confirms it, while the supporting clues remain visible to the player throughout. When: Designing a puzzle whose answer is confirmed late. Why: Visible clues let the player feel they earned the answer when it arrives.
- [heuristic] A quest may stage an accident that seems natural, hiding a beneficiary whose records a player can uncover. When: designing a mystery quest. Why: Gives players a second layer beneath the obvious cause.
- [heuristic] Present a too-complete set of clues as a puzzle, where the player must notice that the evidence was staged. When: when designing a deduction puzzle with a false solution. Why: noticing staging rewards careful reasoning over accepting the obvious.
- [heuristic] A cover-up mechanic can let a villain pre-fill an official report, which the player later checks against physical evidence.
- [heuristic] Sort each disturbed object in a scene into those that fit the stated motive and those that contradict it; the contradicting items are usually the strongest lead. When: a scene shows many signs of disturbance and one explanation is accepted quickly. Why: Most disorder can match a single story, while a few anomalies point to a second story.
- [heuristic] Give a gang NPC a manufactured alibi that the player can break by checking a timeline, using a clock, a lit window or a witness's timing. When: Designing an investigation with an NPC who lies about their whereabouts. Why: A checkable timeline lets the player expose the lie through reasoning rather than luck.
- [heuristic] Vary the sensory cue, such as a hollow sound, an unexpected weight or a turning handle, so the clue is solvable without pixel-hunting. When: designing hidden items for players to discover. Why: Distinct physical cues let players solve the clue through interaction rather than visual scanning.
- [heuristic] Air moving through vents shows a hidden space is not fully sealed, so vents can point toward the space.
- [heuristic] Burying an object deep in mud slows recovery, but does not remove the trail of where it went. When: when an object is hidden in soft ground. Why: depth slows recovery but leaves a findable trail.
- [heuristic] A legitimate commercial enterprise gives a central figure a reason to be present and draws little notice from curious observers.
- [heuristic] Blend into a population by adopting a plausible costume, language, stock phrases and routine, so observers read the person as a local rather than an intruder. When: when passing through a populated area unchallenged.
- [heuristic] A witness's mistaken conclusion is part of the confusion, so a reconstruction should explain it rather than discard it. When: A witness reached a wrong conclusion about an event. Why: The misreading itself has a cause that the reconstruction can account for.
- [heuristic] A suspect's attempt to end their own life after arrest may be read as a sign of a secret they are determined to protect, though it is not proof of guilt. When: When a suspect's conduct after arrest is under consideration. Why: People may guard a secret more fiercely than their own life, but this is a generalization without established frequency.
- [heuristic] Ask whether a suspect was often short of money and how often they sought support, since repeated requests reveal ongoing pressure rather than a single debt. When: Assessing a suspect's financial motive. Why: Recurring need signals stronger pressure than one-off debt.
- [heuristic] A witness protecting someone may say less in a group, so a private meeting is often more productive.
- [heuristic] Secrecy in disposing of an item suggests the disposal is hard to undo. When: Reasoning about why a culprit hid or destroyed an object. Why: Hard-to-reverse disposal is what a careful culprit would choose.
- [procedure] 1) Sew a hanging loop inside the garment. 2) Suspend the object from the loop. 3) Hold its handle through a pocket so it does not swing or show. Concealed carry still ties up the hand and balance. When: A weapon must be carried through public space unseen. Why: Supporting the object from inside the garment keeps it hidden and frees the hand from holding its weight, though the carrier's body must still support and hide the item at the same time.
- [procedure] 1) Remove the top layer first and record each item's position. 2) Sift and record each layer so small clues are not lost. 3) Infer that items higher in a stack were placed later than those beneath them. When: When hidden evidence is stacked or sealed and placement order matters.
- [procedure] 1) List each object's claimed role in the event. 2) Check whether it could have been affected by the physical forces claimed. 3) Check whether the object's state fits the order of events claimed. 4) Flag any object that could not have been affected as described. When: testing whether a scene's details could have happened in the order claimed. Why: A single object that cannot match the claimed mechanism can expose the staging.
- [procedure] 1) Note deviations from routine, such as an object placed somewhere unusual. 2) Compare the suspect object with a familiar one of the same kind. 3) Test the object physically by handling, twisting, weighing or bending it to reveal hidden contents. When: A hidden item may be disguised as an ordinary belonging in a familiar place. Why: Concealment in common objects works because they are overlooked, so physical testing exposes what looks normal.
- [procedure] 1) Ask what the guilty party needs to do with an item and why. 2) Visit the location under the crime's conditions. 3) Count realistic hiding places. 4) Rule out places that offer none. When: Explaining where a concealed object could have gone. Why: Conditions like time of day change what a location actually offers.
- [tradeoff] Burned evidence may support only conjecture when the key item is gone, but a destroyed container does not make its contents unknowable; other tests and records can narrow possibilities. When: When the key item no longer exists. Why: Without the item, its contents cannot be confirmed or refuted, yet other tests can still narrow what it held.
- [tradeoff] Releasing NPC clues in stages gated by trust gives the player control over pacing, but the player must also decide what to keep private and what to share with allies. When: When designing NPC-held information in a game. Why: Gating information rewards relationship building while forcing secrecy choices.
- [pitfall] Culprits may plant, swap or reposition items, such as a suspect's garment or cleaned stain, to point at a false person, so a convincing marker alone does not settle identity. When: clues were left by someone who may be hiding, or a matching item implicates a suspect who denies being present. Why: Deliberately placed or altered items can imitate evidence of presence, and altering an object changes the story it tells about who handled it. (x2)
- [pitfall] Refusal to speak may come from loyalty, shame or fear rather than guilt, so it proves neither wrongdoing nor innocence; treat silence as a motive to probe. Why: A witness may be shielded by a rule or fear.
- [pitfall] A tidy or ordinary appearance is not proof of an untouched or genuine state, because restoration and staging are built to look natural and leave traces. When: judging whether an object, document or arranged scene was handled or genuine. Why: Restoration and staging target what is visible, so they rarely remove every chemical, sensory or positional trace.
- [pitfall] Destroying an object removes the chance to recheck it, so destructive examination belongs last.
- [pitfall] A concealed view is not necessarily unobserved; the watched party can notice the reflection and react to it.
- [pitfall] Overrating your own deception is as dangerous as underrating an opponent; assume they know more than they show and treat easy acceptance with suspicion. Why: an opponent may be letting you win a point on purpose.
- [pitfall] A reconstruction that is too tidy can hide the parts of the record that do not fit. When: Presenting a neat account of a confusing event. Why: Smoothing over contradictions removes evidence that could overturn the account.
- [pitfall] Dust and polish depend on storage conditions, and a single fresh item can have other causes; use surface clues to narrow a search rather than conclude from them. When: Interpreting wear or finish differences. Why: Environmental factors and innocent explanations can produce the same surface signs.
- [pitfall] Assuming a clever purpose where none exists leads to over-reading; the purpose must be checked against the possibility that the position was random. When: Interpreting a moved object's position. Why: Not every displacement serves a plan.
- [pitfall] A refusal to give full identity can read as evasion, even when a stated reason is given. When: a character withholds a name on a form. Why: incomplete information invites suspicion.
- [pitfall] Withholding information for too long can frustrate the player and cause them to disengage. When: Delaying answers in a mystery. Why: Players tolerate uncertainty only while they expect resolution.
- [pitfall] Declining an offer of shelter may reflect social manners rather than suspicion, so refusal alone should not be read as guilt. When: when a witness refuses an investigator's offer of help. Why: Social norms can drive refusal independently of guilt.
- [pitfall] A romantic or sentimental explanation can feel satisfying but fail against facts, so it should be checked before acceptance. When: Forming a theory about a character's motive. Why: Emotional narratives are persuasive and can override the evidence.
- [pitfall] A mark left in plain view can be noticed by other characters, so a hidden or less conspicuous placement helps keep the mark useful for the player. When: A player-placed or world marker is meant to remain private to the player. Why: Visible marks can be spotted and removed or exploited by others.
- [fact] Destroying a document does not erase its evidence: its burning can become a clue, and witnesses to its creation can still date it.
- [fact] Destroying a document that would support an innocent suspect is suspicious conduct but does not establish a crime. When: a suspect is seen destroying potentially helpful evidence. Why: The act is suspicious, but its cause must be checked separately.
- [fact] Hidden objects are often protected in layers: a decoy resembling the real item, a changed format, and a secondary hiding place.

## identity matching and resemblance
- [mechanism] An identity system can rate each identification by the number of confirming witnesses and by whether the mark is unique to one character. When: Designing identity or body puzzles with NPC identifications. Why: Credibility rises with independent confirmation and with rarity of the mark.
- [mechanism] Letting a player compare a mark against other records reveals whether another character shares it. When: Designing a forensic step in an identity puzzle. Why: A comparison exposes shared traits that would otherwise make an identification unreliable.
- [mechanism] A disguise can hide a mark that is revealed only through a targeted interaction, not through a generic inventory or scan check. When: Designing disguise systems in a game. Why: Targeted interaction exposes details that broad checks miss, rewarding focused player action.
- [behavior] A substitute body dressed in a target's clothing can pass for that person at first sight, because clothing is the main cue observers use. When: A false identity must look plausible initially. Why: Observers rely on clothing and general appearance before checking details.
- [behavior] Destroying the face removes recognition by features and forces identification through other traits. When: Identifying a body with a damaged face. Why: Facial features are the main means of recognition, so their loss shifts the burden to secondary evidence.
- [rule] A match to a common or near-identical object shows resemblance, not ownership; only a unique, precise mark settles whose it is. When: A trace or evidence object is matched to a common item. Why: Many people own identical items, so a match does not single out one owner. (x2)
- [rule] Judge a document by several features together; a single copyable feature, such as letterhead or stamp, can be forged alone. Why: A forger can defeat any one visible authentication check on its own.
- [rule] Make some identifying attributes or tags removable, such as a ring, hat or tag, so identification becomes a later task or a later sighting rules suspects in or out. When: Designing character descriptions or evidence whose identity must be established later. Why: Removable items add a separate solving step, and their later presence or absence resolves ambiguity.
- [rule] Avoid clues that single out one suspect when several share the trait, unless another clue removes the alternatives. When: Designing suspect clues. Why: Shared traits without a discriminating clue create ambiguity the player cannot resolve.
- [rule] Confirm a suspected identity with a second, independent source, such as an employer's confirmation or a second sample showing the same defects. When: A physical or descriptive match points to one person. Why: A single match can mislead; independent confirmation strengthens it.
- [rule] When a candidate almost fits, examine the single mismatch and explain it or re-check the object before treating the match as conclusive. When: an object fits a trace except at one point. Why: overall fit alone does not prove the match.
- [rule] Plant a resemblance between characters early and resolve it later; a shared physical trait can link people once it is noticed.
- [rule] Match distinctive profile features against NPCs the player meets, and trigger the next step only on a true match. When: When designing suspect identification in a game. Why: Distinctive features make a match meaningful rather than a coincidence.
- [rule] When deeds and physical boundaries disagree, check the feature against the records rather than treating the feature alone as proof of ownership. When: A physical feature suggests a boundary that the deeds do not show. Why: Features can move, and their names may be recent.
- [rule] Give objects consistent properties such as owner, location and state, so players can test a trace against the question it answers. When: designing clue objects.
- [rule] Let guards recognise a player by silhouette and outfit at range and by face only at close range, so disguises work at a distance. When: when designing disguise and stealth recognition.
- [rule] Treat a feeling of recognition as a direction for the next check, not as evidence.
- [rule] Build a recognition check where the player matches a damage detail from a witness's account to the object that was moved.
- [heuristic] Identify disguised or unknown people by several independent, hard-to-change traits such as teeth, hair colour or hand shape; one shared feature like a costume is weak evidence. When: Matching an unknown or disguised person to a description or earlier image. Why: Single features are shared by many people, while fixed traits are hard to fake; changing teeth alters the whole face. (x3)
- [heuristic] Shared family traits in writing, such as the same letter shape or a consistently missing dot, hint at a blood relationship the game never states outright. Why: Family mannerisms in a document suggest kinship.
- [heuristic] A print's size or shape from faint marks can rule a suspect out but cannot confirm identity; treat such a match as weak evidence. Why: Size and shape are shared traits, so they cannot establish a unique identity.
- [heuristic] Garments with distinctive workmanship, trim or stitching can link an item to a specific owner or household, narrowing the set of likely owners. When: Tracing a found item back to a person, or designing items that reveal their owners. Why: Individual construction details connect an object to its source.
- [heuristic] Before relying on a mark, habit or trace, check whether others could share it; common marks and residue identify little, and a match is strong only if unique. When: Using a mark, habit or trace as the sole means of identifying a person. Why: Shared traits do not single out one individual.
- [heuristic] Verify a suspected match by comparing the two objects directly rather than relying on resemblance.
- [heuristic] A trace-match puzzle can require the player to collect a sample and compare it with items in a character's room.
- [heuristic] When many facts point at one suspect, check whether they all trace back to one fact or one bias, since clustered coincidences can mislead. When: a case rests on many matching details. Why: coincidences with a hidden common cause look like independent proof.
- [heuristic] Matching signatures across incidents, such as the same poison type and similar delivery, suggest one author for those incidents.
- [heuristic] A distinctive mixed trace found on several objects links those objects to one shared location. Why: the same source material appears in each place.
- [heuristic] Smears or smudges where clear prints should be can come from gloves, and wearing gloves is itself a sign of concealment. When: A surface shows smears rather than clear prints. Why: Gloves explain the smears, but they also show the handler wanted to hide contact.
- [heuristic] Matching individual wear features across two prints, such as a missing nail or worn edges, suggests both came from the same footwear and so the same walker. When: Two or more prints are compared. Why: Individual wear marks are unlikely to repeat across different shoes.
- [heuristic] Give several NPCs matching traits so that players must test each suspect against a second clue rather than trust the first match.
- [heuristic] A single retained detail, such as shoes or a hidden item that belongs to the original person, can betray a swap. When: Checking whether a body or item is what it appears to be. Why: Small personal details often remain with the original owner and contradict the disguise.
- [heuristic] Identify a forgery by matching workmanship and lettering to a known maker's style, which requires the investigator to have studied that maker's output. When: Judging whether an object was made by a known artisan or counterfeiter. Why: Familiarity with a maker's habits reveals inconsistencies in a copy.
- [procedure] 1) Obtain a reference from a known living person. 2) Compare several distinctive traits point by point. 3) Judge kinship by the degree of match. When: When a body part or trace can be compared against a known reference.
- [procedure] 1) Describe the hidden person's distinctive features early. 2) Later present a picture or item. 3) Match the features point by point. 4) Reveal only after the main action ends. When: an identity must stay hidden until late but remain reachable.
- [procedure] 1) Ask fixed questions in order about where, when and under whom the claimant joined, and who ranks above that leader. 2) Check each answer against the group's known record. 3) Question the claimant directly about any written record of their past. When: a claim of membership must be checked. Why: Fixed questions tested against known facts are hard to fake.
- [procedure] 1) Take a reference print from the suspect's shoe. 2) Match it against prints in soft ground to trace the route. 3) Confirm the suspect's account against the track record. When: Soft ground such as wet sand or mud holds clear prints. Why: A physical track can confirm or contradict testimony about movement.
- [procedure] 1) Obtain a list of candidates from a register. 2) Compare each candidate against the witness's details, such as the month and setting. 3) Narrow the list by the details that match. When: When a shared name produces many possible matches. Why: Extra details filter false matches from a name-only search.
- [procedure] 1) Travel behind the target. 2) Stop when they stop. 3) Look at what they are looking at, to share their perspective.
- [procedure] Under gentle pressure, have the witness name everyone who could have had a noted feature, to narrow the candidates.
- [procedure] 1) Examine how the object was made, since its workmanship points to its materials and skills. 2) List every plausible acquisition route: buying, borrowing or stealing. 3) For each route, state the culprit assumptions it needs and test them. When: the object is common and has many possible sources.
- [procedure] 1) List the facts the crime and its traces establish. 2) Infer motive and drop options the facts do not support. 3) Infer knowledge, habits, social standing and skill. 4) Link separate acts by shared signatures. 5) Label each inference by strength and test it. When: when no suspect has been named.
- [procedure] 1) List every person who could have produced the trace. 2) Check each against known wounds or physical facts. 3) Drop sources that contradict the evidence and keep the rest open.
- [procedure] 1) List every item found on damaged remains. 2) Ask who else could have had access to each item. 3) Treat an item as identity only after that check. When: Remains are too damaged to recognize. Why: Items may be carried or planted, so access must be checked before trusting them.
- [metric] Compare a measured stride length with the length expected from the person's build and habits; a large shortfall, such as 19 inches against an expected 30 to 33, needs an explanation. When: Footprints are the physical trace being read. Why: An abnormal stride can indicate hurrying, limping or another unusual movement.
- [tradeoff] Object fit, such as size, is only a filter: borrowed or worn items can pass to anyone, so assuming one owner from size alone is a mistake. When: Using clothing or item size to identify a person. Why: Borrowing breaks the link between an object and its usual wearer.
- [pitfall] Treating one footprint or other common trait as decisive is a mistake; innocent people may share it, so any print-based claim needs independent evidence. When: A print is the main basis for accusing someone. Why: One print cannot distinguish a person from others with identical boots, and a single impression can be misattributed or misread.
- [pitfall] A family-resemblance explanation can account for a shared appearance, but a duplicated unchangeable detail, such as a matching dental filling, should defeat it. Why: Shared looks are easy to excuse; a precise matching trait is much harder to explain away.
- [pitfall] Family resemblance can lead a witness to attribute a sighting to the wrong relative.
- [pitfall] A similar scent is not the same scent; a strong rival odor can mask the trace and produce a false match. When: Comparing scents in close succession.
- [pitfall] A match on age, height and colouring can be wrong, and one mismatched item can eliminate a strong lead.
- [pitfall] Treating one memorable trait, such as dress or grooming, as evidence of innocence is a mistake. When: When judging a suspect by appearance. Why: Surface traits do not establish innocence.
- [pitfall] Resting a whole solution on one lucky object makes the outcome look accidental, since the answer would have been missed had that object not been found. When: Designing a case where one item carries most of the proof. Why: Chance-dependent discovery undermines the sense of deduction.
- [pitfall] Assuming who was wounded must be tested, because a blood trace may come from someone other than the suspect.
- [pitfall] A covert signal only works if the receiver already knows the code; a signaler who depends on a guess about receipt is taking a risk. When: when planning a signal between two actors. Why: Without a shared code the receiver cannot decode the cue.
- [pitfall] A checklist of identifiers that is too thin returns many common-name matches, which costs search time. When: Searching records with few identifying details. Why: Common names multiply candidates that must each be checked.
- [pitfall] Multiplying the odds of profile conditions only looks decisive if the conditions are independent, each equally likely by chance, and each genuinely required. When: Estimating how rare a suspect matching a profile is. Why: Correlated or estimated odds make the product misleading.
- [pitfall] A confident early identification steers the rest of a case, so later testimony is read through the wrong identity and the error spreads. When: An identity is fixed early in an investigation. Why: Witnesses and evidence are interpreted to fit the accepted identity.
- [pitfall] Several witnesses agreeing on the same effects can make a mistaken identification look corroborated. When: Multiple witnesses confirm a personal item. Why: Agreement on a shared item does not show that the item identifies the person.
- [fact] Many different categories share the same observable trait, so one trait cannot single out a specific category.
- [fact] A mask covering the face removes the usual identification, so later identification depends on clothing, build or a small visible feature. When: A character's face is hidden during a scene. Why: Partial features are the only evidence left to match later.
- [fact] Comparing traces can show that two match or that one lacks a known feature, but cannot by itself say who made a copy.
- [fact] A match between two trace samples shows that they share a source, not that a particular person left the trace. When: when linking a found trace to a suspect.
- [fact] People of similar height, colouring and facial hair can look alike from behind or in dim light, causing false identifications. When: Identifying someone at a distance or in poor lighting. Why: Shared general traits are enough to confuse an observer under poor viewing conditions.
- [fact] Personal effects such as clothing and jewellery are easy to swap or carry, so they prove identity less reliably than a face. When: Identifying a damaged body or remains. Why: Items can be transferred between people, unlike a face.
- [fact] A heard voice or a sound cue in darkness is weaker evidence than a seen face. When: Evaluating a witness account given in poor visibility. Why: Hearing and dim light give less reliable identification than direct sight.

## culprit profiling
- [definition] Offender profiling infers a likely mind from a crime's precision, pattern and apparent motive, then uses that profile to narrow the suspect group. When: There is no physical evidence yet and the crime's design hints at a state of mind.
- [mechanism] An offender's fear or haste shapes choices such as securing a tool, returning to the scene, or leaving items behind, and those choices create the traces left. When: an offender acts under strong emotion or time pressure. Why: state changes what the offender does, and what they do leaves evidence.
- [mechanism] An NPC with a second persona has different dialogue, access rights and routines, revealed when the player notices a mismatch in appearance or timing.
- [mechanism] Narrating the crime from the offender's viewpoint reveals his routine, plans and handling of objects to the audience before the investigator arrives. When: Designing a story where the audience observes the crime. Why: Shared offender knowledge lets the audience watch the mechanism operate before consequences appear.
- [mechanism] Track a character's resolve as a value that rises with shocks and falls without them, and let it decide whether a confession triggers. When: a confession depends on the character's state.
