- [behavior] A target bound by good manners feels unable to leave a conversation without seeming rude, so a diversion can keep them in place. When: when the target is polite and the other party controls the talk. Why: Leaving early would break courtesy, so the target stays.
- [rule] Give NPC equipment, wear, tan lines, posture, worn hands or old spectacles cues hinting at past occupation, such as soldier, sailor or miner, or at wealth, before any dialogue. When: writing item, body and NPC descriptions. Why: Cues let players infer character from small details.
- [rule] Present each profile marker as a sign narrowing the search, not an identification; never name a suspect on a profile alone, since suspicion without evidence does not justify acting. Why: Each marker alone is only a sign; a list of indicators is not proof.
- [rule] Filter suspects by fixed traits a disguise cannot change, such as height, build, scars or teeth, and give disguisable NPCs flexible traits such as hair or hats. When: Designing an NPC that can appear in disguise. Why: Players can only identify a disguised character if some features persist across appearances.
- [rule] Plant convincing false suspects, such as a hostile NPC with a personal motive or one with an innocent explanation, and reward players who verify rather than confront or accuse. When: designing suspects who look guilty. Why: Tests patience and judgement rather than reflex; verification rewards investigation over premature accusation.
- [rule] Make a culprit's post-event actions and choices, such as cleaning a weapon, relocking a door or avoiding one object but using another, checkable against the scene and included in reconstructions. When: When designing evidence of a culprit's actions. Why: Checkable traces let players reason about how a rational criminal would act.
- [rule] Give antagonist traps, enemy behavior and NPC crimes a consistent style from a stable trait profile, so players can infer the faction, personality or author behind them. When: When designing crimes committed by NPCs or enemy traps. Why: Consistent traits make a crime's style recognizable.
- [rule] Use a culprit profile as a checklist for non-player suspects, testing each suspect's behaviour against it.
- [rule] Build timed NPC events, such as guard rotations, so the player must compute whether a route or claimed location is physically possible. When: When designing NPC schedules that players can verify. Why: Timing constraints turn NPC claims into a checkable deduction.
- [rule] Give an NPC who heard nothing a stated sleep or distraction trait that players can verify.
- [rule] Design a fixated guard or detective NPC so the player has a test to check that suspicion, such as a trace check or a distance check on a witness. When: an NPC focuses on one suspect. Why: Giving the player a test lets them verify or refute the NPC's conclusion.
- [rule] When a scene search finds nothing, reason from a known offender's habits and methods, and question them or their associates rather than searching walls further. When: A scene has been searched without result and the offender has a recognizable style. Why: Habits point toward the person, while building searches follow old stories.
- [rule] Give NPCs personalities that colour their dialogue, such as a grumbler who volunteers useful timestamps, so players must read tone as well as content.
- [rule] Give first-time culprits only scene evidence, so players must read the room rather than match a known pattern. When: designing culprits with no history. Why: Without habits to match, the scene is the only route to the answer.
- [rule] Let an NPC's public speech and private letter disagree, and treat the gap as a clue to the NPC's real aim.
- [rule] Let players deduce an NPC's motive from behaviour before the full plot is revealed, so the reveal confirms a suspicion. Why: Earlier deduction makes later confirmation rewarding.
- [rule] Store each character's known events as data, so a dialogue can be flagged as true yet incomplete when it skips an event that character knows. When: when generating or validating dialogue. Why: Tracked knowledge lets a system detect omissions automatically.
- [rule] Before placing a proposed culprit at a scene, check that they could physically reach it under the level's own constraints. When: A generator places characters who must reach a scene.
- [rule] Give culprits a model of what the player is expected to find; a missed item should be an explainable exception the system can account for. When: when generating hidden items or evidence for culprit NPCs.
- [rule] Give a quest ending a choice to keep or release evidence, with consequences for NPCs who depend on the secret.
- [rule] Give culprits one distinctive feature, such as a ring or limp, so they can be separated from lookalike characters.
- [rule] Structure a culprit-finding puzzle around independent conditions such as room access, device knowledge, tool skill and item possession, each checkable through in-game evidence.
- [rule] Give a meticulous or short-sighted character a higher chance to notice fine details, so the search mechanic has a clear personality. When: Designing character traits that affect searching.
- [rule] Give each NPC a personality-based chance to react to provocation, such as raising an alarm, so players can plan around it.
- [rule] Give each NPC a want, a fear and a loyalty, and use them to predict its actions and decide which clues it reveals or hides.
- [rule] Let NPCs have reasons to accuse others to protect themselves, so accusation effects can chain across a settlement.
- [heuristic] End a deduction puzzle with a journal or summary listing the clues found, how they connect, and the conditions the culprit meets, so the solution feels earned. When: When a puzzle or mystery level ends with a solution. Why: Showing the chain confirms the player's reasoning.
- [heuristic] Restrict a suspect pool by local population so a general profile becomes usable. When: A broad profile is too general to act on. Why: Small communities make a general description specific enough to search.
- [heuristic] A recognisable profile, checked against reference data on known dimensions and armament, identifies a known vessel type quickly. When: when a known type is seen at a distance. Why: the profile matches a catalogued type.
- [heuristic] Give NPCs personal histories and quirks that open or close motive paths, and let players uncover them through conversation.
- [heuristic] A room whose contents contradict its owner's profile is a clue that may point to another occupant, a staged setting, or an unexpected interest. When: comparing a room with its owner's known character. Why: Possessions normally reflect the person who keeps them.
- [heuristic] A combat signature can narrow the list of suspects, but a designer should also plant a misleading assumption that only one strong enemy could have acted, to test the player's reasoning. When: designing an investigation where a combat method points to a suspect class. Why: A narrowing signature gives players a lead, while a decoy assumption checks whether they reason beyond it.
- [heuristic] Store motive as a discoverable record in the level, leaving positive evidence as the deciding factor.
- [heuristic] Make a rare trait of an item, such as an unusual feature of a worn object, the decisive proof linking it to one NPC.
- [heuristic] A generator can draw a crime's motif and staging from a library of known templates, then fill in characters and rooms around that motif. Why: templates give a coherent crime structure that can be dressed with new content.
- [heuristic] Give a stranger NPC an unusual entry route and a specific, checkable cover story, such as a tradesman or neighbour with a plausible reason. When: Designing an unexpected NPC who enters a restricted space. Why: A checkable story lets players test the NPC while the unusual route keeps them curious.
- [heuristic] Where a delivered item mostly reaches certain groups, the offender probably had direct access to them. This inference is weak when the method is widely available. Why: Broad availability means many people could have used the method.
- [heuristic] Ask a local specialist to fill gaps that general records cannot, since local variants such as slightly different schedules can change the answer. When: when reference data leaves a key fact unresolved. Why: local knowledge covers variations that general sources omit.
- [heuristic] In a social-deduction setting, NPCs whose choices under stakes reveal consistent tells can be used as evidence of character, provided their scripted outcomes are logged. When: designing NPC suspects in a table-based deduction minigame. Why: Logged reactions to scripted pressure let players compare behaviour against motive.
- [heuristic] Give each non-player character a timed routine so the player can rebuild a timeline from overheard remarks and visible movements. When: when designing NPC behavior for a mystery game. Why: Routines give players a predictable baseline against which to detect discrepancies.
- [heuristic] A uniform method across a body, repeated at every cut, points to one operator following one procedure rather than several people acting separately. When: when the same cut style appears on several parts. Why: consistency implies a single practice or plan.
- [heuristic] Rank suspects by who had both knowledge and opportunity, since a long undifferentiated list dilutes attention. When: A list of suspects has grown large. Why: Filtering on both criteria focuses investigation on the most plausible candidates.
- [procedure] 1) Decide whether the acts look senseless or precise and planned. 2) Build a psychological model only from established facts and the traits they imply. 3) Hold the model fixed. 4) Test each suspect against it, not shaping the profile to fit a favoured suspect, and check for special pleading. When: Narrowing suspects by temperament or building a profile from the method used. Why: Fitting a profile to a favoured suspect corrupts the test, as an investigator may bend the method's implications toward them.
- [procedure] 1) Describe the method: positions of victim and attacker, aim, choice of target, and how the scene was tidied. 2) Infer the culprit's temperament from it. 3) Eliminate suspects whose known character clashes with that temperament. 4) Check the remaining suspect against physical habits and facts. When: Narrowing a suspect list from how a crime was carried out. Why: Method reveals character, and character filters suspects before factual confirmation.
- [procedure] 1) Lay out the offender's actions in order. 2) Tie each step to an observable trace. 3) Model the offender's state of mind at each step. 4) Predict the next trace that should exist and where to find it. When: traces suggest a plausible sequence but the events are not yet confirmed. Why: ordered steps tied to traces give investigators concrete places to look.
- [procedure] 1) List what the object physically demands of its user, such as fit, pressure points, corrective strength and materials. 2) Convert fit into body measurements. 3) Look for signs that demand would leave on the body. 4) Mark each inference's confidence. When: a single personal item is the only evidence about its owner.
- [procedure] 1) Identify residue of the act, such as a blank wad. 2) Ask where a person would need to act to avoid being seen or heard. 3) Ask what the act needed in terms of safety and loss prevention. 4) Use the answers to infer the site and the actor. When: A scene shows signs of a deliberate arrangement and the actor must be identified. Why: A staged act leaves traces of the stager's practical needs.
- [procedure] 1) List the opponent's goals and constraints. 2) Identify the option it would find least suspicious. 3) Check that option first, before searching hidden spots. When: when a puzzle hinges on an intelligent opponent's choice. Why: a target is often placed where the opponent expects searchers not to look.
- [tradeoff] Profiles built from document conditions narrow suspects fast but may fit several people or be faked; treat them as provisional hypotheses, confirmed by physical, factual and disguise checks. When: Choosing between profile reasoning and factual checks, or drawing a broad conclusion from a suspect's profile. Why: Profiles are fast but can match several people, while facts identify one; some traits can be faked more easily than expected, so a broad inference can mislead.
- [tradeoff] Listing every beneficiary with gain widens the puzzle with several motive-bearing suspects, while a short beneficiary list keeps the pool manageable though motive stays common. When: generating a murder mystery or designing a murder scenario's suspect pool. Why: Several motive-bearing suspects widen the puzzle; a short, manageable list keeps the investigation focused.
- [tradeoff] Explicit NPC trait tables let dialogue claims that contradict stated traits be flagged, so players can catch lies without a hard fact; tables should be written independently of designer assumptions. When: Designing NPC dialogue that players must evaluate. Why: Behaviour then follows the data rather than designer expectations.
- [tradeoff] A partner who follows orders without understanding is easy to control but may misreport what was seen; a perceptive partner reports accurately but may challenge the player.
- [pitfall] Inferring offender type from conduct after arrest assumes a clean split between habitual and first-time offenders, an assumption that is often untested. When: When using post-arrest behavior to classify a suspect. Why: Offender categories may overlap, so the classification may be wrong.
- [pitfall] A dramatic figure or effect can invite a magical explanation that hides a plain human plan, so players may dismiss the simpler solution. When: when a puzzle centers on a striking figure or effect. Why: Visual drama pushes players toward supernatural interpretations.
- [pitfall] Revealing the whole case too early lets a suspect adjust their account before it is tested.
- [pitfall] Treating the heir with the most to gain as guilty without checking whether another person had the same access is an error. When: Building a case around inheritance motive. Why: Motive alone does not establish means and opportunity.
- [pitfall] Assuming a skilled or crude operator without checking the marks leads to wrong conclusions, since clean cuts and rough cuts each need evidence. When: when judging the skill behind a physical signature. Why: skill must be read from the marks, not assumed from the outcome.
- [fact] A first-time offender has no file, no known habits, and no contacts, so records and informers return nothing useful. When: the culprit has no criminal history. Why: Without a past, there is no background to match against.

## cipher puzzles
- [definition] A book cipher encodes a message as numbers giving page, line and word position in a reference book that sender and reader both hold.
- [definition] A numerical cipher assigns a number to each letter, so a message is recovered by mapping the numbers back to letters once the assignment is known.
- [mechanism] In a book cipher, common early words get small line numbers near page tops, while rare words need long searches and produce high line numbers. Why: word frequency and position in the source text determine the numbers used.
- [mechanism] Each book-cipher number is a coordinate: page, then column or chapter marker, then word position. Without the matching book the numbers carry no meaning.
- [behavior] A disputant facing a larger opposing crew may back off after counting forces, so the side with fewer men often yields without a full fight. When: when a physical land confrontation is about to begin. Why: the weaker side judges that continuing would be futile.
- [rule] Identify the shared key book before decoding, since a book cipher's numbers cannot be recovered alone; from outside, any book in the world could be the key. Why: the numbers only index a text the reader must possess.
- [rule] Make an in-world book or text the shared key for a numeric puzzle, placed where the player can find and carry it, and require the player to identify the edition. When: designing a book-cipher puzzle in a game world. Why: a findable key makes the puzzle solvable through play and interpretable; an unreachable key leaves it unsolvable.
- [rule] Build puzzles around an expected event that fails, such as a guard who never reports a theft; reward players who notice the gap, as the absence itself becomes the clue. When: designing clue-based puzzles or NPC-driven mysteries. Why: players reason from expectations, which engages more than simple searching for hidden items.
- [rule] Give faction NPCs a passphrase or gesture that only members answer correctly, so the player must earn trust to pass. When: Designing gated dialogue for a faction. Why: A secret response tests whether the player has learned the faction's signs.
- [rule] Disguise forbidden subjects with innocuous substitute words, so messages look ordinary to outsiders yet carry hidden meaning for insiders. When: messages must pass unnoticed by outsiders. Why: outsiders read only the surface wording.
- [rule] Use a short coded phrase when a contact must signal the investigator without alerting a target nearby. When: when the target must not know they are being watched.
- [rule] Design a puzzle cipher around an in-world object so only characters who saw its location could produce the message, making the answer a deduction.
- [rule] Use a wordplay signature as a hint players can decode. Why: decoding rewards attention.
- [rule] A generated list of absent NPCs with reasons can serve as an accounting puzzle, with each reason acting as a clue to narrow suspects. When: when a nobody-leaves rule is in force.
- [rule] Flag a note whose tone breaks its author's usual style so players can notice it. When: Designing notes or letters with author styles.
- [rule] Signal a hidden-message rule with a visible marker, because players who miss the marker cannot find the hidden content. When: When a clue is hidden within ordinary text.
- [rule] Let the player write or decode a coded message from memory as a mechanic, instead of reading it from a stored document.
- [rule] Provide a cipher's pattern through another clue, such as a cipher wheel or a repeated word, so the puzzle stays fair. When: When designing a cipher puzzle for players.
- [rule] Encode hidden coordinates in notes the player finds, and give them a chance to reread the note before acting on it. When: Designing puzzles that deliver information through written messages. Why: Rereading lets players catch misreadings, making the message's challenge fair.
- [rule] Send the cipher and the message separately, so that if one is lost the message is not exposed at once.
- [heuristic] A paper marker or the page a book falls open to shows what its recent reader was reading. When: inferring a reader's recent reading from a left book. Why: Pages stay pressed open after sustained handling.
- [heuristic] Spread a substitution alphabet across enough inscriptions to tally; make the commonest glyph and a repeated short sequence a learnable first-mapping hint, and let players record solved pairs. When: designing a symbol puzzle.
- [heuristic] Repeated or clustered numbers in a book cipher can reveal its structure to a careful reader who suspects the system. When: Analysing a suspicious sequence of numbers. Why: The encoding method leaves statistical traces in the number pattern.
- [heuristic] A coded note can be written in two styles, and the planner can be identified from blanks and pressure marks left in the text.
- [heuristic] Chain clues so a book found earlier supplies the key for a later encoded message.
- [heuristic] Settle the source language first; wordplay like a pun on a name can hint at it, and a wrong language misleads the letter ranking.
- [heuristic] Test cipher keys drawn from terms the story has made meaningful; arbitrary words from surrounding material rarely work. When: when guessing the key of a cipher. Why: a key tied to the story is more likely to be the one used.
- [heuristic] Make early cipher words easy and later ones harder, so a book-cipher puzzle teaches its scheme first and then tightens the search. When: designing a book-cipher puzzle for players. Why: an easy start teaches the method and rising difficulty sustains effort.
- [heuristic] Use readable label codes on objects as clues the player must match to the correct sample. Why: Codes give the player a concrete check that links objects to their source.
- [heuristic] A repeated name or a predictable reply supplies several letters at once and works well as a crib. When: a message has a likely known phrase. Why: one correct guess reveals many letters.
- [heuristic] Copy every new message, since each fresh sample adds letters to the solution. When: solving a cipher over several samples. Why: later samples confirm and extend earlier guesses.
- [heuristic] A column marker before a number implies a double-column printing, which narrows the type of book the key must be. When: when reading a book cipher's markers.
- [heuristic] Public notices such as classified ads can carry hidden messages; read their initials or codes as possible names and match them to pickup addresses. When: a notice or register may link to a person. Why: Coded messages hide in ordinary public channels.
- [heuristic] Release a cipher's solution by method, such as letter frequency and crowding of characters, so each step feels earned.
- [procedure] 1) Treat each distinct symbol as one letter. 2) Count symbol frequencies. 3) Assume the most frequent symbol stands for E, the language's most common letter. 4) Test repeated three-symbol groups ending in it as the most common short word. 5) Use likely words as cribs. 6) Rewrite messages with dots for unknown symbols. When: solving one-for-one letter substitution with a long message. Why: frequency and common words give the first mappings; cribs narrow the mapping step by step.
- [procedure] 1) Collect a long run of repeated public text, such as back issues of a paper, since partial collections hide the pattern. 2) Extract only the relevant column. 3) Isolate repeated marks such as initials. 4) Compare what each entry requests. 5) Sort entries into sets and review for a pattern. When: a clue is hidden in repeated public text. Why: a full run reveals a pattern that partial collections hide.
- [procedure] 1) Read the initials on a byline. 2) Match them against an independent source naming the author. 3) Replace a first guess that the source contradicts.
- [procedure] 1) Complete a partial word by trying letters in each gap until one reading fits. 2) Replace solved symbols with letters, leaving blanks. 3) Use the new partial words to reveal more letters. When: after several mappings are confirmed. Why: each confirmed word narrows the remaining candidates.
- [procedure] 1) Turn to the stated page. 2) Count to the first numbered word. 3) For each following number, count forward from the previous position. When: A numeric message is suspected to use a known key book.
- [procedure] 1) Write a notice that reads innocently to the general public. 2) Embed a detail that only the intended reader will recognise. 3) Publish it where the target is likely to see it. When: when a message must reach one hidden reader without alerting others. Why: a double-reading lure reaches the target while appearing harmless.
- [procedure] 1) Recount the disputed total to confirm the mismatch is real. 2) List the simple explanations, such as an earlier payout or theft. 3) Keep every explanation open until evidence decides. When: a counted sum falls short of a claimed amount.
- [procedure] 1) Find nulls and stop marks by repeated trial. 2) Choose a candidate key word linked to the story. 3) Map the key onto the number sequence. 4) Check that the full decoded text reads sensibly. When: when a numerical cipher resists direct reading. Why: nulls and stops must be known before the key can be tested.
- [procedure] 1) Split the number sequence into groups and check whether each group reads as three values. 2) Estimate message length from the group count. 3) Test the scheme on the first few groups before decoding the rest. When: numbers appear in grouped sets with no known text. Why: confirms a reference scheme before investing in the full decode.
- [procedure] 1) List books both sender and reader surely own. 2) Reject books with many editions or a small vocabulary. 3) Test each candidate's decoded words for sense, and reject any that yield nonsense.
- [tradeoff] A small word list keeps the key obscure but forces a clumsy, compressed message, leaving part of the meaning for the reader to infer.
- [pitfall] Anyone who identifies the shared book can read every message, so the choice of book is the weakest point of the cipher. When: A cipher relies on a shared book as its key. Why: The book itself is the key, so exposing it exposes all traffic.
- [pitfall] A static phrase passed along a chain of people is repeated and can be compromised by a single leak across the whole system. When: A password is shared through a group. Why: Repetition and transfer multiply the chance of leakage.
- [pitfall] A caption can claim more than its image shows, so check the picture itself before trusting the caption.
- [pitfall] Searching a very large set of candidate words can consume hours without success, since the true key may not be among them.
- [pitfall] A wrong key can produce partial sense, so any partial decoding needs a full check before it is accepted.
- [pitfall] Assuming a message is a cipher when its numbers could be sums of money or a plain list wastes decoding effort.
- [pitfall] A message of only a few symbols gives too little data, and several words may fit one pattern, so context must decide.
- [pitfall] Editions of the same book can differ in pagination, so a correct key may fail until the sender's printing is identified.
- [fact] The frequency-and-common-word method works on simple substitution ciphers; it does not guarantee solutions for harder cipher systems.
- [fact] Some people specialise in breaking ciphers, and a cipher that looks hopeless at first can yield to patience and structured analysis.
- [fact] A victim's private papers may be written in code that needs specialists to decode, so their contents form a separate evidence source. When: a victim left diaries or letters.
- [fact] In English E is the most common letter, but beyond E the frequency order is loose and can reverse in short samples. When: using frequency analysis on short messages. Why: small samples vary widely.
- [fact] Systematic symbol ciphers can be solved from samples, while a purely random code may be impossible to solve. When: judging whether a cipher is solvable. Why: structure gives patterns to exploit.

## clue visibility and salience
- [mechanism] A hint can unlock only after the player re-examines an item from a new angle, such as a different map view or a newly held inventory item. When: Gating a clue behind a change of perspective. Why: A changed viewpoint makes the hidden detail noticeable.
- [mechanism] When physical clues are thin, poor leads multiply, and investigators cannot invent evidence, so the case must be read from what exists.
- [mechanism] A found item, such as a recording device, can reinterpret an earlier sound clue, and a shop or ledger can then confirm the explanation.
- [mechanism] A stain's visibility depends on lighting, colour and the observer's suspicion, so dark or dirty surfaces hide marks from an unsuspecting observer.
- [mechanism] Fog or night that limits sight and sound forces players to piece events together from partial information. When: when a level restricts perception. Why: Missing senses make the player infer what happened.
- [behavior] When confessing brings visible suffering, others in the same position learn the cost of speaking, which shapes who will confess. When: Punishment or torment follows confession publicly. Why: Observers adjust their behavior to the observed cost.
- [behavior] A victim's own item found beside them can show they were acting in response to a threat, which shifts how the scene is read. When: When an object near a victim is interpreted as evidence of their intent. Why: The object reveals the victim's last action, which can point away from the intended suspect.
- [behavior] A hidden trace found by digging feels more convincing to people than one in plain view, because it appears as a secret that was uncovered. When: When a clue is concealed and then discovered by the player or an investigator. Why: Discovery of a concealed item creates a sense of uncovering a secret, which increases its perceived weight.
- [rule] Tag vision or ghost clues pointing to a suspect as unverifiable; they may raise suspicion but need physical or documentary corroboration before unlocking an arrest. When: designing clue gating in a mystery game. Why: keeps unverifiable testimony from driving a final accusation.
- [rule] Show the case as one stated question with listed constraints, visible sub-goal status and searched containers, so players see untested leads and the obstacle blocking progress. When: Designing a quest log or case board. Why: Visible status lets players track what must be proven and what is blocking them.
- [rule] End a suspense sequence on a visible reveal, such as a figure leaving the scene, so the player sees the answer rather than hearing an explanation.
- [rule] Give a suspect a visible, dangerous escape option, such as a hidden vial, so the accusation has an immediate consequence.
- [rule] A rescue that fails should have a cause the audience can see, so the failure does not read as author convenience. When: When designing a rescue that is stopped or interrupted. Why: Unexplained failure undermines trust in the story.
- [rule] Release clues as raw facts and keep the logical link between them for the player to make. When: when designing a deduction puzzle. Why: Players gain satisfaction from making the link themselves.
- [rule] During repeated questioning, have each answer add one new detail instead of revealing the full solution at once. When: when a player interrogates an NPC several times. Why: incremental detail rewards persistence and keeps each answer meaningful.
- [rule] Let a character withhold a clue until the player meets a condition, and make that condition visible to the player. When: gating information behind progress. Why: A visible condition tells the player what to do to unlock the clue.
- [rule] Make public supernatural acts visible to several NPCs at once, so a player's claim about them has independent witnesses. When: Designing a magic event that others must confirm. Why: Shared witnessing lets the player build a case that holds up to questioning.
- [rule] Tie a hidden surface clue, such as an older sigil under a fresh coat, to a skill or knowledge check so that an observant, knowledgeable player gains it. Why: rewards observation and expertise rather than chance.
- [rule] Release clues in instalments through a journal or news feed, holding the decisive finding for the final entry. When: When a game delivers story or clues through readable entries. Why: Staged release builds suspense and keeps the key finding for the climax.
- [rule] Give each kind of trace its own visibility rule, such as a lighting level or NPC suspicion state, so players must examine items in the right conditions.
- [rule] Give the player the same clues the investigator used, and do not withhold a clue needed to test the conclusion. When: designing a deduction mystery where the solution must be reachable. Why: a surprise the player could not predict feels unfair.
- [rule] Make some clues appear to confirm a player's assumption so that the player must test them before acting on them. When: When designing clues that point toward a conclusion. Why: Clues that seem to confirm a belief can mislead, so verification keeps the player from acting on an unchecked reading.
- [rule] Make tactile, olfactory and weight-based details reliable and distinguishable, so players who cannot rely on sight can still detect a fake or tampering. When: Designing clues for players who cannot rely on sight or the visual channel is limited. Why: Non-visual clues give access to the same information through other senses.
- [rule] Set out decisive physical clues at the start, such as size, sightline and residue, so the solution is fair even when the suspects seem unlikely.
- [heuristic] Test a clue's value rather than assuming it from its label or convenience; discarding inconvenient evidence misleads as much as trusting it blindly. When: Evaluating a lead marked as important. Why: Labels do not guarantee usefulness; confirmation bias favors whatever fits the current theory. (x2)
- [heuristic] A deeper inspection tool, such as a camera, can reveal traces hidden by cleaning or enemies that normal sight misses, rewarding thorough play. When: Designing a tool that exposes hidden content. Why: A specialized tool gives players a way to see what normal sight cannot.
- [heuristic] Players follow the most conspicuous element, so hide the real clue in a quiet element, such as a figure who waits unnoticed, forcing players to look past the decoy. When: Designing a scene with a decoy and a real lead. Why: Salient elements draw attention before quiet ones, so chasing the loudest element rewards careful observation.
- [heuristic] Hold back a clue that only one investigative frame reveals, so the player's choice changes what they can prove.
- [heuristic] A disguised NPC can be unmasked through visible seams, such as a pale line or loose hairpiece, that players are invited to notice. When: Designing an NPC whose true identity is hidden behind a disguise. Why: Fair visible clues let observant players deduce the identity before the reveal.
- [heuristic] Use visual signals across a gap when direct contact is unsafe; avoid them when signals are too fast or irregular to be counted reliably.
- [heuristic] Design item clues so their meaning depends on owner, location and time together; each one alone leaves the clue incomplete.
- [heuristic] An incidental witness detail can become decisive when checked against a later physical find; its worth depends on how it relates to the other evidence.
- [heuristic] A short unexplained gap in an otherwise complete alibi can be a clue the player is meant to notice. When: An alibi is otherwise complete and well witnessed. Why: A small gap is easy to overlook but breaks the whole alibi.
- [heuristic] Show witness statements pinned to clock times in a timeline view, with conflicts visibly highlighted, so the player can compare them. When: Players must reconstruct a sequence from several testimonies. Why: Spatial placement of times makes agreements and contradictions easy to see.
- [heuristic] Make trace markers visible enough that the player can find them; a clue nobody can locate does not help the player. When: Designing passive evidence markers for a player to inspect. Why: Markers the player cannot find deliver no information.
- [tradeoff] A clue placed too prominently is noticed by everyone and loses surprise; one hidden too well is unfair, while one too conspicuous gives the answer away.
- [tradeoff] A visible presence deters crime but forgoes catching it in the act; concealment does the reverse. When: Deterring crime matters more than catching it.
- [tradeoff] Hidden clues add mystery and search effort but change the puzzle; obvious clues keep the solution clear but give searchers nothing to find. When: Deciding whether a clue should be concealed.
- [tradeoff] A shuttered lantern lets the player briefly reveal details, trading visibility against attracting threats. When: Designing a light-based mechanic for stealth exploration. Why: Light reveals information but also signals the player's position.
- [pitfall] Dismissing routine records or items as unimportant can hide the real lead; keep dismissed items open and reassess them once new facts arrive. When: Reassessing items after the obvious leads have been checked. Why: Initial judgements may be revised when new facts arrive. (x2)
- [pitfall] Conspicuous investigative actions, such as visible protection, a loud canvass or an obvious grab, reveal interest, so suspects may change accounts, drop trails or lure the investigator. Why: Overt action reveals the investigator's interest. (x2)
- [pitfall] If every witness vanishes at once, the audience sees the device and stops trusting the story; the remaining watcher needs a reason to stay. When: Isolating characters for a plot beat. Why: Abrupt total removal looks contrived, and an unexplained absence of the remaining watcher invites doubt.
- [pitfall] Watchers track the visible route, so an unexpected visitor to a third party's rooms can expose the meeting. When: a discreet meeting depends on an alternate route. Why: An unusual visit draws attention even when the main route is watched.
- [pitfall] A false confession can collapse tension, so the case must still be presented as open.
- [pitfall] Dismissing each clue separately can hide the cumulative force of a pattern, and counting one fact under two descriptions falsely inflates confidence. When: Evaluating a set of related clues. Why: Each clue looks weak alone, and double-counted facts create an illusion of independent support.
- [pitfall] Ignoring unexplained anomalies until they undermine the whole chain causes a theory to collapse late and unexpectedly.
- [pitfall] Writing on the back of an item is easy to miss unless the player turns the item over. When: When a clue is printed on the reverse of a physical item. Why: Players inspect the visible face by default and skip the hidden side.
- [pitfall] Investigators miss a swapped object when the replacement looks identical to the original and they have no record of its normal appearance. When: Searching a room for a hidden item.
- [pitfall] A clue tied to the victim, such as the victim's own belongings, can mislead by suggesting an inward source when an outside actor was responsible. When: When evidence links an object or substance to the victim. Why: The victim's own possessions can explain some traces without implicating anyone else.
- [pitfall] A small visual detail can mislead an observer who has no reason to examine it closely, such as a partly visible object that appears complete. When: Presenting evidence where a key detail is easy to overlook. Why: Without a reason to inspect, observers accept appearances at face value.
- [fact] An object is more likely to be picked up where it can be seen than where it cannot, so its location must be judged against lighting. When: Interpreting where a physical clue was found.
- [fact] A small object concealed in an ordinary place can hold decisive evidence that goes unnoticed until a searcher knows where to look and what it opens.

## inventory tracking
- [definition] A field kit holds tools for four tasks: measuring and marking, lifting prints, collecting and testing samples, and packaging evidence, each item matched to its task.
- [definition] What counts as a weapon or tool depends on how it is used, not its label, so ordinary objects can serve as weapons.
- [mechanism] A known, complete count of items such as keys, torches, potions or relics, per storeroom or NPC, makes absence visible; a shortfall exposes who took or replaced it. When: Items can go missing or move during play, and every item in the group is recorded. Why: A recorded baseline makes absence noticeable and lets the gap be checked against the marked object.
- [mechanism] Changes in food or supply levels in a settlement, or food moving between two locations, can reveal that someone unseen is being fed or lives somewhere unexpected.
- [mechanism] A unique mark on an NPC, such as a scar or missing finger, can make identification reliable and fair for the player. When: Designing identifiable NPCs and traces. Why: A distinctive trait lets a clue be matched to one person.
- [mechanism] When tokens such as tickets are attached to the wrong item, owners can leave with each other's belongings, and the player can spot the wrong gloves or key. When: In a coat-check, locker or item-claim system. Why: The mismatch between expected and actual items exposes the error.
- [mechanism] An item disappearing from a locked container can signal that an NPC has entered the player's private space, changing how the player reads the situation.
- [mechanism] Let the player send an NPC or a servant network to fetch a fact without revealing why it matters.
- [mechanism] A dropped item can be stored in a container, moved by a cleaning NPC, and leave a partly erased clue, so the player must follow its history. When: Designing a world where objects are moved by NPCs and clues decay. Why: Following an object's movement gives the player a reason to trace the world rather than assume it stays put.
- [rule] Give a key item, or one of identical loot, keys or crates, a unique damage mark like a torn corner or scorch so players can follow it. Why: A unique mark lets players recognise the item when it reappears in another location.
- [rule] Track item and costume states, such as a torn sleeve, fresh ink, a changed costume or a stain, so changes become readable clues about recent events. When: When designing character appearance or item state as clues. Why: Persistent, visible state changes let players notice changes and reason about them as evidence.
- [rule] If a player may take an object from a room, track it in game state so that returning it on time affects the scene. When: When designing object pickup in investigation spaces. Why: Scene state should reflect what the player moved.
- [rule] Show when NPCs move or remove items, so players see that characters act on the world and can track what changed.
- [rule] Place misleading footprints or items so the player can test them against each character's inventory, rewarding inspection of equipment. When: Designing a planted clue that implicates an innocent character. Why: Testable misdirection rewards careful checking over acceptance.
- [rule] Give inventory letters and receipts links to addresses or NPCs so that they open new leads for the player.
- [rule] When a hidden item moves to an NPC, create a follow-up task where the player must persuade or trick that NPC to hand it over. When: Item transfers to a character. Why: Transfer converts recovery into a new goal.
- [rule] Author an unusually generous reward or odd condition deliberately as a signal that players are expected to question.
- [rule] Model inheritance and unsigned agreements as explicit game state so the player can see who receives property when the owner dies.
- [rule] Keep reconstruction results private until the full set of trials is complete. When: when testing a theory with repeated experiments. Why: premature conclusions can distort later trials.
- [rule] Prepare a fallback so that if the opponent probes further, you admit the smaller point and keep the larger one concealed.
- [rule] Give players a limited set of tests or tools, each revealing one clue, so they must choose which to try first. When: when designing investigation puzzles.
- [rule] Substitute a clean item before removing a suspect's used item, so the removed object is known to be the one they touched. When: A suspect's container or tool is to be collected for prints. Why: Without a substitute, the removed item could be confused with ones the suspect never handled.
- [rule] Model an investigator with skill checks for observation, psychology and empathy that feed a deduction system. When: Designing character stats for an investigation game. Why: Separating the faculties lets each one gate a different kind of clue or interaction.
- [rule] A field kit supports on-site work only and does not replace laboratory facilities.
- [rule] Give an offender a state that changes choices, such as panic after a near discovery, and show its effects in later clues. When: designing an antagonist whose behaviour the player must infer. Why: state-driven choices leave clues that connect to earlier events.
- [rule] Tampered objects can show a visible seal or disturbance that only a careful inspection reveals.
- [rule] Use a door or phone that changes state as a timed mechanic with a visible cause, since its state can reroute players and NPCs.
- [heuristic] Track an object's or resource's owner, acquisition and location history, such as through shops, banks and records, so its movement reveals when it moved and who had access. When: When an object or substance is meant to act as evidence about who had access to it. Why: Its history and location at a given time narrow who could have acted, so a tracked resource can be followed to find the culprit. (x2)
- [heuristic] Compare a witness's count of expected possessions with the recovered inventory; a mismatch usually signals a substitution, a removed object, or a missing record entry. When: When a witness reliably knows an owner's possessions. Why: Possessions are normally counted accurately, so a difference suggests something was swapped or taken.
- [heuristic] Hide valuables in mundane places, such as a fireplace or a stack of rubbish, to reward players who search thoroughly. When: Placing items for a search-based exploration game. Why: Searching in unlikely places rewards curiosity and thoroughness.
- [heuristic] An improvised extension, such as bracing a short ladder on one's own shoulders, can reach a target that the tool alone cannot. When: a tool falls short of the needed height.
- [heuristic] An inventory system can flag a missing item, such as a torn cloak, so a clue appears when the player checks every character's gear. When: Clues depend on items that should be present but are absent. Why: Checking all characters' items reveals the gap, so the clue surfaces only through thorough inspection.
- [heuristic] A single-copy quest item with no backup, such as a lone negative or record, creates meaningful stakes because losing it cannot be undone. When: Designing a quest where an item must be protected. Why: Irreplaceability makes the item a natural target for opponents.
- [heuristic] Match the timing of new cash inflows against when savings run out, since inflow timing can favour one explanation over another. When: Choosing between explanations for unexplained income. Why: Inflows that begin as reserves fall suggest a new source rather than old savings.
- [heuristic] Store footprint type, direction and overlap for each trace, so the player can reconstruct a route from the scene. When: Building a tracking mechanic. Why: Recorded direction and overlap let the player infer order of passage.
- [procedure] 1) Record how many units should exist, such as copies of a key, potion or weapon the game spawns. 2) Count units found later. 3) Treat any shortfall as a possible theft and compare it against who could reach the stock. When: A stock count is known, recorded and easy to check, such as when designing item distribution. Why: A known expected count makes a missing unit visible; a gap between spawned and found items can point to an NPC holding one.
- [procedure] 1) Take a written inventory, such as a laundry list. 2) Check each listed item against what is physically present. 3) Treat each mismatch as a missing or misreported object to investigate. When: a record lists objects that should be in a room. Why: a written record can disagree with the physical scene.
- [procedure] 1) Record the exact items the player starts with. 2) Record any agreed or requested items. 3) Classify each later pickup as allowed or a rule break against that list. When: a puzzle restricts what the player may bring or take. Why: A recorded baseline makes workarounds auditable.
- [procedure] 1) Agree a distinct signal before the mission. 2) Have the waiting partner watch for it. 3) On the signal, the partner comes to the rescue. When: when a split team must support each other.
- [procedure] 1) Empty each carried bag fully. 2) Read any letters for addresses. 3) Check clippings for links to advertisements. 4) Note cash and keys as signs of lodging and means. When: A person's carried bag is found at a scene or on a suspect. Why: Each item class points to a different lead: a place, a source, or a means of living.
- [metric] A field kit can be kept to about four by six inches and under two pounds, so it can be carried on every visit.
- [tradeoff] Limiting carried tools forces players to choose a loadout; each slot trades one investigation capability for another, with known capability gaps. When: When a portable toolset has restricted capability.
- [pitfall] An itemized list of found or lost objects records what was recorded, not what it means or who caused it; interpretation and identity need separate, stated evidence. When: When a list of found or missing items is compiled, such as a theft report.
- [pitfall] Assuming a stated key custody arrangement is complete ignores handovers that were never recorded.
- [pitfall] A copy count is meaningless when some copies were not tracked, because an unrecorded copy can explain a discrepancy. When: Using document counts as evidence. Why: Missing records make discrepancies ambiguous.
- [pitfall] Counts from a brief message are noisy, so a guess can look convincing yet be wrong.
- [pitfall] A single item may not prove the matter; its meaning depends on how it links to the others.
- [pitfall] Forgetting to restock single-use items such as envelopes and powder leaves the kit unable to package or lift evidence.
- [pitfall] Ignoring an alternative resource source, such as a nearby supplier, can make an agent's range look smaller than it really is. When: when estimating a movement limit from a resource. Why: Agents may refuel from places the estimate overlooked.
- [fact] An itemised list of expenses summed to one figure shows how much financial pressure a person is under. When: when a ledger or spending record is available.
- [fact] Itemized inventories of tools, papers and business shares reveal the daily trades and enterprises of their owner.
- [fact] Packaging tools are glassine envelopes, sealing wax and strong thread for sealing evidence.

## accusation
- [mechanism] A jury that cannot agree produces adjournment rather than acquittal or conviction, and the accused stays in custody until a later session.
- [mechanism] A suspect detained on a witness report of location can be cleared only by producing an alibi item or NPC testimony covering that window.
- [mechanism] An accomplice's confession can change which character the player suspects, shifting the investigation.
- [mechanism] Interrogation dialogue can branch on the accusation into denial, deflection or partial admission, and a stressed NPC may reveal a clue.
- [mechanism] An accusation mechanic where the player presents gathered evidence can trigger a suspect's confession when the correct item is chosen. When: the game has collectible clues and suspect characters. Why: Matching the right clue to the right suspect turns evidence into a dramatic payoff.
- [behavior] Investigators are cautious about arresting a prominent figure, because the arrest draws press attention and any error is costly. When: the suspect is socially prominent. Why: Public visibility raises the cost of mistakes.
- [behavior] People who loudly condemn the accused may later find themselves or their relatives accused in the same way.
- [rule] Gate accusations and confrontation behind enough proven clues; early wrong accusations fail with a visible cost, giving alternative explanations time to surface. When: when designing accusation or arrest mechanics. Why: Weakly supported accusations otherwise succeed by chance.
- [rule] Do not let an NPC's declaration of certainty trigger an ending; the final accusation should rest on several linked, recheckable clues the player found. When: designing a culprit reveal. Why: an accusation without proof would skip the player's investigation.
- [rule] Do not read a hung jury as proof of innocence; it shows only that the case failed to convince everyone.
- [rule] Stage a loud event such as a performance, toast or quarrel during which a second action goes unnoticed, then let the player return to find the change.
- [rule] Forbid hidden group members from going out alone or admitting visitors, to limit chances of exposure.
- [rule] Write an NPC confession with exact, checkable details while a guilt or fear motive colors the rest, so the player must cross-check it.
- [rule] Give the player an accusing note or rumor that names a checkable object such as a chest, ledger or grave, so they can confirm or dismiss it.
- [rule] Require at least two independent checks before the game accepts an accusation.
- [rule] Each object a confession explains must already exist in the generated level data, so the reveal recasts real content rather than adding new facts. When: when a villain's statement explains planted level objects. Why: Objects absent from the data cannot be fairly explained.
- [rule] Store deferred payment and reversion terms as data, so a faction can gain or lose a holding over time as conditions are met or missed.
- [rule] An accusation should fail if the player's evidence does not cover every required proof category. When: the game evaluates an accusation. Why: partial coverage leaves a weak link.
- [rule] Give confrontation visible costs, such as alerting other NPCs, so that patience and quiet observation have value.
- [rule] Require motive, means and opportunity as separate evidence items before a case file allows an accusation.
- [rule] Map each document requirement to a discoverable world state, so an accusation becomes justified once the player has verified the required facts.
- [rule] Show collected clues on a labelled board in the final accusation scene so the player sees the full chain before naming a culprit.
- [rule] Use an enforcement timer to force the final accusation, and have the accusation screen list the reasons in order. When: when designing a final accusation mechanic. Why: Timed pressure and ordered reasons support a decisive climax.
- [rule] An ending can present a mock tribunal in which earlier choices and gathered evidence determine the verdict. When: designing a moral resolution. Why: the verdict reflects the player's own investigation.
- [rule] A threat is credible only when the actor can back it up, so bluffing without the means to act undermines control. When: Making a threat in a standoff. Why: Opponents test bluffs, and an unsupported threat collapses.
- [heuristic] Include an honest-looking suspect with hidden financial pressure so that checking motive is meaningful.
- [heuristic] Keep suspicions hidden while questioning so the witness does not adjust their story to fit what the investigator seems to expect.
- [heuristic] An action that seems pointless often makes sense if a hidden party exists whose presence would change the outcome. When: an act appears to have no purpose. Why: an unknown party can give the act a reason.
- [heuristic] Let a confrontation scene change an NPC's trust or hostility according to which evidence the player shows, so the same scene yields different outcomes. When: When designing an NPC dialogue encounter driven by player-chosen evidence. Why: Evidence-dependent reactions make the player's choice of proof meaningful.
- [heuristic] Make the accuser share the outcome of an ordeal, so players see the risk and weigh the accusation more carefully.
- [heuristic] Design an accusation puzzle where the player builds a checklist from clues and accepts only the suspect matching every point, rewarding thorough work.
- [heuristic] When evidence is thin, keep several plausible suspects in view and wait for a focusing clue rather than acting on a weak guess. When: evidence cannot yet single out one suspect. Why: Acting early on a weak guess risks the wrong target.
- [heuristic] Use a mock trial or accusation as a mid-story turn rather than as the final solution. When: the reveal should redirect the investigation. Why: a turn keeps the question open for later resolution.
- [procedure] 1) Infer the offender's traits from the method of the crime. 2) List every trait as a required point. 3) Accept a suspect only if all points match; a partial fit excludes that suspect. When: Deducing a culprit from the traces of a crime. Why: Requiring full agreement avoids accepting a plausible but wrong suspect.
- [procedure] 1) Record each arrest or verdict as a node. 2) Link each node to the accusers who named it. 3) Trace the graph back to the first accuser to show how the chain began. When: when designing an accusation the player must reconstruct. Why: A visible graph lets players see which first accusation started the chain.
- [pitfall] Quiet stretches read as lost time or stalling unless ongoing progress is signaled, such as a clue or hint; silence without forward signs loses audience attention. When: a narrative slows after a burst of events, or a long investigation is paced without clues. Why: Without any forward signal, waiting reads as stalling rather than building tension; silence without signs of progress loses momentum.
- [pitfall] A hung result can drift into a conviction without a fresh, clearly authorized decision, so the case must be formally reopened. When: when a jury has not reached a verdict but the case continues. Why: informal continuation can convert an undecided case into a conviction.
- [pitfall] A verdict reached quickly after a long list of accusations may reflect momentum rather than careful checking of each claim. When: Many accusations are heard in a short proceeding. Why: Accumulated pressure can outpace individual evaluation.
- [pitfall] Circumstantial suspicion, pressed by an emphatic summing-up, can settle a verdict before the facts support it. When: A verdict rests on circumstantial evidence and strong framing. Why: Emphatic framing shortens deliberation and can override thin support.
- [pitfall] A single loud remark can break a productive silence and end the exchange before the witness says more.
- [pitfall] A quick recovery from an interviewer's slip can hide the error from both the interviewer and the record.
- [pitfall] A coherent account can hide its weakest link, and the most important explanation is often the one left incomplete.
- [pitfall] Treating the loudest account as the most credible is a mistake; separate the firm facts from the noise before judging.
- [pitfall] The most vocal accuser is not necessarily the one who started a chain of accusations.
- [pitfall] A gambit works only if the target can see the trap; hidden framing may be read as honest advice. When: When designing manipulative advice for a character. Why: The target must perceive the framing for the trap to bite.
- [pitfall] Each new accusation looks stronger because it follows others, even when the earlier ones were weak. When: when a case is built on a sequence of related charges. Why: Accumulated accusations create an appearance of corroboration.
- [fact] Mass accusations resting on sight alone can yield many confessions and arrests, and most of those tried may later be cleared.

## reputation
- [mechanism] A village reputation system in which a refused trade raises suspicion among other NPCs, spreading through their dialogue, lets player actions shape community attitudes. When: when designing NPC reputation that travels between characters. Why: Small social slights can propagate into broad distrust.
- [mechanism] Model a town reputation value that raises suspicion on an NPC; only past a threshold does the NPC move to a magistrate for review. When: Designing suspicion systems for town NPCs. Why: A threshold keeps rumor from escalating without limit.
- [mechanism] A public act such as breaking a seal in front of NPCs can serve as the reveal event and change the world state.
- [mechanism] A reputation system can reward open actions and penalise covert ones, so player standing depends on how investigation is conducted. When: Designing a detective's reputation or morality meter. Why: Social standing responds to the visibility of a character's methods.
- [mechanism] An NPC who helped condemn someone and then confesses publicly can unlock new quest paths or change faction standing. When: a town event lets the NPC speak publicly. Why: Public admission alters how other characters and factions treat the NPC and the wronged party.
- [mechanism] Using force against an NPC in front of others can lower the player's reputation, since onlookers may frame the player as the aggressor. When: Violence is used in view of witnesses. Why: Observers judge who started the conflict, not only who was right.
- [mechanism] After cleaning up and changing appearance, a player who has been seen can move openly without drawing a second look from NPCs. When: after being seen by NPCs. Why: a changed appearance lowers suspicion.
- [mechanism] An NPC's suspicion can rise with the player's visible agitation and fall when the player shows resolve or sudden irritation.
- [mechanism] Gossip flags can persist after a player disproves them, so one new fact should shift trust only partly and repeated proof should be required. When: designing NPC reputation systems. Why: slow updating matches how entrenched opinions resist evidence.
- [mechanism] A system where a false accusation costs the player money, reputation or an ally makes wrongful suspicion a consequential choice. When: designing consequences for player accusations. Why: Costs attached to accusation discourage careless blame and reward careful evidence gathering.
- [mechanism] A reputation flag set by an accusation can be cleared by a later repair event, and the cleared state changes which factions accept the player. When: a player is accused by a faction and later cleared. Why: faction standing is tied to the flag state.
- [behavior] Observers side with a well-regarded or officially standing person when accused, since reputation makes the accusation seem implausible, leaving the accuser few supporters. When: the accused is well-regarded or holds official standing in the community. Why: Reputation biases judgment before evidence is weighed; public standing favours established authority. (x2)
- [behavior] A reputation for discretion can make a character both trusted with a secret task and unlikely to be suspected of wrongdoing. When: a respected figure is asked to carry something. Why: Reputation lowers suspicion and raises trust.
- [behavior] Witnesses of good standing can make a legend seem credible, so their reputation stands in for checkable detail. When: A tradition rests on a respected witness's account. Why: Audiences accept a trusted reporter's word in place of verifiable specifics.
- [behavior] A single unfavorable feature, observed in passing, can fix a lasting reputation for a person among observers. When: When a neighbor or passerby forms a lasting judgment from one visible trait. Why: A striking feature overshadows the person's wider conduct.
- [behavior] Wary opponents suspect a decoy and may change the meeting, so a sting can fail in a way the player must notice. When: the opponent is cautious and expects a trap. Why: a suspected set-up raises the cost of walking into it.
- [behavior] A neighbour's long-standing reputation for an unkind remark can turn into a charge once a misfortune follows, even without proven fault. When: a bad event happens after a past dispute. Why: People link misfortune to earlier words and old reputation.
- [rule] Do not drop or clear a suspect because of a trustworthy reputation or fame; a respected person's motive must still be considered, since people routinely conceal real conduct. Why: Reluctance to suspect the familiar lets many crimes go undetected.
- [rule] Model suspicion as a spreading state over a network of grudges and property ties, where each accusation raises the chance connected neighbours accuse too.
- [rule] Make reputation a gameplay variable, so a trusted NPC can carry an item past guards who would search a stranger. When: designing stealth or social checkpoints. Why: Reputation changes what the player can get away with.
- [rule] Make an NPC's stated request a cover for a final question about timing or access, so players must notice the real aim. When: designing NPC dialogue with hidden purpose.
- [rule] A long record of escaping conviction can itself serve as evidence of a group's reputation, though it does not prove any specific act. When: Judging a group's reputation from its legal history. Why: Repeated acquittals signal influence or intimidation, not guilt in a given case.
- [rule] Have a quest giver exploit the player's reputation or identity in a scheme, so a later reversal changes the player's role.
- [rule] Store each rumour with its source and a reliability tag, flagging secret or untraceable reports as unverified. When: Designing a reputation or rumour system. Why: Recording source and reliability lets the player weigh gossip rather than treat it as fact.
- [rule] Tie restitution in a game to who petitioned, leaving unnamed victims without a remedy unless players find them.
- [rule] Record public confrontations in game state so that later NPCs can reference the player's reputation.
- [rule] Show the public record openly, such as completed quests, donations, and reputation, and keep the private deed in a hidden flag that a later scene reveals.
- [rule] Give an avoidant NPC a hidden suspicion value, and let the player's questions raise how often the NPC deflects. When: designing an NPC who avoids a topic. Why: the avoidance then reflects the player's line of questioning.
- [rule] Store an accuser's suspicion as a weighted value that drops when contrary evidence appears, instead of locking it when an arrest is made. When: Designing AI suspicion or accusation systems.
- [heuristic] Reputation may seed suspicion or serve as flavour only; require physical evidence before the player forms a firm conclusion. When: designing suspicion mechanics in a game. Why: Reputation suggests where to look; evidence decides.
- [heuristic] Give a trusted NPC a reputation bonus that delays early arrest, and show the player when that protection is lost.
- [heuristic] An apology or restitution can serve as a second layer of misdirection, making the guilty party look reasonable. When: Designing a culprit who must appear trustworthy. Why: Visible good conduct lowers suspicion.
- [heuristic] Show a public reputation in one place and a hidden lifestyle in another, so players must connect them through records. When: when designing a character with a concealed second life.
- [heuristic] Record faction actions and reputation as visible facts so players can build a case from what a group did rather than from a confession. Why: Observable recorded actions support inference more than stated claims.
- [heuristic] Give extra care to people with unblemished reputations, since an accusation can damage them before any finding is made.
- [heuristic] Introductions and reputation can act as keys to escape, so a player with the right contacts has an easier way out. When: when designing escape or release options. Why: Social access rewards player relationships.
- [heuristic] A respected figure can hide wrongdoing; check their claimed routines against records, especially activity kept out of public view, rather than trusting reputation. When: A reputable person is named as a suspect. Why: Respectability does not establish honesty, and concealment can hide wrongdoing.
- [procedure] Reputation, manner, standing or reported accusation is not evidence, nor the main filter: a bad record is not proof of guilt, a good one not proof of truth, though it shapes how claims are read. Check each claim against underlying documents and a verifiable link to the act; substitution often goes unnoticed. Accusations can prove false. (x2)
- [pitfall] Reticence, distant manners, unusual behaviour, sour looks or a difficult temper are not evidence of guilt; accusers may cite them as motive, so judge motive and opportunity separately. When: a difficult manner, demeanour or unusual behaviour is cited as grounds for suspicion. Why: Temperament is not evidence of action or motive; reputation gets confused with evidence. (x2)
- [pitfall] Judging witnesses by reputation can mislead as easily as it helps: a poor reputation can decide the outcome even when the witness saw something real. (x2)
- [pitfall] Treating a strong suspicion built on an accuser's word and a suspect's bad reputation as if it were proof mistakes suspicion for evidence.
- [pitfall] A profile built on reputation can harden into a fixed belief that filters out evidence contrary to it. When: Profiling an offender from their reputation.
- [pitfall] Reputation can carry an accusation forward without new evidence, so the reputation itself becomes the charge. When: An accusation continues after its original evidence weakens.
- [pitfall] A bad reputation can point to too many suspects at once, which dilutes the case; narrow the field by linking each motive to the crime. When: a victim had many enemies. Why: unconnected misdeeds do not identify the culprit.
- [pitfall] A suspect's reputation can bias how a physical clue is read, making a weak clue seem to confirm what people expect.
- [pitfall] Reputation protected some accused people but not others, so it cannot be relied on as a defence against accusation.
- [pitfall] Trusting a respected figure's appearance or word can lead players to wrong conclusions, because a deceiver can imitate trusted people and engineer how they look. When: when a character's reputation or look is used as evidence of innocence or guilt. Why: Appearance can be manipulated, so visible identity may not match what is real.

## print and fingerprint evidence
- [definition] Fingerprint comparison matches a scene print against a known sample from a suspect, detail by detail, and expresses the strength of the match as a probability.
- [definition] A latent print is an invisible fingerprint left on a surface, which can be made visible with fine powder and compared to a known print by ridge features.
- [mechanism] Gating print recovery by surface type, prior handling, and item type makes the same dusting action yield different outcomes and directs the player's search. When: in investigation mechanics; when designing a forensic interaction. Why: Restricting prints to specific objects directs the player's search.
- [mechanism] Stored print records let a player compare a mark with suspects and rule characters in or out, narrowing the suspect list. When: in investigation mechanics.
- [rule] To judge a footprint's size, press your own foot into it and compare the fit; no tools are needed.
- [rule] Set aside older and smaller impressions made by other hands before matching, so they do not produce false pairings. When: when matching prints from a crowded scene. Why: Overlapping or irrelevant prints can mislead the comparison.
- [rule] Collect prints from a potential suspect only with a clear reason and the subject's knowledge, since unexplained collection can alert them or confuse the sets. When: A person is a potential suspect. Why: Unexplained collection tips off the suspect and muddles the records.
- [rule] A print shows that someone handled an object at some time, not that the person committed the crime.
- [heuristic] Prints of two different sizes in the same soft surface, such as footprints near a moved victim, show that two people passed the spot or moved them. When: Several prints of different sizes appear at one scene. Why: Each person leaves prints of their own foot size.
- [heuristic] Print shape can rule out familiar animals, but a new creature may fit a known family while being larger than any known member.
- [heuristic] Printed capitals in place of handwriting hide the writer, but a torn word or an unnatural word form can still betray the disguise.
- [heuristic] Fingerprints are most usable on smooth, undisturbed surfaces; frequently handled surfaces carry overlapping prints that obscure each other. When: when choosing where to look for prints. Why: Undisturbed surfaces keep individual impressions distinct.
- [heuristic] Marks left by a previous reader, such as underlined words, can identify the correct copy of a key text.
- [heuristic] If latent marks may be disturbed by brushing, photograph them before brushing. When: Marks are fragile.
- [heuristic] Build a fingerprint puzzle as a chain of choices: powder, brush, photograph, then feature match, so the player practises each step.
- [heuristic] Identifying marks such as indelible initials on recovered items can link them to a person, but must be checked against aliases before naming someone. When: Recovered items carry names, initials or stamps. Why: Initials can point to the wrong person when an alias is in use.
- [procedure] 1) Lift prints from the scene. 2) Compare them against prints on file from known people. 3) Accept a match only when a known record identifies the print. When: Physical evidence must be tied to a specific person. Why: A print is identifiable only through a comparison with a known record.
- [procedure] Compare lifted prints against all elimination sets; prints matching no known person are candidates for an unknown visitor. When: Prints have been lifted from an object and elimination sets are on file. Why: Unmatched prints point to someone not yet accounted for.
- [procedure] 1) Trace a print onto paper. 2) Cut out the outline as a pattern. 3) Compare the pattern against other prints to test for a match. When: Matching footwear prints to a candidate's shoes. Why: A physical pattern lets prints be compared consistently.
- [procedure] 1) Place a reversed negative of a genuine print over light-sensitive gelatine. 2) Expose to light, which hardens the gelatine under the ridges. 3) Wash in hot water to dissolve unhardened gelatine, leaving the ridges in relief. When: A genuine print is available to copy. Why: Hardened and unhardened areas leave a raised copy of the original pattern.
- [procedure] 1) Dust a hard, smooth, non-porous surface with fine powder. 2) Brush gently with a soft brush to raise the marks. 3) Photograph them, placing dark paper behind glass for contrast. 4) Transfer the print to paper, dust both sides, and shake off loose powder. When: Recovering prints from smooth surfaces.
- [procedure] 1) Classify the pattern type, such as whorl or loop. 2) Count the ridges. 3) Note bifurcating ridges and scars. 4) Score how many features match the known print. When: Comparing a latent print to a known print.
- [procedure] 1) Dust surfaces with powder to bring out prints. 2) Photograph each print under controlled light and exposure. 3) Sort prints by likely owner. When: prints may be faint or on a shared surface. Why: Controlled photographs preserve detail for later comparison.
- [procedure] 1) Dust a fine powder over faint marks and brush gently. 2) Use printer's ink on softer surfaces such as wax. 3) Photograph the marks before further handling. 4) Compare the recovered marks against inked impressions of each candidate. When: Faint marks are left on objects and a suspect pool is known. Why: Development and comparison includes or excludes each person in the pool.
- [pitfall] Spelling in a printed document may reflect the printer rather than the writer, so it is weak evidence about the author alone. When: Attributing a document's authorship from its spelling. Why: Typesetting conventions can alter the writer's spelling.
- [pitfall] A near-complete match on a partial or smeared print is not automatically conclusive, because fewer comparable points remain.
- [pitfall] A claim that two people share identical traces should be tested, not assumed, before it is used as evidence. When: Someone asserts that two individuals' physical traces match. Why: An unverified similarity claim can mislead a comparison.
- [pitfall] Claims that prints quickly identify culprits overstate the case, because comparison can require substantial time and skill. When: Relying on fingerprint evidence for quick resolution. Why: Careful comparison is slow and needs practice.
- [fact] A print shows someone touched an object at an unknown time, so it may predate or postdate the event and cannot alone place them there. When: Using prints to establish presence. Why: Prints record contact, not timing.
- [fact] Writing pressed on a top sheet leaves an impression on sheets beneath, so a blotting pad can hold a reversed copy of the message. When: a message was written over other sheets in the scene. Why: pressure transfers the writing to lower layers.
- [fact] A print's depth and shape, such as a blurred toe or a deeply sunk heel, indicate the walker's pace and how weight was carried. When: Reading individual prints in soft ground. Why: Weight and pace change how the foot sinks.
- [fact] Experts may treat a fingerprint match as equivalent to an eyewitness account, though it carries different reliability.
- [fact] A printed witness statement looks more complete than the questioning that produced it, because the back-and-forth is removed.
- [fact] Examiners often followed a printed guide that listed signs of guilt and a rule for separating them from ordinary blemishes, so the guide shapes what counts as a finding.
- [fact] Many footprints are identical, so tracking by footprints alone is a primitive method for identifying a person. When: Relying on boots or shoes to identify a person. Why: Common footwear produces indistinguishable prints across many people.
- [fact] An inked relief pressed onto paper can reproduce even small pores, so the copy can look indistinguishable from the original.
- [fact] Print-lifting tools are two colors of fingerprint powder, brushes and a stamping pad for different surfaces.
- [fact] A recovered print identifies no one unless it can be compared with a stored record, so clear prints are useful only when a reference set exists.

## npc trust and third-party sources
- [mechanism] A third person present in a conversation prevents two people who know each other from speaking freely. When: Two acquainted people speak in front of a witness. Why: Their guarded speech changes when someone else can overhear.
- [mechanism] Evidence held by a third party under conditions makes the object itself a source of suspense, since its release depends on who asks and how.
- [mechanism] Negotiation conducted through formal written notes between intermediaries tends to stall, because each side refers the matter back to its principal before answering.
- [mechanism] A witness told to omit something can reveal it later once the player builds trust or removes pressure, rewarding return visits. When: When designing NPCs who withhold information.
- [mechanism] A trusted NPC who can summon help adds a signal mechanic whose reliability the player must weigh.
- [mechanism] An NPC's timed final statement can reach the player only through a second NPC, which adds a step at which the information can be doubted. When: a quest where a dying character holds a key fact. Why: each relay is a point where certainty is lost.
- [behavior] A hypothetical framed as being about a third party can be a probe, and a listener who reacts as if it applied to them reveals their stake. When: someone poses a general case about a claim. Why: a personal reaction shows where the listener stands.
