- [behavior] Third-person phrasing creates distance and formality, and a writer may struggle to keep it up through a letter. When: A writer adopts an impersonal voice for a personal message. Why: Detachment is hard to sustain, so slips back into personal phrasing show the real stance.
- [behavior] A direct question about a third party can trigger sudden excitement and a rising voice, marking a sore point. When: when asked about someone connected to the client. Why: the question touches a sensitive subject.
- [behavior] A subject can signal a third person from a distance to draw them away from a pursuer, a visible clue an observant player can notice. When: a third person is in contact with a watched subject. Why: signals pull attention away from the pursuer.
- [rule] Verify a message against a third-party copy, such as a telegraph or cable office record, to confirm it independently. When: A message's authenticity or content matters. Why: An independent record can confirm or contradict the copy the investigator holds.
- [rule] Let hostile characters intercept messages, so players must choose a trusted route to pass information. When: Designing message systems in games.
- [rule] Assign a trusted third party to handle formal steps, such as paperwork, that would otherwise expose the deception. When: A deception requires official documents or formal processes. Why: Formal records can reveal a trick that appearances hide.
- [rule] Make an NPC who confirms player guesses unreliable, so players must seek a second source before trusting it. When: designing NPC dialogue that offers information. Why: Agreement alone should not settle a question.
- [heuristic] Confirm a witness's history or routine claims by independent inquiry; independent corroboration, like staff recalling an event, is harder to dismiss than the questioner's opinion. When: Relying on a witness for background facts. Why: Independent sources reduce the chance a single account is wrong, and people can contest an accuser's claim but struggle to dispute a neutral witness. (x2)
- [heuristic] Test a beneficiary's account against their motive; in games, show each accusing NPC's visible relationship score so players can weigh their reasons to lie. When: When a new account arrives from a party who benefits from its conclusion. Why: Self-interest can shape the account, so its contents need independent checking. (x2)
- [heuristic] A third party's offer of their rooms depends on trust, so a discreet plan can fail if that trust is misplaced. When: a meeting relies on a host who is not part of the conflict. Why: The whole arrangement rests on the host's willingness and discretion.
- [heuristic] When official requests for samples are refused, reference prints may still be found in unofficial sources such as personal collections.
- [heuristic] Keep a close companion uninformed when that person could not play the grieving part convincingly. When: Planning a staged death. Why: A poor performance from someone who knows the plan would expose it.
- [heuristic] Uniform, scripted denials from a group of NPCs can signal to the player that they share a secret.
- [heuristic] Take witness accounts separately before reconciling them, so that shared details that survive independent questioning can be treated as firmer evidence. When: Several people report the same event. Why: Separate accounts reduce contamination between witnesses.
- [heuristic] Verify a frightened witness's account of danger by direct inspection, since fear can exaggerate the description. When: A witness reports a threat or danger. Why: Emotion inflates descriptions, so key claims need independent checking.
- [heuristic] Build an NPC report with a deliberate blind spot, so a player who asks about hard-to-fake details finds the report incomplete. When: Designing an NPC whose testimony the player can test. Why: Blind spots reward players who ask probing questions over those who accept confident summaries.
- [heuristic] A decoy witness can explain several early clues at once, so the player keeps the belief even when it is partly wrong. Why: One fiction accounts for many unexplained details, which makes it hard to abandon.
- [tradeoff] In a vouching mechanic, a failed contact raises suspicion of the vouched person; elsewhere, an unreachable reference proves nothing and counts as neither confirmation nor refutation. When: A vouching mechanic requires verifying a reference in the world; other third-party reference claims are tested separately.
- [tradeoff] Approaching a third party carries a cost that must be weighed against the value of a confession from the principal.
- [tradeoff] Handing evidence to an authority secures it only if that authority is trusted; an obvious conflict of interest makes the handover unbelievable. When: Choosing who receives a contested object. Why: The receiver's loyalty determines whether custody holds.
- [pitfall] An accusation made by a third party can taint a witness's answers, so each answer must be checked against physical facts. When: when another party has already voiced a theory before the witness speaks. Why: the witness may echo the accusation instead of reporting perception.
- [pitfall] Excluding someone because they were told nothing is an error, since being kept uninformed does not remove access.
- [pitfall] Investigators tend to dismiss a trusted colleague without testing them, and this bias must be corrected by applying the same test to everyone. When: An investigator's trust in someone shapes which candidates are examined. Why: Unexamined trust skews elimination toward a favored suspect.
- [pitfall] A locked door is only as secure as the trust placed in whoever says a missing key does not matter. When: When an NPC's word is the only assurance that a lock is secure. Why: A dismissed absence can hide a real route in, and a careful investigator would chase it down.
- [pitfall] Vague answers such as 'I don't remember' should be recorded as gaps and revisited later, not treated as either confirmation or denial. When: Collecting testimony from memory.
- [pitfall] An account that depends on one party's confirmation stays untested when that party denies it, and a denial leaves the account unverified. When: When the only witness to a key moment is the person being accused. Why: With no independent source, no part of the account can be checked.
- [pitfall] Do not assume a witness saw everything within their view; a witness's certainty about an exit is a claim that still needs confirming, including whether they left their post. When: when relying on a witness's account of who passed an exit. Why: witnesses can misjudge coverage or be absent from their posts.
- [pitfall] Agreement between witnesses who shared the same frightening situation and talked to each other is weaker evidence than it appears. When: Counting agreement between witnesses as confirmation. Why: Shared fear and conversation make their accounts mutually influenced, not independent.

## general misdirection
- [mechanism] An obvious decoy, such as visible interest in the wrong suspect or a crude trap, draws attention away from the real suspect or subtler snare. When: layering traps in a room or encounter. Why: Misdirection works through where attention goes; the flaw of the crude decoy is noticed first and attention stops there. (x2)
- [mechanism] Shifting attention to a subject's character, philosophy, private reading or possessions draws questions away from the scheme or key evidence. When: an investigation turns toward a subject's private life. Why: personal details feel relevant even when they do not explain the scene.
- [mechanism] Strange behaviour draws crowds, and the attention rewards more dramatic displays, so the displays escalate over time. When: when a visible disturbance draws an audience. Why: audiences reward performers who raise the stakes.
- [behavior] A sudden shift in attention, such as eye contact at the key moment or a loss of attention after a test remark, reveals a person's real aim. When: a topic touches the visitor's real aim, or a test remark is dropped in conversation. Why: Interest is shown when the true purpose is reached.
- [behavior] People absorbed in a task pay little attention to other areas, so a distraction can create a window in which an intruder goes unseen. When: someone is focused on a demanding activity nearby. Why: Attention is limited and fixed on one thing at a time.
- [behavior] While trying to recall a name, a digressing speaker may reveal useful details, such as emptying a bag, before being cut off. When: when a speaker is searching for a name.
- [behavior] A staged scene with a decoy character, a deliberately placed label, and openly displayed goods can make an obvious raid look like a theatrical act meant to mislead. When: when designing a theft or crime scene that should be questioned. Why: Players tend to accept the obvious reading of a scene unless attention is drawn elsewhere.
- [rule] Start eyewitness descriptions generic and let players unlock distinctive details, such as an unusual stud or a hair on a shirt, to narrow suspects. When: when designing eyewitness clues.
- [rule] Keep a discovery from suspects when telling them would let them adapt, hide, or act further before the investigator's test is run. When: The evidence's value depends on the culprit not yet knowing it is known. Why: Early disclosure lets the culprit change behavior or destroy the evidence.
- [rule] Track withheld sources as quest flags so NPCs react to what the player claims to know, not only to what the player has proven.
- [rule] Give motive-bearing NPCs a visible stake, so players can weigh their testimony with that stake in mind.
- [rule] Do not let lures contradict each other, because a careful target will catch the inconsistency. When: when designing linked bait or rumours.
- [rule] Make the real bait feel independently plausible, since an obviously false element will be dismissed and may discredit the rest. When: when designing a trap with a visible false element.
- [rule] Show why a red herring fails rather than dropping it silently, so players keep trusting later clues. When: A plausible lead is revealed to be false. Why: Unexplained dismissals can make players distrust the rest of the clues.
- [heuristic] A digression can defer a key reveal and build anticipation, but it must connect back to the case and deliver the payoff; stalling that never returns frustrates. When: a reveal needs to be deferred through digression. Why: Stalling heightens anticipation only if the reveal eventually comes; the delay builds anticipation without losing the thread.
- [heuristic] A person's attention follows their own interests, so what they notice in detail and what they skip reveals their concerns more than the scene itself. When: a witness is detailed on one topic and silent on a nearby one. Why: observation is selective and shaped by personal wishes.
- [heuristic] Make a villain's document readable in full but partly self-serving, with claims the player can verify through map details, dates or other notes. When: designing an in-world villain text. Why: Players must weigh it against other evidence.
- [heuristic] A decoy that weighs like a full container deceives a thief who lifts it, so weight can conceal emptiness. When: when designing containers a player might lift.
- [heuristic] Place one clue in two locations so it implicates two suspects until a second clue narrows it, or design a clue whose meaning flips with another clue found. When: Designing a mystery whose clues should stay open until enough evidence is gathered. Why: Ambiguity delays conclusion and rewards players who combine clues.
- [heuristic] Make a decoy witness whose existence is itself a lure, and tie that lure to a real goal the culprit needs. Why: The decoy hides the real objective behind a plausible reason for the lure.
- [heuristic] A message that looks meaningless can be a lure built to draw a character away from a location, so its real purpose is revealed late. Why: The apparent purpose of a deception is often different from its real effect.
- [tradeoff] A decoy lead that is harmless but costly in time or effort tests the player's judgement about where to spend effort, without ending the game on a wrong guess. When: Designing non-fatal misdirection. Why: Wasted effort teaches prioritisation while keeping the player in play.
- [pitfall] Dismissing a witness's seemingly irrelevant digression as noise can miss the central fact, such as a scheme hidden in family history. When: a witness recounts background at length. Why: The witness does not know which details matter, and the investigator may not yet see their significance.
- [pitfall] Witness grief or digression, or a narrator relaying it, can bury or stand in for key evidence; keep returning to the question asked and the witness's report. When: a narrator relays a witness's report.
- [pitfall] Generic traits such as common clothing fit many people, so they cannot single out one suspect and need other features. When: the setting is crowded and the witness saw only a brief view. Why: Common traits match many candidates.
- [pitfall] General labels, such as a common instrument type or shared product name, can end inquiry early or mislead; test specific candidates and actual contents before accepting them. Why: A label reflects a product line, not the specific sample.
- [pitfall] A purpose never shown, or a deduction needing a clue never given to the player, feels like cheating rather than a fair puzzle. Why: The player cannot reach the solution the designer expects.
- [pitfall] A witness who seems to seek attention may be dismissed even when the report is real, so the report must be checked on its merits.
- [pitfall] Framing the genre in advance can lead readers to hunt for the wrong kind of twist. When: A story or game presents its genre expectations before play. Why: Readers apply the expectations they were given to the material they receive.
- [fact] Generic traces such as footprints or common packaging match many people and rarely identify one individual. When: the packaging is common or has passed through many hands. Why: Generic or handled wrapping carries few distinguishing marks; many people can leave the same sign.
- [fact] A character who attracts attention can gather information that a formal authority would not reach without official standing.
- [fact] Mail sent to a general delivery address can locate a person who has no fixed address.

## evidence certainty
- [model] Suspicion has three escalating levels: inquiry, a matter needing examination without implied guilt; presumption, strong enough to justify detaining a suspect; and conviction, which needs more again.
- [mechanism] Suspicious conduct, willful suppression of evidence and fabricated evidence can together create a strong presumption of guilt without direct proof. When: Evaluating a suspect who hides or alters evidence. Why: Concealment and fabrication are read as signs of guilt when combined.
- [mechanism] A clue that seems to prove one fact can mislead; if the find is only a limited fact, players who overstate its meaning will reach a wrong theory. When: A discovered item appears to settle a point. Why: Stronger conclusions than the evidence supports lead the investigator and player astray.
- [rule] Track suspected and proven states separately; an accusation or arrest requires a specific recorded evidence item, while weak cues only raise suspicion. When: When designing accusation mechanics for a game. Why: Separating the two prevents accusations on hunches alone and keeps plausible guesses apart from confirmed findings.
- [rule] Tell the player which facts are settled so the remaining puzzle concentrates on the disputed points. When: A deduction puzzle has both certain and uncertain elements. Why: Conceded ground narrows the search space and keeps focus on what is still open.
- [rule] Distinguish a finding that is consistent with a hypothesis from one that is specific to it and confirms it; a consistent finding alone does not confirm the hypothesis. When: when judging whether a new fact supports a theory. Why: A fact can fit a theory without being specific to it, and a strong theory can make every new fact look like support.
- [rule] Make a locked or jammed object state impossible to reset, so the player can trust it as evidence rather than treating it as a red herring. When: Designing a clue that encodes a past event in an object's state. Why: A reversible or ambiguous state cannot be trusted as proof, and the clue loses its value.
- [heuristic] Test a physical anomaly with a tool or second check before drawing conclusions, since most objects are what they appear to be. When: A property does not match the expected identity. Why: Anomalies are not automatically significant, but untested ones are often missed.
- [heuristic] Solving an unexplained clue needs both constructive imagination to form a guess and exact reasoning to test it.
- [heuristic] Test a visible detail that seems to settle a question against independent facts, such as distance, since it may have another cause.
- [heuristic] Test a clue that confirms the first theory too easily by asking who gains from it. When: Evaluating evidence that fits a favoured explanation neatly. Why: Planted evidence is often designed to benefit the person who planted it.
- [heuristic] Before committing to one reading of a clue, ask whether another explanation fits it equally well, and treat the current conclusion as a working hypothesis. When: when one clue points strongly to a single place or person. Why: A clue that fits a theory may have an innocent source, so the first reading can be wrong.
- [heuristic] Character evidence shapes what seems likely but must be kept separate from proof when judging who acted. When: When a suspect's personality or history is used to judge probable guilt. Why: Character makes an outcome seem likely without establishing that it happened.
- [procedure] 1) State the baseline quantity. 2) Subtract known offsets to get the actual distance. 3) Divide by an assumed rate to get the time. 4) Flag which inputs are analogy rather than measurement. When: Building a calculation that the player or reader must follow. Why: Visible premises let the reader see and challenge weak assumptions.
- [procedure] 1) Propose an alternative scenario that also explains the absence. 2) Name a concrete way to check that scenario. 3) Compare which scenario the check supports. When: when a theory explains an absence of traces. Why: An absence fits several scenarios, so only a specific check can separate them.
- [tradeoff] A case can be strong enough to act on, such as charging or arresting, without complete proof; sufficiency for action is a lower bar than certainty. When: when deciding whether enough evidence exists to act. Why: Acting early risks error, while waiting for certainty can let the chance to act pass.
- [pitfall] Coherent, tidy reasoning can look certain while being merely plausible; keep plausibility separate from confirmed evidence and still weigh evidence against the favoured theory. When: Presenting a deduction result or evaluating an account that fits known facts well. Why: Tidy structure makes weak reasoning look certain, and fit with known facts does not verify parts only one party can confirm.
- [pitfall] A choice that rests on a single report of no defences fails if that report is wrong, so the core assumption must be checked.
- [pitfall] Losing a unique label reduces strong evidence to mere suspicion. When: When the tag that identifies evidence can be removed or lost. Why: Without the tag, the physical object no longer links to a specific source.
- [pitfall] Accepting a confession as fact because it is detailed and articulate overlooks the physical circumstances that could establish where the writer was. When: Reviewing a volunteered confession.
- [pitfall] An object's position may be planted rather than where it was discovered, so a location inference needs a second test before it convicts anyone. When: Interpreting where evidence was found. Why: The same location can be explained by concealment or by innocent placement.
- [pitfall] Conclusions resting only on impressions the player cannot check leave the reveal unsupported and reduce trust in the investigator. When: A clue or deduction is presented without visible reasoning. Why: The player has no way to verify or reproduce the conclusion.
- [pitfall] A clue whose link to a person is disputed by witnesses is unreliable until its ownership and use are confirmed. When: When witnesses disagree about whether a person carried or used an object. Why: Conflicting testimony leaves the object's connection to the person unestablished.
- [pitfall] A fragment proves that a garment was torn, not that its owner wore it at the time of the event. When: When a physical fragment is used to place a specific person at a scene. Why: Material evidence establishes the object's condition and match, but not the timing of the wearer.
- [pitfall] A broken marker may have an ordinary cause, such as over-tension, so the reveal is spoiled unless the alternative explanations are considered. When: A marker breaks and is meant to signal a hidden event. Why: Mundane causes can mimic the intended signal and undermine the reveal.
- [pitfall] Practical reasoning about distance and noise stays a guess until supported by measurement or testing. When: Inferring where a deliberate act would have been safe. Why: Unverified assumptions about distance and sound can be wrong.
- [pitfall] Treating a few confirmed cases as proof of an entire compiled collection overreaches and hides errors in the unchecked majority. When: Drawing conclusions about a whole collection from a small verified sample. Why: Verified cases are a non-random subset, so they cannot establish the reliability of the rest.
- [fact] A disguise trap alone does not prove guilt; the confirming evidence comes from a confession or from physical traces. When: Designing a deduction case built on a disguise. Why: The reaction is suggestive, so a separate proof is needed to close the case.

## search and access
- [mechanism] Let the player choose when to probe, and let an early, premature probe cost them the witness's cooperation.
- [mechanism] Discovery time depends on search thoroughness: a concealed object can stay unnoticed for days under casual search yet appear within minutes under systematic search.
- [rule] Place two spotters at known positions beside a scale marker so players can intersect their bearing lines, and mark that intersection on the player's map.
- [rule] Collect candidate tools from everyone with access before comparing marks, since a trace that cannot be matched to a tool is lost evidence. When: investigating a mark on a lock or surface.
- [rule] Before springing a trap or confronting a target, check the area for other people who might leave and change who is left alone with the target.
- [rule] Give a search mechanic a fixed check order and escalating passes, so a tempting empty spot can be checked and rejected.
- [rule] Store knowledge in a sealed item that opens only when a defined condition or date is met.
- [rule] Check who may lawfully search a place before recovering a discarded item, because a refusal by the holder can make the evidence unusable. When: Evidence sits in a space owned or controlled by someone else. Why: Searching without required authority can lose the evidence or the case.
- [rule] Restore containers to their prior state when the player leaves, or show a visible mark that the search was made. When: When a player has searched a container in a space the owner or an NPC might later inspect. Why: Consistent state keeps the search undetected or makes its traces discoverable.
- [heuristic] Divide a large area into sections and test each separately, since an explanation may fit one section yet fail in the section that matters.
- [heuristic] Secrets tend to sit in private spaces such as desk drawers and cupboards, so these are high-value places to search. When: designing or conducting a search for evidence.
- [heuristic] An instruction to act alone makes the retrieval a private act and rules out witnesses. When: when a clue should be done without observers.
- [heuristic] A visibly locked door that yields to a probing action, paired with an unnoticed second exit, rewards players who test barriers before accepting them.
- [heuristic] Look for the one mistake a careful planner would overlook rather than for the most visible clue. When: Examining a staged scene for the planner's error. Why: Careful staging removes obvious traces but rarely every subtle one.
- [heuristic] Use a locked or restricted room as a clue, since a denied witness signals that someone wanted testimony kept out. When: Designing investigation spaces where witnesses or evidence are restricted. Why: Denied access to testimony implies a motive to suppress it.
- [heuristic] When a person is unfound after a search, the remaining possibilities are concealment or an exit that no witness could watch. When: when an absence cannot be explained by a search alone. Why: the two possibilities narrow the search to hidden spots and unobserved routes.
- [procedure] 1) Conceal a reflective item while appearing occupied. 2) Angle it to view what lies behind you. 3) Look again, since a first glance may show nothing useful. When: a direct view is blocked or watched.
- [procedure] 1) Leave the hidden item where it is. 2) Control who is present and when. 3) Make the suspect believe the way is clear. 4) Watch where they retrieve it, instead of searching every surface. When: the item's location is uncertain and a full search would be costly or destructive. Why: letting the suspect reveal the spot avoids destroying the environment.
- [procedure] 1) List all candidate containers. 2) Assign each a rough likelihood of holding the item. 3) Check the most likely candidates first. 4) Keep the remaining candidates under watch. 5) Destroy a container only when other checks fail and the cost is acceptable. When: Several identical containers may hold a hidden item. Why: Ordering by likelihood minimizes the expected effort to find the item.
- [procedure] 1) Check whether the contradictory trace is genuine. 2) Identify the premise it contradicts. 3) Question that premise. 4) Search the locations no one considered, including the most unlikely ones. When: A search is complete but yields nothing, or a physical trace contradicts the timeline.
- [pitfall] A search shows only what was found, so a negative result does not prove absence; ask what searchers sought and how they looked. When: when evaluating a witness account of a search. Why: a search in good faith can still miss the target. (x2)
- [pitfall] Treating a large area as a single location skips its sub-locations, such as ceilings or fixtures, so a real hiding spot can be missed. Why: Candidate lists built from coarse assumptions omit hiding spots.
- [pitfall] Fixating on one favored spot wastes search time when the opening lies elsewhere.
- [pitfall] Searching a large table before ruling out whole classes of candidates wastes effort.
- [pitfall] A sample container opened before use can contaminate the sample and make the result unusable. When: a sample must be collected for later testing.
- [pitfall] Accepting a secret door as the answer to a disappearance without checking the geometry of the space skips the test that would confirm or refute it. When: A player or designer explains a vanishing by a hidden route. Why: A hidden route must still fit the layout and the elapsed time.
- [pitfall] A search made for a living person checks rooms and expects to see him, so it can miss a body hidden under furniture, in drawers or cupboards. When: when a search is carried out for a missing person. Why: searchers look where a person could be seen, not where a body could fit.
- [fact] Search methods depend on lawful access, since legal powers such as warrants limit what investigators may examine.

## injury evidence
- [mechanism] Body signs such as rigid muscles, fixed expression or a wound with a foreign object can point to a specific cause, such as poisoning. When: when inferring the cause of death from a body. Why: certain toxins and wounds produce characteristic signs.
- [mechanism] A dead source's claims can be dismissed or revived by rivals, so a source's influence can outlast them and shift with later argument.
- [rule] Do not overstate what a wound shows about a victim's health without further examination. When: when a wound is used to judge a victim's condition. Why: a wound's geometry does not reveal general health.
- [rule] Let a wanted NPC with a visible injury be exposed by a test the player arranges, such as a shove or a trap that makes the injured limb react. When: designing a wanted NPC with a known injury. Why: A player-arranged test turns the mark into an active puzzle rather than a passive check.
- [heuristic] Stains on a seat or floor, or drag marks and irregular furrows in soft ground lacking matching hard-ground marks, show a body was moved after death. When: When a witness describes the order of events around a body, or when judging whether a body was moved. Why: Stains form where the body is when bleeding occurs, so their position reveals sequence and can contradict a witness.
- [heuristic] Distinctive physical marks such as hand shapes can serve as identifying clues for an NPC.
- [heuristic] Record a physical injury such as a missing finger as a character trait that can rule a character in or out of a clue. When: When designing character traits that affect clues. Why: A trait gives a checkable way to exclude suspects.
- [heuristic] Attribute an injury to its likely source by reasoning from the setting, such as a fall onto a hard surface rather than a weapon. When: an injury has several possible causes. Why: the surrounding conditions often make one source much more likely.
- [heuristic] Use injury pattern, position and marks together to separate suicide, accident and homicide, rather than relying on any one feature. When: when classifying a death from its injuries. Why: Single features are ambiguous across causes.
- [heuristic] Compare the height of an injury against nearby fixtures; a match can show how the injury was caused.
- [heuristic] The type of injury, such as a burn rather than a cut, can point to a different cause and narrow the mechanism of harm. When: Interpreting an injury at a scene. Why: Different causes leave characteristically different marks.
- [heuristic] A blow inflicted after death may suggest rage or concealment, and either reading needs other support.
- [procedure] 1) Check the bleeding at each wound. 2) Compare the pattern of marks with the stated struggle. 3) Test burns against each alternative cause, such as sun or a chemical, before naming one. When: Injuries conflict with the stated cause of death or scene story. Why: Several physical signs together give a more reliable verdict.
- [metric] A depression left in bedding can estimate the height of the person who lay in it, and can contradict a claimed identity.
- [metric] Confinement from a serious injury rarely extends past roughly 48 hours, so a recovery timer near that length can set when the person may act again. When: a character recovers from serious injury in a timed scenario. Why: recovery bounds the period in which the character cannot act.
- [pitfall] Injuries inflicted after death can be arranged to suggest an attack by others and may conceal a smaller original mark. When: A scene shows several wounds suggesting group violence.
- [pitfall] Examining a body after death or in a crowd lets the observers' prior beliefs shape what they see, so their findings are weaker than an examination done under neutral conditions.
- [pitfall] Finding no fresh wound after two weeks does not prove innocence, since a cut can heal entirely. When: when a wound is used as evidence.
- [pitfall] An item found near a body may have fallen there earlier or belong to someone unconnected, so check its chain of custody before linking it.
- [pitfall] Ignoring blurred or heavier marks in a footprint trail can miss a stumble that changes the reading of the track.
- [pitfall] A confident injury inference is a long shot without a second source, and a frightened witness or suspect gives unreliable descriptions. When: Relying on an injury guess or a frightened witness's account. Why: Fear impairs recall and a single guess can be wrong.
- [fact] The position of an injury can indicate how a victim was moving at impact, such as running, and where they went afterward.
- [fact] Signs of grooming or cleaning on a corpse, such as recent shaving or residue in the mouth, can show the body was prepared after death. When: When examining a body for signs of handling after death. Why: Such care is unlikely to occur naturally after death.
- [fact] Wounds inflicted after death bleed very little, so the amount of bleeding at a wound separates pre-death injuries from later ones. When: Examining an injury whose timing is uncertain. Why: Living tissue bleeds and dead tissue does not.
- [fact] Physical geometry often leaves several candidate positions for an attacker, so direction alone seldom identifies a single culprit. When: judging whether positional evidence is conclusive. Why: one physical constraint can fit several people standing in different places.

## locks and keys
- [model] A theft-by-concealment puzzle hides the stolen item in the room it was taken from, so the reveal depends on a container already in plain sight.
- [model] A locked-room puzzle can be solved by a trained animal and an ordinary household object, so thefts that look like intrusion are an insider's method.
- [mechanism] Rotating a key item can change which lock it opens, so one key yields different outcomes and rewards experimentation. When: The key's pattern changes arrangement with orientation. Why: A changed arrangement matches a different target.
- [mechanism] A door handle with stripped mounting holes gives no grip, so the door cannot be pulled open even if the latch itself is free.
- [mechanism] A container that opens only at a fixed future time protects its contents by schedule rather than lock, so anyone seeking them must wait or break it. When: Designing a hiding place that reveals at a set moment. Why: Time-based access makes the location public while the contents stay hidden until opening.
- [mechanism] A small offset in a measured bearing from a landmark can make a search miss entirely, so the failure can look like honest error, keeping a deliberate offset fair.
- [rule] Let a minor character hide a detail, and let the player catch it by checking the story against a known fact. When: designing minor NPC testimony.
- [rule] Design a missing key as a clue by leaving a trace, such as a call log or desk record, showing someone tried to get in.
- [rule] Show the connection between a key and its lock, such as a matching pattern near the lock, so the solution does not feel arbitrary. When: A hidden lock is opened by a physical key. Why: An unshown link makes the solution feel like luck.
- [rule] Let an NPC carry a key that the player must find by searching them, and let a wrong key give a clue about its owner. When: designing key puzzles.
- [rule] Require two items held by two different NPCs to open a vault door, so the player must recover both before entering. When: When designing vault or door puzzles. Why: Split credentials turn the door into a multi-step objective.
- [rule] Give an evidence room a timed unlock, or a door left ajar for a fixed number of turns, so the player can reconstruct who could have taken an item. When: designing a clue location in a game. Why: a bounded window makes the access question answerable.
- [heuristic] A key missing from where it should be can indicate who could have secured a room from inside. When: Investigating a locked room.
- [heuristic] One assumption can unlock a whole line of work, so state it explicitly and treat it as a hypothesis the next result may overturn. When: A plan depends on a premise accepted without proof.
- [heuristic] A high page number followed by a restart at page one suggests the key book is short, so the largest page number used gives a rough bound on its size. When: the key book is unknown and the sender's numbers are available. Why: a sender who restarts at page one reveals how far the book extends.
- [heuristic] Let players test a tool on a door or lock to confirm which object produced a scratch. Why: Hands-on testing turns a clue into a verified conclusion.
- [heuristic] Make each kit item unlock one distinct action, such as magnify, measure, sample or seal, so choosing a tool becomes a puzzle.
- [procedure] 1) Identify who ordered the lock. 2) List every key's holder and when held; confirm keys by tag or marking, locating the master key. 3) List who could hold a copy, such as tenants, staff, security or central office sets. 4) Narrow to holders with actual custody and reach to the container. 5) Test each holder's claim against other facts. When: Access to a locked space or container is disputed, or few people could open it, and key holders can be identified. Why: Access records reveal who could have acted.
- [procedure] 1) Go to the suspected disposal spot. 2) Lift out each item separately. 3) Inspect and return it before moving on. 4) Do not rely on a quick glance over the pile. When: when an object was seen dropped into a cluttered or burnable place. Why: Small objects are missed by glancing or sweeping.
- [procedure] 1) Remove a key from a locked room's connecting route. 2) Have an NPC dismiss the key's absence as unimportant. 3) Prevent staff from searching for it. 4) Enter through the hidden connecting door at a time the owner is away. When: When designing how a player can enter a sealed room without breaking the lock. Why: Controlling what others believe about the key keeps the loss from being investigated.
- [procedure] 1) State who holds keys to the space. 2) State what is sealed and which windows or doors exist. 3) State which force or methods are ruled out. 4) Plan entry only within those stated limits. When: A locked space must be entered or explained and access rules can be stated. Why: Explicit constraints define what counts as a valid solution.
- [pitfall] A missing key item lets its holder deflect questions by claiming it cannot be produced. When: when an item central to the case is unavailable. Why: the item cannot be checked.
- [pitfall] Failing to consider a second location can make an investigation look more hopeless than it is. When: A person is missing and only one destination is checked. Why: The unchecked location may hold the answer.
- [pitfall] A size-fit test can be defeated because an item can be carried rather than worn, so eliminate suspects on both wearing and carrying grounds.
- [pitfall] A timed mechanism meant for one person to open is weakened when another person holds a key. When: Designing a locked or timed container with access controls. Why: A second key holder can bypass the intended access restriction.

## timeline and impossibility
- [mechanism] A timed event can be paused while the player gathers evidence and released when the player acts, so pacing follows player progress rather than the clock. When: when a countdown or scripted threat would otherwise run unattended. Why: Holding the timer keeps the player from losing options before they are ready.
- [mechanism] A timeline puzzle can use world-state indicators such as shop prices, town notices or NPC gossip, letting the player date an incident without a witness.
- [mechanism] A witness's account of when an object was moved can rule out a suspect by showing the planted object could not have been placed at the time claimed. When: A suspect is charged on the basis of evidence whose placement time is in question. Why: Fixing the time of the object's movement removes the opportunity for the accused to have planted it.
- [behavior] When a timed object conflicts with testimony, the investigator must choose between trusting the theory and trusting the evidence, and must test which one to doubt. When: a timekeeping object shows a time that conflicts with the believed time of an event. Why: both the object and the testimony can be wrong, and the conflict forces explicit reasoning.
- [rule] Define a presence window, such as about ten minutes, in which events are impossible for a suspect; a longer claimed interval cannot fit and becomes a timeline problem. When: A presence window is known or defined, or a longer interval is claimed. Why: Narrows the search without a direct confession, since the claimed interval cannot fit inside the available time. (x2)
- [rule] Do not resolve an impossible-access puzzle with an unexplained supernatural cause when the design promises a rational solution. When: the design promises a rational answer. Why: an unexplained cause breaks the fairness the player expects.
- [rule] Give players a timestamped event log with a discoverable constant offset, so they must detect and correct it before ordering events. When: designing timeline-based puzzles. Why: Correcting the offset is what makes the sequence solvable.
- [rule] Cross-check a timed record such as a log, message or clock reading against a suspect's claimed location to show who could have been present. When: when a character offers an alibi.
- [heuristic] A scene timer that contradicts the player's assumed sequence can be reset by an NPC and then re-verified by a second witness.
- [heuristic] Give a found object a verified timeline by checking the finder's account of where and when it was found. When: When an object's location must be tied to a time. Why: A dated, checkable account anchors the object's position in the sequence of events.
- [heuristic] A locked container that opens only after a timed event can anchor a timeline the player must reconstruct. When: designing a quest timeline.
- [procedure] 1) List the chain of deductions that produced the impossible timeline or result. 2) Test each assumption about distance, sound or other physical facts. 3) Test candidate explanations against the physical facts. 4) If none fits, find the stated premise that is wrong. When: A timeline or deduction looks impossible. Why: An impossible timeline may rest only on an unchecked assumption, so assumptions must be tested before accepting the contradiction.
- [procedure] 1) List every condition the apparently impossible action requires. 2) Identify which conditions the suspect lacks, testing each candidate explanation against all conditions. 3) Test simpler explanations first, accepting the ordinary route that satisfies every condition before supernatural causes. 4) Search for a place where the missing conditions do not matter. When: A witness account or scene seems impossible given a person's known condition, yet evidence still constrains it. Why: Necessary conditions narrow candidates until one ordinary route remains; impossible-seeming actions point to alternative explanations such as a hidden location.
- [procedure] 1) Build a timeline for each incident in proper order, marking each time firm or estimate. 2) Record who was where, noting second-hand absences and observer gaps. 3) Place the central figure first, then move outward so each answer anchors the next. 4) Keep unplaced people as suspects; set aside anyone who could not have been present. 5) Use expected arrivals as deadlines. When: Several incidents, several people, testimony given from memory, or assembling a timeline to test alibis and locate conflicts. Why: Later facts can be fitted to earlier ones, missing information becomes a question rather than an accusation, and firm and weak times must be kept apart to judge alibis fairly.
- [procedure] 1) Identify the object's maker or seller. 2) Obtain the sale or record date from that source. 3) Check the date against the timeline of events. When: When an object's ownership must be established. Why: Dated seller records give an independent, checkable link between an object and a buyer.
- [procedure] 1) Find the known incubation or delay period of the cause. 2) Subtract it from the onset date to get the exposure window. 3) Clear suspects whose contact fell outside that window. 4) Cross-check remaining suspects against known whereabouts. When: The mechanism works with a delay, such as an illness or slow poison. Why: Working backward from onset narrows when the cause could have acted.
- [procedure] 1) Give the player each staged event. 2) Let them attach a suspect, means and opportunity to each. 3) Reveal the consistent timeline only when all events are linked. When: When designing a mystery or deduction puzzle. Why: Linking each event to a suspect, means and opportunity turns the mystery into a checkable timeline.
- [procedure] 1) Compare the time shown with the time of the event. 2) Ask whether the object was running or was stopped by the event; a stopped object records only when it stopped. 3) Check whether it could have been wound, started or moved afterward. 4) Weigh alternatives against other evidence. 5) Restore the object to its found state after examining it. When: a timed object conflicts with testimony. Why: a stopped or tampered timepiece can mislead anyone who reads it at face value.
- [procedure] 1) Collect each witness's times from clocks, memory and estimates. 2) Set them side by side in one sequence. 3) Identify a single reliable fixed point to anchor the account. 4) Check whether the story of events fits the hours. When: several witnesses give times that must be placed in one sequence. Why: a fixed point lets conflicting estimates be tested rather than averaged.
- [metric] Distance travelled can be estimated from fuel consumed. The result is a rough range to be checked against road distances on a map. When: Fuel consumption is known or can be inferred.
- [metric] Bones clean of soft tissue and showing no fresh change set a lower bound on time since death, here at least eighteen months. When: when estimating how long remains have been exposed. Why: decay and cleaning take time, so their state gives a minimum interval.
- [pitfall] A timer based on fixed flow fails when the flow changes, so its calibration must be repeated whenever the rate may have shifted. Why: timing depends directly on rate.
- [pitfall] A quiet interval can look like the end of a recurring pattern when it is only a pause, leading players to a false conclusion.
- [pitfall] A delayed cause can make a recent visit look harmless, and a single preparatory act is weak evidence unless it aligns with the timeline. When: Evaluating suspects against a delayed mechanism. Why: Delay hides the link between contact and effect, and isolated clues mislead.
- [fact] A witness's time can be vivid yet wrong, and a nearby clock may be inaccurate, so any interval between events is only as reliable as the clock and memory behind it. Why: memory and timekeeping instruments both introduce error.

## suspect dossiers and interrogation
- [mechanism] Let players assemble a motive chain from evidence cards and present it to a suspect, with a confession or flight as the result. When: A detective-style interrogation system is designed. Why: Presenting a coherent chain turns evidence into a social outcome.
- [mechanism] When household members are absent, the premises become open to anyone who knows the arrangement, so absence can both explain and enable access. When: Assessing who could have acted during a period when staff are out. Why: Known absences remove the people who would otherwise block access.
- [behavior] A suspect who hears a detailed recital of known facts may turn pale and sweat before speaking, showing pressure builds as evidence is laid out. When: Known facts are recited in detail to a suspect. Why: Hearing the full case removes the option of maintaining a false account.
- [behavior] A suspect may reveal knowledge while answering about a related subject, so asking about neighbors of the target is more revealing than asking about the target directly. When: A witness is likely to conceal a connection to a person or object. Why: The witness does not notice the real subject of the test, so answers carry unguarded information.
- [heuristic] Judge self-serving accounts, such as recasting an act as blackmail or a written confession, separately from the speaker's wish to be excused; candor is not proof. When: A suspect offers a self-serving account or a written confession. Why: Self-interest can shape a confession even when it contains some truth, and accepting a reframing can mislead. (x2)
- [heuristic] A sudden, convenient error that forces an early exit is a warning sign and should not be accepted at face value; it may be a planned evasion. When: A suspect's explanation for a sudden change of route conveniently removes them from tracing. Why: Planned decoys are designed to look like innocent mistakes.
- [heuristic] A suspect dossier can list evidence for and against each suspect, leaving the player to weigh it.
- [heuristic] Judge witnesses' trustworthiness by temperament, and do not depend on the discretion of one who is volatile or weak. When: Choosing whom to confide in during an investigation. Why: Temperament predicts whether a witness will keep quiet.
- [heuristic] An interrogation option that unlocks only when the player holds the right clue makes the confession match the evidence. When: Designing dialogue challenges that reveal information. Why: Gating the option on evidence ties the outcome to the player's investigation.
- [heuristic] Give each suspect a record of access such as keys, schedule and presence, and check it when a crime occurs. When: Designing crimes where access determines who could act. Why: Recorded access lets the game and player test opportunity objectively.
- [procedure] 1) Make the arrest formal, stating the charge and the caution that statements may be used. 2) Recite the known facts in detail to break pretence. 3) Offer a conditional incentive, such as no prosecution if an object is returned unopened. 4) Let the suspect explain in their own words. 5) Reduce the account to plain facts. 6) Verify each claim against objects and records. When: There is enough independent evidence to test the suspect's account. Why: Laying out facts before the suspect speaks reduces room for a self-serving story.
- [procedure] 1) Choose a topic the person is likely to enjoy. 2) Observe physical traits during the talk without showing special interest in the person. 3) Confirm any noticed trait with a second observation before relying on it. When: A suspect is recognizable but not yet confirmed and the trait can be observed without alarm. Why: A casual remark is weak evidence, and a second observation strengthens it.
- [procedure] 1) Hint at a discovery without revealing it. 2) Watch the other party's reactions for tells. 3) Ask hypothetical questions to learn their rules. 4) Trade information only after learning their position. When: Both parties hold partial information and each wants the other's cards first. Why: Withholding keeps leverage while probing reveals what the other side already knows.
- [procedure] 1) Ask about a related subject instead of the target. 2) Compare answers across related subjects. 3) Hide your interest with a distraction. 4) Stop before naming the target. 5) Treat tone shifts and dismissive replies as data. When: Probing a witness who may conceal knowledge. Why: Indirect probing yields hints about what the witness knows without alerting them.
- [procedure] 1) Withhold your own conclusion. 2) Make a bold, unexpected statement. 3) Read the reaction in tone, pauses and evasions. 4) Establish who was where using arrival questions. 5) Ask the other party to confirm or deny each point. When: The other party controls information you need. Why: A withheld conclusion cannot be dismissed easily, and reactions reveal whether a claim touched something real.
- [procedure] 1) List heirs in succession order. 2) Note who is next if the chain changes. 3) Restrict suspects to those present in the household. 4) Check alibis last. When: A death gives someone a gain through inheritance. Why: Ordering heirs and limiting to those present narrows the suspect pool before alibis are checked.
- [procedure] 1) Start from the physical object. 2) Identify its maker or source. 3) Trace the people connected to that source. 4) Assess the plausible motive for each link. When: A single object must lead to a set of suspects. Why: Chaining from object to people builds a case step by step.
- [pitfall] A bluff can turn into a dare, as the other side pushes harder once a party refuses to yield. When: Both sides are bluffing during a negotiation. Why: Refusal to concede invites escalation rather than settlement.
- [pitfall] The questioner's own prejudices can distort the reading of answers, especially judgments of manner or spending, so over-interpretation of indirect answers is a common error. When: Interpreting indirect or ambiguous answers. Why: Personal judgment colors what counts as a meaningful signal.
- [pitfall] Stating a conclusion too firmly gives the other side a reason to dismiss it, and trusting one's own sense of sincerity is risky when one sympathizes with the suspect. When: Presenting a theory to a witness or interpreting their sincerity. Why: Firm claims invite rejection, and sympathy biases the read of sincerity.
- [pitfall] A witness who reveals findings too early risks losing their advantage in the exchange. When: Deciding how much to reveal during an interview. Why: Early disclosure gives the other side information to adapt to.
- [pitfall] Assuming the closest household is the only possible source can miss leaks from outside people who hold information. When: Tracing how a secret became known. Why: Information often travels through contacts beyond the immediate location.
- [pitfall] Second-hand alibis need direct checking before they are trusted, and a stopped clock can mislead anyone who relies on it. Why: reports passed along by others can disagree or be mistaken.
- [fact] A witness's anxieties, such as fear of paying for damage, can color how they report times and events. When: Interpreting a witness's account. Why: Emotional stakes bias recall and reporting.

## object search and seeding
- [mechanism] Wearing an object for a long time can leave a lasting pale mark on the skin that matches the object's outline.
- [rule] Let players search containers one item at a time, revealing items in turn with a record of what has been checked, so systematic search is rewarded over partial search. When: When designing the interaction for searching a container. Why: Step-by-step search gives the player a process to follow and reveals items gradually. (x2)
- [rule] Place clues so a systematic search finds them while a hasty first pass does not, rewarding careful play. When: designing a timed search puzzle. Why: thorough inspection should be rewarded over quick scanning.
- [rule] Give staged crime scenes a checklist of unchangeable traits, such as a ring, a scar or a bandage, so players can test the staged story against them. When: Designing a scene arranged to mislead. Why: A fixed checklist gives players a concrete way to find the contradiction.
- [rule] Give hidden items in containers a physical property, such as weight or solubility, so the player can test a theory instead of guessing. When: Designing a puzzle involving a sealed, cracked or bought container. Why: A testable property makes the puzzle checkable rather than arbitrary.
- [rule] To establish who could open a secured object, determine who knew the access code, when it last changed, and whether anyone's memory of it is unreliable. When: A locked object such as a safe or combination lock is involved. Why: A key alone may not suffice, and the code's holder and timing decide who had access.
- [rule] Seed a puzzle object early with a plain, discoverable function, and keep the materials needed for its solution in the level before they are required. When: when placing puzzle objects.
- [rule] Reassemble discarded items from fragments recovered from waste, turning waste into evidence. When: A discarded object is found in a bin or dump. Why: Fragments can be pieced together to reveal the object's identity and link.
- [heuristic] An object too large to carry out unnoticed is probably still on the premises.
- [heuristic] An object left behind by an absent figure promises their return and keeps the audience expecting them. When: a character has gone missing or left the scene. Why: a left-behind item implies the owner will come back.
- [heuristic] Searches for a discarded item long after an event are hard, so concentrate effort on the most probable hiding places. Why: Limited search time is best spent where the item is most likely to be.
- [heuristic] When testimony conflicts, look for one object that can settle who owned or touched something. When: witness accounts disagree about possession or contact.
- [heuristic] A generic scan that returns nothing is a valid result; a second, location-specific action can be required before the clue appears. When: designing search mechanics.
- [heuristic] Place a small residue in a hidden spot far from the central event, so players must reason about why the staging was done there. When: Designing a hidden clue for a staged event. Why: Players who reason about the location of the act can find the trace through deduction.
- [procedure] 1) Examine the object's condition before disposal. 2) Weight it and open a way for water to enter. 3) Watch through a telescope until it vanishes. 4) Treat a longer float than expected as a warning. When: when a guilty party disposes of a vessel or object at sea. Why: verification confirms the disposal worked.
- [procedure] 1) Search every surface in a fixed order. 2) If one trace type is erased, check other surfaces where it may survive. 3) Examine small fragments under magnification. When: a scene has been cleaned, weathered or disturbed. Why: Erased traces can leave residues elsewhere.
- [pitfall] Exhaustive search through many mostly irrelevant objects without clear purpose tires players and invites hasty wrong guesses; give each search a clear purpose and readable result. When: When a puzzle requires sifting through many mostly irrelevant objects. Why: Sustained fruitless search lowers attention and motivation, raising the chance of a rushed guess.
- [pitfall] An inference from a missing trace is only as sound as the timing of the search and the accuracy of witness claims. A late or partial search weakens the conclusion. Why: A trace may have been removed or may have faded before anyone checked for it.
- [pitfall] Collecting only the large pieces hides the shortfall or surplus that reveals where breakage happened.
- [pitfall] A systematic search can still miss the opening, which may turn up by accident rather than by reasoning.
- [pitfall] Assuming a tool was used because it was found near the damage can mislead, since proximity does not establish that the tool made the marks.
- [fact] Older marks such as scratched initials and symbols reveal earlier occupancy, separate from recent use.
- [fact] Writing that is invisible under normal conditions can be revealed by heat, so a document can hide a clue until a physical process is applied.

## footprint and tread evidence
- [mechanism] A creature's footprints can encode its size, speed and diet, letting players infer its behavior before they meet it.
- [mechanism] Footprint puzzles where tracks double back or cross reward players who scan both sides of a trail.
- [mechanism] Fragile markers such as hairs, seals and tacky floor wafers reveal any disturbance by breaking or shifting, so unseen movement leaves a visible sign that the player can read later. When: A player must detect unseen movement and the event repeats, so markers accumulate evidence over time. Why: A marker that breaks or moves records passage, letting evidence build across repeated events.
- [rule] Give suspects footwear or stud-wear details so marks narrow the pool without naming one; keep one mark unique to a single character, others shared. When: Designing physical clues for a suspect pool. Why: A unique mark identifies one character while shared marks keep several in play.
- [rule] Store gait, weight and contamination flags for each track, so players can reconstruct who passed where. When: Designing a tracking mechanic.
- [rule] Footprints are useful evidence only if laid down after the surface stopped changing and before other traffic obliterated them, so fix timing first. When: using prints in snow or soft ground. Why: Weather and traffic alter prints, so timing determines whether they still reflect one movement.
- [heuristic] Footprints can point toward a person but rarely prove identity, so they should narrow candidates rather than settle who was present. When: Using footprints in an investigation. Why: Prints share features across people and do not establish identity alone.
- [heuristic] A material unlike its surroundings points to a different source, so compare it with the local environment. When: An unfamiliar substance is found in a known place. Why: Material that does not match the local setting was likely brought in from elsewhere.
- [heuristic] Footprints that contradict a guard's dialogue let a player catch a lying NPC. When: An NPC denies a route that physical evidence shows was used. Why: Physical traces can expose dishonest statements.
- [heuristic] Ask what a conspicuous object was used for, since its function can reveal the intent behind a crime and not only who handled it. When: A conspicuous object is present and its use is not obvious. Why: Function reveals intent, which traces of handling do not.
- [heuristic] Judge direction of travel by where toe impressions point and where heel marks fall at a water's edge. When: Footprints are visible and their orientation is unclear. Why: Toe and heel placement show which way the walker was moving.
- [procedure] 1) Look for sinking heel marks that show weight or running. 2) Find overlapping prints. 3) Order the movements by which mark lies over which. When: Soft ground holds several overlapping footprints. Why: Overlaps and depth show the order in which people moved through an area.
- [procedure] 1) Judge each impression's quality for slipping or stumbling. 2) Act out the proposed explanation on the first print. 3) Rule out alternatives in order, checking visibility, print clarity and motive. When: Footprints are the only physical trace and must be read in detail. Why: Acting out and ranking alternatives tests an explanation before accepting it.
- [procedure] 1) Assign each household member their footwear. 2) Compare each member's size and type with the prints. 3) Account for every print found, including each member's prints. 4) Clear members whose footwear does not match. When: sorting suspects using footprints and fingerprints. Why: Each print must be assigned to a source, so unexplained prints remain visible.
- [tradeoff] Footprints show where someone passed and in what order, but identify who only when corroborated; alone, traces record movement without carrying identity information. When: An investigator must establish an event and reconstruct an actor's route. Why: Traces record action and location but carry no identity information. (x2)
- [pitfall] Assuming a print belongs to a known person can lead to misreading the evidence, as an investigator nearly did by taking a print for a known individual's.
- [fact] An object foreign to its setting, such as a foreign coin, can tie its holder to a place or to a past service abroad.
- [fact] Wet or muddy footwear in a room can show recent presence even when the occupant denies being there.
- [fact] Fresh soil inside a dry room cannot come from ordinary footwear, so matching it to a nearby source such as newly dug beds points to a likely visitor. When: the room is dry and the soil is fresh. Why: ordinary boots would not carry it in dry weather.
- [fact] Soft ground such as mud, snow or sand keeps footprints, while dry chalk and grass usually keep none.
- [fact] A footprint match often establishes only a size and sole pattern, which other footwear of the same size and last may also share. When: matching prints to a candidate's footwear. Why: Many people share the same shoe size and last, so the match is partial.

## stealth and stakeouts
- [mechanism] When a target notices a watcher and remarks on it, surveillance becomes a known threat, which raises the immediate stakes for the player. When: when watcher NPCs are visible to the player. Why: knowing one is observed changes how the target acts and how tense the scene feels.
- [mechanism] A staged noisy event draws a guard's attention away from a target, letting the player swap or retrieve the object unobserved. When: when a scripted heist-style sequence needs cover. Why: attention is pulled toward the noise, opening a window.
- [behavior] A watcher gathers information unnoticed via a harmless, context-appropriate task keeping them moving; a concealed mirror aids observation. An out-of-place task makes the observer suspicious. When: when a target must not know they are watched, or when designing or staging a covert action. Why: A routine task that grows naturally from the situation is not questioned, so the observer is not suspected.
- [rule] A stealth mission can let the player adopt a disguise or assumed role to enter a restricted area or talk to a guarded NPC.
- [rule] Let the player judge lighting, noise and timing when deciding whether an observation is safe. When: designing stealth observation puzzles.
- [rule] Do not stay in one spot so long that passers-by or witnesses begin to notice the watcher. When: During a stakeout. Why: A lingering, visible watcher becomes conspicuous and risks exposure.
- [rule] Offer a stealth action that lets the player feign distress to be placed beside a target. When: Designing stealth gameplay.
- [rule] Let a stealth disguise be tested by a physical action, such as wiping a face, that reveals the fake. When: Designing disguise mechanics for stealth play. Why: A physical test gives the player a way to expose the disguise without relying on luck.
- [rule] Build a timed stakeout mechanic that requires several in-game nights, a hidden vantage point, and a fatigue meter.
- [heuristic] Offer disguise costume tiers, each opening a set of NPC conversations and blocked areas.
- [heuristic] Make a stealth window depend on stated assumptions, such as guard attention, so the player can test them and discover when they fail.
- [heuristic] A slow, silent action such as turning a knob gently suggests the actor wanted to avoid waking the occupant. Why: quiet movement signals intent not to be noticed.
- [heuristic] A diversion trap feels fair when the player can anticipate it by noticing the agent who staged it. When: when scripting a distraction the player must overcome. Why: visible setup lets players reason toward the trap.
- [procedure] 1) Watch the target's residence from a fixed spot. 2) Follow at a distance that keeps the target in view. 3) Note his stops. 4) If he uses a vehicle, hire another and follow it. 5) Record landmarks to memorise the destination. When: The investigator needs a target's address or movements that the target will not volunteer.
- [procedure] 1) Build a timeline of when the target is busy and how long a usable window lasts. 2) Confirm the target's position before the diversion. 3) Fit the full action inside the window, with a margin for surprises. When: a target's schedule is predictable and the action needs a short window. Why: a reliable window is the only time the action can succeed.
- [procedure] 1) Give the target a reason to leave the room or look away. 2) Post a lookout on the approach. 3) Keep a clear exit. 4) Retrieve the object before returning. When: An object must be examined while the target is present nearby. Why: A distraction needs cover and an escape route, or the attempt is exposed.
- [procedure] 1) Give the suspect a credible warning whose substance is accurate. 2) Make the source seem authoritative. 3) Set a watch on the suspect and plan the follow in advance. 4) Follow the suspect to the hiding place they move the item to. When: The suspect's move will reveal a hiding place or accomplice. Why: A credible tip provokes movement, and the movement exposes the cache.
- [procedure] 1) Survey a hostile room by listening and by fixing the positions of people, exits and windows. 2) Identify single controllable elements such as a light. 3) Plan the action around the elements. When: entering a hostile room with limited sight. Why: positional knowledge makes later action precise.
- [tradeoff] A stakeout works only when the cache location is known and the suspect's timing can be controlled; it fails if the cache may be elsewhere or the suspect is armed. When: Choosing between a stakeout and other approaches.
- [tradeoff] A controlled tip can move the item but may be doubted or checked by the suspect, and a false tip risks endangering innocent people. When: Deciding whether to use a planted warning.
- [pitfall] Opponents watch whatever object is being manipulated, so an action on a controllable element must look innocent or it will be noticed. When: manipulating a shared object in front of opponents. Why: observers track changes to objects near them.

## supernatural claim evaluation
- [mechanism] If an apparition stops once the suspect leaves a place, the cause may lie in that location or association rather than the person themselves. When: Testing whether an anomaly follows a person or a place. Why: Removing the person from a location separates location-linked causes from person-linked ones.
- [rule] Rule out mundane causes one at a time, unlocking the supernatural option last. Offer a physical probe, such as throwing an item, that shows an apparition is not solid, letting the player try rational explanations first. When: Designing a disturbance or an ambiguous, impossible object that could have a trap, monster, or other cause. Why: Sequential elimination gives the player a reason to test each option and makes the final cause satisfying.
- [rule] Use motive as the test for an explanation: a mechanism that explains how something happened does not identify who had reason to do it. When: Evaluating a supernatural or mechanical explanation in a mystery. Why: Method and motive are separate questions, so a complete answer must address both.
- [rule] Give the player a simple test, such as a light flicker or a sound cue, that reveals a status effect or hidden state. When: A game has hidden status effects or conditions. Why: A visible test lets players detect a state they cannot see directly.
- [rule] Link each possible reading of a clue to an independent fact the player has already collected, so the player can rule options out.
- [heuristic] Build a working conclusion from several minor signs that point the same way; no single fact is decisive, but independent weak facts reinforce one another toward one conclusion. When: Hard proof is missing, each fact allows more than one reading, and a hypothesis is needed to test against further evidence. Why: Agreeing signs support a likely conclusion, while a single sign is too weak to settle the matter.
- [heuristic] Offer a natural explanation alongside a human one, and let a test separate them, so the puzzle rewards careful checking. When: designing a poison or disease puzzle. Why: Players must test rather than assume.
- [heuristic] End the escape with a counting detail, such as a headcount that exposes an extra person, so the exit can be proven. Why: A numeric check turns suspicion into verifiable proof.
- [heuristic] Use a sensory clue, such as a scent, that the player can test against each suspect one at a time, eliminating suspects through repeated checks. Why: A repeatable test lets the player narrow suspects by direct comparison.
- [procedure] 1) List each supernatural claim. 2) Find an ordinary mechanism for each; a known mechanism can expose a human act. 3) Keep only what ordinary mechanisms cannot explain. 4) Do not accept a claim merely because it fits the facts. When: An event seems to have a supernatural cause. Why: A claim that fits every fact may still have a human cause, so each element must be checked separately.
- [procedure] 1) List every candidate cause, including the supernatural one. 2) Test each against the evidence using checks that can fail. 3) Eliminate causes; keep the dramatic one open only while testable rivals remain, stating which is unproven. When: A reported effect fits several explanations, including a supernatural one. Why: Testable elimination separates real causes from dramatic ones; naming rivals openly prevents the only suggested reading from being accepted by default.
- [procedure] 1) List ordinary explanations: imagination, intoxication, excitement, sleepwalking, drowsiness, illness, observer error. 2) Check each against witnesses' actual conditions. 3) Keep unexplained details open rather than assuming a cause. When: A case includes reported phenomena such as an illusion, a strange sighting, or an odd witness experience. Why: Checking witness state removes many apparent mysteries, since intoxication, drowsiness or illness can produce convincing but false perceptions.
- [procedure] 1) Ask a question only the culprit could answer. 2) Compare the answer against information the player has not shared with the NPC. 3) Treat a match as real insight only if no leak could explain it. When: Designing tests that separate genuine insight from a cold read. Why: Questions that only the true source could answer expose trickery.
- [procedure] 1) List each reaction or clue that does not fit the working theory. 2) Check whether each is a real contradiction or a trivial anomaly. 3) Frame the remaining conflicts as a choice between two explanations. 4) Test the starting assumption before adding ingenuity. When: Several clues conflict and the theory is not converging. Why: Revisiting a wrong assumption is cheaper than defending a broken theory.
- [procedure] 1) Group scattered anomalies that seem unrelated. 2) Propose a motive linking the people involved. 3) Reconstruct the sequence of events, from how parties met to what triggered the conflict and what was used. 4) Explain how any unseen party escaped detection. 5) Test the theory against physical clues elsewhere and list unresolved contradictions before acting. When: several anomalies appear that no single explanation covers. Why: a theory that explains all clues and exposes its weak points is more reliable than one built on a single anomaly.
- [procedure] 1) Show an injury or clue that reads as guilt. 2) Let an unexplained phrase sit in the journal. 3) Hold a withheld interval of inspection. 4) Reveal a physical proof of the hazard, then recall the phrase. When: Designing a mystery where the apparent culprit is innocent. Why: Recalling a planted detail at the end ties the hazard to earlier evidence and makes the reveal feel prepared.
- [procedure] 1) Give the player a test that checks an NPC's physical trait, such as a hand-touch check. 2) Use the result to learn a mechanic or expose an impostor.
- [procedure] 1) Check the witness's record against the case file. 2) If no record exists, treat the testimony as fabricated. 3) Re-examine every planted object the testimony touched.
- [pitfall] Dismissing an explanation as absurd can miss a real event: an implausible theory does not falsify the coincidence, and a supernatural connection may grow harder to dismiss as evidence accumulates. When: A coincidence or reported effect is explained by a theory the investigator finds absurd. Why: Absurdity of a rival theory does not establish that the observed event is false.
- [pitfall] A test that yields no result can look as though it was never run; make the failure visible and meaningful to the player.

## test and comparison methods
- [mechanism] Evidence objects with testable properties, such as scent, torn edge or a duplicate pair, let players compare two fragments to confirm they match.
- [behavior] Testimony collected by questionnaire is only as trustworthy as the questions asked and how answers were recorded; leading questions or editing can shape the results. When: Evaluating a body of statements gathered by survey or inquiry. Why: Question wording and compiler edits can confirm what the collector already believes, and the originals are needed to detect this.
- [rule] Make some player checks return nothing, so players learn from negative results rather than only from successful ones. When: When designing investigation loops in a game.
- [rule] Mark meaningful features such as islands, loops and bifurcations, not an arbitrary grid whose lines fall at random. When: when comparing fingerprints.
- [rule] Plant an overt clue that could be a message or pure chance, and let the game confirm it against a second source before it counts. When: when designing ambiguous clues.
- [rule] Have the player memorise key details rather than copying documents, and record notes only after leaving the scene. When: a covert action must avoid carrying evidence.
- [rule] Test for concealed machinery with an instrument that would register its operation, so that a mechanism, if present, reveals itself.
- [heuristic] When earlier cases left no trace of a suspected agent, treat a new method as needing its own test rather than assuming it. When: when earlier searches came up empty.
- [heuristic] Confirm a timing estimate from decaying objects against other traces, such as the count of glasses or cups present. When: A timing estimate is drawn from a single decaying object. Why: A single timing can be wrong, and other traces can corroborate or refute it.
- [heuristic] Compare a passive recording against a reliable baseline taken under normal conditions so that differences can be isolated. When: Interpreting a faint or uncertain passive record. Why: Without a baseline, faint marks are easily misread.
- [heuristic] Spot-checking a few independently verifiable statements confirms only those statements, not the rest of the collection. When: Verifying a large body of testimony by checking a sample. Why: A confirmed sample does not establish the reliability of unchecked items.
- [procedure] 1) Identify two candidate causes that produce similar effects. 2) Place a proxy subject in the suspected environment. 3) Observe the subject's reaction over a short exposure. 4) Remove the suspected source and observe whether the effect ends. When: A source must be identified from its effects and the environment can be sampled. Why: Similar effects cannot distinguish candidates alone, but timing and removal can confirm which cause operated.
- [procedure] 1) Identify a physical property of the item that varies between sources. 2) Collect reference samples from each candidate. 3) Compare the item against each sample using a standard scale and chemical tests. 4) Exclude candidates whose samples do not match. When: Ordinary identifiers such as handwriting are inconclusive and samples can be obtained. Why: Physical properties can separate sources that look identical to the eye.
- [procedure] 1) Give the player an authentic known sample. 2) Give a disputed document. 3) Let them compare magnified details. 4) Provide a confirming record that supports or refutes the match.
