When: building an investigation puzzle around a disputed written item. Why: Pairing a visual comparison with a verifying record makes the answer deducible instead of guessed.
- [tradeoff] A narrow test checks one substance and gives a clear answer, but the tester must already suspect it, and a negative result says nothing about others. When: when choosing which lab test to run.
- [tradeoff] A short test exposure may cause no lasting harm yet still show an effect, but extrapolating to longer exposure is uncertain and a single sample is weak. When: Testing with a subject who bears real discomfort or risk. Why: Extrapolation from short to repeated exposure depends on assumptions that one sample cannot verify.
- [pitfall] A search that finds nothing shows only that nothing was found where it looked, not that nothing is hidden.
- [pitfall] Competing motives can make a plan switch between methods, which wastes effort; choose one method and stay with it. When: when conflicting aims pull on the plan. Why: switching methods loses progress.
- [pitfall] Recovery after removing a suspected source is persuasive evidence, but other changes occurring at the same time may also explain the recovery. When: Confirming a cause by observing improvement after removal. Why: Removal and recovery are correlated with other changes that were not controlled.
- [fact] An absent sighting, expected trace or instrument reading is a clue: it narrows possibilities, can rule out false suspects, or points to a special cause; record it. When: When a witness, artifact or instrument lacks an expected sighting, feature or detection. Why: Absence read against the witness's position or the expected features narrows possibilities even though nothing was found. (x2)

## theory testing and rewards
- [rule] Hold a theory provisionally; test it against the timeline and remaining contradictions before acting, since plausibility and conviction do not establish correctness. When: a working explanation is formed from clues before the evidence is complete. Why: plausible theories often fail once fuller facts arrive. (x2)
- [rule] Let the game reveal the result of a player's prediction, so wrong guesses teach the underlying rule. When: Players are invited to guess outcomes in a puzzle or system. Why: Visible results turn failed guesses into learning.
- [rule] Let the player meet a rude expert who proves correct, and reward checking the expert's claims. When: Designing an NPC expert who opposes a helpful NPC. Why: Rewarding verification teaches players not to judge by manner.
- [rule] Give players facts that contradict their first theory, and reward them for revising it, so revision becomes part of the play. When: when designing investigation progression. Why: Revision is a skill that needs rewarding feedback.
- [rule] Place a reward notice on a board or in a rumour system so that posting it prompts certain NPCs to come forward with information. When: Quest design where information is held by unknown NPCs. Why: A posted reward gives NPCs a motive to act, so the player receives leads without direct contact.
- [rule] Reward a confirmed theory by unlocking the next location or suspect.
- [rule] Reward players who test a hypothesis against alternative explanations, and do not reward those who accept the first explanation offered. When: Designing deduction challenges where players must judge a claim. Why: Testing alternatives is the step that separates a trick from a real cause.
- [heuristic] Reward players who track the in-game clock and map distances, since they can check claims that others cannot.
- [heuristic] Reward a player for noticing that an object was sunk, burned or moved, and for finding the leftover trace of that action. When: when designing disposal puzzles.
- [heuristic] Route bait replies and follow-up messages through intermediaries so the investigator stays hidden. When: The investigator must avoid revealing their identity or the test. Why: Hidden senders keep the target and other parties from learning they are being tested.
- [heuristic] A single matching reply is not conclusive when the predicted description is generic enough that many people could fit it. When: The bait's criteria match a common profile. Why: Coincidental matches are likely when the description is broad.
- [heuristic] Trust a witness's observation more when a second independent record, such as a photograph taken in calm conditions, supports it. When: Evaluating a stressful witness account. Why: Independent records offset distortion in the witness's memory.
- [procedure] 1) Enumerate rival theories that fit the clues. 2) Test each against the same evidence, including the same timing and objects. 3) Test what each theory requires to be true, and reject one whose requirements are absurd. 4) Check that its reasoning is internally consistent. When: several explanations fit the same clues. Why: comparing theories on identical evidence exposes weak ones.
- [procedure] 1) Derive what a theory would observably cause. 2) Design a public bait that only the predicted witness would answer. 3) Treat a matching reply as support and silence as inconclusive. When: Two explanations predict different observable events that a public call could reveal. Why: A bait filters for the predicted response, separating rival explanations without direct proof.
- [procedure] Pair a public bait with a direct request to a better-placed informant to strengthen the test. When: A second informant can confirm or refute the prediction. Why: A well-placed source gives stronger evidence than an anonymous public reply.
- [procedure] 1) Identify the lead. 2) Find an unnoticed clue that bears on it. 3) Test the clue directly, such as by sending a helper to check. 4) Keep or drop the lead by the result. When: A lead is plausible but unconfirmed. Why: Direct tests separate real leads from decoys without relying on the most striking evidence.
- [procedure] 1) State the meaning an item seems to have. 2) Check it against domain knowledge or a second source. 3) If the inference fails, accept the narrower fact the item actually supports. When: A clue seems to settle a point and must be verified before acceptance. Why: Expert knowledge or a second source can overturn a plausible reading of an object.
- [tradeoff] A bait must describe the predicted witness closely enough to be recognized, but omitting details limits the risk of alerting authorities or the target. When: Designing a public call that others will read. Why: Too much detail exposes the investigation; too little draws no reply from the right person.
- [pitfall] Many replies may arrive; each must be checked against the prediction, not accepted just because it suits the working theory. When: A public test draws several responses. Why: Confirmation bias makes a loosely matching reply look like proof.
- [pitfall] A plausible theory about where an item went stays a plan to search, not a finding, until physical evidence confirms it. When: Reasoning about a drain, gutter or other hiding place. Why: Confident reasoning can run ahead of the evidence.

## anomalies
- [mechanism] A sudden rise in spending that departs from a stable routine prompts questions, and the person who caused it must produce an explanation the household will accept. When: A person's spending or resource use departs sharply from an established pattern. Why: Observers expect consistency, and a departure without a credible reason invites scrutiny.
- [mechanism] Scattered debris on a road can reveal that an apparently accidental breakdown was engineered, which tests the official account of events. When: When an incident is reported as accidental and the scene holds physical traces. Why: Deliberate placement of debris leaves physical evidence that contradicts an accident.
- [rule] An act or item that a hypothesis cannot explain shows the theory is incomplete and a key factor is missing; list every such unexplained item.
- [rule] Hold stray anomalies aside as open questions, neither discarding nor forcing them to fit the theory, and treat any explanation as a hypothesis. When: A theory's main chain holds but a few items do not yet fit it, or one element of a clue cannot be explained. Why: Unexplained items may later fit the revealed method or prove coincidence, so they remain testable; early conclusions can close off the true meaning.
- [rule] Record every clue, even minor acts or anomalies like a figure entering a building, a key turning or a stumble, since omitted sightings can matter later. When: during observation. Why: A recorded small anomaly lets a later investigation reopen the incident and resolve its meaning.
- [rule] Record every physical trace of a disguise before removing any layer, because washing or stripping destroys the evidence. Why: Removal alters the physical evidence the investigator may later need to check.
- [rule] Give registries and ledgers an access log so NPCs who view restricted documents leave a trace the player can follow to a hidden party. When: Designing a town record, guild ledger or similar archive in a game. Why: Traceable access turns record checks into a usable investigation path.
- [rule] Give players an item whose origin is not shown, so they must decide whether to reveal it to NPCs.
- [heuristic] An item that does not fit its owner, such as a costume bill or something too new or careless for them, signals a hidden motive or second life worth probing. When: Reviewing items found in a scene. Why: Mismatches between an object and its context reveal something the owner would not normally have. (x2)
- [heuristic] A violent reaction to accusation shows a touched nerve, not proven guilt, and the same outburst may read as honesty or guilt depending on the watcher's mood. When: a witness or suspect reacts strongly to questioning or an accusation. Why: emotion reveals sensitivity, not factual truth; the watcher's mood colours the interpretation.
- [heuristic] Out-of-place traces or visual details, such as dress or bearing breaking social rules, mark a clue or person; compare each find with what its setting should contain. Why: Settings with exclusion rules make mismatched appearance readable at a glance.
- [heuristic] An unnatural figure in a personal record, such as a suspiciously round sum, can signal a hidden transaction worth investigating. When: When a record does not fit the owner's known habits or position. Why: Real balances and payments are rarely round, so the deviation invites a question.
- [heuristic] When a normally tame creature or person acts out of character, look for a specific cause such as provocation, interference or outside control before accepting the change. When: A familiar actor behaves in a way that contradicts its known habits. Why: Behavior that breaks a known pattern usually has a reason that can be found.
- [heuristic] An item absent from a suspect's possessions, despite witness belief that they owned it, is itself a clue that should be investigated rather than ignored. When: When witnesses expect a suspect to possess an item that is not found among their effects. Why: The absence contradicts witness expectations and may point to concealment or disposal.
- [heuristic] Leave one anomaly unresolved on purpose. It holds tension and can be tied to a later reveal.
- [procedure] 1) Identify the last known location of a missing person. 2) Collect institutional records such as payments, registers and staff contacts. 3) Use each record to name the next location or person. 4) Confirm each stage with a second source. When: a person has gone missing after moving across several places. Why: Chained records reconstruct a route when no one saw the whole journey.
- [procedure] 1) Define search criteria precisely, such as region, date range, age, stated cause and the signer's qualifications. 2) Filter the records by those criteria. 3) Collect the candidates. 4) Check each candidate against the known story before travelling to it. When: When one event must be found among many similar records.
- [pitfall] A vague warning can confuse rather than guide, so the clue must eventually become recoverable for the audience.
- [fact] A record entry that sits in the wrong section of its original can expose a forgery even when its stated date looks correct.

## puzzle state and logic
- [mechanism] A puzzle system can track a knowledge set for each secret, so an NPC knows a hidden location only if a named source told them. When: designing secrets that spread through NPCs. Why: tracked sources make knowledge spread consistently and checkable.
- [mechanism] A container can carry a state flag, such as handled or untouched, and the player must preserve that state for the evidence to stay valid.
- [rule] State a challenge's constraints exactly before the attempt begins, so players can judge whether any workaround is allowed. When: designing a locked-room or restricted-resource puzzle. Why: Written rules make the solution checkable and fair.
- [rule] Track 'death looks natural' separately from 'death was caused' in game state, so foul play must be established before the motive board opens.
- [rule] Represent torn or split items with edge data so the player must rotate and match fragments to confirm they belong together. When: Designing a fragment or clue item. Why: Fitting gives the player a checkable, physical test.
- [rule] Let the player's own action, such as knocking on a surface, trigger the proof, so the reveal is interactive rather than read off. When: designing the moment a hidden truth is exposed. Why: The player feels responsible for the discovery.
- [heuristic] Partial text becomes meaningful only when combined with a second clue found elsewhere, so split key information across locations.
- [heuristic] A reconstruction puzzle can ask the player to order a set of tracks and check the answer against the ground layout. When: When designing a tracking or deduction puzzle. Why: The ground layout gives an objective check.
- [heuristic] An elaborate mystery rests on one simple core fact; its complexity is a set of distracting details arranged around that fact.
- [heuristic] A path leading to a secluded spot is a shortcut for anyone using that spot, so the path alone does not identify one particular user.
- [heuristic] Keep small past facts on record, since a fact from an earlier case may bear on a present one and can be drawn out when needed. When: A past fact may relate to a current matter.
- [heuristic] A test-action mechanic lets players compare a character's attributes with evidence on site, turning a stat check into an investigation.
- [heuristic] A requirement narrows the field only when requirements stack, since many people may share any single requirement.
- [heuristic] Gate a puzzle's final solution behind separate checkable conditions, such as motive, means and access, so the player confirms each before solving. When: Designing multi-step deduction puzzles. Why: Each confirmed condition gives feedback and narrows the answer.
- [procedure] 1) Tally every symbol's occurrences over the full message, noting the rarest ones too. 2) Equate the top symbol with the language's most frequent letter. 3) Verify the guess using another pattern, such as how often the symbol appears in doubled pairs. When: simple substitution ciphers with enough text for counts to be meaningful; avoid short messages or ciphers designed to flatten frequencies.
- [pitfall] Conditions can change between events, so an assumed snow cover or gap must be checked against the actual record before it anchors a window.
- [pitfall] A predicted item may not exist, and an intended break in the pattern can make the prediction wrong. When: Predicting a missing item from a sequence. Why: Patterns are not always continuous by design.
- [pitfall] A sequence of only two or three points can fit many rules, so extrapolation from it is unreliable. When: Inferring a pattern from few records. Why: Too few points underdetermine the rule.
- [pitfall] A list of profile conditions written after a suspect is chosen can be tailored to fit that person. When: Building a suspect profile. Why: Post hoc conditions confirm the chosen suspect instead of testing them.

## quest stakes and rewards
- [mechanism] When an NPC's stake changes, such as a rival's consent or a contract's worth after a world event, their motive weakens or intensifies, and players can recompute it. When: Designing rivalries that evolve over a playthrough, or a world event alters an agreement's worth. Why: Incentives follow the current value of what is at stake, so changing stakes shift which rival has a motive.
- [mechanism] An NPC who abruptly drops a demand signals that they learned something hidden, so the reversal can serve as a clue.
- [mechanism] A reversed expectation at the end, such as a secured object found gone, produces a sense of impossible disappearance. When: a locked or secured setup is followed by a surprise. Why: the expected state is overturned after the object seemed secured.
- [mechanism] A recurring demand from an authority figure for a quick result can act as a timed pressure mechanic that changes available dialogue but not the underlying facts. Why: pressure alters options without altering evidence.
- [mechanism] Unresolved disputes compound over years or decades, with an early issue growing more violent until it erupts into a public crisis. When: a community dispute is left unaddressed. Why: Each unresolved wrong adds grievance.
- [behavior] An opponent who has planned around a condition, such as a rival being incapacitated, will act on that assumption and may expose their plan while doing so. When: an antagonist counts on a safeguard failing. Why: they commit to the expected condition and act on it.
- [rule] Delay a completion reward until the quest-giver has been briefed, so payment follows completed duty. When: designing mission rewards.
- [rule] Tie a mercy choice to a condition the player can check, such as whether the victim is still at risk.
- [rule] Give each NPC in a conspiracy system a concrete reason to join. Players can then test a theory by checking each NPC's gain or loss before acting on it. When: designing a game with hidden conspiracies. Why: Motivated conspirators make the theory checkable and believable.
- [rule] Post bounty notices in towns describing a missing item or NPC, and let rumours spread so other NPCs carry the lead.
- [rule] Allow an arrest on a case board only when motive, opportunity, and instrument are all filled. When: When designing an accusation mechanic. Why: Requiring key elements prevents premature accusation.
- [rule] Make opposing NPC factions co-own a shared task, such as a bridge repair, before a locked area opens.
- [rule] Let an appeal to a higher authority revise a severe sentence into a lesser one, such as exile.
- [heuristic] Show contract terms and the consequence of failure on screen, so players can see what is at stake before committing.
- [tradeoff] An arrest system where the accused must prove innocence can add restricted access and time limits, such as limited visits, that shape how the player can respond. When: Designing detention or legal-process mechanics. Why: Limits on access create pressure and force the player to prioritize actions.
- [tradeoff] Execution permanently ends an accused character, while release on a condition removes them from the play space instead of killing them. When: choosing an ending for an accused character. Why: the two outcomes differ in finality and in whether the character survives.

## accusation and theory building
- [mechanism] A hierarchy clue such as a uniform or footprint can be overturned by a later witness, forcing players to revise their working hypotheses about who is in charge.
- [mechanism] A final accusation that requires evidence in a set order leaves the case unproven when items are wrong or missing. When: a player must present clues to win an accusation scene.
- [rule] Make an accusation require proof across several categories, such as an object, a witness statement and a timing, rather than a single suspicion. When: designing an accusation mechanic. Why: a single suspicion is too easy to challenge.
- [rule] Build a mystery from a fixed set of explanations, each predicting a different clue, so the player can rule them out one by one.
- [rule] Record evidence as smudged or partial, so players must combine several weak clues rather than rely on one clean result. When: Designing an evidence system for players.
- [rule] Have an NPC point at a rival with a motive, then let the player check the claim by finding another record.
- [rule] Absence from a scene only exonerates a person if their timing is firmly fixed; never suspect or clear someone before knowing where they were. When: Judging whether a witness could have committed an event. Why: A person cannot be in two places at once, so unfixed timing proves nothing.
- [heuristic] Pick the target the opponent least expects, while still meeting the practical needs of the attack, by reasoning from the opponent's expected form of attack. When: the opponent's model of the attack is known and can be exploited. Why: Unexpected targets face fewer prepared defences.
- [heuristic] Conclude an event from several small anomalies that point to one cause, rather than from any single unusual sign. When: Reading a scene from weak, individually ambiguous signals. Why: Converging signs are more reliable than any one of them alone.
- [heuristic] Pace an investigation by alternating small confirmations with a setback that leaves doubt, keeping the player uncertain about the next step. When: when structuring a long investigation.
- [procedure] 1) Treat the case as open by default. 2) Build a profile of the culprit as a list of conditions. 3) Narrow the field only through evidence matching those conditions. When: No motive is yet established. Why: Keeping the field open avoids concluding too early.
- [tradeoff] A confrontation that lets the player stake partial evidence trades a possible gain in pressure against a penalty if the bluff is called. When: designing a confrontation mechanic. Why: Raising stakes rewards confidence but risks loss if the opponent is not fooled.
- [pitfall] A detective can be right about one fact and wrong about its meaning, so each conclusion needs its own test. When: An investigator's reasoning is partly sound. Why: Correct premises can still support a wrong inference.
- [pitfall] A theory built on early assumptions can persist after evidence weakens it, because it already shapes how new facts are read.
- [pitfall] A theory that explains one anomaly while ignoring another is incomplete, and a theory that explains every anomaly is not thereby proven. Why: Coverage of the evidence is necessary but does not confirm the explanation.

## visible deadlines and progress
- [mechanism] Timed messages that delay a rescue or investigation's outcome make the player act while still uncertain.
- [mechanism] Setting a time limit on recovering an item creates urgency, since each delay lets the item be copied or moved. When: When an item can change state or location over time. Why: Elapsed time increases the chance the item is lost or duplicated.
- [mechanism] Trail signs that fade as light changes impose a time limit, forcing trackers to act before the signs disappear. When: when a tracking chase takes place in changing light. Why: The fading evidence creates urgency.
- [rule] Show a clock during timed sequences, and let players extend it by completing tasks or by delaying the opposing character. When: Designing escape or heist challenges.
- [rule] Do not hold back action when a victim may be in immediate danger, even if surveillance is still incomplete. When: A victim may be in immediate danger.
- [rule] In poison or trap systems, make opportunity windows visible, showing when an object is unattended and who can reach it, so players can reason about timing. When: when designing timed hazards or hidden acts. Why: Visible windows let players work out the timing rather than guess.
- [heuristic] Show a quest's remaining time, such as days left, and let players pick sub-goals before expiry; pressure grows as each interval passes. When: Designing a quest with a fixed end date. Why: A visible countdown turns the timeline into urgency and makes choices meaningful.
- [heuristic] Making a slow process's progress readable, such as a burned-down candle, lets players reconstruct when a trigger happened, so the mechanism itself becomes a clue.
- [heuristic] Add a world rule change or deadline, such as a decree or curfew, that makes an NPC's death advantageous to a particular suspect. When: when designing motives for a mystery.
- [heuristic] Pair an external threat that multiplies copies with urgency, so the player must act before the mechanism is fully proven.
- [tradeoff] A short deadline makes players act before evidence is certain, which speeds play but raises the chance they commit to a wrong trail. When: timed investigation or rescue scenarios.
- [pitfall] No-slack plans fail at the first setback; one delay, such as a late arrival, can shift windows other actors depend on, so every timeline needs spare time. When: Timed sequences where each leg is timed exactly or several actors depend on one another. Why: Any delay pushes the whole plan past its deadline or shifts the window the rest of the plan depends on. (x2)
- [pitfall] A fixed waiting deadline gives the culprit time to flee, and a witness's mental state can shift during the wait. When: Setting a delay before a reveal. Why: Time lets the suspect act and changes the witness's reliability.
- [pitfall] Repeating the same threat without change dulls tension; each beat should escalate or shift the threat. Why: the audience adapts to an unchanged threat and stops reacting to it.

## stealth and ambush tactics
- [mechanism] Cutting a room's lights at a chosen moment, especially its single controllable power source, removes sighted opponents' advantage, letting players who rely on sound or touch gain the upper hand. When: a standoff or fight in a room with lights or one controllable power element. Why: Darkness removes visual information and shifts advantage to whoever does not depend on sight.
- [mechanism] A sudden inner warning prompting immediate withdrawal can save the party just before violence strikes; tying it to past cases makes it read as learned, not random.
- [mechanism] When a character loses a short interval of control and traces of the character's own actions appear afterward, players discover a hidden action through evidence.
- [mechanism] A test that works only when the subject can see it makes the outcome depend on line of sight, which a player can exploit by repositioning.
- [rule] Design puzzles where players anticipate a rival's move from known traits rather than from a visible clue. When: when building a puzzle around an opponent.
- [rule] Use ambient warnings such as npc unease or glimpses of an unseen threat so players sense danger before they can act on it. When: when building tension in a level or scene.
- [rule] Let a new arrival trigger a change in the active plan, so the player must adapt the search. When: a visitor appears during an investigation. Why: Changing plans keeps the scene responsive.
- [rule] Offer a choice during a wait, such as holding a door shut or retreating to another room, so the suspense is interactive. When: the player is waiting for an approaching threat. Why: the player can respond while uncertain.
- [heuristic] Perpetrators choose the moment to act when the target's activity is lowest, and they pick a time that leaves the most margin for escape. When: Planning when an action should occur in a scenario. Why: Quiet periods reduce the chance of interruption and extra time improves the escape margin.
- [procedure] 1) Hide concealed where you can see the room, watching the fixed hiding place from spaced positions so watchers don't collide and one can move while another observes. 2) Wait until the target commits to retrieving or exposing the item. 3) Move in once the target is committed and the guard drops. When: a target must return to a fixed, known hiding place to retrieve or expose an item. Why: Timing the strike to the exposure moment removes the target's chance to resist or flee. (x2)
- [procedure] 1) Identify the defender's expected form of attack. 2) List candidate sites that suit your craft and resemble your starting region. 3) Narrow them by explicit criteria. 4) Prefer routes that are short and free of hazards.
- [pitfall] Raising a signal for help after a disposal draws outside attention to the place the actor wanted kept quiet. When: when an actor wants a scene to stay unnoticed.
- [pitfall] A suspect who checks the surroundings before exposing the item may escape, and crowds or passersby can force delays that blow the stakeout. When: Running a stakeout in a public place.

## wounds and weapons
- [mechanism] Physical damage to an everyday object or weapon persists after the blow, such as a split; its state records how it was used and can be checked against an account of the attack. When: An ordinary object is used in a fight, or items persist after combat. Why: Damage persists after the act; physical state can serve as evidence.
- [mechanism] A damage system that records the pattern of a wound lets players deduce the weapon or creature from the detail of the injury. Why: the injury's shape carries information about its cause.
- [mechanism] The entry angle of a blade shows which side it was driven from; a slanted stroke cannot easily come from the opposite side. When: the attacker's position is in doubt. Why: entry geometry limits which positions could have produced the wound.
- [rule] Keep the cause of a wound separate from the cause of death, since a wound made during life can coexist with signs of drowning. When: when a body shows both an injury and a fatal condition.
- [rule] If a weapon's state is shown early, such as an empty magazine or removed cartridges, later combat must respect that state. When: a weapon's ammunition or condition has been shown to the player. Why: a displayed state is a fact the player will check against later play.
- [rule] Bag a weapon in cloth to protect fingerprints, and record any hair or fibres stuck to it before further handling. When: A weapon found at a scene may carry prints or fibres. Why: Handling without protection can destroy prints and loose trace material.
- [heuristic] Let players notice when an NPC displays the wrong limb or side, as a clue that something is being hidden. When: an NPC performs a display involving a body part. Why: A mismatched display gives players a visible inconsistency to investigate.
- [heuristic] Compare the weapon used against the other objects present; a mismatch with the violence of the wound or the available tools is a first anomaly to investigate. When: the weapon looks wrong for the injury or the scene. Why: An object out of place for its use can reveal who chose it and what they could do.
- [heuristic] Use warnings instead of attacks when the antagonist aims to deter and the target needs a real choice. When: The antagonist wants deterrence rather than a kill. Why: A warning gives the target a decision before any harm occurs.
- [heuristic] An attack usually produces more than one blow, and a second blow often lands on a raised arm defending the head, so a wound pattern suggests likely injuries. When: Inferring a suspect's injuries from an attack. Why: Defensive posture during a multi-strike attack leaves predictable injuries.
- [procedure] 1) Identify the physical force the blow required. 2) List which suspects could generate that force. 3) List which suspects would choose the weapon used. 4) Keep only suspects who pass both tests. When: a weapon choice and the force of the injury both point to a suspect. Why: Strength and intent are separate constraints, and a theory must satisfy both.

## evidence authenticity and provenance
- [rule] Make a clue meaningless alone meaningful when an independent second source, such as a directory, map, ledger, NPC or object, matches it; mark single-source clues uncertain. When: When designing clues that should gain meaning from a second source. Why: Corroboration converts an isolated detail into a verifiable connection.
- [rule] Store an item's appearance, such as colour, shape and wear, as data so that a later clue can refer back to it.
- [rule] Take a reference sample openly and beforehand when a suspect's presence must later be tied to a surface they touched. When: A physical trace will later be compared with a suspect's reference sample. Why: A sample taken in a social setting can be questioned, and the reference should be clean.
- [rule] Give players a way to verify a title through records, witnesses or a seal, so that acceptance has to be earned. When: an NPC claims rank or lineage. Why: Verification lets players test claims instead of accepting them on status.
- [heuristic] A proof the audience can watch being performed is more convincing than a verbal argument. When: Delivering the key evidence of a mystery. Why: Seeing a test reproduced builds trust more than an explanation does.
- [heuristic] Let players test a recovered item cheaply, so that a fake reads as a hypothesis to check rather than a settled fact.
- [procedure] 1) Record where and by whom an object was found. 2) Have a witness present at discovery. 3) Track who controls keys and containers. 4) Note the object's specific contents. When: When an object's origin or who had access to it must be established. Why: A witnessed, documented discovery with controlled access creates a traceable chain of custody.
- [procedure] 1) Trace an object back to its maker or order. 2) Match the order to a specific description. 3) Confirm when it was worn through a witness account. When: An object can be linked to a purchase or a date. Why: Provenance links an object to a person and a time.
- [pitfall] A date given with a precise day may still be wrong if the record behind it was not written at the time. When: Relying on a precise date from a later record or recollection. Why: Memory and later reconstruction introduce error even when the date looks exact.
- [fact] A fingerprint trace on a surface lasts until something touches or rubs it away, so it works as a durable physical trace. When: A surface is left undisturbed after being touched. Why: Unless the surface is handled by others, the trace persists.

## fair clue design
- [rule] A red herring must be fair: the clues that expose its falsity should already be available to the player. When: Designing a misleading but reasonable conclusion before the truth is revealed. Why: A decoy that can only be disproved by unseen information feels like a cheat.
- [rule] Build puzzles where one clue has two valid readings, and let a later clue select the correct one.
- [rule] Let a puzzle clue point to two or three locations or characters, each confirmed by a different in-game check.
- [rule] Give a concealed clue a unique tag such as a seal or label so the game can verify it, and do not let a generic version of the object solve the puzzle. When: When a clue must link to a specific person, item or event. Why: A generic container or object cannot prove identity; only a unique mark can.
- [rule] Spread several minor traits across a level, such as a scar, a gait and a voice, so that together they identify the culprit. When: designing a mystery level. Why: Players must combine clues, which rewards careful note-taking.
- [rule] For each clue object, ask who placed it where it was found and why, before treating its location as evidence of its owner's involvement. When: Evaluating physical evidence that appears in several locations. Why: Duplicates and planted items can mislead about who used them.
- [heuristic] Place valuable loot in the same container as a small story-relevant item, so the player must decide what to keep. When: Designing containers in a dungeon or level. Why: The choice forces the player to evaluate the small item against the reward.
- [heuristic] Plant an anomaly early, such as a missing physical feature, so the later reveal pays it off as a payoff rather than a surprise. When: Designing a twist that depends on a physical clue. Why: A set-up anomaly makes the reveal feel earned and fair.
- [heuristic] Place one clue in each suspect's room in an investigation hub so each clue shifts blame, forcing the player to re-evaluate the household.
- [procedure] 1) Stage an early clue that clears the obvious suspect. 2) Later, stage a dense set of physical traces without explaining them. 3) Pay off each trace at the reveal. Why: suspicion then moves to the real culprits as evidence accumulates.

## timelines and event dating
- [model] A title and estate pass along a fixed line of heirs, and the line can shift if the first heir fails to inherit. When: A death gives someone a claim through inheritance or succession. Why: The order of heirs determines who benefits from each death.
- [behavior] An observer who cannot see a scene can order the events by their sounds, using each sound as a marker for the sequence. When: Visual access to an event is blocked or partial. Why: Audible cues such as shots and cries give a usable timeline.
- [heuristic] Leave one supernatural event unexplained while giving other events testable natural and hidden human causes, so the player must weigh the uncanny against the human. When: designing a mystery with supernatural elements. Why: A single open event keeps the player uncertain and invites weighing of explanations.
- [heuristic] Look for periodic patterns, such as events recurring at fixed intervals or on the same weekday, to predict and check dates. When: Events recur in a case and their dates are uncertain. Why: A regular interval lets the investigator predict the next occurrence and verify the timeline.
- [heuristic] Use fixed timeline windows, such as a half-hour gap, so the player must reconstruct where a suspect was during that window. When: when designing investigation timelines. Why: Fixed windows give the player a concrete period to reconstruct.
- [heuristic] Schedule an interruption, such as a rival entering, to force a decision at a critical moment. When: when designing tense confrontations. Why: A timed interruption compresses the player's decision window.
- [procedure] 1) Anchor the timeline on an event with a certain date, ideally recorded in more than one place. 2) Date other events against the anchor. 3) Verify each key onset date against a second record before building reasoning on it. When: A solution depends on precise timing of several events, who was where and when, or records conflict. Why: A fixed reference orders scattered dates, and a chain of dates is only as strong as its weakest link.
- [tradeoff] Explaining a plan before a stakeout turns the final scene into a test of that plan; hiding it turns the scene into a reveal.
- [fact] Dates tied to known events such as weddings, sailings or recorded visits are more reliable than dates that depend on a witness's guess about another event. When: Assessing how reliable a witness's date is. Why: A date fixed by an independent event can be checked, while a guessed date inherits the uncertainty of the guess.
- [fact] Succession rules can change through legal challenges, so the line of heirs may not be stable. When: Relying on a fixed inheritance order as a clue. Why: Disputes over birth or marriage proof can alter who inherits.

## warnings and enemy defense
- [mechanism] A cluster of defensive measures around one location signals the real objective, since each measure can be read as harmless alone. When: a boss or trap-setter places defenses. Why: Players infer intent from repeated defensive placement around one spot.
- [rule] Let a witness who saw the player act give a conditional warning, so the player may heed it or ignore it and face later consequences. When: An NPC has seen the player's hidden action. Why: The choice makes the warning a decision, not just information.
- [rule] Offer a bluff option whose claimed evidence can be checked later, so a bluff can be called and punished. When: designing interrogation choices for the player. Why: A checkable bluff makes the player's claim a risk rather than a free threat.
- [rule] Make sensor loss a designed failure state so that players must act on partial information. When: A player depends on a planted sensor.
- [rule] Make staged evidence verifiable through a second channel, so the player can confirm or refute it independently of the first source.
- [heuristic] Torn paper recovered from debris can be a multi-step task, where the assembled document unlocks the final dialogue.
- [heuristic] Design each deduction branch to lead to an informative answer rather than an empty dead end, so each elimination advances the player. When: Building a deduction puzzle with several explanations. Why: A dead end that gives no new information leaves the player with no progress, so elimination feels wasted.
- [heuristic] Give the player early requests that seem harmless, such as money or shoes, and let them return later as escape tools or disguises. Why: Ordinary-seeming requests planted early pay off as concealed means when recalled.
- [pitfall] A deceptive pretext can distort the judgment of helpers who cooperate because they trust the investigator, so the cost of deception falls on them too. When: An investigator uses a false role to gain access. Why: Helpers who trust the investigator may act on that trust in ways that later cause harm.
- [pitfall] A warning that always proves right removes tension; let some warnings be wrong or ignored, with the cost of ignoring them made clear.

## player journals and logs
- [mechanism] A log of recorded reactions lets the player build their own reading rather than accepting the first explanation offered.
- [mechanism] Recording timestamps for game events lets players see gaps, revivals and cycles, including hidden triggers that reset after a quiet period. When: when a recurring phenomenon is meant to be discovered. Why: Comparing dates reveals a pattern that a single event hides.
- [behavior] Players notice hidden connections when NPC actions fall inside the same visible time window, especially on a timeline view. When: The game shows NPC actions on a timeline or in shared in-game time. Why: Shared timing invites players to infer a link between actions.
- [rule] Limit the player's journal to the player's own observations, and deliver NPC statements through dialogue with the speaker named. When: Designing a journal or case log for the player. Why: Keeping sources distinct preserves the player's reasoning about who said what.
- [rule] Let the player choose an investigative frame, such as procedural or intuitive, so each frame highlights different clues in the same scene. When: in an accusation system.
- [rule] Organise the player's notes around three questions (who, why, how) so their theories stay ordered. When: Providing a journal or notes system for a mystery. Why: A fixed structure keeps theories sorted by question.
- [rule] A quest log can split one investigation into tasks such as witness canvass, record checks, background reports and surveillance, each with a clear time window or location. When: Designing a multi-step investigation quest. Why: Scoped tasks give the player concrete next actions.
- [rule] In multiplayer deduction, give each player a private clue ledger and a clear rule for when clues become shared with others. When: several players investigate one case. Why: private clues keep reasoning independent while sharing stays controlled.
- [heuristic] Keeping a search quiet, so suspects do not know a fragment matters, can add stealth and tension. When: When designing stealth or tension around a hidden clue.

## pursuit
- [mechanism] A long chase exhausts the pursuer's transport and budget, because horses and drivers tire and each extra mile costs money. Why: Pursuit consumes finite resources at a rate set by its length.
- [mechanism] A timed group decision can show a countdown and reactive NPC chatter that changes as progress is made. When: Designing a group decision or timed puzzle with NPCs. Why: Changing chatter makes progress and time pressure visible.
- [rule] In stealth or urban detective levels, make pursuers react to sudden stops and doubling back, so players can learn to test for a tail. When: Designing followers.
- [rule] Have the player keep pace with an NPC while staying out of sight, using crowds as a cover mechanic.
- [rule] A stealth tail mission can model a building with two exits and a restricted room, requiring the player to cover both routes or use a disguise item. When: Designing a stealth pursuit set in public spaces. Why: Two exits make a single watch insufficient.
- [rule] Post a partner at a wall or doorway to cover an exit while the follower keeps the target in view. When: A single pursuer risks losing the target.
- [heuristic] Choose a response by weighing the resources at hand against how fast the threat is spreading. When: A threat is growing and resources are limited.
- [heuristic] Place doorways, shop fronts and crates along routes a player follows, so they have places to hide from a target who looks back. When: designing a route where the player tails an NPC. Why: nearby cover lets the player break line of sight.
- [procedure] 1) Move the player into a physical activity or new location 2) Deliver fresh material 3) Return the player to the puzzle with that material. Why: A stall needs a new stimulus to keep the player moving.

## false solutions
- [behavior] Antagonists can keep a target engaged and unsuspecting by staging spectacles, offering plausible alternative theories, and placing blame on other suspects. When: A target must be kept interested or misled while the real plan proceeds. Why: Plausible decoys give the target a reasonable but wrong conclusion, so attention stays away from the true threat.
- [rule] Reward a player who finds the one premise that conflicts with another clue, such as an NPC's route contradicting a map.
- [heuristic] Let a misleading message be true in one sense, such as a note that really concerns the escape, so it misdirects without lying. Why: A literally true clue misdirects the player more convincingly than a false one.
- [heuristic] Build one false answer that authorities accept and make it cheap to act on, so the player's test of it becomes the story. When: when designing a mystery with a wrong solution. Why: A cheap false answer invites action, and testing it drives the plot forward.
- [procedure] 1) Make the clue source trustworthy. 2) Set up a plausible wrong suspect with fitting evidence. 3) Let the player reach a confident wrong conclusion. 4) Reinterpret the same clues to reveal the true cause. When: Building a mystery whose solution must surprise the player. Why: Early clues that are later reinterpreted reward attentive players and keep the reveal fair.
- [procedure] 1) Let the player make a first accusation that a hidden mechanic later removes, such as a self-destructing document. 2) Save the true answer for a late, staged confrontation.
- [tradeoff] A plausible environmental decoy, such as a curse or storm, misleads well, but the player must be able to disprove it with a later clue. When: Choosing a false explanation for a mystery. Why: A decoy that is plausible misleads, but one never disprovable undermines trust in the design.
- [pitfall] A detail that seems to rule out a mechanism can cause designers and players to ignore that mechanism entirely, hiding the true cause. When: An observed detail appears to exclude a possible explanation, such as a dry object suggesting no water contact. Why: The assumption that the mechanism is absent stops it from being considered, so the true cause stays hidden.

## hidden stashes and containers
- [definition] Decoy loot is a recovered object that misdirects the player about how the target was actually taken, so the real method stays hidden after the item is found.
- [mechanism] A disguise mechanic that lets the player take an NPC's place for a set period makes guards and servants react to the new identity.
- [rule] Place misleading evidence, such as a stolen item tied to a dismissed suspect, in areas the player visits early, before the true culprit is revealed. When: Designing a mystery in a level or dungeon. Why: Early exposure lets the decoy mislead the player before the real answer appears.
- [heuristic] Let players expose a mimic or impostor through footwear, fingerprints or inventory mismatches, so the disguise is solvable from clues already in the world.
- [procedure] 1) Hide key evidence in an ordinary container, such as one with a false bottom. 2) Mark it with a loose or misaligned element a careful player can notice. 3) Verify the identification against a unique mark. When: When designing a concealed clue inside an interactable container. Why: Subtle visible signs reward close inspection without making the item trivial to find, and a unique mark makes identification checkable.
- [procedure] 1) Remove each object from the container in turn. 2) Group the objects. 3) Check papers in order, from top layer down. 4) Turn each item over to read its reverse. 5) Return every object to its original place. When: When a player searches a container that may hide evidence. Why: Methodical emptying finds concealed items, and restoring the arrangement avoids alerting the owner.
- [tradeoff] A captive antagonist controlling an encounter through a hidden item keeps threat alive after capture, but the item must be discoverable or the threat feels unfair. When: designing a dungeon boss held in a cell or cage. Why: Fair discoverability balances tension against player frustration.
- [pitfall] Players who accept a recovered objective item at face value stop investigating how the original theft happened, so the real method goes unseen. When: investigation puzzles where the target is recovered early. Why: recovering the item feels like closure.

## quest flags and replay
- [mechanism] Recording an item's removal as a state flag implies a second actor and gives the player a concrete lead.
- [mechanism] When a captured NPC refuses to answer, the quest flag stays locked, so the player must find another route, such as a document that the refusal protected. When: An NPC refuses to give information in a quest. Why: The refusal blocks the flag, so the quest can only advance through an alternative path.
- [mechanism] A prisoner held in an intermediate state, neither dead nor free, can regain a retrial when a stated condition is met. When: a judicial outcome that is neither final nor clear. Why: the condition keeps a path to retrial open.
- [rule] Keep a timeline of world events the designer can replay, so an apparent NPC delay can be traced back to a known cause. When: Designing a simulated world where NPC actions must be explainable. Why: A replayable record lets designers verify that delays have causes.
- [rule] Require two independent NPC witnesses before a quest flag advances, so one mistaken witness cannot trigger an accusation.
- [heuristic] Offer a replay mode that resets room conditions such as heat, locked doors and latch positions, letting players repeat or time an action under identical conditions to confirm a theory. When: when designing a deduction mechanic involving room conditions. Why: resetting state lets the player test a claim repeatedly.
- [heuristic] An absent key document can act as a quest pivot whose absence implicates a faction the player must work out. When: designing a quest built on a missing item.
- [heuristic] Reward dialogue that pushes the player to stay or accept a gift can hide a binding mechanic the player should notice later. When: Designing rewards that encourage a player to stay or accept an item.

## trials and assessments
- [mechanism] Each reconstruction trial can change an investigator's assessment, so repeated trials turn a timing claim into measured evidence. When: when testing a theory over several sessions. Why: measured results replace speculation and shift conclusions.
- [mechanism] A gate that closes a branch with no action when evidence falls below a threshold ends the matter without combat.
- [rule] Do not accept a natural cause or an impossibility until physical traces have been examined.
- [rule] Design a trial-style ordeal so that its outcome exposes the test's flaw instead of revealing the truth.
- [rule] Build trials as staged mechanics, from charge to appeal, where each stage changes what the player can do.
- [procedure] 1) Accept the witness's conditions only if she can be watched at all times. 2) Assess her mental state and involvement. 3) Judge whether her word can be trusted. When: A witness imposes conditions on her testimony. Why: Monitoring confirms the bargain is honored and reveals whether the witness is reliable.
- [tradeoff] Accepting a witness's conditions, such as a waiting period and a fixed meeting, gives time to confirm her account but also gives the suspect time to escape. When: A witness will speak only on her own terms. Why: Delay trades the witness's cooperation against the risk of losing the suspect.
- [pitfall] Holding a suspect on a weak charge risks release, and the offence may be reclassified by authorities.

## trigger and lock mechanics
- [mechanism] A small physical arrangement, such as a cord hitched to a bell, can give a hidden actor both a reason and a window to leave a room.
- [mechanism] A trap or event can chain small harmless states, such as a disabled alarm and an absent guard, so players can discover the sequence.
- [rule] Give players an examine or test action that confirms the mechanism, so they can move from hypothesis to proof. When: when designing investigation interactions.
- [heuristic] Tie a state flag to a player-visible object so the scene changes only after the player moves or interacts with it, such as a clock starting once wound. Why: Visible causes make later changes feel earned and readable.
- [pitfall] Casual handling of an object with a concealed mechanism can injure the person who inspects it. When: An object looks harmless but contains a hidden trigger. Why: The mechanism is designed to act when the object is disturbed.
- [pitfall] A door can look locked while its bolt is disabled, so an apparently locked door is not proof that the room was closed. When: Judging whether a room was sealed. Why: Visual lock state hides mechanical sabotage.
- [pitfall] Plans to disable a system depend on knowing how it is wired, and wrong assumptions about wiring can make the plan fail. When: planning to disable a system. Why: hidden wiring may not match expectations.
- [fact] Breaking a suspected hidden part and seeing the illusion end confirms that the part caused it.

## access control and enclosures
- [definition] A closed-cell escape puzzle is a locked-room problem in which the player reconstructs an exit from props, notes and a decoy placed in the room.
- [behavior] A keyhole view shows only what the viewer could see, so a blocked view means the room is unknown, not that it is clear. When: Using a keyhole or narrow gap to check a room. Why: Partial sightlines can look like confirmation when they only cover a narrow area.
- [behavior] A closed door between two rooms can make an occupant unable to hear a disturbance, so access depends on layout as well as on ability. When: When a sound or event in one room is meant to be missed by someone in an adjacent room. Why: Physical barriers limit perception and reach, so layout determines who could plausibly have acted.
- [rule] Build enclosure logic so breaches are possible and visible, otherwise a test seems to fail for no reason. When: Designing a controlled-isolation puzzle or test.
- [heuristic] Make the suspected character appear to hold exclusive access to something, while the true route runs through a helpful-seeming character. When: building a red herring. Why: Visible exclusive access draws suspicion while the real access stays hidden.
- [heuristic] A tracking mechanic such as dogs or guards can be set up so the culprit engineers it to point at an innocent party. When: when a pursuit system is part of the investigation. Why: The player must realise the pursuit was rigged rather than accept its result.
- [procedure] 1) When the trail stalls, return to the premise. 2) Re-test the starting assumption, such as the location. 3) Gather evidence on the corrected trail.

## red herring suspects
- [rule] Record each NPC sighting with a confidence value, and require a suspect description to match at least one physical clue before an arrest is possible. When: the player can arrest based on witness reports. Why: Sightings alone are weak, so a physical match guards against wrong arrests.
- [heuristic] Place a decoy figure in the bed that the player is told to trust, then reveal it as a placed prop once the escape is found. When: designing a locked room where the occupant appears to be present. Why: A trusted decoy hides the escape until its falseness is shown.
- [heuristic] Clear the most obvious suspect early with a timing alibi, so the suspect's hiding window is closed before the decoy item is found.
- [procedure] 1) List everyone close to the object or with knowledge of the plan. 2) Note each person's presence, absence and access. 3) Extend the list to people who hold information but not physical presence. When: A theft or secret depends on private knowledge or restricted access. Why: Mapping both presence and information reveals all plausible sources of a leak.
- [procedure] 1) List physical traces at the scene. 2) Infer one trait from each trace. 3) Intersect the traits into a single profile. 4) Check each candidate against that profile.
- [procedure] 1) Show that the framed party is the easiest suspect. 2) Plant physical evidence with a timing detail that rules out later planting. 3) Reopen an early dismissed clue. 4) Prove the self-framing with a reconstruction. When: Designing a mystery where the obvious suspect is innocent and the victim framed them. Why: The reveal inverts the case, and each step is visible before the final proof.
- [tradeoff] A single obvious suspect carrying most suspicious clues makes the mystery easy to read, so a real route to the answer must run through a different character. When: designing a mystery with a misleading suspect. Why: Players who see every clue pointing one way suspect a trick and stop checking alternatives.

## skill and tool gating
- [rule] Make detection of hidden items or residues depend on character skill, so the same object yields different results for different abilities. When: Designing detection of hidden items, or residues meant to reward specialist characters. Why: Specialist readings reward investment in the skill.
- [rule] Make small traces collectible only with the right tool, such as a lens or powder, so that tool choice matters. When: designing item-based investigation. Why: Gating makes tool knowledge part of the puzzle.
- [heuristic] A method requiring specialised technique implies the actor's knowledge, such as chemical knowledge, while a crafted device implies mechanical skill.
- [heuristic] Test whether a tool could have been used during an event by checking the physical actions it needs, such as the number of hands required to operate it. When: a tool is claimed to have been used during a struggle. Why: actions that were impossible during the event rule the tool out.
- [heuristic] Let a disguise or cover identity give the investigator access to a clue that would otherwise be unavailable, such as a sample of a suspect's handwriting. When: When a key clue is held by someone who would refuse direct questioning. Why: A cover role opens doors and conversations that formal questioning keeps closed.
- [heuristic] Plant the solver's key tool in plain sight early, so that it can later serve as the answer to a puzzle. Why: Early planting makes the late use feel fair.
- [pitfall] Specialist inference from an object depends on the examiner's knowledge, and a non-expert or an expert outside the object's field may learn little or give no verdict. When: Judging authenticity or origin of an object. Why: Recognising a fake depends on prior experience with that specific field.

## witness and testimony checks
- [mechanism] A witness at a fixed position reports only what that position shows, so events out of view must be inferred or left unknown. When: A witness watches from a window, tower or other restricted viewpoint. Why: A vantage explains what was seen, not what happened outside the field of view.
- [heuristic] Let the player control different characters in turn, each with different knowledge, so the same event reads differently from each viewpoint. When: Designing a multi-viewpoint mystery. Why: Differing knowledge makes one event look like different events.
- [heuristic] Expose an impostor through a skill check, such as a chess-like move or timed sequence, where a visual check cannot separate the fake from the real figure.
- [procedure] 1) Set secrecy terms before sharing anything. 2) Share conclusions in measured amounts, promising to reveal the rest later. 3) Ask about delicate matters indirectly so the witness can reject a suggestion first. When: Interviewing sympathetic witnesses who may talk too much. Why: Measured disclosure protects the investigation and gives the witness no ammunition to leak.
- [pitfall] A witness account needs independent confirmation, such as a matching physical trace, because vivid detail, pride, or a dramatic claim can make it persuasive yet inaccurate. When: A witness reports a dramatic or checkable claim from a limited vantage, or describes a close call in vivid detail. Why: Physical traces can confirm or contradict a single observer, and memory can be misremembered while self-image colors the telling.
- [pitfall] A surface handled by others loses its value as evidence, because a later trace may not be attributable to the intended person. When: A surface has been touched by several people before it is examined. Why: Foreign contact makes the trace ambiguous.
- [pitfall] A subject who notices a collection of records can change behaviour to avoid the check. When: A subject knows their traces or identity are being recorded. Why: Awareness of the check prompts evasion.

## single-marker dependence
- [heuristic] Pair markers that fail in different ways, so a single marker is not a sole source of truth and the player must reason about which indicator to trust. When: Designing evidence that should require deduction rather than direct reading. Why: Conflicting indicators force interpretation, and a single marker can be ambiguous.
- [pitfall] Building a theory on one anomaly or a timeline on one estimate makes it look firmer than it is; forcing details to fit while ignoring timeline contradictions hides problems. Why: a single clue can be coincidental, forced fits hide contradictions, and a single source provides no cross-check.
- [pitfall] Relying on a single marker or chance encounter when other routes to the target exist makes the progression feel thin. When: Designing a tracking objective. Why: Players sense when success depends on luck rather than their choices.
- [pitfall] A false alarm loses its force when repeated, so use it once or space repeated uses far apart. When: When a stop or reset event is used more than once in a scene or session. Why: Repetition makes the threat predictable, so players and agents stop reacting to it.
- [fact] A weapon that is never recovered leaves its owner unknown, and circumstantial links must then fill the gap, which can weaken the case. When: When the key physical instrument of a crime is absent from the evidence. Why: Without the instrument, its ownership cannot be proven directly.
- [fact] A torn fragment in a victim's hand that matches a suspect's garment creates a direct physical link between suspect and scene. When: When a physical fragment found at a scene matches a suspect's possession. Why: A material match ties the possession to the scene, though it does not prove who wore it at the time.

## accusation mechanics
- [mechanism] A deduction journal can require enough clue coverage for each explanation before an accusation is allowed. When: Designing a case-solving system with accusations. Why: Gating accusations on evidence coverage prevents premature or unsupported solutions.
- [mechanism] A confessor's visible disfigurement or emotional cost makes a confession carry weight, so the revealed truth feels earned rather than merely stated. When: A character confesses to a crime during a late reveal. Why: A visible cost to the confessor makes the admission feel consequential and believable.
- [heuristic] Accept a verbal promise of secrecy when a witness refuses to be bound by a sworn oath. When: A witness will not take an oath. Why: A verbal commitment keeps cooperation without forcing a refusal.
- [tradeoff] Block accusations until corroboration (witness item or second testimony) exists, or allow premature accusations with a penalty; blocking suits proof-gated games, penalties suit games allowing early accusations. When: a game requires proof before accusation or arrest, or accusations can precede complete evidence. Why: Suspicion alone cannot justify punishment; unsupported accusations should fail, building tension toward proof; a cost for premature accusation makes gathering corroboration worthwhile.
- [pitfall] Revealing your theory to win cooperation can tip off the suspect. When: Persuading witnesses to help. Why: Witnesses may repeat the theory to the people being investigated.

## combat and damage
- [mechanism] Several individually harmless failures occurring together can disable a defence that none of them would defeat alone.
- [rule] Vary how precisely npcs describe threats, so players learn to weigh tone against content. When: when writing npc warnings across a game.
- [rule] Prying or lockpicking mechanics can leave tool-specific damage patterns, so players can match found tools to damaged doors or locks. When: designing lock and damage systems. Why: Distinct marks give players a way to link evidence to a tool.
- [fact] A soft-nosed projectile flattens on impact, causing a deadly wound and leaving a deformed round that can be recovered.

## companion party mechanics
- [rule] Hide one clue from a companion NPC so the player decides when to share it with them.
- [rule] Pair companions with complementary strengths, such as a methodical fact-tracker and an intuitive noticer, because each is weak alone and effective together. Why: Each strength covers the other's blind spot.
- [rule] Let a companion flag an unusual observation, such as a missing object, so the questioning character can pursue it. When: Designing companion systems that support investigation.
- [tradeoff] Compact tools are efficient only at low and moderate detail but are still better than none; a simple fallback, such as a pocket lens, covers work beyond their range. When: when a portable toolset must be small. Why: carrying size limits what the tools can do.

## procedural rules and modes
- [rule] A game can enforce a formal rule, such as two witnesses with a clear line of sight, so the player must arrange the setup correctly to succeed. When: designing formal-procedure puzzles. Why: Enforcing the rule makes the player engage with its logic.
- [rule] Offer a sealed case file solvable by deduction alone and a second mode requiring location visits and NPC questioning. When: Designing investigation modes for players. Why: Both reasoning styles can be supported.
- [rule] Expose the governing rule to the player; an unexplained premise that decides outcomes feels arbitrary and unfair.
- [heuristic] Enforce test order so that a print check before the trace check forfeits the trace, teaching the player correct procedure.

## rules and magic limits
- [mechanism] Breaking a rule of engagement that the player set corrupts the witness record, so the player's collected data becomes less reliable. When: Players choose rules before an observation event. Why: Rule violations change what was observed and how it can be compared.
- [heuristic] Check for a human cause, such as who touched or wound an object, before attributing unexplained behavior to supernatural forces. When: An object behaves in an unexplained way. Why: Most anomalies have a mundane explanation that a witness can supply.
- [tradeoff] Make mystical effects real but bounded so players can test them, or resolve magical-seeming events with a testable physical cause, not an unexplained magic answer. When: when designing supernatural or magical-seeming elements in a mystery. (x2)
- [pitfall] A procedure that looks complicated may still be lax in practice, so complexity should not be taken as proof that a system is secure. When: Judging whether a routine or safeguard is reliable. Why: Staff or systems may skip steps even when the procedure appears elaborate.

## npc reactions
- [rule] Give evasive NPCs a dialogue state that refuses a topic, and let a narrower fact-based question unlock the answer. When: Designing NPC dialogue for a topic the NPC will not discuss directly. Why: Players can still progress by asking answerable questions, which keeps the refusal meaningful rather than a dead end.
- [rule] Track an NPC's reaction, such as a flinch or pause, as a tell, but require a fact-based confirmation before the player concludes anything from it. When: An NPC reacts visibly to a player's question. Why: Reactions suggest a lead but are ambiguous, so confirmation prevents false conclusions.
- [heuristic] Make NPC and animal reactions or animations, such as a pause before answering, ambiguous, so players must compare them with other cues before judging.

## scripted waiting
- [pitfall] A wait in which the player cannot act makes the tension feel scripted rather than interactive.
